A login folder that answers as another account drops what it kept of the old one when the profile names the new one, so a failed claim-status read can no longer date the new account's cloud credit from the old claim. The heal turn's output is read as UTF-8 with bad bytes replaced, and its process group is killed however the turn ends. `subfleet cards --help` no longer says "read only": `--refresh` may spend a heal turn. `lapsed` stays "`claude_free` or `canceled`", either on its own, now with its grounds.
- C-9.10, after the reviews of #133 (2026-10-05): a login whose only card ended unused no longer reads "reset card ended unused (…); no reset card": "no reset card" is said only when the cards block lists none. The `card-lost` and `credit-lost` lines of `status` and `subfleet cards` name the card or credit; they had read `grant`, which those warnings do not carry, and named nothing. A card or credit recorded at a lapse whose end, as last seen, had already passed is recorded `expired`, not `lapse`, so its alert no longer says it was lost with its plan (a card listed unused that ended while the reads between listed no cards, or failed, and then the plan lapsed).
- C-23.31: a lane run is recognised by the `entrypoint` Claude Code stamps on its transcript entries, not by `promptSource` (2026-10-03). The desktop app now sends its prompts as `promptSource: sdk`, so v1's rule (one or two `sdk` prompts) read desktop sessions that one or two messages started as lane runs: 5,884 of one machine's 8,202 desktop and terminal sessions, 19 of them holding pending notices, were left out of `sessions` listings, nudges, cold revives and handoff. The same rule read 3,238 `claude -p` runs (probes, one-shots, resumed lanes) as sessions. The prompt rule now reads only prompts that name no entrypoint.
- C-5.11, C-5.12: the control loop gives a worker only the live attempts a pass could act on; a quiet running attempt costs one `stat` and its share of one statement a tick, not a pass that reads 8 KB of evidence and two receipts (2026-10-02, the compute-contention study's daemon profile).

## 1. Identifiers and vocabulary

- **C-1.1** A job id is `YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS-<slug>` in local time, the v1 format, unique in the store. `<slug>` is the `-n` name or the task name, lowercased, `[a-z0-9-]` only, at most 40 characters.
- **C-1.2** An attempt id is `<job id>/a<seq>` with `seq` starting at 1.
- **C-1.3** A lane id is `<provider>-<n>` (`codex-4`, `claude-3`). Lane ids are stable; a lane whose account or credential is rebound gets a new id and the old one is disabled. One exception (D-ENV1, review r4 of #222): a stored env-kind lane whose variable every launch inherits never held a credential (C-10.5), so a `lanes.json` row for the same lane id, provider and account that names a variable no launch inherits, and none the session probe reads (C-10.5), binds it in place at daemon startup: its `credential_ref` takes that variable and its credential epoch moves on by one, with a `lane.reference_repaired` event naming both variables and the epoch. Nothing else about the lane changes.
- **C-1.4** An account key is `<provider>:<account id>`. For Codex the account id is the `account_id` from the home's `auth.json` claims when present, else the email; For Claude it is `<account_uuid>:<org_uuid>` as returned by the provider profile endpoint (C-10.6) for the lane's own credential; the email is a display label and never the key.
- **C-1.5** A submission request id is a nonempty caller-supplied string of at most 128 characters or a CLI-generated UUID4, printed back on submission. A valid caller id reaches the payload unchanged; validation never rewrites it. Recognized credentials are refused, exit 2, before the CLI stages a prompt or contacts the daemon, without printing the rejected value. The id is read whole and from just after each `-` and `_` (`ids.holds_credential`), since the C-23.14 scrubber starts no token straight after either. The protocol correlation `id` has its own token rule (C-25.7); a submission key outside that alphabet uses a separate correlation token.
- **C-1.6** Providers are exactly `codex` and `claude`. Model names in policy are short names (`astra`, `opus`) mapped to provider model ids in `policy.json`.
- **C-1.7** Timestamps in the store are ISO 8601 UTC with a `Z` suffix and second precision unless a column says otherwise. Epoch seconds from providers are converted on write.

## 2. State root and files

- **C-2.1** The state root is `$SUBFLEET_HOME`, default `~/.subfleet/`. Nothing is written outside it except: job workdirs and worktrees the caller named or the daemon allocated under `$HOME`, salvage refs inside the job's git repository, the `-o` export path, and `~/.claude/settings.json`, which only an explicit `daemon install --hooks` writes (C-23.25), never a job or a timer. The guard preflight's scratch home lives under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/tmp/` (C-23.23) and its verified-verdict markers under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/guard-cache/` (C-23.5), or under the directory `SUBFLEET_CODEX_GUARD_CACHE` names: an absolute value is the operator's explicit choice, a relative value must resolve inside the state root and is refused otherwise; the markers hold hashes, paths and timestamps only. The lane transfer (milestone 4, `docs/migration.md`) is the other exception: it edits the v1 roster it names, with a backup beside it, and relocates a transferred Codex home between `~/.codex-<n>` and `lanes/<lane id>/` under the state root (C-10.4).
- **C-2.2** Contents: `state.sqlite3` (plus WAL and SHM), `policy.json`, `lanes.json`, `daemon.sock`, `daemon.lock`, `daemon.log`, `jobs/<job id>/`, `lanes/<lane id>/` (provider homes when a lane has one), `worktrees/`, `conversations/<conversation id>/` (message text), `attachments/<sha256>.<ext>`, `catalog.json`, and `run/` (guardian control sockets, C-26.4).
- **C-2.3** `jobs/<job id>/` holds `prompt.md` (bytes as sent, written once), `manifest.json`, and per attempt `a<seq>/` with `stdout`, `stderr`, `start.json`, `exit.json`, `deliverable.md`, `lane.log`, any provider raw stream, and `abandon.json` once the daemon has given up a launch (C-4.2). Files are created with mode 0600 and directories 0700.
- **C-2.4** A workdir under `/tmp` or `/private/tmp` is refused at submission (exit 7) unless `--allow-tmp`.

## 3. Store

- **C-3.1** One SQLite database, `journal_mode=WAL`, `synchronous=FULL`, `foreign_keys=ON`, `busy_timeout=5000`. The schema is `subfleet/store_schema.sql`; `store.py` applies it and records `schema_version`. Migrations are additive and numbered.
- **C-3.2** Every state transition of a job, attempt, notice, lease, or action is one transaction, and every transaction that changes state also inserts an `events` row in the same transaction.
- **C-3.3** Transactions never span a subprocess call, a network call, a sleep, or a filesystem sync other than SQLite's own commit syncs. Artifact copying, `ps`, `git`, and provider probes run outside transactions. SQL reads of the conversation store are allowed inside a main-store reservation transaction to keep the ownership recheck atomic with reservation. Native binding also takes the main-store lock before its conversation-store SQL write and synchronous=FULL commit; both paths acquire locks in that order, and catalog and filesystem work finishes before either lock.
- **C-3.4** Readers other than the daemon (the CLI in offline mode, the menu bar app, Logpile) open the database read-only. Only the daemon and the guardian receipt path write, and the guardian writes files, not rows. The desktop app (section 29) never opens the database; it reads `status.json` and `catalog.json` and changes state only through daemon ops.
- **C-3.5** The CLI refuses to write to a store whose `schema_version` is newer than it knows (exit 1 with both versions).
- **C-3.6** The daemon says who holds a store lock too long and who waits too long for one. The main store's lock, the conversation store's lock and its file-write guard (`conversation-files`, held across a file write's fsyncs, C-25.3) are `lockwatch.WatchedLock`s: re-entrant locks that record the thread holding them and when its outermost hold began, and change nothing about who gets them or when. A hold longer than `HOLD_REPORT_S` (2 s) is written to `daemon.log` once, with the Python stack the holder is running at that moment (a `subfleet-lockwatch` thread samples every 0.5 s), and again when it ends, with its length; a hold no sample caught says, when it ends, where it was held. A thread that has waited `WAIT_REPORT_S` (5 s) for a lock writes one line naming itself, the number of threads waiting and the longest wait, the holder and how long it has held, the waiter's stack and the holder's live stack, and then keeps waiting. Each lock and kind of report (`hold`, `released`, `wait`, and C-3.7's `read-pool` and `read-hold`) writes at most one line per `REPORT_EVERY_S` (60 s); the rest are counted, with the longest, in the next line, or in a summary line once the window has passed. A report that raises is dropped: it never fails an acquire, and never makes a release raise after what the lock guarded is done (the review of 5841d8b found an error in a report could make `transaction()` raise after its rows were committed). SIGUSR1 makes the daemon write the Python stacks of its threads (faulthandler writes at most 100) to `daemon.log` from `faulthandler`'s signal handler, so the dump arrives when every Python thread is stuck; it is registered as soon as the log is open (SIGUSR1's default action ends the process), and a log closes only once faulthandler cannot hold it (rule 3 below); a daemon taking the signal fresh first lets go any registration an earlier one left (tests build several), since registering over a live registration would not put the handler back over the SIG_IGN below. A daemon that registers the handler writes its identity to `daemon.lock` only once it has, with `"stack_dumps": true`, and rewrites it without the flag before it unregisters the handler (one that declines, below, writes its identity without the flag); whenever `faulthandler` takes the signal fresh (no daemon in the process is in `_ADVERTISED`, below), the daemon first sets SIGUSR1 to be ignored, with `signal.signal` on the main thread and libc's `signal()` off it, so what `faulthandler` puts back whenever it lets the signal go ends nothing; that replaces any SIGUSR1 handler the host set first (one in Python's table, off the main thread, makes the daemon decline instead), and a child the host starts after the last close inherits SIGUSR1 ignored. A host that sets a SIGUSR1 handler of its own while any daemon stands replaces the daemon's, which no daemon can survive: that is out of scope. `subfleet daemon stacks` sends the signal only to the identity `daemon.lock` records, as `daemon stop` does (C-5.3, C-5.4), and only when that lock says `stack_dumps`; otherwise it signals nothing and exits 69 with the fix (restart the daemon on this version, or `sample <pid> 5`). It reads the lock again just before the signal, and when the lock has changed since (a daemon that began to stop has dropped the flag) it signals nothing and exits 1, asking to run it again. A daemon built before the flag (the installed ad2c208 reports the same version, 2.0.0a0) is refused, not killed: the review of 5841d8b showed a process without the handler exits -30 on SIGUSR1. It waits up to `--wait` seconds (5) for the log to stop growing, and prints what was written; exit 69 when no daemon runs. Incident: 2026-09-25, a `list` whose statement takes 1 ms took 34-94 s, with 18 threads waiting on the store lock, and neither the log nor a native sample could say which Python code held it. While the interpreter runs, SIGUSR1 keeps a handler as long as any daemon in the process has a `daemon.lock` that says `stack_dumps` (`_ADVERTISED`, in the order they took the signal): `daemon stacks` reads a living pid and the flag, and with no handler its signal would be ignored and no dump would come. Three rules hold this, wherever an exception lands (a KeyboardInterrupt in an embedded host, at any line of a take, a close or a failed construction's cleanup), rather than by the order of their steps. (1) One routine, `_settle_sigusr1`, makes the registration match the set: the signal dumps into the stream of the member that took it last, so a dump for one member can land in another's log, and when none is left its handler goes, back to the SIG_IGN beneath it. Every take and every leave runs it, whoever held the signal, under one lock, so whatever an exception cut short in one, the next repairs; a take that raises leaves the set and settles again. (2) Membership follows the lock: a daemon joins just before it takes the signal, and marks itself leaving before it rewrites its lock without the flag; it leaves once its lock, read back by its path as `daemon stacks` reads it (a record that does not parse says nothing), no longer says `stack_dumps`, at its own close or, should an exception cut that short, at the next settle, even once its daemon object has been collected (a member that began to leave, or whose daemon is gone, is judged by what its entry keeps: its lock's path and whether it came to write the flag). A failed construction whose cleanup an exception cut short before it marked itself leaving is judged once its object is collected; until then it stays, its stream open, and dumps for others may land in its log. One whose lock may still say so (it came to write the flag, recording just before that write that it may, and the lock reads back with it, or cannot be read) and cannot be rewritten (EIO or ENOSPC) stays, holding SIGUSR1 or not, its log stream kept open, until its lock read back stops saying so (a later daemon at that root rewriting it); one that never came to write the flag leaves whatever its rewrite meets. (3) A log stream closes only when no hand-off can pick it (its daemon is not in the set) and faulthandler cannot hold it (`_HELD_STREAMS`: a stream joins before it is registered, the others leave once that registration has returned, all once the handler has gone; a stream in it closes only after a settle has moved the signal off it). A stream that may not close stays open while its daemon is a member or faulthandler may hold it (for good, if its lock keeps saying `stack_dumps` or cannot be read), which costs a descriptor, never a dump into a closed or reused one; once a settle has pruned it and moved the signal off it, it is collected when nothing else holds it (a handler an interrupted close left on its logger holds it until a later daemon given that logger's `id()`-based name lets the handler go). When the last member leaves, on either thread, and as the interpreter exits, faulthandler puts back the SIG_IGN beneath it, so a signal racing either ends nothing. A daemon that cannot make the signal safe neither registers the handler nor says `stack_dumps`, logging why, and never joins the set, so `daemon stacks` refuses it: when setting SIGUSR1 ignored fails, on either thread, and when it is built off the main thread while Python's own table holds a SIGUSR1 handler of the host's (Python's exit resets that to the default action before faulthandler lets go, and off the main thread nothing can put SIG_IGN in the table in its place). A second exception landing inside the recovery itself is out of scope (reviews of 02c6320, eac0706, 4fc5b49, 1efa0ef, 2300b43, 78bfbf0, 3cac1e8, 67b9adc, 6506619, 9a514a7, 674b99b and 8ebe6b6).
- **C-3.7** A read never waits for a writer. The daemon's store keeps `READ_CONNECTIONS` (6) read-only connections (`query_only`) beside its one writing connection, opened on first use. A read made outside a transaction (`Store.query`, `Store.one`) takes one of them for one statement instead of the store lock, so reads never queue behind a transaction, and SQLite steps them side by side. Snapshots may hold at most all but `STATEMENT_RESERVE` (2) of the read connections (`snapshot_share`, 4 of 6), so a one-statement read (a hook's lookup, the wait hub, the control loop) always has connections no snapshot can take. No read waits for a connection longer than `READ_WAIT_S` (1 s): after that it opens one of its own for that read alone and closes it after. Every such wait is counted (`daemon.status` `read_pool`) and written to `daemon.log` under C-3.6's rate limit (kind `read-pool`), naming the waiter, whether it opened its own, and every thread holding a read connection, for what and for how long. `Store.close()` closes a read connection only once its read has ended (it waits up to `CLOSE_WAIT_S`, 5 s; a read still going then closes its own when it ends) and refuses any read that starts after it, and one that was still opening a new pooled connection, which it does not wait for: that read closes the connection it opened. The write-ahead log is bounded: the writer sets `journal_size_limit` to `WAL_SIZE_LIMIT` (64 MiB), so each time SQLite restarts the log the file is cut back to that size, and the only checkpoints are SQLite's automatic ones, which are PASSIVE: a commit never waits for a reader, it checkpoints up to the oldest snapshot still open, and the commits after that snapshot ends checkpoint the rest and restart the log. The daemon runs no RESTART or TRUNCATE checkpoint, which would hold writers while it waited for readers. Since a long read holds the log back, a read connection in use longer than `HOLD_REPORT_S` (2 s) is written to `daemon.log` once with its thread's live stack (C-3.6's sampler, kind `read-hold`). A read made by a thread that holds the store lock goes to the writing connection, so a transaction still sees its own uncommitted rows. `Store.snapshot()` gives a block of reads one committed state without the store lock (inside a transaction, the transaction's state; a store without read connections holds the lock for the block instead). No transaction begins inside a snapshot on the same thread: it raises `SnapshotWriteError` before it writes, with or without read connections, because its reads would go to the snapshot and miss its own writes (the review of 5841d8b saw `add_artifact` record a duplicate that way); a commit that races a snapshot comes from another thread. `Timers.snapshot` and every capacity view read their rows inside one and build the view after it ends (`capacity.store_rows`, `Daemon._capacity_rows`, `Timers.view_rows`), so a view build holds no read connection; `Timers.snapshot` used to open a write transaction only to read, and the first version of this clause built each view inside its snapshot, so six views building at once held all six connections and every other read waited, with no bound and no log line (review of 5841d8b: 1.53 s with the store lock free). The hot reads are bounded by what they return, not by the history kept: a capacity view reads only the readings that can be the newest of their lane, scope and window (`Store.latest_reading_candidates`: per key, those at the greatest canonical `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ` timestamp, plus every reading with another timestamp shape), not every reading, plus at most 24 hours of weekly provider history for status projections (C-11.9); a probe record is one step of the partial index `events_probe_holder` (`json_extract(data_json,'$.holder')` over the `probe.state` payloads `json_valid` accepts), and a gate's state the newest matching event found by SQLite (`json_extract`, newest first, one row), not every `probe.state` or `gate.state` event parsed in Python; batch labels and submission records are looked up by job id (`+kind`, so SQLite uses `events_job`), not by walking every `job.submitted` event; `sessions` filters session events by session in SQL, or takes only the newest per kind and session; and a view reads the reset-credit history, its reconciliations and the lane rows once (`ResetCredits.override_context`), not once per lane. Each rewrite answers exactly what the old form did; differential tests over randomized stores pin that (`tests/unit/test_store_contention_queries.py`). The one exception is a payload with a repeated key, which no writer makes (every payload is `json.dumps` of a dict): SQLite reads the key's first value and Python its last, so such a payload is found only under its first value, and Python checks what it parsed, so it is never returned or counted as another holder's or session's (review of 1516f3b). A probe record lookup takes only the newest row the index holds for a holder, so such a payload also hides its first value's older records: that holder gets none where the old walk found its previous one (review of 0e43105). A payload `json_valid` refuses is never filtered in SQL, and never given to `json_extract` (the session query guards it with `CASE`, since SQLite does not promise to test WHERE terms in the order written): a NaN or an Infinity, which `json.dumps` writes and `json.loads` reads, or a row that is not JSON at all. The partial index `events_not_json` hands every such row of the kinds asked to Python, which parses it as the old form did; a row that is not JSON, which no writer makes, is passed over where the old form raised. Both indexes are in `store_schema.sql`, so a store gets them the next time the daemon opens it, and neither can stop a store opening: `json_extract` is evaluated only for payloads `json_valid` accepts (an index on it alone fails to build over a single row that is not JSON). The store needs SQLite's JSON functions, built in since 3.38. `Timers` writes to the store only after releasing its own lock, which the control loop takes every tick. Incident: 2026-09-25, with the machine at load 85-100, a socket `list` whose statement takes 1 ms took 34-94 s while 18 daemon threads waited on the store lock; `docs/reports/2026-09-25-store-lock-contention.md` has the reproduction and the measurements.

## 4. Job and attempt state machines

- **C-4.1** Job states: `queued`, `running`, `waiting`, `succeeded`, `failed`, `cancelled`, `lost`. A `waiting` job has `wait_reason` in {`capacity`, `dependency`, `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`, `route`} and a `next_check_at`. Only `capacity` holds back the jobs behind it in its tier, and not while no lane can admit the job (C-11.8); a job waiting on its own `workspace` (C-6.8) or `route` (C-6.12) delays nothing else.
- **C-4.2** Attempt states and the boundary each one guards:

| State | Set when | Recovery if the daemon dies here |
|---|---|---|
| `reserved` | Lane lease, output lease, and any worktree lease taken; attempt row inserted | Release leases, mark attempt `failed` with class `unknown` and detail `reserved-no-launch`; the job may retry |
| `starting` | Guardian spawned; no `start.json` yet | If `start.json` holds a receipt, treat as `running`; else wait `start_grace_s` (10) then run containment (C-5.5) on the guardian pid and read `start.json` and `exit.json` again, because the guardian can write both and exit while the census runs: a receipt found then is taken on the next tick, `start.json` as above and `exit.json` as in the `running` row. Else, while the census is verifiable (or unverifiable only because the marker reads of processes other than the guardian failed, D4: such a census still read the group, the walk, responsibility, every identity and the guardian's own marker, and the extension decides nothing), its only member is the attempt's guardian by its recorded identity (pid, boot id, proc start), beside at most its own identity readers (D4: direct children of the guardian, by the table's parent, in its recorded group and among the census's group members, with a ps state that is neither stopped nor a zombie (an empty state is refused, as the guardian's is), that the census did not find marked; before `start.json` these are `procs._read`'s `sysctl` and `ps`, and the `start.json` read after the census shows none was the provider), and that guardian's ps state is present and neither stopped (`T`) nor a zombie (`Z`), the deadline is extended and the census taken again at its own pacing (C-5.12), until `START_GRACE_MAX_S` (60 s, 6 × `start_grace_s`) after the deadline began: at launch, or at the first look of a daemon that restarted since, as the deadline lives in daemon memory. The first extension is an `attempt.start_extended` event (D-QUAR-START1 (a): under load 60 to 100 on 2026-10-26 a guardian launched its provider about 31 s after its attempt started, 21 s after its job had ended `lost`). The first read that then finds an extended attempt's `start.json`, before or after the census or as the abandon's last read, is an `attempt.start_landed` event, once: `seconds` from the deadline's start to that read, `deadline_start` (`launch`, or `restart` when it began at a restarted daemon's first look, where a `start.json` already there reads 0 s) and the grace and bound in force; it is the bound's production record and decides nothing (review r3 of #250, check 5). Past that bound, or as soon as a condition fails, the attempt is abandoned (D-QUAR-START1 (b)): the daemon publishes `abandon.json` (`{"at", "reason"}`, the reason being the detail the attempt ends with) in the attempt folder by temp file and rename, and only then reads `start.json` for the last time. The guardian publishes `start.json` and only then looks for `abandon.json`, immediately before anything of the provider's is opened or spawned, and it looks once its launch gate opens too. Each side writes its own file before it reads the other's, so at least one sees the other: when the daemon's read finds no `start.json`, the guardian's look after publishing one finds the marker and it spawns nothing; when the guardian's look finds no marker, the daemon's read finds its `start.json`. With no `start.json` nothing launched: an empty census (cwd holders aside) releases and retries, after C-5.9's cwd wait; anything else (not verifiable, or not empty) runs C-5.6's kill protocol against the full census first, with `killed_by` `recovery` and the group KILLed at once (D-KILL4), and a verified-empty end (cwd holders aside) also ends the attempt unlaunched: `failed`, `starting-no-receipt`, the job queued again while it has attempts left (C-4.5), with one state and one retry decision whatever process sits in the worktree (D-KILL4 fix round 2b, review r2's P3-1); anything else is `quarantined`, and no retry attempt is reserved before then. With a `start.json` the attempt may have launched: the protocol runs against a census taken then, its TERM first, and the end is decided as a `running` attempt's is, from the receipt. A guardian that finds the marker publishes an exit receipt (`rc` 127, `spawn_error` `abandoned before launch: <reason>`, and `abandoned`: `at`, `reason`, and `stage` `gate` or `spawn`) and exits; such a receipt ends the attempt unlaunched, after containment as any receipt is, never as a provider's result. Every path that ends or signals a `starting` attempt writes the marker first: start grace, a cancel or the wall while starting, a restarted daemon's recovery (which is start grace's) and the launch gate's own failure. A marker that cannot be written ends nothing; the worker retries (C-5.10). Nothing else reads the marker. A receipt is a file whose JSON value is neither empty nor false (`{}`, `[]`, `null`, `0`, `false` and `""` are none), on each tick and in this re-read alike |
| `running` | `start.json` read: pid, pgid, boot id, proc start recorded | If `exit.json` holds a receipt, move to `finalizing`; else if the guardian is alive by (pid, boot id, proc start), re-adopt; if its identity cannot be inspected, leave the attempt for the next tick; if it is dead, read `exit.json` once more (the guardian writes the receipt and then exits, so a receipt can appear between the first read and the liveness check) and move to `finalizing` when it is there; else run containment; empty means `lost`, else kill survivors and re-check, else `quarantined`. A receipt present at finalization always wins over a `lost` verdict reached before it was read (incident: 2026-09-05, under a load average above 45 a job whose provider had exited 0 was recorded `lost` with its receipt on disk) |
| `finalizing` | `exit.json` read; classification, salvage, export, notice pending | Re-run finalization idempotently; every step checks for its own completed output before acting; a census that is not verified empty waits out `exit_settle_s` (C-5.9) before quarantining |
| `succeeded`, `failed`, `interrupted`, `lost`, `cancelled`, `quarantined` | Terminal | None; `quarantined` needs an operator action (C-5.7) |

D-WALL1: `max_wall_s` counts summed provider running time across attempts, from each recorded start to its known provider exit, or to now only while that provider may still be running. The guardian's exit receipt supplies the exit time; otherwise a census (including recycled-PID evidence) that establishes exit supplies the observed end. A receipt-less live attempt is inspected under C-5.12 before wall enforcement. If inspection finds it gone, it settles as lost and retries under C-4.5; its charged end is no later than its last confirmed liveness (`evidence_json.last_observed_alive_at`, saved durably at inspection), or its recorded start for a legacy attempt with no such observation. That observation belongs to the attempt's recorded boot, so a provider from a previous boot is never charged past that boot's end; host and daemon downtime after the last observation spend no budget. A known receipt always takes precedence over this conservative loss endpoint. An attempt's `finished_at` preserves this provider end through finalization and quarantine; a census end is saved before fallible finalization so a retry cannot extend it. The job's `finished_at` records settlement. Starting records its start before the guardian gate opens; the start receipt preserves that earlier start. An unsuccessful launch records its end immediately and spends no finalization time. Recovery uses those durable timestamps; legacy attempts with no recorded start contribute zero. Reservation, queues, capacity and operator holds, waits between attempts, and post-exit classification, salvage, export and settlement contribute zero. The wall cancels only a provider inspection confirms is still running when summed running time reaches the cap, recording `killed_by=max_wall_s`. Before acting at the cap, the wall asks liveness afresh and re-reads the receipt, including immediately before signalling. A receipt supersedes a pending wall stop only while the effective requester is still the wall, and only when no signal was sent or `finished_at` proves the provider exited strictly before the signal. The daemon records `job.wall_cancel_superseded`, clears the effective cancellation and `killed_by`, and keeps the original `job.cancel_requested` in the audit trail. Online and offline `runs show` then show `cancel: null`, the provider's own outcome and any `wall_overrun` evidence; a later client request becomes the displayed `cancel`. A client request received during a pending wall stop is recorded with its session, socket audit pid and reason and becomes effective; it always ends the job `cancelled`, rc 130, whatever the receipt says. The wall defers its dependent cascade until the parent attempt settles `interrupted` by `max_wall_s`, atomically with that settlement. A parent whose receipt wins never cancels or signals its dependents. A final wall cascade leaves descendants with a known provider exit to settle with their own outcomes; stopped descendants carry `killed_by=max_wall_s`, with the parent's job id in the detail and notice. Operator and client cancellations cascade at request time (C-7.3). A provider that exited on its own retains its outcome and may have its result accepted even if it ran past the cap while the daemon was down or delayed. Its attempt records `wall_overrun` evidence with summed `running_s`, `max_wall_s`, `overrun_s`, `provider_finished_at` and the reason; online and offline `runs show` expose that evidence. The guardian records `finished_at` with millisecond precision immediately after provider wait returns, before relay or stream cleanup; this is an additive timestamp-format change, and existing receipts remain readable. `attempt.kill_started` records the first delivered signal's millisecond time as both `ts` and `data_json.signal_at`, captured after identity checks immediately before the signal syscall. A final receipt check that avoids the signal records `signal_sent: false` and `signal_at: null` on the intent, with a millisecond `ts`. An exit after the signal is `interrupted`/`unknown` with `killed_by=max_wall_s`, even with rc 0 and `turn.completed`. Ties are conservative: equal milliseconds do not prove earlier exit; an old second-precision receipt in the signal's second counts as after. A historical signal record without precise ordering, or a crash between the durable kill intent and recording the signal time, cannot prove earlier exit either. There is no wall check at terminal settlement. Finalization has no overall elapsed-time deadline: individual Git calls use `workspace_git_timeout_s` (default 60 s), transient salvage has at most three tries per daemon (C-13.1), and workers retry failures with bounded backoff (C-5.10). Filesystem reads, walks and publication do not have an overall deadline (C-25.3); this provider wall does not bound them.

- **C-4.3** A job has at most one `accepted_attempt_id`. Acceptance and the notice insert are one transaction. A result from an attempt whose `seq` is lower than the job's current attempt, or whose state is not `finalizing`, is never accepted. For provider attempts other than conversation turns, after containment, first capture selects the first existing directory entry at the launch's raw-stream path, then the attempt's `stream.jsonl`; any size and any object type is authoritative. Only absence of both candidates permits first-capture fallback to the launch's stdout, then the attempt's `stdout`. A regular candidate is opened once. Its descriptor's `(st_dev, st_ino)` is compared with stdout, stderr and the launch's raw path (and Codex `last.md`), each resolved through `realpath` and opened safely or fstat-ed through the already-open descriptor. A file the provider can still write is an inode reached by one of those output destinations or a retained writable descriptor, including through directory aliases, hard links, and after unlinking its last live name. A pathname or link count does not establish authority. Every regular candidate, including an empty one and one with no observed writable alias, is conservatively copied from that one descriptor into a fresh daemon-owned file in bounded chunks, with file fsync, rename and directory fsync (C-8.1). Thus any identity, metadata or directory-entry change between the decision and the copy still leaves classification reading only the fresh copy, not the provider's inode. A non-regular entry (directory, FIFO, socket or symlink, even a dangling one) supplies no events and is never followed or read; finalization preserves the entry and durably captures no events. An entry that disappears after selection likewise supplies no events. If an entry becomes non-regular between inspection and open, ENXIO, EOPNOTSUPP (102 on Darwin), ENOTSUP, ELOOP, EISDIR and EINVAL become `NotRegularFile`; EACCES/EPERM do so only when reinspection proves a non-regular replacement. Genuine regular-file permission and I/O errors propagate, including an injected post-check ENOENT when the regular entry remains present. If first capture raises an I/O or unexpected exception, settlement still records the attempt's end in that pass, marks the stream unreadable in classification, and logs the failure after recording end. No live stream, cached verdict, or cached deliverable can supply completion proof after that failure; detached Codex attempts are transient and other non-turn provider attempts unknown, while a missing exit receipt remains a loss. Diagnostic artifact reads are best effort on that path. `stream.freeze.json` identifies the published copy by filename, device/inode, byte count and SHA-256, and records whether the source supplied events. Replay and restart validate that identity and content, including comparison with current writable destinations, and use only that same copy. They never re-copy a live/original path and reuse the old verdict or deliverable. A capture failure is recorded durably in `finalization.json` with its class and error, including failures recorded by earlier daemons in outcome evidence. Replay and restart honour that verdict without another freeze, extraction or completion check. A valid recorded copy and its saved verdict are reused; if the recorded copy is missing, replaced, changed or newly writable, or its receipt names another file, the verdict becomes a capture failure, never a fresh read of a live candidate. An I/O or unexpected exception that interrupts validation is not proof of invalidity: it leaves the saved verdict untouched and raises for the worker to retry with its existing backoff (C-5.10). Whenever no verdict has been saved, or a saved verdict has no `stream` token, finalization first checks whether its receipt exists. A published receipt establishes an existing copy even when a crash, a full disk, or classification or attestation failure interrupted verdict publication; its validation uses the same bounded retries. If it is absent, the next source read is first capture, whose failure is recorded immediately and stays sticky even when a cached verdict and deliverable already exist. An error that prevents checking receipt existence uses the same bounded validation retries. After `STREAM_CHECK_TRIES` (three) interrupted checks of an attempt in one daemon run, the attempt settles as a sticky capture failure with reason `copy cannot be checked` and detail `provider stream copy could not be checked: retry checks exhausted`, even if its copy may still be valid; as with salvage retries, the count is in memory, restarts at zero in a new daemon and clears after a completed check. Rejected copies are unlinked before another copy is tried and on capture failure, including rejection after receipt publication. Before a fresh capture when no receipt exists, regular files and symlinks matching `stream.frozen-*.jsonl` or `.stream.frozen-*` in the attempt folder are unlinked; other non-regular entries are preserved. Proven invalidity of a recorded copy also removes matching full and partial crash leftovers, including a later copy stranded behind an earlier rejected receipt, while preserving files named by either the receipt or the verdict. Symlink cleanup removes the link only, never its target. A cached `ok` remains subject to C-9.2 completion proof and C-12.6 captured-content checks before acceptance.
- **C-4.4** A job whose final attempt ends without an rc is `lost`, never `succeeded`.
- **C-4.5** `max_attempts` default 3, bounding retries independently of D-WALL1's running-time cap; no separate job-age cap applies; a new attempt is admitted only for classes `limited` (next candidate), `transient` (same lane after 60 s, once, then next candidate; a model's capacity refusal that steers its job, C-9.5: at the next pass, that model skipped while its steer lasts), `lost` on a read-only job, and a lane fault (next candidate). A lane fault is an `auth-dead` attempt (C-9.3) of a job with no lane pin, that is no conversation turn (its conversation decides a turn's failover, C-26.7), and that left its workspace as it found it: the job is read-only, or it is writable, its model never answered (`model_answered` false in the attempt's evidence, C-6.14: a writable attempt runs with permissions skipped, and what its model pushed, commented or wrote outside the worktree no tree shows; where an adapter cannot say, or read no event of the stream, so recorded `model_answered` null, it is taken to have answered), and its salvage at finalization (C-13.1) found the end tree equal to the attempt's start snapshot (no ref written, nothing left out, no error) and HEAD where the attempt began. Its lane is disabled as C-23.44 says, and the job moves on to the next candidate as for `limited`; the attempt is `failed` with class `auth-dead` and records `lane_fault` in its evidence (`class`, `lane_id`, `seq`, `model_answered`, and `workspace`: `read-only`, or `unchanged` with `head`). A job has at most one lane fault, and `max_attempts` does not count it: the lane fault grants the job one more attempt, so a job may have `max_attempts` attempts besides it (`Daemon._attempts_left`, which `_unlaunched` asks too), and a job allowed one attempt still moves on once: a writable job's lane fault ran nothing the job asked for (no model answered), and a read-only job's repeats only reading. (A revive's refused attempt may still have left its prompt and Claude Code's placeholder in the revived session's transcript, which the next attempt continues from; nothing else.) A job that moved on is no pilot (C-6.14): on a lane not proven it waits for Subfleet's own probe of that lane (C-11.4: the probe's fixed prompt, on the lane's credential, with none of the job's settings, `Daemon._needs_probe`), whose `auth-dead` is the lane's and disables it (C-23.44) and whose answer proves it. So with `admission.prove_idle_s` set, a job that moved on meets `auth-dead` again only on a lane a model answered on within the window, which points at the job. A second `auth-dead` of a job that already moved on ends it, `failed` with rc 5, and that lane is left enabled (C-23.44): two lanes refusing one job points at the job (a tool's `invalid_api_key` on stderr, a project setting that overrides the lane's credential), and a job that moved on without end would disable every lane it reached, where before this rule it disabled one. So no job's attempts disable more than one lane, as before (a probe disables only a lane whose own credential it found refused, C-23.44); a lane that really is dead is disabled by the next job that meets it, whose first `auth-dead` it is. `auth-dead` on a pinned job, or on one whose workspace changed, `cli-too-old`, `content-filter`, and any class on a writable job after the workspace changed require reconciliation first (C-13.3) (incident: 2026-09-30 15:43Z, an organisation disabled claude-5's Claude Code access; admission had put 37 jobs from about 20 sessions there within 40 s, and every one ended `failed` with rc 5 after a single attempt while lanes were coming back; C-6.14 now keeps such a burst to one attempt).
- **C-4.6** An attempt never changes lane or model. A new lane or model is a new attempt.

## 5. Process ownership and containment

- **C-5.1** The daemon spawns each attempt through `subfleet-guardian`, which calls `os.setsid()` so it leads a new session and process group, then spawns the provider command as its child with the same pgid, stdout and stderr redirected to the attempt directory, and an environment that contains the lane credential, the provider home, `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT=<attempt id>`, `SUBFLEET_JOB=<job id>`, and `SUBFLEET_ROOT=<state root>`. At startup the daemon checks whether its own responsible pid equals its pid. Only when that read succeeds and it is self-responsible does it start all three kinds of guardian with `posix_spawn` and responsibility disclaimed, using its own `sys.executable`: each guardian becomes its own responsible process and remains the responsible process for the provider and its descendants. Only the guardian is disclaimed; the provider retains the guardian as its macOS privacy (TCC) identity, the same running interpreter and code-signing identity as the launchd daemon. A framework Python launcher re-execs Python.app at the same pid before provider launch; the running interpreter is the identity compared, rather than the temporary launcher path. A daemon started under a terminal or app keeps that inherited responsibility and privacy attribution by using Popen, records no root, and uses three census sources. An unavailable startup responsibility read takes that same path. The spawn uses `POSIX_SPAWN_CLOEXEC_DEFAULT`, file actions opening standard streams on `/dev/null`, explicitly inheriting the launch fd, changing cwd, and the given environment. The daemon records `responsible_root: {pid, unique_id}` beside the guardian identity (attempt evidence, or the probe/re-enrolment record). The unique id orders process creation within the recorded boot independently of wall-clock corrections. Without a recorded unique id, a matching live snapshot and fresh start-time read are required before responsibility member reads. An early rejection skips that source and makes the census unverifiable. After the scan, both kinds of root use the current holder's kernel record to exclude members born at or after a readable successor; unavailable final identity preserves collected provisional members as evidence with an unverifiable result; a known-id root already gone without provisional members can still verify empty (C-5.5). A successful disclaimed spawn always records a root, even if its metadata read fails. If the SPI symbols are missing at import, or startup does not authorize disclaiming, the daemon uses its existing subprocess spawn, records no root, and logs once. The guardian never receives a secret in argv. It starts the provider only after it reads the byte `1` from its launch gate, the read end of a pipe the daemon passes it above descriptor 2, where the guardian's /dev/null standard streams cannot replace it. The daemon writes that byte only after it has recorded the guardian's identity; a guardian that reads anything else there, end-of-file included, starts nothing, writes neither `start.json` nor `exit.json` (C-5.2) and exits 127. A probe (C-11.4) and the turn a Claude lane's re-enrolment runs pass their guardians the same launch gate. The daemon runs at the default QoS: `daemon install` writes launchd `ProcessType` `Interactive`, because `Standard` clamps the daemon and every process it starts to the `utility` QoS (priority 20, which no thread can raise), and `Adaptive`, which launchd moves out of Background only on XPC activity (launchd.plist(5)), runs it darwinbg (priority 4), since its clients use a Unix socket. The guardian keeps its daemon's QoS and starts the provider under a `utility` QoS clamp (`/usr/sbin/taskpolicy -c utility`), which the provider and every process it starts inherit and none can raise, so agent work stays below the operator's apps however the daemon is scheduled; a thread's own QoS and `nice` reach no child, so neither would do. taskpolicy execs the provider in its own place: its pid, process group, environment and argv are those it would have had unclamped. When taskpolicy cannot spawn the provider it exits 66 with `taskpolicy: posix_spawn: <reason>` as the first line of the provider's standard error, and the guardian records that as C-5.2's spawn failure, rc 127 and a `spawn_error` in the words Popen's own failure has, and empties that standard error, as that failure leaves it, so no adapter reads taskpolicy's reason as the provider's. `SUBFLEET_PROVIDER_QOS=inherit` in the daemon's environment runs providers at the guardian's QoS instead, as does a host without taskpolicy.
- **C-5.2** Before spawning the provider, the guardian writes `start.json` = {`guardian_pid`, `pgid`, `boot_id`, `proc_start`, `started_at`} via temp file and rename. After the provider exits it writes `exit.json` = {`rc`, `signal`, `finished_at`, `wall_s`, `child_pid`} the same way, then exits with the provider's rc. A guardian that is its own responsibility root first waits until no other live process names it as responsible, taking its first look immediately, then polling every 0.1 s through the daemon's `exit_settle_s` window (default 3 s), before increasing delays capped at 2 s. Each empty look re-lists until it adds no unread pid, reading every new pid, to close a fork-and-exit race; exhausting eight rounds is an unavailable look, never emptiness. Provider and receipt exceptions also pass through this wait in a `finally` block. Failed reads prove nothing and are retried; a guardian without responsibility support, or proven not to be a root, exits as before. This wait follows the exit receipt, so C-5.9 still begins when the provider ends. If the provider cannot be spawned, `exit.json` carries `rc: 127` and `spawn_error`. After its launch gate opens, and again after it publishes `start.json` and before anything of the provider's is opened or spawned, the guardian looks for the daemon's `abandon.json` in its attempt folder (C-4.2, D-QUAR-START1); any entry there, readable or not, is one. If one is there it spawns nothing, publishes `exit.json` = {`rc`: 127, `signal`: null, `finished_at`, `wall_s`, `child_pid`: null, `spawn_error`: `abandoned before launch: <reason>`, `abandoned`: {`at`, `reason`, `stage`: `gate` or `spawn`}} and exits 127; a reader that predates `abandoned` reads a provider that could not be spawned.
- **C-5.3** Boot id is the stable `kern.bootsessionuuid` from `sysctl`; legacy receipts may carry `kern.boottime` seconds. Process start identity is the `lstart` column of `ps -p <pid> -o lstart=`. A pid is "the same process" only if both identities match. A legacy boot timestamp that differs from the current timestamp means unknown, not dead: macOS wall-clock correction can shift `kern.boottime` during one boot. A missing recorded boot/start identity, or an unavailable start, for an observed live process is unknown. Unknown identity never permits a signal or establishes death; a responsive daemon socket remains usable. Absent processes, reused start identities, and differing boot-session UUIDs still establish death.
- **C-5.4** The daemon may signal a process group it recorded, only after confirming the group leader's identity by C-5.3; and, in C-5.6's kill protocol (D-KILL4), a single live member of the attempt's census (C-5.5: the group, the walk and its recorded seeds, the marker, responsibility or the parent-chain rule; never a cwd holder or a held parent) whose identity that census captured, only after confirming that identity afresh by C-5.3 immediately before the signal. Marked members and parent-chain members are such members in any census. A member of the recorded group is one only when that census verified the recorded leader alive with its recorded birth and boot (the group's TERM then reaches it anyway), or when its identity is in `owned_identities`, recorded while the guardian led that group (`_record_owned`), and not on another boot than the attempt's (D-QUAR-REUSE1); a member only a leaderless group at the recorded pgid names is never one, in any census, since that leader may have exited long ago and its pgid been taken by a stranger's group (D-KILL4 fix 1, review r1 P1). A member only the walk or responsibility found is one only in a verifiable census, and never when it, or an ancestor in that census's shapes, was first recorded by an unverifiable census (`unverified_identities`, kept while a seed key still holds that birth; a seed a census finds below such an identity is itself added to `unverified_identities`, event `attempt.taint_recorded`, so its taint outlives that ancestor's exit, fix 1, P3-5): such a census may keep a seed whose birth it could not compare or a responsibility stranger it could not bound (C-5.5), and every census records what it sees. It never signals launchd, itself, its parent, or the recorded guardian of another attempt, whatever a census says.
- **C-5.5** Containment enumeration collects live, non-zombie pids from four sources for attempts that recorded a responsibility root, and three for legacy or in-flight attempts without one: the members of process group `<pgid>`; a walk of parent links descending from the guardian pid and the recorded child pid; whole NUL-delimited strings anywhere after the executable path in each candidate's raw `KERN_PROCARGS2` buffer that equal `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT=<attempt id>` and, when a root is supplied, `SUBFLEET_ROOT=<state root>` exactly; and every live same-uid pid whose `responsibility_get_pid_responsible_for_pid` equals the recorded guardian root. The marker also counts a matching argv string, which can only over-include. Candidates are the snapshot's live pids union a kernel pid listing taken afterwards, so processes born since the snapshot are read too. Other users' processes denied with EPERM are skipped, as `ps -E` skipped them; a failed read of a same-uid live process makes the census unverifiable, and the census names that process in `markers_unread` (D4, 2026-10-27: on the shared host every such failure measured was of a newborn of this user, refused in its first milliseconds; only C-4.2's start-grace extension reads them apart). A full buffer or ENOMEM retries with the in-process `KERN_ARGMAX` sysctl when that maximum exceeds the initial allocation. A read still filling the kernel maximum may be truncated and remains an error, and that process is named in `markers_unread` as well. In particular, a near-maximum argv/environment plus the executable path and kernel-added strings can exceed the readable maximum: that same-uid process keeps every census unverifiable while it lives (the kernel refuses buffers larger than its maximum). A refused argument read is checked against fresh kernel state: a gone process, zombie, or process already exiting runs no further code and is skipped. A failed responsibility read for an attempt with a root prevents release. A root proven recycled by the census disables the responsibility source entirely. For any other root recorded without a unique id, this source requires the snapshot to show its pid live with its recorded start time and one fresh start-time read before the kernel member reads to match. A missing, changed or unreadable early identity skips responsibility reads with `responsibility root identity unavailable for pid <pid>`, preventing verified emptiness. The scan reads the holder first; a different holder's readable unique id bounds members of a known-id root. Immediately after the scan, one in-process `kernel.process(root.pid)` read checks the holder again for both kinds of root. A matching recorded unique id, or matching start when no unique id was recorded, retains every observed member. A different holder with a readable unique id excludes only observed members whose unique ids are at or above the successor's; a real member observed naming the living root was born before any successor could acquire its reaped pid. Members with unreadable unique ids are retained. An absent or unreadable final holder, or a successor without a readable unique id, retains collected provisional members and records the identity diagnostic, making the pass unverifiable. A known-id root already gone with no provisional members needs no such diagnostic and can verify empty; a failed final inspection still prevents verification. A changed final start for a root without a recorded unique id also records that diagnostic, even when a readable successor supplies a ceiling. These retained identities are evidence only: they seed later walks and can hold an attempt, but never authorize signals or establish verified emptiness; the unverifiable census that retains them records them as `unverified_identities`, which C-5.4 excludes from C-5.6's targets (D-KILL4). A responsibility member created before the recorded root is excluded using kernel unique-id order in the same boot; a proven different boot excludes all members of that old root, and unavailable ordering keeps candidates. Every discovered responsibility member with a captured birth identity that survives the birth-order bounds is persisted as `responsible_identities` and seeds later walks, because the kernel drops the responsibility link when the guardian dies. Its live state and start are captured from the same kernel record as its unique id, with the start rendered using ps's C-locale, UTC, second precision; neither an older snapshot row nor a later `identity(pid)` supplies a different holder's state or birth to this observation. A stale zombie row cannot discard the observed live member. A live snapshot row without a start still makes that pass unverifiable, while an independently captured kernel birth is persisted. Snapshot shapes from a different birth are omitted. Every other live member a census counts, by the group (its leader verified, C-5.4), the walk or its marker, other than the guardian and with a captured identity not already kept under the same birth in `owned_identities`, `responsible_identities` or `counted_identities`, is persisted as `counted_identities` (event `attempt.counted_recorded`; a probe keeps it in its record) and seeds later walks the same way (D-KILL2): the kill protocol's SIGTERM ends the provider and reparents its children to init, and a process whose argument buffer shows no environment has no marker to be found by. Neither key ever receives an identity whose boot-session UUID differs from the attempt's recorded boot, whichever source counted it (D-QUAR-REUSE1: kept, release/217 seeded such a stranger in every later census, and a dead attempt read as live for as long as the stranger lived). Neither key grants ownership; a live member either seeds is signalled only as a census member by C-5.6's kill protocol (D-KILL4). Steady-state inspection also records live walk descendants outside the group from its shared table as counted identities (D-KILL3), without a marker or responsibility scan or another process listing. A runner observed in one inspection remains counted after a provider exits by itself or ends on a stopped turn's interrupt; the record grants no ownership. The walk seeds the guardian pid, the recorded child pid, and every pid in `owned_identities`, `responsible_identities` or `counted_identities`. When those sources describe different births at one pid, all are retained as alternatives: the walk excludes that pid only when every recorded birth is provably different; any matching or incomparable birth retains the seed. Seeds are scoped to the attempt's boot, the boot its guardian identity records (the row's `boot_id`, else `start.json`'s), because a reboot ends every process (D-QUAR-REUSE1). A stored identity whose boot-session UUID differs from that boot's is not the attempt's (a census on a later boot counted it): it never seeds the walk and is never counted, and the census names it under `other_boot` (by pid, a key present only when there is one) without rewriting or deleting the attempt's stored identities. When the attempt's boot is a boot-session UUID that differs from the current one by `boot_identity.matches` (False after the fresh read of a cached mismatch, C-5.12), no recorded pid seeds the walk and the recorded group is excluded: each seed that the snapshot shows or whose pid is a row's parent, and the group's leader when the group has members, is recorded under `recycled`, for the reason its recorded birth proves where the comparison above decides it, and otherwise for `different boot identity (attempt's boot)`, adding no error; an identity-less seed (an exit receipt's or the row's `child_pid`) is one of these. An attempt boot that is unrecorded or legacy `kern.boottime` seconds, or that cannot be compared with the current boot (unreadable, or seconds after a failed UUID read), keeps the rules above, so an identity-less live seed still makes the census unverifiable; on the attempt's own boot every source counts as before. The marker source matches the attempt's environment strings, not a pid, and is unchanged: a process of a later boot that carries the attempt's exact marker pair is still counted, though never kept (below); responsibility already excludes every member of a root on a proven other boot. That root is the attempt's guardian, so once the attempt's boot is over no check of the root's identity holds the attempt either (its pid is free or a stranger's for good), and the final holder check cannot make the census unverifiable. For a root with a recorded unique id the final holder read is still made, unless the census has proved the root recycled, which disables the source (above; on the later boot a root pid the snapshot shows is a seed recorded under `recycled`), or a member's read has already proved the other boot; only its failure is dropped (a read that fails, or a holder that cannot bound the members: none while members name the root, or one without a readable unique id), and a successor's readable unique id still excludes the members at or above it. A root recorded without one, whose start is absent or changed, drops the responsibility source without an error, so neither its members nor its holder are read. Recycling an older owned birth cannot discard a newly observed responsibility birth. These alternatives do not change `owned_identities` or signal authority. An absent or zombie seed remains a parent-link anchor, since `ps` may collect a child's row before the parent exits; matching recorded survivors remain counted even when reparented to init in a separate session after an ancestor dies or is recycled. The guardian uses the attempt's guardian pid, boot id and process start; the child and other owned processes use the identities captured while the recorded guardian still led the group. Missing, unreadable or incomparable identities retain observed live processes and make the census unverifiable. Receipt times and process birth times are wall-clock samples, so bounds derived from attempt start or exit cannot establish reuse without a recorded identity. Receipts and the recorded attempt or probe supply these facts through the same `_contain` path for start grace, finalization, kill, quarantine resolution and probes. If a process now occupies pid == pgid with a proven different leader identity, the group source is excluded: XNU `forkproc` refuses to allocate a pid still in use as a process-group or session id ([XNU 12377.1.9](https://github.com/apple-oss-distributions/xnu/blob/xnu-12377.1.9/bsd/kern/kern_fork.c#L970-L972)). An absent leader (gone, or a zombie) leaves its surviving group members in the census, where they hold the attempt, but they are signal targets only as C-5.4 allows (an owned identity, or another source's attribution), never for their group alone, and they are not persisted as counted identities for it (D-KILL4 fix 1). A recorded leader boot that is provably not this boot excludes the group source (`recycled`: `group of another boot`), as no process outlives its boot. One that cannot be compared, including a shifted legacy boot timestamp, leaves the group's members holding the attempt, never signalled for their group, and makes the census unverifiable (`boot identity unavailable for group <pgid>` for an absent leader; a present one's comparison records its own error). These leader checks decide only for an attempt on this boot or with an unknown boot: one whose recorded boot is known to be over has its group excluded by the boot scope above first, with its leader recorded once under `recycled` for that scope's reason, so the group's members neither hold the attempt nor are counted for it, and nothing is recorded as `group of another boot` (D-KILL4 with D-QUAR-REUSE1). Unknown identity retains the root and group; a failed or unknown comparison makes the census unverifiable. The raw-buffer source finds markers hidden from `ps -E` by NUL-filled argv, and responsibility lineage finds a process that erased its markers altogether while the root remains live (D-CEN1). On macOS 26 the kernel returns the buffer of a process whose code-signing status (`csops`) marks it both an Apple platform binary and hardened-runtime with its argv and no environment strings (observed 2026-10-18 on 26.6.2 for `/usr/bin/lockf`, `/bin/sleep`, `/bin/cat`, `/usr/bin/perl` and `/bin/zsh`; Xcode's `git`, platform without the runtime flag, and `bun` and Codex's native binary, runtime without the platform flag, showed theirs), so such a process is found by the group, the walk, responsibility or a recorded identity, never by its marker (D-KILL2). GitHub's macos-15-arm64 runner (macOS 15.7.9, observed 2026-10-19) returned `/usr/bin/lockf`'s buffer with its environment, so there its marker finds it; no other macOS version has been observed. A previously unseen member whose root died cannot be discovered by this source, and a process a member starts with responsibility disclaimed can escape it; if it also leaves the group and parent links and erases its markers, the census cannot find it. For an attempt without a root, a process whose markers are hidden (as above) that leaves the group and the walk before any inspection or census of the attempt counts it is seen by none of those lineage or marker sources (D-KILL2, D-KILL3). The separate cwd hold below may still retain a job-owned worktree, without attributing or remembering that runner. In particular, a hidden-marker runner born and reparented between inspections remains a documented limitation, even if it then outlives the provider. Members already recorded, including those observed just before root death in that same pass, remain walk seeds afterwards. A root without a recorded unique id cannot distinguish same-second reuse by start alone (C-5.3). The boot scope above covers only an attempt whose recorded boot is known to be over: same-second reuse within one boot and missing successor ordering remain limits. Kernel member and responsibility reads are not an atomic snapshot. Reuse between a member's metadata read and its separate responsibility read can misattribute or miss that member's identity; the after-member-read identity race is closed, but this earlier handoff remains an observation limit. A successor ceiling bounds the holder observed at that read; an intervening holder may leave older strangers below a later holder's ceiling, and unavailable ordering may also retain strangers, including when a successor lives and disappears entirely during the scan. Such evidence still grants no signal authority. A member whose identity cannot be captured makes that pass unverifiable; persistence requires a readable identity. Attempt ids are a timestamp and a slug, so two daemons on one machine (or two test state roots) can mint the same id in the same second; the root is what tells their processes apart (incident: 2026-09-05, sleeping fakes from an earlier test quarantined a later test's attempt). "Verified empty" means all applicable sources returned zero live pids and no comparison was unverifiable; it is impossible while any process matching a recorded identity is alive. Counting a newly found process never adds ownership (`owned_identities`). Since D-KILL4 the census itself is C-5.6's signal authority: every member with a captured identity is signalled one by one after a fresh identity check (C-5.4), except a member only the walk or responsibility found when the census is unverifiable or that member's attribution was first recorded under uncertainty (C-5.4's `unverified_identities`), and a member only a leaderless group names (C-5.4). Markers are signal authority wherever the process runs: a process that inherited the attempt's environment is signalled even outside the attempt's lineage, for example a pane of a tmux server the agent started; that over-reach is accepted (D-KILL4 fix 1). When a responsibility observation supplies a different birth from a pid's snapshot group row, that old group membership is excluded; the new identity cannot inherit its signal authority. If any required process-table, kernel listing, marker, responsibility or identity read fails, the result is "unverifiable". The group and the walk are read from one `ps -axo pid=,ppid=,pgid=,stat=,lstart=` snapshot, so a process cannot be present in one of them and absent from the other because it exited between two reads. A live row without `lstart` is retained and makes the census unverifiable. The snapshot's readable `lstart` supplies identities outside the responsibility source (C-5.3); responsibility observations use their own kernel birth records. A census uses one process-table `ps` read, plus an in-process pid listing, state/unique-id/birth and raw-buffer read per accessible live candidate, and a responsibility read per candidate when the root is eligible. For a job-owned worktree, the cwd read below adds an in-process pid listing of its own, one `PROC_PIDVNODEPATHINFO` read per listed pid, and one path resolution for the worktree and per distinct cwd spelling. Every applicable responsibility scan also reads its root's current holder once in process afterwards, unless a member's read has already proved the root on another boot. A boot-identity read is needed when a live pid or a recorded root/leader comparison needs one, or, for an attempt whose recorded boot is a boot-session UUID, when a seed, a parent link to one or the recorded group shows in the snapshot, or when a check of its responsibility root's identity fails (D-QUAR-REUSE1: the table's read, shared with every other comparison, and none when nothing recorded shows and no root check fails); a cached boot-session mismatch is read afresh before establishing death or another boot (C-5.12). A root without a recorded unique id adds one fresh start-time `ps` read when a live snapshot row matches its recorded start, including when that early read rejects it; known-id roots add no root-start `ps`. A pid outside the responsibility source that the snapshot cannot describe is asked about singly, and leaves the census if it is gone. Responsibility members reuse their captured kernel live state and birth even when absent from the snapshot or when its row describes an older holder; no single-pid ps is needed for those observations. Except for the root checks and the kernel candidate reads, pids the snapshot does describe are not asked about again, so a census can include a process that exited just after the snapshot, where each used to be read again and dropped if gone. That errs only toward holding on: the kill protocol and C-5.9 read the census again until it empties, `--confirm-dead` can be repeated, and start grace, which reads it once, reads the receipts again after it (C-4.2). The census records each source's pid set (including `responsible_pids`), captured identities and available snapshot shapes that do not contradict a responsibility birth. Its `recycled` entries name each excluded seed or leader with its recorded identity (and `also_recorded` alternatives when present), available current identity and reason; excluded identities are also persisted in the attempt's evidence for `runs show --json`, including on verified-empty success. For a job-owned worktree (C-13.4) the deciding census also reads cwd in process through `PROC_PIDVNODEPATHINFO`. A live process whose canonical cwd (resolved and spelled in the disk's case, as admission does) equals or is inside the canonical worktree holds only worktree release, never the attempt's TMPDIR (C-12.10), even when it is an operator's shell or editor with no attempt lineage. Evidence names only `pid <pid>: cwd in worktree` (`cwd_holders`), never commands. Cwd-only observers do not enter the census identity sets or any persisted walk seed. A cwd inside the worktree authorizes a signal only for an ancestor of a marked census member (the parent-chain rule below, D-KILL4), never for a cwd holder alone; leaving the worktree clears that worktree hold on the next deciding census, or, after the verdict, at the next terminal recheck (C-5.9). A cwd-only census never changes an attempt's verdict: after C-5.9's bounded wait, its provider rc, receipt and deliverable are captured exactly as with an empty census, and a start grace whose kill protocol leaves only cwd holders ends unlaunched, in the same state and with the same retry decision as with none (C-4.2; D-KILL4 fix round 2b, review r2's P3-1). Its worktree stays held while any cwd holder lives, with an operator notice naming the pids, never commands. Once the job has ended, however it ends (C-5.9), that hold is a reader row on the worktree (`worktree-read:<folder>:<job id>`, held by `cwd:<job id>`, C-8.4): it keeps retention from removing the folder and excludes no writer, so a resume of the job, or a writable turn into its worktree, is neither refused nor held for it (C-6.5, C-24.5). In-place workdirs and probes have no cwd hold. A failed required cwd read makes the result unverifiable; inaccessible other-user processes are skipped, as in the existing kernel scan. A cwd snapshot can miss a process that enters afterwards, as other census sources are observations rather than an atomic fence. It never records commands or environments. Parent-chain rule (D-KILL4; incident 2026-10-20, `hub-us10299-review-shell-b/a2`: marker pid 80881, born after the kill, whose parent loop 78382, a shell with hidden markers, was never in the census): when an attempt's census finds a marked member whose parent is not itself a member, it follows the parent links of its own snapshot. It adds each ancestor that is live, below launchd (pid > 1), not launchd, the daemon, its parent or another attempt's recorded guardian, of the daemon's uid by its kernel record, and whose canonical cwd is the job's own worktree or inside it, with the reason `pid <pid>: ancestor of marked pid <pid>, cwd in worktree` in the evidence (`chain_pids`, `chain`), never its command; added ancestors are census members and so signalled by C-5.6. Later censuses keep them by their birth in `chain_identities`, with each reason in `chain_reasons` (event `attempt.chain_recorded`), from whichever census added them, on every path; they are never walk seeds or counted identities, so their other descendants are members only when another source attributes them (D-KILL4 fix 1, P3-3, P3-4). An ancestor this attempt counted is a walk seed and so already a member, and the chain stops there. The first ancestor of the daemon's uid that does not qualify (no owned worktree, or a cwd outside it) is held, never added: `held_pids` and `held` (`pid <pid>: parent of marked pid <pid>, outside the census and the worktree; held, never signalled`), remembered by its birth in `held_parents` (event `attempt.parents_held`). A held parent keeps the census from being empty, so the attempt quarantines rather than finalizes, but it seeds no walk and is never signalled for its hold; nothing above it is looked at. A hold is that census's alone: it never authorizes a signal, and it vetoes none either. A later census that attributes the same process by a source of its own (the walk from a counted seed, its marker, the parent-chain rule, or a recorded group whose leader it verified) signals it as any member, so no process is signalled from a census that held it, and lineage that a later census establishes supersedes an earlier hold (D-KILL4 fix round 2b, review r2's P3-3: a parent held while its only link to the attempt ran through another user's counted process is TERMed once the walk from that counted process reaches it). A later census holds it again by its birth only while the last census saw a marked process name it (`held_marked`, event `attempt.parents_marked`); the kill protocol's censuses count as one, so a respawning driver stays held while the protocol ends its children. So a held parent with no marked descendant left releases (D-KILL4 fix 1, P3-2). So an operator's shell outside the worktree, which was never counted, is never signalled, and a cwd holder that is no ancestor of a marked member is neither added nor held. An ancestor that is gone, exiting or another user's is neither added nor held, and a failed read of an ancestor makes the census unverifiable. In-place jobs have no worktree, so their chains add nothing and hold the first same-uid ancestor. These keys are scoped to the attempt's boot as the seeds are (D-KILL4 with D-QUAR-REUSE1): `chain_identities`, `held_parents`, `held_marked` and the taint in `unverified_identities` never receive an identity whose boot-session UUID differs from the attempt's recorded boot, and a stored one that does is not the attempt's, so it is never re-added, held or matched as taint, and the census names each such chain ancestor or held parent it reads under `other_boot`. A held parent is held again by its birth only when its boot, and not only its start, matches this one; a comparison that is unknown or cannot be read keeps it held. The kill protocol carries what its own censuses held through the protocol without the attempt-boot filter, as that is this boot's and is not stored. So a marked process on a later boot is a member and a signal target, and so are the worktree ancestors the chain adds for it in that census, but none of them is kept.
- **C-5.6** Kill protocol, against the full census (D-KILL4): mark the cancel request, when there is one, and record the durable intent; SIGTERM the recorded pgid after its leader's identity (C-5.4) and every other census member C-5.4 allows, one by one; SIGCONT each of them that the census shows stopped (ps state `T`) right after a TERM that reached it, so that it can act on it (measured 2026-10-23: a stopped process with TERM's default action ends on the TERM alone, while one that handles TERM, as node does, stays stopped with the TERM pending until it is continued; Fleet ops' governor and the clamshell guard both stop processes). The group's TERM is credited only to the members, by identity, of the census taken before it that are still in the group; a member found since is sent a TERM of its own before any continue, so every continue follows a TERM delivered to that same process and nothing the protocol is not signalling is continued. The guardian is never continued: it ignores TERM by design, so a continue would only resume its launch. A `starting` attempt whose last read of start.json, taken after its abandon is written (C-4.2), finds none has its group KILLed at once, with no TERM: at an expired start grace, and at a cancel or a wall stop while it is starting (review r3 of #250, P3-2); a guardian that group's KILL did not reach (stopped before its setsid) is KILLed alone, never TERMed (D-KILL4 fix 1, review r1 P2, P3-1); wait `term_grace_s` (15), re-taking the census and sending TERM (and that continue) to members found since; SIGKILL the pgid, enumerate (C-5.5) and SIGKILL every surviving member one by one (KILL needs no continue); enumerate again, repeating for up to `kill_settle_s` (3) while the census is not verified empty and killing members found since, because signalled processes leave the process table only when the kernel finishes tearing them down and one read taken 50 ms after SIGKILL reports an exiting process as live under load (incident: 2026-09-05, two full-suite runs quarantined attempts that a rerun did not). The window ends early on the first verified-empty census and never widens what counts as contained. Each run records `attempt.containment` and `terminal_containment` evidence: the group's signals with their reason; each signal it sent one by one (`signals`, up to 256) with the pid, the reason that pid is the attempt's, the census sources that counted it then and its parent; whether it ended contained; and the parent-chain reasons and held parents of every census it took (fix 1, P3-4), never commands. The event also keeps the census the run started from and the one it ended on. Verified empty releases the workspace and finalizes with `killed_by`. Anything else is `quarantined`. No attempt ends `failed`, `lost`, `interrupted` or `cancelled` with live census members: a cancel or wall stop, a dead guardian with members (C-4.2), an expired start grace with members (C-4.2), and writers after the exit receipt (C-5.9) all run this protocol first.
