The last records no `killed_by`: its provider ended before any signal, so its verdict stands. Ends of a `reserved` attempt, which has no guardian yet (`reserved-no-launch`, a cancel before its launch, an adapter or workspace refusal, an unfinished launch-time check), need none; a `starting` attempt that ends `cancelled-before-launch` runs this protocol first, its group KILLed at once as above; a guardian whose launch gate closed unopened, which starts nothing and exits 127, is waited for and if need be killed by its spawn handle (`GATED_REAP_S`, 2 s) before its attempt ends. `--force-release` stays the operator's recorded override (C-5.7). Probes keep their own containment (C-11.4: the recorded group and owned identities).
- **C-5.7** A `quarantined` attempt keeps its leases (lane slot excluded), its workspace, and its worktree. `subfleet runs show` prints the evidence. Resolution is `subfleet kill <job> --confirm-dead` after the operator checks, which re-runs C-5.5 and releases only on verified empty, or `--force-release` which records the operator's override in `events`. Re-containment (D-KILL4): a plain `subfleet kill <job>` on a finished job whose last attempt had a guardian and whose census (C-5.5, cwd holders aside) is not empty, a quarantine or an attempt an older daemon finalized, answers `containment requested` with the census pids and runs C-5.6's kill protocol against that census on a worker; it records `attempt.recontainment_started`, then `attempt.recontained` and `recontainment` evidence (when, contained or not, survivors), and leaves the attempt's state as it was: a quarantine still needs `--confirm-dead`, and a finished verdict is not reopened. Once that census is empty (cwd holders aside), the next `kill` of that job reports the last result; while it is not, the next `kill` answers from its own census as this one did: the protocol again when that census has a member it may signal or one a verifiable read could attribute, else `already finished` (below). `--confirm-dead` on a finished job that is not quarantined answers `not quarantined` while its last attempt's census is not empty, naming the pids, and releases nothing: it never reports dead while a census member lives. Both answers name held parents too (`held parent, never signalled`), which only their ending or, for a quarantine, `--force-release` releases while they hold (D-KILL4 fix 1). A census with no member C-5.4 lets the protocol signal runs no protocol when it is verifiable (only members a leaderless group at the recorded pgid names, or held parents), and when it is not, unless a verifiable read could attribute a member: one the walk or the responsibility source names, other than a pid C-5.6 never signals and a member an unverifiable census first recorded or that descends from one (C-5.4: neither source attributes it, in any census); a marked or parent-chain member, a member of the recorded group under a verified leader, or a member whose `owned_identities` record is not on another boot than the attempt's, at a pid this census has not proven recycled, whose identity this census could not read, other than a pid C-5.6 never signals (C-5.4 attributes it in any census, an owned one when its identity is the recorded one, and only that identity keeps it from a signal; review r1 of #276, P2-1, review r2 for an owned member, review r3 for a recycled pid); or no member at all, as when the listing failed. Such a `kill` answers `already finished` and names the members `held, never signalled`, with no `recontainment` round or TERM grace spent, and `--confirm-dead` says none of them may be signalled. An unverifiable census with a member a verifiable read could attribute, or with none, is re-contained, since the protocol's own censuses may verify it (review r3 of #250, P3-1: a stranger's leaderless group at the recorded pgid with a legacy attempt boot or an unreadable current boot is unverifiable on every read, since the absent leader never verifies, and each `kill` ran a protocol that could signal nothing; D-KILL4 fix round 2b, review r2's P3-2: three such kills each spent a full TERM grace and settle window, signalled nothing and pushed the attempt's real round out of its kept four, and `--force-release` answers `already finished` for a job that is not quarantined).
- **C-5.9** After `exit.json` is read, a census with attributed survivors or an unverifiable result does not quarantine at once. The daemon allows `exit_settle_s` (3), measured from the first such census, for the guardian, which writes the receipt before C-5.2's responsibility wait and exits after that wait, and for processes it already reaped to leave the process table, re-running the census on each tick. Survivors or an unverifiable census past the window are contained first: C-5.6's kill protocol against the full census, with no `killed_by` (D-KILL4). A verified-empty end finalizes from the attempt's own receipt as an empty census does; survivors of that protocol, or writers that reappear after `TERMINAL_CONTAINMENT_ROUNDS` (3) protocol runs of one attempt, quarantine it with detail `writers remain after exit receipt`. When only cwd holders remain at finalization or start grace, re-take the census at inspect_interval_s for up to 30 s, measured from the first cwd-only result. This wait and its cadence are in daemon memory, so the 30 s window starts over after a daemon restart. If they leave within that window, proceed at the next due census. If they remain, finalize from the attempt's own outcome exactly as with an empty census (start grace with no receipt ends as unlaunched; and a start grace whose kill protocol leaves only cwd holders ends unlaunched at once, with no wait, as nothing launched, C-4.2), preserving provider rc, receipt and deliverable; never quarantine for cwd-only evidence. Persist cwd_holders and a pid-only operator notice. Release lane and output leases normally. Where the job ends with a cwd holder in its worktree (its final export, a verdict without an accepted attempt, an unlaunched attempt that ends it (no attempts left, or cancelled before launch), quarantine resolution, a cancel while it is queued or waiting to retry, or a failure while it prepares its retry), the transaction that releases its other leases turns each worktree lease of the job or that attempt into a reader row on the same folder, held by `cwd:<job id>` (C-5.5), so the folder is never without a row and no writer is refused or held for it. Where no census decided the ending (the export, an ending from the queue, and an unlaunched attempt other than start grace's, cancelled before launch included), the worktree is scanned before that transaction; a scan that cannot be read keeps the row. A cancel requested by a thread that already holds the store's write lock takes no scan, so no scan of every process's cwd holds that lock (0.65 to 3.70 s on review r5 of #240's host): the wall's cascade, which a parent's quarantine resolution or finalization requests inside the transaction that settles the parent's attempt, keeps each job's row with no pid line, as a scan that cannot be read does, for the cwd worker to settle. Nor does a cancel scan a job that has finished, one whose export is pending included: its transaction leaves that job alone, and the export takes its own scan. The job's notice names the pids its census or scan found (`worktree retained`) at every ending but two, which add no notice line: the export, whose verdict's notice named those its census found, and the wall's cascade, which takes no scan. A job queued again after an unlaunched attempt is still the writer there and keeps its worktree lease. Finished jobs recheck that row at inspect_interval_s, measured from before each scan, including after restart, releasing it on the next empty cwd scan. A failed scan retains the row. The cwd worker releases only these rows, never a worktree lease or another job's row, and only for a finished job that, with its attempts, holds no other lease, so its export, push and every other lease have settled; it revalidates that atomically before releasing them. Quarantine confirmation and salvage use containment evidence alone: cwd observers neither block --confirm-dead nor prevent --force-release salvage of a contained attempt, and the worktree stays held, as a reader row (C-5.5). A dead guardian with cwd-only evidence settles as lost without a kill event. That receipt-less loss path is paced as the waits above are: its census and its loss wait for the attempt's cwd clock, which runs at inspect_interval_s from when its inspection fell due but never restarts sooner than inspect_interval_s after the clock that allowed the census before it (an attempt given a shared table that expires early is inspected sooner than that, C-5.12), the loss is decided from that one census, and its end (`attempt.lost_end`) is recorded once. This never authorizes a signal.
- **C-5.8** The daemon holds an exclusive `fcntl.flock` on `daemon.lock` and writes its pid, boot id, proc start, and version there. A second daemon exits 69. The CLI treats a lock whose recorded identity is dead as no daemon.
- **C-5.8a** A stopping daemon that has not ended `stop_grace_s` (30 s) after its stop was armed normally writes a managed Python snapshot of up to 100 thread stacks, up to 100 frames each, to `daemon.log` and calls `_exit(1)`. The preexisting stop watcher captures frame references through `sys._current_frames()` and formats file, line, function and thread name without reading source lines or locals. This avoids the C watchdog's unsynchronized raw frame walk, which can stop at `<invalid frame>` on Python 3.14 while the thread changes frames. Its deadline is monotonic and starts before watchdog setup, so delayed C watchdog creation does not restart the Python drain wait. The existing `STOP_DUMP_MARGIN_S` (3 s) is divided equally: the independent C watchdog dumps and exits 1 at the drain deadline plus 1.5 s (31.5 s by default), while the kernel `ITIMER_REAL` timer, installed first, terminates with SIGALRM at the drain deadline plus 3 s (33 s). A working Python snapshot finishes before the C fallback; the C fallback cuts off a slow Python snapshot; the kernel cuts off a failed, cancelled, slow or blocked C dump. A GIL hog can prevent the Python snapshot, and scheduling or blocked output can still make diagnostics absent or incomplete and cause exit by SIGALRM. The independent kernel timer retains its original deadline. It needs no GIL, successful `Event.set`, watcher wakeup, or new thread at stop time. If the kernel timer cannot be installed, arming exits 1 immediately instead of continuing without a bound. `close()` still releases `daemon.lock` only after every pool has drained, so the store keeps one writer. The bound ends the process instead of releasing the lock early, and the kernel frees the flock with the process. No guardian is signalled, and forked children do not inherit the interval timer; the next daemon adopts attempts still running (C-4.2), as after a SIGKILL. One nonblocking claim, created at startup and never released, permits only the first `arm` invocation to install the timers. A handler interrupting that invocation returns without setting the event until arming completes, so it cannot deadlock the owner before timer installation. Later invocations cannot replace either deadline. The stop is armed on the thread that begins it, before `stopping` is set: `close()` and the end of `serve_forever` call `arm` directly. A SIGTERM or SIGINT arms when the main thread runs its handler; a thread holding the GIL can delay that handler indefinitely. The handler skips `Event.set` once the event is set. A signal nested inside `set` remains bounded by the already installed timers. The startup watcher also arms when anything else sets `stopping`, then waits only the time remaining to the original drain deadline before taking the managed snapshot. The C and kernel fallbacks do not depend on this watcher making progress. The stopping-line write follows timer installation and can itself block, so the line is not guaranteed to precede the dump. launchd's `ExitTimeOut` (which `daemon install` writes as `stop_grace_s` + 10 s; with none set, `launchctl print` reports 5 s) and `subfleet daemon stop` each wait `stop_grace_s` + 10 s for stops they initiate. After its wait, `daemon stop` re-verifies the identity (C-5.4) and sends SIGKILL. Raw signals and self-initiated stops do not start either external backstop. The grace outlasts probe containment during a stop: SIGTERM, up to `term_grace_s` of census polling, then SIGKILL and one census. The descriptor for the dump is opened when the daemon starts, so a daemon stopping because it ran out of descriptors can still write. A daemon built inside a test process arms nothing. The bound can end a stop that is slow but healthy, including the session mirror's flag publish, which is not crash-safe; launchd's SIGKILL, a crash or an operator's kill already cut that window. (Incident: 2026-09-25, pid 93697 shut its socket and then waited without a deadline on pool threads parked in `RLock.acquire`. It held the lock until a restart at 19:26Z, while every client was refused and every replacement exited 69; `docs/reports/2026-09-25-daemon-stop-wedge.md`.)
- **C-5.10** A worker that raises is retried with backoff, not on the next tick. The control loop offers every live attempt a pass could act on (C-5.11), every pending export, and admission to the worker pool each tick (50 ms), and a worker that raised used to be eligible again at once, so a cause that fails fast (a repository git refuses, a store error) was retried, and logged, twenty times a second for as long as it lasted. Now a key the control loop offers (an attempt id, `export:<job>`, `admission`, `timer-recovery`, `retention`) is not run again until 0.5 s after its first consecutive failure, doubling to a 60 s ceiling; a success clears the count; a running attempt's pass that stops at its pacing gate or cannot inspect, while an inspection that raised has not yet run to its end, is neither (C-5.11). A one-shot request is never paced: `resolve:<job>`, an operator's `kill --confirm-dead` or `--force-release`, is offered once, by the request itself, so holding it back would drop it after the caller was told it was accepted; it runs whenever it is asked for and its failure is logged each time. `daemon.log` gets the line on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th (each power of two) consecutive failure, with the count and the next retry, and still only the exception's type, never its message (a provider or keychain error can carry a secret). The count is in memory: a restart forgives it, and recovery re-offers every live key as before (incident: 2026-09-20, `worker 20260920-081856-native-other-disability-opus/a1 failed: SalvageError` forty times in `daemon.log`; that cause was a git timeout, which paced itself at its own 15 s cap, and nothing in the daemon would have paced one that failed at once).
- **C-5.11** A running attempt or probe costs the daemon a bounded number of subprocesses, however often it is offered, and a tick costs a bounded number of store statements, however much history is kept. The control loop still looks at every live attempt each tick (C-5.10), and a pass still reads its exit receipt, its cancel request and its wall limit and inspects a running attempt's processes as C-5.12 says, from one table every running attempt shares. What the loop gives the worker pool is only the attempts a pass could act on: one statement a tick (`LIVE_TICK`) reads, for every live attempt, its state and where its receipts are, and its job's cancel request, start and wall limit, naming no column SQLite keeps on overflow pages; a `running` attempt with no file at `exit.json` (one `stat`), no cancel request, its wall limit not reached, no inspection due (C-5.12), no failed or deferred pass to repeat (C-5.10; offered so that a pass that succeeds clears the failure count, which a withheld attempt would leave stale) and, if this daemon started its guardian, that guardian not ended, is not given a worker that tick, because its pass would read those same facts and return having done nothing; the answer is for the tick it is given in, so a due time or a receipt that arrives after it is seen on the next tick, 50 ms on. Every other live attempt, and every doubt about what a pass would do (a `stat` that fails for a reason other than absence, a job row it cannot find, any error the filter itself raises), is given one as before, so an error is the pass's to raise and C-5.10's to pace, never the control loop's; a doubt about whether v1 still owns the attempt is a no (migration principle 3): it is given no pass, asked again next tick, and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th and later powers of two, and a pass that finds the attempt `imported_external` returns having done nothing; a `stat` that hangs holds the control thread, which is the cost of not reading the receipt on a worker. `imported_external` is read from the attempt's evidence until it says no, and an attempt this daemon owns is remembered as such until it is no longer live (property: in every combination of state, receipt, cancel request, wall limit, inspection clock and pending retry, an attempt the loop withholds is one whose pass returns having acted on nothing, committed nothing and changed no pacing; measured 2026-10-02 on `main`, whose control loop has no conversation or turn keys, with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py --running 26 --history 2500 --waiting 4 --evidence-bytes 8192` on the live daemon's interpreter build, three alternating rounds at a load average between 135 and 300: the saturated window took 20.2%, 17.7% and 16.3% of a core before, 11.9%, 12.2% and 4.8% after). A probe's wait loop, which has no shared table, reads its receipt, its job and its deadline every pass, and asks `ps` whether its guardian is still the recorded process (C-5.3) once every `inspect_interval_s` (1 s), and once more in a check that finds a new group member, to confirm the leader before recording it (C-5.4). It records the probe's owned group members from C-5.5's group source alone, one `ps -axo pid=,pgid=,stat=,lstart=` snapshot, inside that check and at most every `OWNED_CENSUS_INTERVAL_S` (0.5 s, so with the default interval on every check), capturing an identity (C-5.3) only for a member it has not recorded or whose start has changed. A running attempt's inspection that raises leaves no due time, so the retry C-5.10 schedules repeats it in full: it asks for a table at once, and is given the last one while that is less than an interval old (C-5.12). Until an inspection has run to its end, a pass that could not inspect (another attempt's read was running; the table, its boot identity or a table to record the group from could not be read; the guardian's liveness was unknown) or that stops at the pacing gate returns `DEFERRED`, which neither clears C-5.10's count nor adds to it, so a cause that lasts is retried and logged on C-5.10's backoff (review of the merge, 2026-09-26: such a pass returned normally, which counted as recovery, so whenever a `ps` read took 0.5 s or more each retry landed inside the next read and the count went 1, 0, 1). The boot identity is read as C-5.12 says; the three-source census, whose marker source reads every process's environment, is taken only to decide a release, kill, loss or quarantine. Pending exports are found with one statement (`PENDING_EXPORTS`), not a lease lookup per accepted job. A `wait` re-reads the store only as C-15.5 says, after a top-level transaction has committed a change or every `WAIT_RECHECK_S` (1 s), through one reader for all waiters; a worker pass wakes waiters only when the store's generation moved while it ran, and makes that decision before its key is released. Incident: 2026-09-24, the per-tick inspection (three subprocesses per running attempt per 50 ms tick, and every 0.5 s a census that dumped every process's environment, 2.3 MB), waiters re-reading their jobs on every worker pass, and an export sweep of 266 statements a tick saturated the store lock; `daemon.status` took 38.8 s, every client timed out, and shutdown hung until SIGKILL (docs/reports/2026-09-24-daemon-inspection-wedge.md). (Merged 2026-09-26 with C-5.12, which replaced this clause's per-attempt `LIVENESS_INTERVAL_S` pacing of running attempts with the shared table.)
- **C-5.12** Inspection is rationed; receipt and cancel decisions are not. Wall enforcement follows inspected liveness. Each tick (50 ms) looks at a running attempt's exit receipt, its cancel request and its wall clock, which are files and rows, and a pass reads its `start.json` and `exit.json` (C-5.11 says which attempts are given a pass). Without an exit receipt, a starting or running attempt is inspected before the wall can act: a provider confirmed gone settles lost, one whose liveness cannot be read waits, and only a provider confirmed alive is subject to the wall on that pass. At the cap the wall asks liveness again, then re-reads the receipt before requesting cancellation, and re-reads it immediately before signalling; a shared table's earlier liveness cannot override a known exit (D-WALL1). The start grace still decides an absent launch with no receipt. Its processes are inspected at most once per `inspect_interval_s` (1), from one process table (C-5.5's snapshot) that every running attempt shares; an inspection that raised is repeated at C-5.10's backoff, on the same table while it is less than an interval old (C-5.11). A table expires one interval after its read began. An attempt falls due when the table it was last given expires (one this daemon launches, one interval after it started the guardian, so that its first table is read after the guardian started; one it recovered, when it first asks), and is given the last table read if that expires later, which means a table read after the one it last had and less than an interval before it fell due; otherwise it reads a new one, and that is the only time a read begins. So the table's `ps` begins at most once per interval however many attempts ask and however long it takes (when it takes longer than an interval, reads run back to back, one at a time), and only the inspection that reads waits for it, and for the table's boot identity, which the reader reads before any attempt is given the table: one that finds a read running returns and asks again on its next tick, so a slow or hung `ps` or `sysctl` holds one worker, not one per running attempt (except the `kern.boottime` read below, which the legacy attempts given the table wait for together), and delays only the reading attempt's per-tick reads. The reader is whichever attempt asks first once the table has expired, and the control loop offers running attempts in the order the store lists them, so one attempt can be the reader interval after interval and wait out every slow read. An abnormal guardian death is seen by the first table read that begins after it, which begins by the first tick one interval after the death that finds a free worker, or as the read then running ends if that read (`ps`, then the boot identity) is slower than that, once that read and one fresh read (below) have run. A read that failed is rationed like one that worked, and an inspection given a failed read asks nothing singly and decides nothing, as a guardian that cannot be inspected decides nothing (C-4.2), so that a `ps` outage costs at most one capped read per interval rather than one per running attempt. The table says that the recorded guardian is alive (C-5.3), when it is, and lists the members of its group, whose identities are recorded as owned (C-5.6) only while that same table shows the recorded guardian alive and leading the recorded group; the same table also walks from the guardian and previously recorded matching identities, retaining absent or zombie parent-link anchors and excluding proven different births, and records live walk members outside the group as `counted_identities` (C-5.5). Only the group grants ownership; neither the raw-buffer marker scan nor responsibility scan is run here, and the walk adds no process listing. A shared table may say "alive" and nothing else, and says it only where `liveness` would (C-5.3): on an exact match of pid and start, and of the boot identity or, for a guardian recorded with a legacy boot timestamp (its UUID `sysctl` failed once as it started), a timestamp that C-5.3 matches to the table's `kern.boottime` seconds. A table reads those seconds the first time a legacy record needs them and keeps them for its life, failed or not, so every legacy attempt given the shared table shares one `sysctl`, and one that asks while it runs waits for it. A guardian it does not show alive is asked about afresh (C-5.3's own reads) before anything follows, and one that the fresh reads find alive has its group and walk recorded from a table read for it alone, one `ps` outside the shared read (rare, since a table read after the guardian started shows it); and every census that decides (one fresh process-table `ps`, one fresh root start-time `ps` read when no unique id was recorded and a live snapshot row matches its recorded start, a kernel pid listing, state/unique-id/birth and `KERN_PROCARGS2` reads per accessible live pid, responsibility reads when the root is eligible, captured kernel state and birth instead of per-member ps for responsibility observations, and one in-process root-holder read after each applicable responsibility scan for either kind of root (none after early rejection, a failed listing or proven other boot); start grace, a dead guardian, the kill protocol and its settle window, C-5.9's window, quarantine resolution, probe containment) is read fresh, never from the shared table. The shared table's reader reads its boot identity just after `ps`, and a census's table the first time a live pid needs it; each reads it once, whatever the answer: every attempt given the shared table shares that read, and when it fails no attempt asks singly and nothing is decided, as for a failed `ps`; a UUID read that fell back to `kern.boottime` seconds has failed for every attempt recorded with the UUID, which C-5.3 could only call unknown against them. One boot-identity read is reused for 5 s, and only a boot session UUID is: a failed read is not remembered, and neither are the `kern.boottime` seconds that a failed UUID read falls back to, against which every process recorded with the UUID would compare as unknown and so could not be signalled; a process whose start matches is never called dead on a boot-identity mismatch until a fresh read agrees. `procs` starts `ps` and `sysctl` without `fork` (`close_fds=False`, the condition under which CPython uses `posix_spawn` on macOS), because a fork costs in proportion to the daemon's memory; the daemon's `git` starts still fork; guardians use the in-process disclaimed `posix_spawn` helper (C-5.1), with the existing subprocess fallback when its symbols are unavailable or the daemon is not verified self-responsible at startup. Every descriptor the daemon opens is close-on-exec, but macOS has no `pipe2` and no `SOCK_CLOEXEC`, so a pipe or socket that another thread is creating at that instant can be inherited, by a `ps` or `sysctl` only, which holds it until it exits within its 10 s cap: the most that follows is a close seen that much later (`close_fds=True` would close it, but CPython on macOS then forks). The one descriptor this package hands down on purpose, the guardian's launch gate, is closed by the guardian before it inspects anything. The per-tick check for exports to retry is one query driven from `leases`, and the desktop login hint (C-10.3) is parsed only when `stat` says the file changed. Inspection pacing bounds how soon an abnormal guardian death is noticed, a wall stop can act, and a new group member is recorded as owned, and the attempt whose inspection is reading the table waits out that read, `ps` and `sysctl`, before its own per-tick reads; and a member missed is never signalled individually (C-5.4): while it stays in the group `killpg` reaches it with the rest, and one that has left the group is quarantined (incident: 2026-09-20, with four attempts running the daemon held a full core, about fifteen process starts a second: three to ask about each healthy guardian on every tick and a census of `2 + 3N` every 0.5 s; the laptop's thermal guard, which pauses the operator's command-line processes at or above 20% CPU while the lid is closed on battery, SIGSTOPped it for two hours; measured 2026-09-24 with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py`, production tick, timers off, `origin/main` and the first version of this clause (845d663): idle 7.8% and 0.5% of a core; four running attempts and twelve waiting jobs 46.5% and 3.9%, with the `ps` it started 71.5% and 2.7%; measured again 2026-09-26, after this clause's review, at a load average near 7: idle 3.0% and 0.2%; four running and twelve waiting 29.0% and 1.9%, with its `ps` 49.3% and 2.1%). Counted memory is bounded by live walk members (including live recorded seeds) plus entries seen in the latest two distinct inspection tables, rather than run length. Drop a counted entry only after two consecutive tables show it absent, a zombie, or provably of a different birth; an unreadable live birth is no proof of absence or reuse. Never drop a live recorded birth. First-miss tracking is a bounded persisted counted_absent map, paired with the recorded birth, and survives restart; only current-table deduplication is in daemon memory; re-inspecting the same table counts no additional miss and makes no transaction. A fresh table also persists D-WALL1's last_observed_alive_at, coalesced with identity changes when possible; repeating that table does not advance the observation clock. Each deciding census copies its seeds before inspection can expire stored entries, and retains those seeds throughout that census. Counted identities never authorize a signal.

- **C-5.13** A verified `daemon.lock` holder whose `ps` state begins with `T` (including flags such as `T+`, `Ts` and `T<`) is alive but stopped: it retains its flock and socket but cannot answer. Before sending a request, the client reports it unavailable (exit 69), names its pid and raw state, and gives `kill -CONT <pid>` as the repair; diagnosis never sends that signal. State and start time come from one `ps` read, and only a matching recorded identity licenses the diagnosis. Only rc 0 with exactly one well-formed state/start row can verify a live or stopped holder; rc 1 with no output means gone, and every other failed or malformed observation is unverifiable. Each send gets a fresh preflight observation, including on reused clients. One client's `ps` process starts, including failed reads and failure diagnostics, use a token bucket holding at most four tokens and refilling at one token per `HOLDER_PROBE_INTERVAL_S` (1 s): four `ps` processes may start back to back, after which another waits for a token. A token authorizes a fresh state/identity read, never a cached running verdict; it is spent immediately before `ps` starts, after boot reads, and elapsed time refills only the bucket's four-token capacity. Thus every interval of duration `t` admits at most `4 + t / HOLDER_PROBE_INTERVAL_S` `ps` process starts, the processes that load the host. The client waits for an available slot before boot reads without spending the token, then rechecks and spends it at ps. Boot reads and any following slot recheck remain inside the same observation ceiling. Slot waits consume the request's remaining deadline; if its next slot is too late, the call ends at the deadline without another send, with `wait` retaining exit 124. Preflight, connect, send, response reads, busy/refused retries and settlement requests share each call's deadline, and a success observed after that deadline is rejected. Every whole identity observation has one `HOLDER_PROBE_BUDGET_S` (5 s) budget covering all boot-UUID reads, legacy boot fallback/comparison reads, and `ps` together; there is no separate boot-read allowance. Each stage receives only the time left in that observation. Preflight and post-send or failed-connect diagnosis also share the same absolute ceiling, the original call deadline plus one probe budget; no diagnostic restarts that ceiling, so their combined work cannot return the call past it. A diagnostic waits for a token only until the original call deadline; an already available token may fund a final observation within that shared ceiling, without licensing a late success. Exhausted observations remain unverifiable, never proof of death. Preflight costs at most one `ps` per request for identity and state; failure diagnosis may take a further observation through the same bucket and deadlines. Parsing and OS scheduling are outside the subprocess budget, so there is no 1.5 s wall-clock guarantee. C-15.6 retains the hook preflight opt-out. `Z` with any flags remains gone; `R`, `S`, `U` and `I` with flags remain live. Unreadable process or boot identity stays unverifiable, never proof of death. A verifying client's connect failure asks about the holder again. The C-15.6 inspection opt-out also applies to a refused connect that follows no busy answer; a refused connect after a busy answer asks once whether the holder lives, opted out or not, so a dead holder ends a hook's loop at once (C-16.7). A read timeout after sending still reports an unknown outcome (exit 1, C-16.3), names a stop observed then, and requires preflight even for a client that opted out, so a retry cannot add another request to the paused buffer. A silent verified running holder keeps exit 1 with logs/stop advice rather than starting another daemon; no verified holder keeps the ordinary start advice. Read verbs retain C-17.5's offline behavior and print the stopped diagnosis and repair. `daemon start` refuses a second daemon over the stopped holder; `daemon status` names it as stopped and includes `holder_state`, `holder_stopped` and `diagnosis` in JSON while retaining busy and descriptor reporting. A later `DaemonStopped` observation replaces the earlier state, liveness, diagnosis and repair in text and JSON (`fix`), with the same exit 69 as an initial stopped holder. Doctor's live descriptor row and daemon start/install readiness checks also retain a later stopped diagnosis and continue repair. Doctor's lock and live rows fail with the same repair. A known pauser is named only if its own marker lists this pid: the clamshell guard's `~/.local/state/clamshell-guard/paused.pids` selects its lid/power/resume advice; missing, undecodable or unrelated markers attribute nothing. Release's C-5.8a stop and escalation remain unchanged; this diagnosis adds no automatic continue signal.


## 6. Admission

- **C-6.1** Submission validates before touching the store: workdir exists and is not under `/tmp` unless allowed; prompt file readable; `-o` path's directory exists; sandbox in {`read-only`, `workspace-write`}; `--task`/`--tier` valid per policy or `-m`/`-a`/`-H` pins consistent; exclusions (`-x`) a list of names, which admission merges with lane ids (C-6.12); a read-only job with `-o` inside `-C` is allowed (the daemon writes it).
- **C-6.2** The payload digest is SHA-256 over the canonical JSON of: prompt bytes (as base64), resolved workdir, git head of the workdir or null, task, tier, pinned model, pinned lane, sandbox, sorted exclusions, resolved `-o` path or null, `allow_desktop`, policy hash. A repeated request id with the same digest returns the existing job id (exit 0); with a different digest, exit 2. Submission request ids are opaque nonempty strings of at most 128 characters, preserved unchanged, including colons, other punctuation and Unicode; recognized credentials are refused without echoing them. The protocol correlation `id` is a separate field with its own token validation (C-25.7).
- **C-6.3** Admission of an attempt is one transaction that: chooses the lane and model from the decision (section 11), takes the lane slot lease `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` (a turn's is `lane:<lane id>:slot:turn-<n>`, C-26.9), takes the output lease `out:<resolved path>` if `-o` is set and not already held by this job, takes the worktree lease if writable (on the write target, C-6.5: `worktree:<target>` for a detached writer, and for a conversation turn a row of its own, `worktree-turn:<target>:<job id>`, beside which it needs `worktree:<target>` free, C-24.5; a session is not a place and takes no lease), inserts the attempt as `reserved`, and records the decision. Launch happens after commit. The decision is evaluated before the transaction, off the store lock, on rows read in one read snapshot (C-3.7); the evaluation `_prepare_route` makes to decide on a probe is the one reserved on, so a job is evaluated once, not twice, before its reservation (a transient retry's pinned pair is also evaluated, as before, by `_retry_waits_on_a_slot`; and when the lane chosen is not the job's to probe, C-11.4's choice evaluates it again with lanes left out, and an evaluation again after a moved route leaves the same lanes out). The transaction never builds or reads a capacity view, reads no table whole but the small lane, lease and reset-credit ones, and never calls `evaluate`. It checks the decision on rows it reads with a few small queries (`Daemon._route_rows`, `route_check.still_stands`): every lane row, marked and merged as a view marks and merges them (`capacity.mark_desktop`, `Timers.merge_lane`, `capacity.credential_latched`); the attempts in flight (`attempts_live`); the probe leases; the readings added since the snapshot, by id (a reading id is reused only after the newest reading is deleted, so while the snapshot's newest reading stands, every reading added since has a greater id); the closures not released (`closures_active`, and by id any the snapshot held with an empty `released_at`); the lanes a confirmed reset-credit override (C-23.17) covers now, decided for the lane rows now at a view's clock (`Actions.override_context`), since whether one covers a lane turns on the lane's account key and home, so a lane enrolled or moved since can gain or lose one with no action changing (the review of 9dd4f35 found a `hard` job placed without its probe on a lane enrolled meanwhile); and the parents of the jobs a parent cap counts. The decision looks at the lanes of the models it walks (`scheduler.model_lanes`: each model's provider's, or the one lane the job's pin names), and only those are judged. A lane whose values of the fields `evaluate` reads (`scheduler.LANE_FACTS`), readings, open closures, attempts in flight in the job's pool, slot block and override are what they were, and whose own clock has not reached its horizon, is judged as it was; each other lane is judged again, alone, in memory, at the check's clock (`scheduler.judge_lane`), and ranked against the candidates that did not change (`scheduler.rank_key`). A lane's horizon (`capacity.lane_horizons`, recorded off the lock with the early decision) is the first instant at which the clock alone could change how that lane is judged: a reading it counted as fresh stops being so (its `observed_at` plus `reading_ttl_s`, or its `resets_at`), a latest provider window renews even when its observation is already stale (C-11.3; a reset at or before its own observation is already unusable and adds no clock), a reading observed after the view's clock becomes fresh, or a closure ends. `scheduler.evaluate` reads the clock through those usage/reset guards and a closure's `until_at`, so before its own horizon a lane whose rows did not change is judged as it was, whatever any other lane's clock does. Each of these can close a lane, not only open one: the review of 5841d8b found a reading going stale, and the review of f48df54 an override ending (the lane's reading, held out, is below the floor) and a reported closure on a reserved model ending (the lane's slack behind a probe becomes `unmeasured`, C-11.7). `Timers.enrich_view`, which relabels an overridden lane's readings, and `_pick`, which holds them out, both decide an override at the view's clock, so one view never does the one without the other; a lane whose override began or ended since is judged again with its readings held out or put back. A fleet or parent cap that began or ended refuses or frees every lane alike, so every lane of the walk is judged again then; and when the model the decision chose has no candidate now and the chain goes on, the next models' lanes are judged, as `evaluate` walks them. The check then makes the decision an evaluation of the rows the transaction sees, at its clock, would make, exactly and in `evaluate`'s order (`route_check.as_now`): each changed lane's verdict, detail and evidence put in place, each reading it carries aged and labelled at the check's clock (a `provider` reading past `reading_ttl_s` is `stale-provider`), `evaluated_at` that clock's second; so the probe a lane needs (C-11.4) is read from now's detail, and a wait recorded on the decision is clocked by now's verdict (C-6.10). It goes on with that decision, the same lane or another, or none. It does not decide, and the transaction rolls back before it has written anything, only when it cannot rebuild what the decision rests on: the job's pin now names another lane (a re-enrolment moved the binding it follows, C-11.2); an evaluation now would raise, as the one made again off the lock then does; a policy loaded since; a clock earlier than the instant the early decision's view was built at (a clock that stepped back); a reading the snapshot held that was deleted (retention deletes readings only with a pruned job's attempts); or a row it cannot read (a timestamp that does not parse), which the evaluation made again then settles as C-6.12 says; a check that raises on every try settles the job so too (a `route` wait naming the error, backed off), never leaving it `route-moved`. Then the route is evaluated again, off the lock, and checked again, up to `ROUTE_TRIES` (3) times in one pass; then the job keeps its place (`route-moved`, C-6.11: the later jobs it competes with wait behind it, C-6.9) and the next pass, which follows at once, looks again. A clock or a row on a lane the decision does not look at never sends it back: on d04b8b3 the check refused any decision past the fleet-wide horizon, and sixty Claude lanes' staggered readings, refreshed on a 120 s cycle, refused every check of a Codex job three seconds after its evaluation, thirty passes running (review of d04b8b3); on 5d14f98 it refused a cap that began or ended, and a timer probe's lease (which counts toward the detached fleet's cap, C-6.4) held at each evaluation and gone at each check refused a job every try (review of 5d14f98). Age alone voids nothing: `ROUTE_REUSE_S` (5 s), which sent every evaluation older than that to be made again inside, is gone, since the check is exact and, on 2026-09-26, the store lock was waited for longer than that. The transaction also finds the job no attempt in flight or quarantined before it reserves one (C-26.9's two passes never take the same job, and this makes that a rule of the store). Two changes the check does not see, as the evaluation it replaces did not: `Timers` updates its in-memory probe status (`Timers.metadata`) just after the commit that records the probe, so a check made between the two reads the status from before the probe; and a commit by another process, which C-3.4 allows only as a hand edit of a running daemon's store. `daemon.status` counts under `admission.route_evaluations`: checks that kept the early decision's choice, a lane or none (`reused`), and checks that chose again from the lanes judged again (`rechosen`); evaluations made again after a check could not decide (`again`), and why (`moved`: a pin that names another lane now, an evaluation that would raise, or what the check rests on could not be rebuilt; `old`: a clock earlier than the view's; `error`: the check raised); jobs left for the next pass (`deferred`); and the lanes checks judged again (`rejudged`). Incidents: with the daemon held to 5% of a core, evaluating a whole capacity view inside this transaction held the store lock for 13.5 s (`docs/reports/2026-09-25-store-lock-contention.md`); on 2026-09-26, at load 110 to 170 with 48 jobs queued, it held it for 7.7 to 12.6 s at a time while other threads waited up to 43 s, because some commit had landed since the early evaluation nearly every time (`docs/reports/2026-09-26-admission-latency.md`).
- **C-6.4** Caps with defaults in `policy.json`. The concurrency caps are `max_active_attempts` (attempts in flight across the fleet, a probe's reservation included), `max_in_flight_per_lane` (detached attempts on one lane), `max_in_flight_unmeasured` (on a lane with no `provider` reading fresher than `reading_ttl_s`, 120; with it unset, `max_in_flight_per_lane` applies there too) and `max_active_attempts_per_parent` (attempts under one ancestor, C-6.9). The submit counts are `max_child_jobs` (children one parent may create in all) and `max_writable_per_session` (live writable jobs one session may hold, C-6.5). Each of these six is null by default, which is no cap, and the shipped policy says so; a positive whole number sets one, and anything else is refused naming the key. Until 2026-09-27 the concurrency caps were 4, 2 and 1, a fixed 1 beneath the unmeasured cap that no policy could raise, and a parent cap of 1 that the policy did not show (Max: "we should uncap everything and instead use prioritization"); until 2026-09-28 the submit counts were 8 and 8 (Max: "remove *all* caps"). `policy.cap` and `policy.lane_slot_cap` are their only readers. What bounds the fleet without them is the providers' own limits and `headroom_floor` (C-11.3, C-4.5), and C-11.3's load band, which spreads work without refusing it; the machine guard (C-6.13) can be turned on. The other bounds are not counts and keep their defaults: `max_wall_s` 21600 seconds of summed provider running time per job (C-4.2), ending at each known exit and excluding waiting and finalization; it stops only a provider that may still be running, and an exited provider keeps its outcome with any overrun recorded; `max_attempts` 3; `max_tokens_observed` optional per job; `workspace_git_timeout_s` 60, `worktree_add_timeout_s` 180, and `workspace_retry_max` 8 (C-6.8).
- **C-6.4a** (2026-10-12, D-RT1) `providers.<provider>.max_active_attempts` is a positive whole number or null; an absent key or section is null (off). The loader refuses booleans, floats, zero, negatives and malformed provider sections naming the exact key. Shipped default policy bytes and the exact-byte hash of an existing policy without this key are unchanged (C-11.1). An attempt on that provider is reserved only when fewer than this count of its attempts across all lanes are `reserved`, `starting`, `running`, or `finalizing`, plus unresolved `quarantined` attempts whose recorded containment has live PIDs or is unverifiable (missing or unreadable evidence is unverified). A quarantined attempt stops counting when a recorded census verifies no processes remain or an operator releases it; resolution changes its attempt state (C-5.7). Each pool observes counted quarantines independently; when one stops counting, its next admission pass rechecks backed-off capacity waits as for a freed lease (C-6.10). The count is checked inside the reserving transaction before taking any lease. Detached attempts and conversation turns both count and are held: the D-RT1 runtime failures were correlated with their combined count. Timer and admission probes are exempt: provider runtime concurrency may exceed this attempt cap by the number of live probes. Status counts their held reservations separately as `provider_caps[].probes`, including uncontained quarantined probes conservatively until their leases are released. Both kinds share `lane:<lane>:slot:0`, so at most one probe reservation per enrolled lane is held (C-5.7a, C-11.4, C-18.1). This keeps recovery probes available when ordinary work fills the cap; D-RT1 operation must account for this extra probe budget. This opt-in provider count is shared across C-26.9's otherwise separate pools; attended turns retain admission priority, and each pool keeps C-6.9's order among peers sharing the provider count, including different models and lanes. Checks proceed in this order: admission class/tier/FIFO traversal and applicable fleet saturation; route evaluation and transactional route revalidation; fleet/lane/family competition using demand narrowed to the selected provider; competition against older peers of that selected provider and its transactional count; then leases and reservation. Existing fleet, lane, pool and family ordering (C-6.9, C-6.16) and the last fleet slot kept for an older waiter remain in force; provider FIFO adds no older peer from a provider the job did not select, even with those other caps configured. A held job takes no lease; `why` and status name `providers.<provider>.max_active_attempts` and say `<provider>: N of N running`, or identify an older peer keeping a slot. Other providers remain eligible.
- **C-6.5** Refusals are exit 7 with the fix named: API-key home, workdir under `/tmp`, writable job that would write in a checkout on `main` or `master` (C-13.2), `-o` path held by a live job of another request id, a symlink loop in the requested `-o` path, a writable job for a session id that already has one running from another instance, a second writable job on the same worktree (a detached in-place job where a writable conversation turn is live, queued or running, or its attempt quarantined and its `worktree-turn:` row still held, is such a second writer; turns among themselves are not, C-24.5), C-8.5 host-push metadata above its config cap or sparse objects and objects above its cumulative intake cap, a writable job beyond `caps.max_writable_per_session` when a policy sets it (none by default, C-6.4), a child beyond `caps.max_child_jobs` when a policy sets it (none by default), an isolated review (`-I`) that would inherit `CLAUDE_CODE_MANAGED_SETTINGS_PATH` or a remote managed-settings variable, and an isolated Codex review whose home carries managed requirements or a non-empty system or project config layer (C-23.3). This list is the index of refusals: a new refusal is added here, not in a clause of its own. What holds a writable job is where it writes. The write target of an in-place job is the real path of the checkout that contains its workdir (`git rev-parse --show-toplevel`), or for a conversation's turn outside git its folder (C-26.10), so `/repo` and `/repo/sub` are one target and two linked worktrees of one repository are two; a target is spelled one way (`folders.canonical`: `~`, symlinks, `.` and `..` resolved, and each name as the kernel stores it, whatever case or Unicode form it was typed in), so on a case-insensitive volume `~/Scratch` and `~/scratch` are one target, as a conversation's workspace is (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-4); the target of any other writable job is the worktree the daemon allocates for it (C-6.6), which no other job shares. Such a job holds that worktree, never the checkout it is cut from, and a read-only job holds no folder: neither waits for an ordinary writer's lease on its source folder, but each waits while retention retires a tree that folder is or is in (C-8.4). Submit records that folder, the workdir spelled as a target is, as `folder` in the `job.submitted` event, so admission reads the fence by string operations alone. One instance of a session may therefore hold any number of writable jobs on distinct targets, with no count unless a policy sets `caps.max_writable_per_session`, a backstop against a runaway caller and not a throttle (C-6.4: no count bounds what runs by default). An instance is a process: the caller's pid with its boot id and start time (C-5.3), read by the daemon at submit and recorded with the target in the `job.submitted` event, so a recycled pid is never mistaken for it and no schema change is needed. A submission is refused when the session has a live writable job whose recorded instance is another process that is still running (the twin), and also whenever the two cannot be told apart: the submission carries no pid, either process cannot be inspected, or the live job recorded no instance. An instance that is provably gone (no such pid, or the pid now belongs to a later process) is not a second instance: a session resumed after a restart or an account switch is one instance whose detached jobs survived, and it may dispatch again. A revive is always another instance of the session it continues (C-23.54), so a revive and any other writable job of that session refuse each other as before. Both questions need `ps` or `git`, so they are answered under the submit lock before the inserting transaction, which re-checks with SQL alone and refuses any live job the submission was not cleared against (C-3.3) (incident: 2026-09-20, a session handing five stalled threads to parallel lanes was refused every writable job after the first with "caller_session is held by a live job", whichever worktree it named; the code compared session ids and never instances, so it refused the one instance this clause never meant to stop). Milestone 9 adds: a socket `submit` of `kind: "turn"` or a `turn:` request id (C-26.1); a person-only operation from an agent (C-25.6); a writable turn whose workspace contains the state root, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, a lane home, or the guard's directory, and a turn on `main` or `master` without `allow_main` (C-26.10). Output paths, including their parents and absent destinations, compare by the shared folder identity at conflict checks, reservation, export and recovery: resolve the kernel spelling, then NFC on a sensitive volume; on an insensitive volume strip Unicode default-ignorables, equate dotless i with i, and use NFD(casefold(NFD(path))). Display spelling stays as supplied and old raw lease aliases still exclude another owner. Known conservative gaps lean toward over-collision: full casefold expansions, dotless i on APFS, default-ignorables on APFS, and canonical normalization on sensitive volumes can refuse distinct files. An OSError, including PermissionError, ELOOP, EIO or TimeoutError, falls back to the exact supplied string and is logged once per path rather than stopping unrelated jobs. This may under-collide aliases while the filesystem is unreachable, matching 2.1.11. An identical stored output path remains refused even if a quarantined owner holds only its canonical lease. External path replacement or directory renaming during a live lease remains an under-collision limit of path-based ownership (audit L4). Identity answers are memoized only for one submit, admission pass or export, outside the main-store transaction, with no stored column or back-fill. For legacy alias lease pairs, the oldest acquired_at wins, breaking ties by holder then lease key; other holders persist export_error: superseded by <job>, append that reason to their notices, and release all their job and accepted-attempt leases at export. Every output export either records an export artifact or export_error, including missing ownership or deliverables, and releases those leases.
- **C-6.6** A writable job runs in a worktree the daemon allocates under `worktrees/<job id>/` from the caller's repo at its current head, unless the caller passes `--in-place`, in which case the daemon takes the worktree lease `worktree:<realpath>` on the checkout that contains the caller's directory (C-6.5; a conversation turn, always in place, holds a row of its own there instead, C-24.5); the provider still starts in the directory the caller named. A Claude job in an allocated worktree starts at the caller's place in it (a job run from `repo/pkg` starts in `<worktree>/pkg`), that place named as git spells it (`git rev-parse --show-prefix`, so a directory given in another case, in decomposed Unicode or through the `/System/Volumes/Data` firmlink is the committed one), and a resume starts where its source attempt's launch started; a Codex job starts at the worktree's top, because Codex's workspace-write sandbox writes only under its working directory; a directory the caller's head does not hold (untracked or ignored) is not in the worktree, so the job starts at its top. A review whose sandbox comes from a `worktree-write` policy entry (C-11.1) gets in its worktree what its caller sees: the caller's uncommitted tracked changes (`git diff --binary <head>`, applied) and untracked, non-ignored files (`git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`, copied) as they were at submit; ignored files are never copied. Submit plans the copy, checks in a scratch index under the state root that the patch applies to `<head>`, and keeps it in `jobs/<job id>/uncommitted/`; the worktree cut applies it before the attempt's baseline is taken, so salvage holds only the job's own changes, and then marks it applied (`jobs/<job id>/uncommitted/applied`, naming the worktree); a worktree found without that marker was cut by a daemon that stopped before the copy was in, and is cut again before any attempt (#221 review r2 P3-1), and its note says the worktree holds the caller's uncommitted changes and how many files. The copy is bounded: at most 1,000 paths (changed tracked plus untracked) and 16 MiB of patch and file bytes together. A plan over either bound, a patch that does not apply, a changed submodule, or an untracked nested repository makes the review read-only in place from submit (git lists only files, symbolic links and nested repositories as untracked, never a FIFO or a socket, which are simply not copied, so a nested repository is the only untracked path that reaches this fallback; #221 review r2 P3-2) (D-SH1 round 6; never lowered later, d261). A kept copy that does not apply at the cut fails that attempt's launch (`uncommitted-apply`). An explicit `-s workspace-write` job is unchanged: its worktree holds only what is committed.
- **C-6.7** `prompt.md` holds the caller's prompt bytes exactly as submitted; it is the digest input and is never rewritten. Prepended text is applied in two places and recorded separately: at submit, the daemon prepends to a workspace-write job that is not in place a note naming its worktree, the caller's checkout it must never write, that an ignored file the task names (one under `.diag/`) is not in the worktree and may be read in the caller's checkout at the same relative path (#221 review r2 P3-9), and the caller's place in the repository (with or without `--no-preamble`), and before that the write template unless `--no-preamble` (the sandbox is known at submit); a review's or gate round's template is the review one, which allows tests and scratch files in its workspace and forbids commits, pushes and publishing (#221 review r1b, P3-8); the manifest records whether the template was given (`preamble`), and a resume keeps its source's choice; at launch, the Claude adapter prepends the headless block unless the prompt already contains the marker line `<!-- subfleet:headless -->` (the provider is known only after routing). The adapter writes the text actually sent to `<attempt dir>/prompt.sent.md`, the launch's `stdin_path` points at it, and it is recorded as an artifact with role `prompt-sent`.
- **C-6.8** Workspace preparation (the main or master re-check, worktree allocation, reading HEAD, and the baseline snapshot) runs before every admission, so a waiting job repeats it on each pass and one slow pass must not end the job. C-8.5 standalone push checkout construction runs on the push thread instead; admission checks its readiness without waiting. Its checkout/Git refusals, including its fixed 60-second Git timeout, use C-8.5's exit 7; host OS errors use the workspace handling and backoff below. Other workspace Git calls are capped by `caps.workspace_git_timeout_s` (60; `git worktree add` by `caps.worktree_add_timeout_s`, 180); finalization's salvage uses the same cap, and a caller with no policy in hand reads `SUBFLEET_GIT_TIMEOUT_S`. A git call that did not finish is never read as an answer: a timed-out `rev-parse` is not "no HEAD" and a timed-out `symbolic-ref` is not "not on main", and neither is a git killed by a signal (a negative return code: a memory-pressure kill, or a signal sent to the process group), which, as a timeout, is transient. At submit it is exit 1 with nothing stored; at launch nothing is launched, and the attempt ends with the cause as its detail: one that is transient ends as an attempt whose guardian could not be started does (`failed`, class `unknown`, never a spawn error), so the job is queued again while it has attempts left, waiting first under the backoff below (`wait_reason` `workspace` and a `job.workspace_deferred` event with `stage` `launch`), 5 s doubling per consecutive attempt that ended so, counted from the store, so an admission pass that succeeds between two of them does not reset it, and its next admission asks again (review of ceacf18b: as a spawn error it was classified `unknown`, which is never retried, and a killed git ended the job; review of that fix: queued at once, a check that kept getting no answer at launch while admission's got one used up the job's attempts in as many passes); a conversation turn has one attempt, so such a turn fails; at admission it is a failure classified here. Transient failures are a timeout, an `OSError` whose errno is EAGAIN, EINTR, ENOMEM, EMFILE, ENFILE, EBUSY, ETIMEDOUT, ENOBUFS, or ENOSPC, and git ending one of its `error:` or `fatal:` lines with a lock file another git process holds (`Unable to create '….lock': File exists.`) or a full disk (`: No space left on device`, or `write error. Out of diskspace` for a write that stored nothing), its `strerror` text for those transient errnos, or its errno-less index/ref write, allocation, or reftable lock and transaction I/O failures, whatever their cause (an I/O error or a permission problem writing a lock file reads as a full disk would), read under the C locale (C-13.1); a file name git prints in such a line could end it with one of these texts, and is then read as one: bounded, as every transient failure is: the job moves to `waiting` with `wait_reason` `workspace` and a `next_check_at` 5 s out, doubling per consecutive failure to a 300 s ceiling, holds no lease, blocks no other job, and returns to `queued` on the first pass that succeeds, which also resets the count. After `caps.workspace_retry_max` (8) consecutive transient failures, or at once for any other failure (git exiting non-zero, a permission error), the job is `failed` with rc 1. Nothing is discarded: each deferral is a `job.workspace_deferred` event and the terminal failure a `job.workspace_failed` event, both carrying `error_type` (the underlying exception, so `TimeoutExpired` rather than its wrapper) and `error` (git's verb, the cap, and its stderr, or the OS error; a path and an errno, never a credential); the notice text ends with the same two fields; `show` returns the latest record as `workspace`; and `daemon.log` gets one line per deferral and per failure, naming the job. The consecutive count is held in memory, so a daemon restart forgives it while the events keep the history. A `worktrees/<job id>/` directory that is not a worktree (a `git worktree add` killed at its cap) is removed and rebuilt before the job is admitted; no attempt has run in it. The baseline snapshot seeds its temporary index from a copy of the real index (mtime kept) and reads the baseline with `read-tree -m`, so its entries are the baseline's while the stat data of every unchanged path carries over and `add -A` hashes only changed files; the copy's assume-unchanged and skip-worktree bits, which `read-tree -m` keeps, are cleared across the whole index before `add -A`, since `add -A` skips such paths and the empty-index read has none, so both reads give the same tree; bytes and mtime come from one open file; with no usable real index (none yet, unmerged entries, a copy git cannot read or clear, or any other failure of a seeding step, a git killed by a signal included) it reads the baseline into an empty index in a fresh temporary directory (review of ceacf18b: git 2.55 is killed by SIGSEGV reading an index whose entries are garbage, a killed git is transient, and so every try of the snapshot failed the same way, the fallback also tripping on the crashed git's `index.lock`). A seeding step stopped at its cap did not finish rather than fail, and the snapshot fails with it, transient, as any git call past its cap does: under the load that stops the seeded read the unseeded one, which hashes every tracked file, is slower still (the incident below), so falling back would spend a second cap on each try before the same failure (review of the P3-1 fix). Trusting stat data is git's own model: a rewrite that keeps size, mtime and inode is read as unchanged (incident: 2026-09-23, axiom-corpus, 59,822 tracked files and 14 GB: the empty-index snapshot took 39 to 51 s at a load average near 6 and timed out at the 60 s cap on all eight tries, so no writable job could be admitted there; seeded, the same tree took 0.5 s). A refusal raised during preparation (C-6.5) is still exit 7 with its fix and never waits (incident: 2026-09-20, under a load average near 10 a writable job went from queued to `failed` in 19 s with "workspace preparation failed", no attempt, and nothing in `daemon.log`; the same snapshot took 0.6 s minutes later and an identical resubmission ran; twelve jobs ended that way between 2026-09-19 and 2026-09-20, read-only ones after as long as 70 minutes in the queue, because the caps were 15 s and 5 s, the exception was discarded, and every failure was terminal). A folder that a turn's row or an in-place writer's lease would name and that is not there when admission spells it again (`folders.present`) is such a failure, transient, and takes no row, unless retention's fence on a tree it is in holds it (C-8.4).
- **C-6.9** Queued jobs are considered in priority order: class, then tier in the policy's declared order, then oldest first (plan amendment 11), except `priority` jobs use FIFO across tiers (C-6.16). The classes, first to last (2026-09-27, Max: "uncap everything and instead use prioritization"): `attended`, a conversation turn from the Subfleet app (it has a pass of its own, which runs first, C-26.9); `priority`, a detached job whose caller or ancestor caller the operator selected (C-6.16); `session`, a detached job someone is waiting on now: a gate round, or a job whose caller's Claude Code session is live (a `~/.claude/sessions` row names its `caller_session`, in either case, and the row's pid is still the process that wrote it: `registry.validated` compares the row's `procStart` with one process-table read) or whose parent job is unfinished; a caller's pid alone (`caller_pid`) is not evidence, since a live pid proves a process, not the caller; and `background`, every other job (a caller that has exited, a launchd or cron submission, a batch whose dispatcher is gone). A batch a live session submits is `session`. Liveness is read once per pass, off the store lock but for one indexed query (`Daemon._liveness`), and `scheduler.priority_class` and `scheduler.ordered_jobs` are pure functions of it; with no liveness to read every detached job other than `priority` is `session`. The hold-back below applies only while the job's pool has a count cap (`scheduler.hold_scope`: for a detached job `max_active_attempts`, `max_in_flight_per_lane` or `max_in_flight_unmeasured`, and then behind any older competing job; for a turn either turn cap, likewise; for a job with a parent whose only count is `max_active_attempts_per_parent`, only behind an older competing job that shares an ancestor with it, since that count is shared only within a family). In an uncapped pool, the default, no job waits behind another: a later job never takes a slot an older one needs, because there are no slots to take, and a job no lane admits waits on its own clock (C-6.10) without holding anyone. Two scarce things keep their order with no cap at all. A lease: C-26.9's queue, which asks no job's kind (submit refuses a second writable job in one checkout and a second job on one output path, C-6.5, so detached contention is rare); a job is never queued for, nor held behind, nor kept from the last slot for (capped or not), a job waiting for a lease the first job holds itself: a retry keeps its job-held leases, and the waiter moves only once the retry has run (review of PR #72: two read-only resumes of one native session, the retrying one `background` and the waiter `session`, each waited for the other until `max_wall_s`). And a lane's probe lease: a detached attempt takes the lowest free `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` from n = 1, so `slot:0` is the admission probe's alone (C-11.4) and a probe waits for no attempt (review of the uncap plan: with no per-lane cap a busy lane nearly always had an attempt on `slot:0`, and an older writable job waiting to probe it lost the slot to each later job that needed no probe). And a probe's turn (2026-10-08): an admission probe (C-11.4) says nothing about the job that carries it, since its prompt is fixed and it runs on the lane's credential in a private directory, so the jobs of a detached pass that wait on a probe form a line in the pass's order (`Daemon._probe_line`), and a job carries the probe of a model on a lane only when no job ahead of it in the line waits on a probe of that model and could use that lane (`scheduler.probe_turn`): a lane of the model's provider, its pin's if it has one, and none that its own exclusions or a transient retry's name (`scheduler.probe_reach`; review of #153: a job that excluded a lane held that lane's turn from a job pinned to it). Otherwise it goes to a lane of the same model whose probe is its own (C-11.4's choice), or, with none, it is held `probe-pending` with `behind` naming the job whose turn it is, on a capacity clock (C-6.10), and is looked at on the first pass that finds nobody ahead of it waiting on that probe. A job is in the line while its last look left it waiting on a probe, its clock running or not, and not while it waits on an operator (`uncertain`); each detached pass builds the line again, so a job that started, ended or now waits for anything else holds nobody. Like a lease's queue this holds with no cap, and it holds only jobs that want a probe of the same model on a lane both could use: never a job that needs no probe. So among jobs that want the same model on the same lanes, starts follow the pass's order whatever the probes answer, and C-11.4's rotation bounds how long a job waits behind another for a lane it could use (incident: 2026-10-03, a `hard` review waited 64 minutes and never started while hard reviews submitted after it probed the lanes its own probes had run out of time on, and started; `docs/reports/2026-10-08-probe-vehicle-starvation.md`). A job that cannot be placed on this pass for want of a slot or a lane (it is `waiting` on `capacity` with a `next_check_at` still ahead and its last look found no slot or lane, its probe is pending, or no lane is admissible) holds back the later jobs of its tier **that compete with it**, and no others. It does so whether or not it is itself held behind an older job on this pass (stateful admission properties, finding 5, 2026-10-12: such a job held nobody, so a younger job that competed with it and not with the older one was placed past it); held there it gets no look, so nothing starts it a new clock, and once that clock is due it holds nobody until a look finds it waiting again. A job that starts in this pass is no longer an older waiter: its running attempt counts against the applicable caps, but it holds no later job behind it as a waiter. So a hold that runs through a job that is itself held lasts no longer than the clock that job's own last look set (a transient retry whose pin was let go is looked at when due, and that look may set its next clock, C-6.12). A job waiting for a lease holds back none, capped or not: one held `lease-held` (a key another holder has, among them retention's fence, C-8.4, and a detached writer's checkout, C-24.5, or a free key kept for an older job, C-26.9), on this pass or on its clock since. It waits for no slot a later job could take: its lease keeps its order through C-26.9's queue, and in a pool with a fleet count a job that passes it keeps the last slot for it, as for any older waiting job (below), so it starts the moment its lease is let go. In a pool whose only count is per lane (`max_in_flight_per_lane`, `max_in_flight_unmeasured`, `turn_slots_per_lane`) there is no one slot to keep: such a job that finds no slot once its lease is let go waits for one, and from then holds back the later jobs that compete with it (review of fenced-turn-precheck, P3-4, 2026-10-06, measured on 0812d5c5: with `max_active_turns` 5, an older turn waiting on retention's fence, on a detached writer's checkout, on its conversation or on its native session held a later turn of its model `behind-older-job` on each of four looks with 0 or 1 of 5 turn slots live, and with `max_active_attempts` 5 a job waiting for an output path held a later detached job the same way; the hold lasted as long as the lease, a whole retirement or a whole turn). A job no lane can admit (C-11.8) holds back none: one whose lane pin names a lane that refuses it for a standing reason, or one that every lane of every model of its chain refuses for one. It waits for no slot another job could take, so no later job is held behind it, kept out of a slot for it, or queued behind it for a lease. With a live capacity steer, standing refusal is judged on the whole original chain, including the steered models, both while its clock runs and at a due look or after exhausted route retries; a steer that ends is not a standing refusal (C-9.5). Admission asks this on every pass, not only at a look: a pin before the job is compared with any other, a job without one as it is put among the waiters or keeps a lease's place (`scheduler.unadmittable` on its clock, in a pool with a hold-back or for a job holding a lease's place; `scheduler.refused_for_good` of a decision with no lane). So a job put on its clock by a path that is not a look (a transient retry's 60 s) or whose lanes turned since its last look holds nobody while no lane can take it (incident: 2026-09-29 ~21:53Z, a job pinned to claude-9 when an account switch made claude-9 the desktop login held 38 younger standard Opus jobs `held behind` it while claude-7 was open). Two jobs compete when some model could serve both: a pinned job's models are its one pin; a task job's are its chain from its tier upward, exactly the chain routing walks (C-11.2), excluding models whose capacity steers are live for that job (C-9.5). Such a model holds back no later job for that model; its demand and FIFO place return at steer expiry. If a later job could use a model steered away from the waiter, their model competition is checked against its selected model before reservation: it may pass on the skipped model, and still waits behind the older job if it selects a model that waiter continues to demand; a job whose models cannot be told (a lane pin with no model) competes with every job. Lane pins narrow it further: two jobs pinned to different lanes never compete, whatever their models, because neither can take a slot the other waits for; a job with no lane pin, or a pin that cannot be resolved, could use any lane and so competes on models alone (incident: 2026-09-22, three Fable gate reviews pinned to three lanes; the oldest waited on its own lane's provider limit until 19:00Z and held the other two, whose lanes were open, for six hours). So an older Opus review keeps its place against later Opus work, including work that could promote into its chain, and a Fable-pinned job does not queue behind it. With `max_active_attempts` set (for turns, `conversations.max_active_turns`), a job that passes an older waiting job of its tier, one waiting for a lease included, leaves one active-attempt slot free (`max_active_attempts` minus one), so the older job starts the moment its capacity or its lease appears rather than waiting out the jobs that passed it; with nothing older waiting, the full cap applies; and at `max_active_attempts` the pass ends: nothing later is evaluated. In a capped pool the order above still decides who is older: a waiting `session` job holds back the later `background` jobs of its tier that compete with it. Tiers never hold each other, as before (incident: 2026-09-20, an Opus review that no lane would admit, every Claude lane `reserve:fable:unmeasured` and Astra closed until the next day, headed the `standard` tier; admission held the whole tier behind it, so three Fable-pinned jobs, tier-less and therefore `standard`, stayed queued beside eleven free Fable lanes and nothing at all was admitted for more than three hours).
- **C-6.10** A capacity wait is rechecked on a clock that backs off, and a recheck that reaches the verdict it reached last time adds no decision row. The first recheck is 1 s out (plan amendment 11); each consecutive recheck with the same verdict doubles it, to a 30 s ceiling; a reset the decision knows about (a closure's `until_at`, a reading's `resets_at`) that falls sooner is still checked on time. The verdict is the chain walked, the lane and model chosen, the policy hash, and each rejected lane with its reasons (`scheduler.verdict_signature`); readings, utilization, and `evaluated_at` are evidence and are left out, and so is `no-slot` on a lane that is rejected for another reason as well: a lane that is closed, reserved, or excluded is no nearer to taking the job when its slot empties, slots fill and empty all day, and a probe's reservation counts toward the fleet cap, so each probe cycle (C-18.1) marks every lane `no-slot` for the second it runs. A lane whose only reason is `no-slot` keeps it: that job is waiting for a slot, and that is its verdict. A changed verdict is recorded and restarts the clock at 1 s. The same doubling applies to three waits that are not on a closure or a reading, so the known-reset rule has nothing to say about them: a job waiting on a lease another job holds, which was a fixed 1 s; a job whose route could not be prepared with no clock set (its probe's slot is held, or the lane changed hands); and a job whose chosen lane and model changed after its probe. The last two had no clock at all and were prepared again, with a new probe directory, on every tick; a probe directory made for a reservation that then does not happen is removed, since no record names it and recovery would never collect it. Backing off is not meant to cost a start: each pass compares the leases no probe holds (the turn pass also those admission probes hold, C-26.9) with those it saw on the pass before, counting the ones it took itself and retention's fences it found a job waiting for after it read them (C-8.4), and when one has gone (an attempt ended, a job released its worktree or its output path) every backed-off capacity wait other than a probe's own is looked at on that pass. With a provider cap configured (C-6.4a), each pool also compares its counted quarantines with those it saw before; a recorded verified-empty census or a resolved attempt frees capacity even when quarantine already released all its leases. Capacity that appears any other way (a fresh reading, a closure an operator lifts, a lane enrolled) is seen at the next recheck, so the most this clause can add to a start is 30 s.
