Only a capacity wait is brought forward. A capacity wait held `behind-older-job` is also looked at immediately when its named predecessor no longer holds back later jobs in this pass: it started, ended, became unadmittable, or otherwise holds nobody back (including a lease wait). The fresh look still respects every current cap and older waiter. A job whose wait became `workspace` (C-6.8) loses its record and keeps that clock, because a look brought forward there spends one of `workspace_retry_max` retries, and eight unrelated releases would end the job. The wait after an inconclusive probe keeps its clock too, because bringing it forward would re-probe the provider on every release. That clock is `PROBE_RETRY_S` (60 s) from the probe's reservation, not from its end, so a probe its deadline stopped leaves its job due at once, and the job, which keeps its turn (C-6.9), is looked at first on the next pass; one that failed in a second does not make a probe a second (until 2026-10-08 it was 60 s from the end, and since probes run inside the detached pass the job's next look came a whole pass later: 10 to 21 minutes on 2026-10-03). It gets the other half of the rule, and a probe that ends the same way adds no second decision row; its `job.probe_waiting` event names the lane, the model, the probe's class and the clock. The record (verdict, consecutive rechecks, first and last look, and the latest hold in full) is held in memory per job, replaced whole on every look so that another thread never reads half of one, and dropped when the job is placed or ends. A restart therefore forgets it, as it forgets C-6.8's count: recovery makes ordinary capacity waits due once, but preserves the future `next_check_at` of a job whose latest attempt was `transient` (C-4.5's retry backoff, including legacy rows), since what each ordinary wait was waiting for may have changed while no daemon ran and nothing else could bring a persisted wait forward, at the cost of one decision row per waiting job; `why` reports the record (C-6.11). C-11.5 is unchanged: the decision is still stored per attempt, and a waiting job still has the latest verdict it reached (incident: 2026-09-20, three jobs no lane would admit were each re-evaluated once a second for hours; every look prepared the workspace with git, scored every lane, and wrote a 22 KB decision row whose only new content was fresher readings: 1,628 rows in ten minutes with no attempt reserved, 29,923 rows and 681 MB of a 709 MB store, the file growing about 170 MB an hour, and the daemon at a full core; the decision said `earliest reset: 2026-09-20T22:20:00Z`, more than an hour off, on every one of them; by the next morning the store was 2.3 GB, 2.2 GB of it 98,014 decision rows. Replayed over the 80,996 rows of the six worst jobs, the verdict changed 74 times).
- **C-6.11** A job that is not placed always has a stated reason, and a fleet that places nothing says so. Each admission pass records, per job it leaves unplaced, why: `behind-older-job` with the older job's id and the tier (C-6.9), and `for_good` (below) when its capacity clock runs and every lane refuses it for a standing reason (C-11.8); `fleet-full` with `max_active_attempts`; `slot-kept` with `kept_for`, `live`, and `max_active_attempts`, when a lane would take the job and the fleet is one short of its cap because C-6.9 keeps the last slot for an older waiting job of the tier (it is not reported as a full fleet, which an operator can see it is not); `parent-cap` (these four, and `behind-older-job`, only in a pool with a count cap, C-6.9; in an uncapped pool `no-slot` means a probe, a latched credential or, with other lanes held otherwise, a pilot holds the lane); `lane-proving`, when every lane that could take a detached job has room only but for its pilot (C-6.14), each rejection's `slot_block` naming the pilot (`proving:<attempt id>`); `disk` with `free_gb`, `reserved_gb`, `floor_gb`, `resume_margin_gb` and `placement_reserve_gb` (C-6.17); `ramp` with `used`, `limit`, `window_s` and `next_slot_at` (C-6.18); `machine-busy` with `class` and the reading that held it (`cpu_idle` and `cpu_idle_min`, `disk_busy` and `disk_busy_max`, `load_per_cpu` and `load_threshold`, `memory_pressure` and `memory_threshold`, plus CPU/disk release progress, C-6.13); `lease-held` with the lease keys another job holds (output keys use C-6.5's canonical comparison identity even for a legacy raw guard; the job's `out_path` keeps its display spelling), among them a key the job needs free but does not take (a turn's `worktree:<target>`, the `worktree-turn:` rows of the turns in a detached writer's folder, C-24.5, and retention's fence on a detached job's source folder or on a folder above any job's, C-8.4), which is never queued for, on a look on the job's clock as at a full one; a hold with a retention fence carries `retiring`, those keys, and the checked `folder`, and `why` describes retention removing that finished job's tree (C-8.4) (and, for a turn, `queued`: free keys kept for an older turn, C-26.9); `probe-pending` with the `lane` and `model` of the probe, and `behind` when that probe is an older job's turn (C-6.9); `route-moved` with `tries`, when the job's route could not be settled in C-6.3's `ROUTE_TRIES` reservations in a row (a commit before each check moved the lane its pin names, or removed a reading the decision rested on) and it keeps its place for the next pass; `attempt-live` (an earlier attempt of the job is still live or quarantined, found before the job is looked at or again inside its reserving transaction); `conversation-blocked` with `conversation_id`, `blocked_by` and `legacy_hold` (a turn whose conversation is blocked, C-24.5), or with `error_type` and `error` when its turn manifest is missing, cannot be read, or does not name its conversation, message and provider, or its conversation could not be checked for a reason other than a store error, which holds that job alone (a store error is the pass's, C-6.12); `message-settled` with `conversation_id` and `state` (its message was withdrawn after the job was made; the job is cancelled, C-24.7); `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace` (C-4.1); `route` with `error_type`, `error`, and `deferrals` (C-6.12), which is reported as that whatever else holds the job while its clock runs; `pin-unadmittable` with `lane_id`, `reasons`, `closures`, `since` and `fail_at` (C-11.8: its pinned lane can never admit it), which is reported as that whatever else would hold the job, except a wait on `approval` or `uncertain`; `task-held` with `task`, `until` and `note` (C-11.10: the operator holds the job's task), reported as that whatever else would hold the job, except a wait on `approval` or `uncertain`, and, like a wait on a person, counted toward no idle stretch and never a warning; otherwise the commonest first reason lanes rejected it for (`reserve:fable:unmeasured`, `closed:account`, `no-slot`, ...), with the `next_check_at` of any wait that has one, and `for_good` with the standing reasons when every lane refuses it for one (C-11.8), in which case `why` adds that it holds no other job back; a pass that asks it again on the job's clock replaces the last look's `for_good` with its own answer. A wait that is a person's or a retry's to end (`approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`) is reported as that even when the fleet is also full or an older job is also ahead of it. `hold` is the last pass's answer and `recheck` describes the last look (`recheck.label` is what that look found): the reason is the latest look's, not the first's, whether or not the verdict repeated; a look that ends without changing a verdict or a clock still refreshes it; and a pass that does not look at the job repeats the last hold in full (`leases`, `max_active_attempts`), so `why` never degrades between looks. A `behind-older-job` hold whose named predecessor no longer holds back later jobs is instead rechecked on this pass (C-6.10), so a stored hold never keeps a job behind a predecessor that has started or ended or now holds nobody back. A pass that raises publishes nothing: the holds and the `admission` snapshot stay as the last whole pass left them, because half a hold set would read as nothing left pending, and C-5.10 logs and paces the failure. When every lane has a standing reason the label is the commonest of those and not `fleet-full`: a probe's reservation counts toward the fleet cap, so such a job would otherwise read `fleet-full` for the second each probe runs. The record is in memory and replaced whole each pass; C-26.9's turn pass and the detached pass each replace their own jobs' part, and `hold` is read from the two together. `subfleet why <job>` returns it as `job` (`state`, `tier`, `wait_reason`, `next_check_at`, `hold`, `recheck`, `decision_source`, `decided_at`) and as `queue`, the same in sentences, ahead of the decision; for a turn it also returns `lanes`, its lane ids by reason, the first reset among the closed ones and the capacity blocks (`conversations.waits.lane_summary`), from which its message says why it waits (C-24.4). A queued or waiting job with no decision row (admission held it back, or no pass has reached it) is evaluated for the answer and marked `decision_source: evaluated-now`; no decision is recorded and no job changes, because answering is not admission. The evaluation asks who the desktop login is, as every evaluation does (C-10.3), and a newly verified identity is recorded as the `desktop.identity` event it would have been on the next pass. A job that ended without a decision prints its state and `No decision recorded.`, unless admission refused it (C-6.12), which prints `Refused at admission: <message>` in its place The CLI never prints `null`, also against a daemon that predates `queue`. `daemon.status` carries `admission`: `pending` (unplaced jobs other than those held as `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`, `attempt-live`, `conversation-blocked`, `message-settled` or `task-held`, which are a person's, a retry's or the operator's to end), `placed_at`, `idle_for_s` and `idle_since` (null unless jobs are pending and none has been placed), `reasons` (count per reason), and `open_lanes`; it is one snapshot, replaced whole at the end of a pass, so a status request never reads half of an update. `open_lanes` is the lanes owned by v2, enabled, not the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (C-10.3), identity not mismatched, under no account-wide closure, with a slot free (always, while no per-lane cap is set) and no probe or pilot (C-6.14) holding it. Open means capacity exists, not that it fits: a job can still be refused an open lane by a model-scoped closure, the reserve, or its own exclusions, and the reasons say which. `daemon.status` also carries `machine_guard`: its enabled state and, when enabled, C-6.13's values, units, ages, readability and last read cost; `subfleet status --json` preserves that object. `subfleet status` prints one `admission:` line while `idle_for_s` is set, and its `running jobs:` count and table cover live jobs only. When jobs have been pending with none placed for 60 s, `daemon.log` gets one line (count, seconds idle, open lanes by id, reasons by count, and the first job in admission order), and one line when placing resumes or nothing is left pending. The line is a warning when a lane is open and some job is held for a reason other than ordinary queueing (`fleet-full`, `slot-kept`, `parent-cap`, `no-slot`, `lane-proving`, `lease-held`, `probe-pending`, `behind-older-job`, `route-moved`, `machine-busy`, `ramp`); otherwise it is information, because with no lane open or a fleet at its cap waiting is the expected answer. The fleet is looked at again every 600 s while the stretch lasts: a warning is written each time, information at most every 3600 s, so a wait that was expected when it was first logged becomes a warning within ten minutes of becoming one (incident: 2026-09-20, fifteen jobs from six sessions sat queued for more than three hours beside open lanes; `subfleet why` printed `null` for every one of them, because a job admission skips gets no decision row and the CLI printed the missing value instead of the daemon's text; `subfleet status` said `running jobs: 519`, the size of the store, because `daemon.status` carries every job as `jobs`; and `daemon.log` had not gained a line since the daemon started).
- **C-6.12** A job whose route cannot be evaluated is that job's problem and never the pass's. Evaluating one job (`scheduler.evaluate`, and `scheduler.probe_required`, which checks an unmeasured-reserve authorization) is isolated from the rest of the pass, both where the route is prepared and inside the reserving transaction, which rolls back before the job is settled; a `ValueError` (a `PolicyError` is one), `KeyError`, `TypeError`, `AttributeError`, or `IndexError` from it settles that job and the pass goes on to the next, in its tier and every other. What it settles to depends on whose the error is. A `scheduler.RouteError` is the job's own: its pin names several lanes, its pin and its model are of different providers, or its authorization is not whole. (A pin that names one lane which can never admit the job is not a route error: C-11.8 holds it and, after a grace, fails it with rc 3.) No wait can fix that and only the caller can say what was meant, so the job is `failed` with rc 2, what submit would have answered, its `wait_reason` and `next_check_at` cleared (as for any job `_fail_queued` ends); its notice and a `job.route_refused` event carry the message (`error_type`, `error`), `daemon.log` gets one line naming the job and the message, `why` prints `Refused at admission: <message>` ahead of any decision recorded before it (`refused` in its result), and a worktree admission cut for it under `worktrees/<job id>/` that no attempt used is removed, since `jobs.worktree` is set only at reservation and retention would never collect it. Two of those errors can also come from a policy edit rather than the job: a provider conflict (a tier's chain moved to another provider under a job already pinned to a lane) and an authorization's model check. Those are refused only under the policy the job was accepted with (`jobs.policy_hash` equal to the running policy's); under any other they are treated as below. Anything else is not the job's: bad capacity data, a policy that no longer knows the job's task, tier, or model (submit checked each against the policy it ran under, so a policy edit is the operator's to fix), or a defect. That is never terminal, because a restart can bring the fix and failing it could fail every queued job at once: the job waits with `wait_reason` `route`, 5 s out doubling per consecutive failure to a 300 s ceiling, each deferral a `job.route_deferred` event carrying `error_type`, `error`, `deferrals`, and `next_check_at`, and a `daemon.log` line naming the job and the error's type only (C-5.10: the message is in the event and in `why`). A route wait is not a capacity wait: it holds back no other job (C-6.9) and no released lease brings it forward (C-6.10). While its clock runs its hold is `route` with the error (C-6.11), whatever else would hold the job; once the clock is due the job goes through the pass like any other, and a pass that evaluates its route resets the count. `why` names the error, from the hold and, when `why`'s own evaluation raises, in place of the decision (`route_error` in its result); `daemon.status` counts the hold under `admission.reasons`. The count is in memory; recovery makes every route wait due, as it does capacity waits. Route holds are not ordinary queueing, so beside an open lane the idle line (C-6.11) is a warning. Merging a job's exclusions with the lanes its attempts excluded (C-4.5) is part of evaluating its route and is checked where the route is prepared: when its attempts have excluded a lane, a stored exclusion that is not a name (on a row older than C-6.1's check) fails that merge, and the job waits on `route` with its deferrals counting. A store error is not isolated: that is the pass's, and C-5.10 retries it. An accepted job is always evaluable as far as submit can tell: an empty task or tier is stored as none. A transient retry (C-4.5, C-9.5) is the daemon's choice of pair, not the job's, and is pinned to the last attempt's lane and model id only while that pair could run: the running policy resolves the id (a current id or a `retired` alias) to a model of the lane's provider, and the lane (or its re-enrolled successor) is enabled, v2-owned, and not identity-blocked. While it is pinned, its C-6.9 demand is that one model on that one lane; once a look has let the pin go, the job's demand is its own on that pass and while its clock runs, so it neither passes an older job it competes with nor is passed by a younger one (held behind one after a look, it waits on a C-6.10 capacity clock like any other held look, so the passes before that clock prepare no workspace and score no lane), and the next due look evaluates the pair again (a lane that recovered takes its one retry; a restart, which forgets the look, changes nothing). When the job is due, the pair is evaluated as admission would evaluate it. If the lane refuses it only for want of a slot (in flight, the fleet or a parent at its cap, a probe holding it), the retry waits for that lane. If the lane refuses it for anything a slot will not end (a closure, the desktop login, the job's own exclusions, a latched credential, the floor, the reserve), or the pair cannot be evaluated, the job routes as submitted, which is C-4.5's "then next candidate". Before this, a pinned retry waited on its lane until `max_wall_s` cancelled the job (incident: 2026-09-22, five jobs pinned by email, `-a max@axiom.org` and `-a max@thesisinstitute.org`, with a task and no model, named a Claude lane and a Codex lane at once in the capacity view (C-11.2); `resolve_lane` raised inside `_admit_pass`, which caught nothing per job, so every pass aborted at the first of them: `worker admission failed: ValueError (128 in a row)`, no attempt reserved between 14:45:05Z and 18:02:45Z (10:45 to 14:03 EDT), and every later job in every tier waited; `why` caught the same error and printed `No decision recorded.` for those five, so nothing pointed at the stalled pass. Admission resumed within a minute of the five pins being rewritten to `claude-11` and `claude-9` by hand. The same resolution had stalled admission once already that day: a gate review pinned `-a max@axiom.org -m fable` held every pass from its submission at 13:24:34Z until it was cancelled at 13:48:53Z, no attempt was reserved from 13:24:08Z to 13:50:32Z, and `daemon.log` shows the count reaching sixteen twice only because the lane-pins cutover restarted the daemon at 13:35:39Z).
- **C-6.13** (the machine guard, 2026-09-27; D-MG2 revised 2026-10-23; off by default, Max's to turn on) Admission can hold detached `background` and `session` jobs at the door while this machine is saturated, with or without a count cap (C-6.4). It never holds an `attended` turn or a `priority` detached job (C-6.16); the validator rejects both classes. `admission.machine_guard` is null by default, which holds nothing (2026-09-28, Max: "remove *all* caps", "nothing should be queued"), and a class it does not name is never held. Each named class may set any of `cpu_idle_min` (a finite fraction in [0, 1]), `disk_busy_max` (positive finite **milliseconds per operation**, no cap or validator ceiling), `load_per_cpu` (positive finite) and `memory_pressure` (`warn` or `critical`); invalid values, booleans, unknown keys, oversized integers that cannot be represented finitely, and non-finite floats are refused naming the key. Conditions combine by OR. CPU idle below its minimum holds: Mach `host_processor_info(PROCESSOR_CPU_LOAD_INFO)` supplies per-CPU unsigned tick deltas, weighted by total ticks. Disk service at or above its maximum holds: IOKit `IOBlockStorageDriver` read/write service nanoseconds divided by read/write operation deltas, converted to ms/op, on the physical device reached through the state root volume's IOService ancestry. It never substitutes the busiest unrelated device. Uncapped service seconds per wall second, the average requests in flight, are recorded alongside it for calibration. Diagnostic enumeration reads at most **1,024 devices**, including device 1,024; later entries are ignored. An unreadable ID or statistics skips that device, preserving its readable neighbors. Both signals use bias-corrected elapsed-time EWMAs with **60 s time constants**, `alpha = 1-exp(-elapsed/60)`; numerator and accumulated weight start at zero and the value is their ratio. Native counters run **at most once per second** and intervening passes reuse the last snapshot. A daemon with new-signal policy takes a priming read and allows a full one-second interval before offering its first detached pass. Null and load/memory-only policies run no new native reads and preserve every legacy decision and hold record against `473fe2941`. The attended-turn pass reads no machine source. Each source catches `Exception`, never `BaseException`. An unreadable read or zero-tick interval holds nothing and cannot release a latch: it preserves the previous valid counter baseline and EWMA, and interrupts that signal's recovery timer. Reset counters or changed CPU/device topology require a new counter baseline. On each actual disk sample (at most once per second), a cheap state-root `stat` checks `st_dev`. A changed root-device identity, unresolved driver, or failed selected-device counter read re-resolves the IOService ancestry (at most 1,024 ancestors); unchanged readable devices read the retained resolved-driver handle with no registry-ID lookup or device enumeration. A selected handle detached from the IOService plane before or during a statistics read is unreadable and released; the next selected read resolves by registry ID unless ancestry resolution has already supplied a replacement handle. The handle's registry ID must match the selected device before its counters are accepted. A root-device or resolved-driver identity change, including an observed reused ID, discards the old disk counter baseline and disk EWMA. While the root or driver is unresolved, disk service and requests in flight are unreadable. The first successful counter sample on the new device is baseline-only; the second successful sample, at least one sampling interval later and with positive operation deltas, restores raw service, smoothed service and requests in flight without recreating the sampler. Missing or zero-operation samples extend that wait until a positive delta is available. These unreadable samples hold nothing and release no latch; CPU and legacy signals remain independent. A transient failed read that resolves to the same identity preserves its valid counters and EWMA. A CPU latch releases only after readable idle stays at least **0.03 above** its minimum (clamped to 1), and a disk latch only after service stays **10% below** its maximum, for **ten seconds of sampled recovery**, rather than a count of passes. A marginal or unreadable sample interrupts recovery. Another job, repeated snapshot or `why` cannot advance time. Threshold edits clear latches. The proposed setting (`policy.MACHINE_GUARD_PROPOSAL`, an explicit policy edit under d1377) is background idle minimum **0.15**, memory **warn**; session idle minimum **0.05**, memory **critical**; **no load or disk threshold**. A disk threshold waits for calibration. The old capped concurrency term would have held the 10/11 shape by disk; its new service latency is unknown because that incident did not record operation service counters. At 32% idle its CPU signal starts no hold. The 10/20 builder's measured 0% idle holds both classes; the earlier 1,358% whole-host CPU sum would imply about 25% idle on 18 CPUs and alone holds neither. The quiet disk baseline is the tenth percentile of at most 1,440 minute samples from the preceding 24 hours on this device, restored from the store after restart; it is provisional calibration evidence, never an automatic threshold. `daemon.status` and `subfleet status --json` carry `machine_guard`: CPU idle, disk ms/op, requests in flight, quiet baseline and last `read_cost_s`, each with units, age since its last successful observation and current readability. These status reads do not sample or advance recovery. At most one `machine.sample` per minute is recorded, with the last 1,440 samples and their 1,440 audit rows retained; the minute gate survives restart using persisted recording time, independently of snapshot observation time. The baseline accepts only positive finite raw service observations from the resolved device within the preceding 24 hours, and its age is the age of the newest observation satisfying that same predicate, with value and age evaluated at one timestamp and cutoff and one device ID captured before the history scan. An unresolved device has no readable baseline. A new device with no retained eligible observation has an unreadable baseline until its first positive finite minute observation is recorded (up to 60 seconds after service readings resume if such observations continue); one eligible observation suffices for a provisional baseline. Absolute ms/op readings do not depend on that calibration percentile. Observed registry-ID reuse on another root device discards calibration under that ID too. A restored baseline uses the same eligibility and age rule. Cost per tick/read and store bytes per day are measured in `docs/reports/2026-10-20-mg2-cpu-idle-guard.md`. Legacy load/memory reading remains `os.getloadavg`, `os.cpu_count` and `sysctlbyname` (`kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level`: 1 normal, 2 warn, 4 critical), once per pass: max(load1, load5)/CPUs at or above the class's load threshold, rounded as before, or memory at or above its level holds. The larger average holds quickly and lets go slowly. All readers start no subprocess. The check comes before workspace preparation, so a held job costs no git and records no decision; its `machine-busy` hold and `why` name readings, thresholds and elapsed recovery (C-6.11), ordinary queueing never logged as a warning, and it holds no other job back. It never counts attempts. Why: the load was what jobs run, not the providers: measured at about 20:15 EDT on 2026-09-27, at a load average near 120 on 18 logical CPUs, Claude's own processes used about 31% CPU in all while one job's `bfs` search used 453% and test suites and `uv` builds about 430%, and at load 110 to 170 the day before the daemon itself starved (C-6.3's incident). - **C-6.14** (a lane is proven before a burst, 2026-10-03) A lane is *proven* while a model has answered on it within `admission.prove_idle_s` (900 s by default; a positive number of seconds, or null, which holds no lane). A model answers when an event of an attempt's stream shows it (`Adapter.model_answered`: for Claude an `assistant` frame a model served, a model id that is not Claude Code's `<synthetic>` sentinel, no API-error marker and no `error` kind at all, a `result` whose `usage` counts a token and that is not marked `is_error`, or a `system/thinking_tokens` event with a positive count, which Claude Code 2.1.284 writes while the model streams its thinking; when in doubt it says no, since a lane read as proven takes a burst; for Codex an item only the model produces (`agent_message`, `reasoning`, `command_execution`, `file_change`, `mcp_tool_call`, `web_search`, `todo_list`) or a `turn.completed` whose usage counts a token; never `system/init`, a hook event, a placeholder frame, `thread.started`, `turn.started` or an `error` item), when an attempt's classification records `model_answered` or its adapter's verdict is `ok` (no adapter reaches `ok` without an answer, and a stream shape the predicate does not know must not leave a lane one attempt wide for good), and when an admission probe (C-11.4) or a timer's keepalive or heal turn ends `ok`. On a lane that is not proven, a detached attempt in flight that has not answered is its *pilot* (the least attempt id when there are several), and while one is in flight the lane is `no-slot` to every other detached job, with `slot_block` `proving:<attempt id>` laid over the view's `unavailable_lanes` as a probe's lease is (C-11.4); a lane a probe or a latched credential already holds keeps that reason. So a burst onto a lane idle that long places one attempt, and the rest go to lanes that can take them (C-11.3's order) or wait. The pilot's first answer proves the lane and frees it the way a released lease frees a slot (C-6.10: every backed-off capacity wait is looked at on the next pass; only an answer on a lane that was not proven does this, since a proven lane's every start answers too); a pilot that ends without one leaves the lane unproven and the next job its pilot; a pilot that has not answered `admission.prove_wait_s` after its reservation (300 s by default; null waits however long) is a pilot no longer, so its lane takes one more attempt, the next pilot, and a pilot that hangs costs its lane one attempt per wait, not every attempt until `max_wall_s` (`daemon.log` says so once for the attempt; five minutes because the incident's refusals took 78 to 181 s to arrive under its load); a pilot's `auth-dead` disables the lane (C-23.44) with the rest of the burst still queued, not failed, and its job moves on when C-4.5 says so. A job that moved on from an `auth-dead` lane is never a pilot: on a lane not proven it waits for Subfleet's probe of the lane (C-4.5, C-11.4), so a pilot is never a job that moved on, and a pilot's `auth-dead` disables the lane. A probe that answers `auth-dead` has disabled its lane, so the job's next evaluation goes on at once, as after a `limited` one, rather than on the clock an inconclusive probe sets (C-6.10). A probe that ends `ok` proves its lane even when its identity does not bind (C-10.6): the lane answered, so it is not dead, though the probing job waits for the identity as before. A pilot whose wait lapses wakes admission as an answer does. A conversation turn is never held for a pilot and is never one (C-26.9: a person is waiting). The hold is room, not a standing refusal: in an uncapped pool it holds no other job back (C-6.9), it is never `for_good` (C-11.8), a transient retry pinned to the lane waits for it (C-6.12's "only for want of a slot"), and a job it keeps off every lane it could use reads `lane-proving` (C-6.11), ordinary queueing. The attempt worker reads each live detached attempt's stream file for that answer (`Daemon._read_answer`): at most once a second, 256 KiB at a time, only as a regular file and never waiting in `open()`, a line still unfinished past 4 MiB dropped and skipped to its newline, never after the first answer, and nothing it finds or fails to read is the attempt's trouble. What the daemon hears is in memory (`_lane_answers`, `_attempt_answers`, on the clock its views are built on) and recorded as a `lane.answered` event (lane, attempt, `source`: `stream`, `finalization`, `probe`, `keepalive` or `heal`); a starting daemon seeds its memory from the newest 5,000 of them, so a restart neither proves again a lane that answered a minute ago nor takes an attempt that answered before it for a pilot. The early view (`_capacity_view`) and the reservation's check (`_route_rows`, C-6.3) lay the marks with one pure function (`capacity.pilot_marks`) over the rows and clock each reads, so a pilot placed, or an answer heard, between the two changes that lane, which is judged again, and the check's decision is the one an evaluation then would make. Why a lane is proven by an answer and why 900 s: usage readings, probes of the usage endpoint and the profile endpoint do not show this (on 2026-09-30 claude-5 read above its floor while its organisation had turned Claude Code off), so the only proof is a model answering, and the window bounds how long the last one is trusted. A lane in use proves itself with each attempt it starts and never waits; one idle for fifteen of C-18.1's 60 s probe intervals costs one serialized start, its pilot's time to a first answer (seconds; one to three minutes at the incident's load), and nothing while another lane can take the work. The limit of the rule: a lane whose access goes within the window of its last answer is still taken as proven, and can take a burst; each of those jobs then moves on once (C-4.5). With PR #72's load band (`admission.lane_spread`, C-11.3): the band orders the lanes that can take a job and spreads a pool across them, but the only lane above its floor still took every job; the hold refuses a lane before the ranking, the band orders after it, and a proven lane rejoins at its band. - **C-6.16** (priority callers, 2026-10-06, Max: "give axiom promise maximum priority") Policy `admission.priority_callers` is null by default, or a list of nonempty Claude Code caller session ids, canonicalized by stripping whitespace and lowercasing, as native-session checks do. A detached job is `priority` if its `caller_session`, or any ancestor job's caller reached by `parent_job_id`, is in that list, in either case; inheritance includes finished ancestors and is independent of caller liveness. Missing parents end the walk; cycles terminate after visiting each parent at most once. Admission reads only the ancestors it needs, once per detached pass, with indexed queries; when the list is null or empty it reads no ancestors for priority. Classes order `attended`, `priority`, `session`, `background`; within `priority`, FIFO ignores tier and tied timestamps preserve submission order. Other classes keep tier then FIFO, and null or omitted priority callers preserve the previous ordering exactly. A priority job waiting for a slot holds back later competing detached jobs of every tier, priority or not, and a job that passes it keeps it the last slot of a capped pool, as C-6.9 does for a waiter of the job's own tier; a waiter that is not priority still holds back only its own tier. A priority job waiting for a lease holds back no later job, and passing jobs still keep its last slot (C-6.9). Turns remain `attended` even with a selected caller, and their separate pass and waiter queue are unchanged (C-26.9). The machine guard never holds priority work for any load or memory reading (C-6.13), while every other hold, capacity and lease rule still applies. `subfleet why <job>` includes `class priority (admission.priority_callers)` for direct and inherited priority, and its job object carries `class`. `daemon.status` carries configured `priority_callers` as session ids and available registry names; `subfleet status` lists those names with their ids and uses the id alone when no name or registry is available. - **C-6.17** (disk admission, d636, 2026-09-29; paced recovery, 2026-10-09) `admission.disk` is off by default (`enabled: false`); the hub opts in through its live policy. Defaults are `floor_gb: 40`, `resume_margin_gb: 5`, `placement_reserve_gb: 1.5`, `reserve_ttl_s: 600`, and `path: null` (the state root's volume). The loader accepts a boolean enabled flag, finite nonnegative floor and margin, finite positive reserve and TTL, and an optional nonempty path string; it rejects unknown keys naming the exact setting. Each detached admission pass reads available bytes once through an injectable `os.statvfs(path).f_bavail * f_frsize` reader; GB means 1e9 bytes. Before workspace preparation, a detached placement, including a retry and a C-6.16 priority caller, needs `measured_free - outstanding_reservations - placement_reserve >= floor`. A refusal latches disk admission until effective free reaches `floor + resume_margin`, after which placements consume reservations one by one. Each committed detached attempt reserves its budget from `reserved_at`, released at execution end (`finished_at`, including finalization) or TTL, whichever comes first. Reservations are in memory and reconciled from live attempt rows before each pass; size and TTL in attempt evidence preserve the same budgets on restart, and recent older attempts use policy defaults. Latch transitions are events so a restart does not bypass hysteresis. Attended turns never read, wait on, or reserve disk capacity; probes remain exempt. An unreadable enabled path holds detached work with its error. As C-6.13 does, the gate records a `disk` hold in the admission pass's in-memory wait reasons, without a lane lease or a capacity backoff; `why` reports free, reserved, floor, margin and placement budget, and status carries one disk line with free, reserved, floor and holding/open/disabled. Disabled or absent disk policy reads no disk, records no reservation and preserves existing admission decisions. Optional absolute `lower_path` and `raise_path` default to null; `min_floor_gb` defaults to 20 (finite nonnegative) and `max_lower_h` to 16 (finite positive). With both paths null, admission decisions are unchanged. Each enabled detached pass reads the configured JSON files with a 16 KiB bound, nonblocking/no-follow opens, regular-file checks, and bytes and mtime from the same descriptor, outside the store lock; missing files are absent rulings. A lowering needs a future `until`, no more than `max_lower_h` from `min(now, mtime)`, a truthy ruling that stringifies and trims to nonempty, and a numeric-coercible floor at least `min_floor_gb`; its release margin defaults to zero and clamps negative values to zero. A live numeric-coercible raise alone uses `max(policy floor, raise)` with the policy margin. When both are live, a raw raise above the lowered floor wins with the policy margin (even below the policy floor, matching the external agent); otherwise the lowering wins. NaN raises are expired and NaN lowerings refused, matching the agent. Ignored files preserve their `lower_error`, `lower_expired`, `lower_refused`, `override_error` or `override_expired` reason. Invalid top-level JSON is an error. `drop_gb` and `drop_window_min` never trigger native holds, because reservations pace placements; their numeric validation is retained from the external agent, so malformed values reject the lowering (the agent code wins over wholly ignoring these fields). The next pass applies starts and expiry without a restart and re-evaluates the latch against changed floor/margin even without queued jobs. `why` and status show the floor, `floor_source` (`policy`, `lowered until <t> by <ruling>`, `raised until <t>`), optional `why` text and ignored reasons. A source transition records one `admission.disk_floor` event; repeated passes and restarts in that source do not. This paces launches and does not replace retention. - **C-6.18** (placement ramp, 2026-10-10, D-MG1) `admission.ramp` is off by default (`enabled: false`, `per_minute: 12`, `window_s: 60`); the hub opts in through its live policy. The loader accepts a boolean enabled flag and finite positive integers for rate and window, rejects booleans and floats as integers, and rejects unknown keys naming the exact setting. It also requires `per_minute * window_s` to be a multiple of 60, enabled or not, and otherwise refuses the policy naming `admission.ramp.window_s`, so each window's allowance `K = per_minute * window_s / 60` is a whole number of starts. The rule below admits a fractional allowance's ceiling: 12 per minute over a 1-second window would place one job each second, 60 a minute. The ramp is one rule: applied first at the door, before the job's C-6.10 clock and preparation, then decided per reservation by the reserving transaction. A non-priority detached placement of any accepted non-turn job kind (`dispatch`, `resume`, `revive`, `gate-review`, or `handoff`), including a retry, commits only if fewer than `K` detached non-probe attempts have a recorded `reserved_at` later than `t - window_s` (future-dated ones included), where `t` is the `reserved_at` it will record (whole UTC seconds, sampled inside its reserving transaction) and `K` comes from the ramp settings this daemon run loaded at start. The reserving transaction makes this check against durable rows at that exact `reserved_at`, before taking leases; a spent budget holds that job and later ordinary jobs in the pass with the `ramp` reason and takes no new leases or disk reservation. Each enabled detached pass reconstructs from attempt rows and adds its own committed placements immediately; reservation checks reconstruct again because a clock rollback during workspace preparation can revive rows that expired at pass entry. Every retained attempt row counts whatever its state (completed, failed and recovered reservations too), and so do future-dated ones: for every start still in the store, a backwards wall-clock step can only make the check stricter, including after restart. What the check bounds, and what it does not. Among retained starts (those still in the store) that the check admitted under the current settings, every half-open window of `window_s` seconds of recorded `reserved_at` values holds at most `K` non-priority starts, including after backwards clock corrections; starts admitted any other way count toward later windows without being bounded by them: starts reserved while the ramp was off, under other ramp settings (a larger allowance, or a shorter window even at an equal or smaller allowance) or under a different `priority_callers` list (an attempt row does not record its class, which is recomputed from policy), and v1 runs the importer inserted (review of #180 r5: with the ramp off, 3 starts reserve at 100; restarted at 101 with 1 per minute, the window holds 3 against `K = 1` and status reports `used 3, limit 1`). The ramp pins against retention the job of every start later than the prune's clock minus `window_s`, and only while the ramp is enabled, so a backwards step taken after a start was pruned can admit a start the pruned one would have held (review r4: reserve at 100, prune at 160, step back to 100, and a second reservation at 100 passes). In elapsed time the bound holds only to whole-second resolution and only at the sampling instants: truncation lets two starts be sampled up to one second less than `window_s` apart (review r3: samples at 100.999 and 160.000 record 100 and 160, and both place), and positive wall-clock advances must take at least that much elapsed time (forward clock jumps are outside a wall-clock ledger's guarantee). Detached passes are serialized and each reserving transaction holds the store's writer lock until it commits, so each reservation is sampled after the previous one commits; but a commit delayed until the next slot opens can commit, and launch, together with the next one, and a provider launches on a worker after its commit with no second ramp check. The ramp spaces reservations and promises no spacing of launches (reviews r2, r3 and r5). C-6.16 priority jobs bypass the hold as they do C-6.13's guard, but their committed placements count in the same window and slow ordinary work that follows; a priority burst that leaves more than `K` starts in the window delays ordinary work until enough of them expire. Attended turns never read, wait on or count against the ramp; probes are exempt and never enter its ledger. Held jobs have a `ramp` reason in the pass's in-memory holds, with no lane lease and no capacity backoff, and are re-evaluated next pass. `why <job>` reports "placement ramp: N of M starts used in the last W s; next slot at <time>"; status carries one ramp line. The existing periodic control loop (`_control`, `tick_s`, `_schedule("admission", ...)`) runs admission when the next slot opens without an unrelated event. The ramp's retention pins are rechecked inside retention's deleting transaction. Disabled or absent policy reads no ramp history, counts no placement and preserves existing admission decisions exactly; a policy file without `admission.ramp` keeps the policy hash of its exact bytes (C-11.1), and the shipped `default_policy.json` keeps its pre-ramp bytes. Order and time. The ramp keeps admission's priority and FIFO order among jobs whose own C-6.10 clocks are due. It does not carry C-6.10's freed-lease expedite through a ramp hold: a capacity waiter whose lease or slot frees while the window is full keeps its backed-off clock (at most 30 s), so a newer job can take the next slot first (review r5: at `K = 2`, with the ramp on `newer` placed at 60 s and `older` at 91 s, where with it off both placed at 32 s). With free capacity, no continuing priority arrivals, no job on a C-6.10 backoff clock and a wall clock that eventually advances, every unchanged eligible finite queue eventually places. The time bounds that follow further assume no backwards clock corrections (they can delay expiry) and that each reservation samples its clock in the second its slot opens. Things that can break that assumption include preparation (a reservation's `reserved_at` is sampled after its workspace and route are prepared), a wait for the store's writer lock, and the reserving transaction's own checks before the sample, route re-evaluations included. A delay of p seconds from any of them delays that start, and every later start the ramp spaces from it, by p, and such delays add up across windows (review r5: at 2 per minute, 6 jobs with 5 s preparations placed the last at 140 s against a bound of 130 s; review r6: 12 jobs with 10 s writer-lock waits placed the last at 370 s against a bound of 320 s, past `ceil(n / per_minute)` minutes, 360 s). Under those assumptions, from an empty window n jobs place within `(ceil(n / K) - 1) * window_s` seconds plus one pass, which is less than `ceil(n / per_minute)` minutes; from a window already holding up to `K` starts, they place within `ceil(n / K) * window_s` seconds plus one pass: exactly `ceil(n / per_minute)` minutes for the default 60-second window, and at most `window_s` seconds more than that for any window (at 3 per minute over 40 seconds, 3 jobs behind a full window can take 80 seconds). [owner unit, fake; module `subfleet/ramp.py`, `subfleet/policy.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/render.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`]

## 7. Cancellation

- **C-7.1** `kill` inserts a cancel request in the job row (`cancel_requested_at`) in one transaction and returns; the daemon performs C-5.6 asynchronously. `kill --wait` blocks until terminal.
- **C-7.2** If the cancel commits before acceptance, the job becomes `cancelled` even if an attempt later reaches `finalizing`; that attempt's artifacts are kept and it is marked `interrupted`. If acceptance commits first, `kill` returns 0 and prints "already finished".
- **C-7.3** A parent's client or operator cancel cancels every child job not submitted with `--independent`, in the same transaction that records the parent's request. A `max_wall_s` request leaves descendants untouched while pending; it cascades only in the transaction settling the parent's attempt `interrupted`, and never if the parent's receipt supersedes the wall request. For that wall cascade, C-4.2's known-exit rule also applies to descendants: a child whose provider exit is known keeps its own outcome. Receipt evidence is read before the settlement transaction, and durable exit state is checked again inside it.
- **C-7.4** A job that is `queued` when cancelled becomes `cancelled` immediately and releases its leases. An admission probe is never reserved, and its gate never opened, for a job whose cancellation has completed.

## 8. Artifacts and export

- **C-8.1** Every file the daemon publishes (deliverable, `-o` export, `manifest.json`, receipts) is written as: temp file in the destination directory, `os.fsync(fd)`, `os.rename`, `os.fsync` on the directory descriptor.
- **C-8.2** The deliverable is captured once at `finalizing` from the adapter (C-12.6) and recorded in `artifacts` with role `deliverable`, path, bytes, and SHA-256. The raw stream, stdout, stderr, and lane log are recorded with their own roles. The `raw-stream` artifact is the daemon-owned copy of the authoritative regular candidate selected by C-4.3, including a canonical source beside an absent custom raw path; it records the copy's path, byte count and SHA-256. A non-regular, absent, or unreadable source supplies no events and yields no raw-stream artifact. Readers of a finalized attempt use the recorded `raw-stream` artifact path; the capture need not create or replace `stream.jsonl`. Nothing appended to a transcript after the attempt's exit changes a valid captured deliverable. Losing or invalidating the recorded frozen copy becomes a sticky capture failure under C-4.3; cached classification and extraction cannot supply completion proof.
- **C-8.3** The `-o` export is a copy of the accepted deliverable published by C-8.1 after acceptance. A failed export sets `export_error` on the job and emits an event; the job stays `succeeded`.
- **C-8.5 Host push (d1098, option A).** A dispatch opts in at submit with `subfleet run --push-branch <name>`, the `push_branch` key of a `run --batch` manifest job, or the `push_branch` argument of the daemon's `submit` op; no MCP tool exposes it. Absence grants no push authorization. The host refuses an opted-in submit with exit 7 and a fix line when `push.enabled` is false (the default in code and shipped policy), when the checkout has no committed HEAD or allowed origin, when the checkout cannot supply a full history (it is shallow; or, for a job not in place, it borrows objects through `objects/info/alternates` or is a partial clone, by `extensions.partialClone` or a promisor remote, all read as data), or when the branch is invalid or protected. A branch matches `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._/-]{0,199}$`, contains no `..` or `@{`, ends in neither `/` nor `.lock`, starts with no `/`, and must also be a valid Git branch name (no empty or dot-prefixed components or component ending `.lock`). `main`, `master`, `release/*`, the remote's default branch, and patterns in `push.protected` are protected after NFC normalization and casefolding. `HEAD` and names starting with `refs/`, `heads/`, or `remotes/` are refused in any case. A new branch whose normalized, casefolded name equals an existing remote branch is refused; updating an existing branch requires the exact spelling this family owns. Only the CLI sends `push_branch`, and only after reading `capabilities`: `run` and `run --batch` refuse it with exit 69, submitting nothing, when the daemon does not advertise `push.v1`, because an older daemon ignores the unknown field (C-16.2) and would never push. `push.allowed_remotes` matches normalized GitHub owner/repository names such as `github.com/MaxGhenis/*`; explicit `file://` patterns permit offline host-owned test origins. No other transport or URL credentials are admitted. Submit reads local origin and HEAD as data on the host: it looks at `.git` with lstat (a directory or a gitdir file, never a link or special file) and reads each metadata file (gitfile, `commondir`, `config`, `config.worktree`, `HEAD`, loose refs, `packed-refs`) without following a final link and without waiting in open(), only as a regular file, and within a cap (64 KiB, 256 KiB for each config, 64 MiB for `packed-refs`); anything else there refuses the submit promptly with exit 7, and no later submit waits on it. Each look parses each config at most once, sharing its data snapshot between metadata and object-store checks; the next look reads fresh files. It records `push_remote`, `workdir_head`, `push_branch`, and `push_default_branch` in the job row; it queries the recorded remote's default through a quarantine. An undiscoverable default is refused. The policy section also carries positive integer `max_bundle_mb` and `max_commits` (defaults 256 and 100); enabling live policy is a separate hub action.

  The accepted attempt supplies `.subfleet/push.bundle` inside the directory the job starts in, a regular file holding a full self-contained bundle advertising exactly `HEAD`, made by `mkdir -p .subfleet && git bundle create .subfleet/push.bundle HEAD` (use `--git-dir=.git-local` when committing there). The daemon tells the job the absolute path in `SUBFLEET_PUSH_BUNDLE` and its prompt preamble. Intake opens the worktree root as a daemon-held directory handle and every component below it with `O_NOFOLLOW`, adding `O_DIRECTORY` for directories; a missing file, link, FIFO, directory, or oversize file yields `push_error`. The host copies at most `max_bundle_mb` MiB into `<state root>/jobs/<job>/a<sequence>/push.bundle`, then verifies and fetches only this daemon-owned copy into a freshly created bare quarantine. It never runs Git against a job-controlled worktree, `.git`, `.git-local`, config, hook, alternate object store, or environment for intake or publication. The quarantine uses an empty template and minimal config, hooks disabled, `core.fsmonitor=false`, `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, global config disabled, no includes, and no replacement refs. Only directly configured host global credential helpers are passed explicitly; no token is copied to a lane. All Git processes for push run with the quarantine as their cwd and gitdir, a fixed environment derived from host identity locations, and a timeout. For opted-in writable jobs, workspace allocation copies Git object files as data into a quarantine and creates a standalone checkout there, renamed into place whole; subsequent host salvage does not run Git in that checkout. Each object must be regular and non-sparse (`st_blocks * 512 >= st_size`). The named `OBJECT_COPY_CAP` limits source object bytes copied per workspace build, including APFS snapshots, to 600,000,000, two fifths of C-6.17's default 1.5 GB placement reserve. Budgeting a similarly sized Git clone object store leaves nominal checkout headroom; Git's generated object store and checkout expansion have independent sizes. The intake cap remains a fixed limit when disk policy is disabled or customized. Descriptor-based APFS `fclonefileat` is preferred, with an independent bounded byte copy on unsupported volumes, never a hardlink to writable source objects. Fallback reads cannot grow past the remaining budget; changed source objects and oversized clone snapshots are refused, and an oversized snapshot is removed. Sparse or over-budget objects fail the job with exit 7 and a notice, never a workspace retry, and jobs behind it continue. After acquiring `daemon.lock` at startup, before the first pass or any push worker, the daemon removes all of `push-quarantine/`; links are unlinked without following their targets and a cleanup error aborts startup. A second daemon that cannot acquire the lock deletes nothing belonging to the live daemon. Admission queues standalone checkout construction on the existing single push thread, sharing its queue with publication, and returns immediately with a workspace/preparing hold. No lane lease or attempt is reserved until a later pass consumes a completed build. Repeated looks queue no duplicate build. At most one preparation future is outstanding and at most one new build is offered per detached pass, after its fresh disk and admission checks. Completed results are consumed before unrelated pin, approval, or capacity holds. A finished build releases the preparation slot once admission has recorded its outcome, the checkout's ownership or the job's failure: a completion write that reports `SQLITE_BUSY` or `SQLITE_LOCKED` (including extended codes), `SQLITE_FULL`, `SQLITE_PROTOCOL`, or `SQLITE_IOERR_READ`, `SQLITE_IOERR_WRITE`, `SQLITE_IOERR_FSYNC`, `SQLITE_IOERR_DIR_FSYNC`, or `SQLITE_IOERR_TRUNCATE` keeps the completed result and its slot, holds the job as `workspace`/preparing and retries on a later pass. It fails the job only once both bounds are spent: at least eight failed completion writes, and at least `PUSH_WORKSPACE_STORE_RETRY_WINDOW_S` (600 s) since the first, because admission passes run every tick and eight tries alone took about 350 ms (review r3 of #183), far less than a full disk takes to recover. Any other store error fails that job at once (`job.workspace_failed`); no further ownership write is attempted after exhaustion. The cause and slot remain pending until the failure and its notice commit together, then the slot is released and the unused checkout is discarded on the push thread. A successful completion or cancellation clears the count and the window; a daemon restart resets both. Other `SQLITE_IOERR` codes, including checksum or filesystem corruption and unknown I/O failures, are not classified as transient. A cancelled build whose worker is still running keeps the slot until the worker returns. A skipped or discarded build never opens the readiness gate. Admission uses the explicit prepared checkout path: a checkout missing at its validation is refused with exit 7 and a notice, and it never rebuilds synchronously even if the checkout disappears after that check. The worker builds the checkout and renames it into place whole; admission's completion transaction then records it in `jobs.worktree`, only while the job is still queued or waiting and not cancelled, and before the job can be placed. That record keeps the checkout owned by retention across a restart. A restart reuses that checkout or rebuilds after quarantine cleanup. Cancellation during a build discards the unused checkout; a late cancellation of a checkout still tracked by this daemon, with no attempt, queues deletion on the push thread. If a restart loses that tracking, the recorded checkout remains owned by retention. Standalone construction's host Git calls each have a fixed 60-second cap, and a timeout refuses only that job with exit 7 and a notice. In-place metadata checks stay in the pass, within the config cap above. Admission never lets an allocation failure or an in-place job's recheck of its checkout stop jobs behind it. In allocation, an `OSError` (the host's own trouble, Git that could not start included) goes to C-6.8's workspace handling (a transient one waits, any other fails the job with its cause), and any other error (the checkout or Git refused, its metadata included) fails the job with exit 7 and a notice; the in-place recheck refuses every error with exit 7 and a notice. Every job behind it is placed as before. An in-place push job's write target, the folder its `worktree:` lease names (C-6.5), is the checkout top `host_push.validate_submit` read from that metadata, spelled at submit (`folders.present`, a name not there kept as given) and never marked `unspelled`, also when the checkout moved after validation. Admission never asks Git for it (C-8.4's rediscovery skips push jobs), so the checkout's own `core.worktree` cannot name another write target, and a writer holding the checkout keeps the job waiting. Admission spells that top again before taking the lease, as any in-place writer's; a checkout not there at admission is refused by the in-place recheck (exit 7, `workspace refused`), as before (review of #141 at 2cbf6fca, P2-B).

  Before any push, bundle verification and strict object checking must succeed, the advertised tip must be a commit descending from the recorded `workdir_head`, and at most `max_commits` commits may be introduced. Any addition, edit, or deletion under `.github/` in any introduced commit, including a subsequently reverted change, is refused with a notice directing a human or hub push because CI can use repository secrets. The `.github` check compares only normalized, casefolded root entries against the base, without recursive diff expansion. Tree entries are streamed under an entry cap, and every Git call in verification shares one overall deadline. Gitlinks are refused. Symlinks whose targets are absolute, contain a `..` or `.git` component in any case or normalization, or pass through another symlink in the same tree are refused in the tip and every introduced tree. A `.git` component is also matched without the characters HFS+ ignores in names (Unicode Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, as `folders.identity` drops them; dotless i as i) and in the NTFS spellings Git itself refuses (`git~1`, an alternate data stream such as `.git::$INDEX_ALLOCATION`, trailing dots and spaces); paths compare without those ignorable characters when a link is checked for passing through another. Residual risk: existing push or PR workflows and preview deploys still run the job's changed code with this repository's secrets; refusing `.github/` changes does not prevent that execution. Current policy and the remote's current default branch are rechecked. A branch can be created; an existing branch can be updated only when `job_owned_branches` records ownership by this job's family and a previous successful publication, and its fetched remote tip is an ancestor of the new tip. Ownership keys use `remote_key(remote)` and the normalized, casefolded branch name, so equivalent HTTPS/SSH repository spellings share one claim. Independent children start a new family. Publication is exactly `git push <recorded URL> <verified SHA>:refs/heads/<declared branch>` with the host credential helper; there is never a force flag, force-with-lease, `+` refspec, deletion, or branch inferred from lane metadata.

  Push follows committed acceptance and runs outside every store transaction (C-3.3), serialized on one dedicated push thread without occupying pool workers while queued, with a timeout. A push failure never alters accepted state or rc: it sets `push_error` and appends a notice line; success records `push_sha` and appends the SHA, remote and branch to the notice. `job_pushes` records one operation per job, with branch, recorded remote, SHA when known, start/finish times, result and error; ownership and audit survive job retention. `runs show`, including JSON and offline reads, surfaces this audit. A durable pending record precedes intake and publication, so re-export never performs a duplicate push or creates a second record. A push job's leases, its output's included, stay held until its push settles; its `-o` export then runs as any job's (C-8.3, C-6.5), whether the push succeeded or failed: of every holder of any spelling of the output, the oldest publishes, and a lease not held or a newer owner sets `export_error`. Export of a push job without an accepted attempt queues no publication and takes the export failure. A durable `pushing_at` marker is committed immediately before `git push`. A restart before that marker safely retries verification, at most three intakes; a restart after it fails with an "interrupted; inspect the remote" notice. Retention archives a push job's standalone `.git` directory as data, without running Git in that checkout. The implementation's bundle/workspace conventions and the prescribed command's remote creation race are documented in `docs/reports/2026-10-09-host-push.md`.
- **C-8.4** Retention: newest 500 jobs or 2 GiB (policy `retention.jobs`, `retention.bytes`), whichever binds, counted over jobs that are not conversation turns (turn jobs have their own budget, C-26.12), computed outside transactions in a maintenance pass; jobs that are active, `quarantined`, have unread notices addressed to a session, hold salvage refs referenced nowhere else, or are a gate's evidence are never pruned. A notice with no session can never be read, so it pins nothing (review IR-17). Probe and keepalive results live in `readings` and `events`, not in `jobs`. I5: a job with its own allocated worktree (writable, not in place) is protected as `turn-folder` by the archive driver's `_pin_reasons`, at selection and again before committing, while a live `worktree-turn:` or `worktree-read:` row names its recorded worktree or a folder inside it (a conversation in a repository nested there keys its row on that repository); a row on the in-place workspace keeps no in-place job, whose workspace retention never removes. A row on `jobs/<job>` or a folder inside it keeps that job, including an in-place job, because retirement moves the job directory too. Before moving either tree, `Retirement._fence_job_folder` checks turn rows and queued or live turn workdirs and fences the canonical `jobs/<job>` directory atomically, including on recovery. Quarantine cleanup keeps live turn cwds and reader or turn rows, including detached jobs and quarantined attempts, and fences its location before reclaiming it. Its retirement transaction checks `folders.turn_holds` on the canonical worktree and every folder inside it before taking `retire:<job>` and `worktree:<folder>` together, and the commit transaction checks them again on the journal's canonical spelling, so a recorded worktree spelled in another case, or none recorded, does not hide a row; turn and detached-job reservation check that fence in the same store transaction, on the folder and on every folder above it (`folders.retiring`: string operations on the canonical spelling, no filesystem work), and hold writable turns, readers and detached jobs there `lease-held` until retirement releases it, also one in a repository nested inside the tree, whose row or `worktree:` lease is keyed on that repository; a retention hold for either a turn or a detached job names the fence keys as `retiring` and the folder checked, and `why` says retention is removing the finished job's tree (C-6.11). Its `leases` list names all held blockers; `retiring` names only the retention-held `worktree:` keys among them, in that order, and is omitted when none exist. The early look names retention's fences in both lists, while a reserving hold can additionally name ordinary writer or other held leases in `leases`. Every reservation fences its canonical resolved run folder in the same transaction that takes its leases. A turn has a reader row there when it differs from its Git hold; every detached job has a job-held reader row there for the whole lease lifetime, including retries and quarantined attempts. Detached launch uses that reservation's recorded resolved folder, so retargeting the submitted symlink after reservation does not redirect the provider away from its pin. These reader rows exclude no writer and do not change C-6.5's exclusive contention on the write target. Admission spells that submitted target again before reservation (`folders.present`, with the detached in-place answer taken before `_workspace`); if its pathname now resolves through a replacement symlink, the exclusive lease names the resolved folder. A read-only detached job's folder, and an allocated writer's source folder, is its workdir spelled once at submission (`folder` in `job.submitted`). Every detached admission look also spells where the workdir resolves now (`_detached_folders`, outside transactions), so a workdir replaced by a symlink into another fenced tree is checked there as well as on its recorded folder; Each modern recorded/current source carries its own absence answer to the early and reserving checks, using the same absent-name folding as row subjects independently of the row answer; no spelling is cached between looks. A legacy detached job with no recorded folder or write target uses that current spelling with NFC normalization and casefolding (`folders.retiring_folded`), including when quarantine has made its original names unspellable. This can conservatively hold a legacy job on a case-equivalent name on a volume that distinguishes case. An admission look that reaches the early fence check reads these folders before `_workspace`; a fence still present in the transaction recording the wait skips workspace preparation and runs no workspace Git. An `unspelled` in-place job can first run `_rediscovered`'s read-only lookup. Operator holds, standing pin refusals, disk and machine checks and pool ordering can hold a job earlier. A fence acquired after the early read is still checked in the reserving transaction, though workspace preparation may already have run. Fences found after the pass's lease census count as seen, so release brings the next look forward (C-6.10). An early fenced priority job keeps its priority place across tiers under a pool cap (C-6.16). Admission also checks the fences on both a turn's Git folder and its canonical actual run folder, and on every folder above either, before workspace preparation, using the same `folders.present` spelling and folded comparison for absent names as the reserving transaction, with a plain read followed, when a fence is found, by a transaction that checks the job's state and re-reads the fences. The Git folder this look reads is the one the reserving transaction names: for a turn submit could not spell in full (`unspelled`, below), the folder found again before the look, whose one answer the transaction uses too (`_rediscovered`), so a persisted `core.worktree` naming a fenced top outside the turn's folder holds the turn before its workspace is prepared (review of #141 at 2cbf6fca, P2-A). An eligible turn whose fence is still held in that transaction skips workspace preparation and runs no git in the tree being retired but, for an `unspelled` turn, that lookup: `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` in its folder (a second time when git finds no checkout under a `.git`), which only reads, run without git's diagnostic trace variables (`GIT_TRACE*`, dropped from every git Subfleet's salvage helpers run, and Trace2's three targets set to `0`, which turns off a `trace2.*Target` in the global or system config too) (measured on APFS: no content, mode, mtime or ctime of the tree, its nested `.git` and index, a linked worktree's admin directory or an external top moved; a read may move an atime, which retention does not sign). When that lookup cannot finish (C-6.8), a turn whose recorded folder is fenced still waits on that fence, and any other waits for its workspace. The turn receives the hold the reserving transaction records when retention's fences are the only blockers it finds: `lease-held` on the same fence keys in the same order, the same folder named (its actual run folder when that is fenced, otherwise its Git folder), a capacity wait under the same signature (so the same backoff), and the same `waiting` row and clock. Where the transaction would also find a key retention does not hold (a detached writer's `worktree:` on a writable turn's own folder, a lease another job holds or is queued for), its hold would list that key too and its signature would cover it; the look's lists retention's fences alone, and the first look after they are let go finds the rest. This hold precedes the placement checks and refusals that follow workspace preparation. For a still eligible turn, a fence released before that transaction allows the look to proceed; a job ended or cancelled meanwhile is neither held nor prepared. The reserving transaction still checks the fences and holds a turn if retention acquired one after the early read; that look may already have prepared its workspace in the tree. An in-place writer's folder is its write target, its checkout's top level recorded at submit and spelled again at reservation (C-6.5), and a fence on that folder itself is its own `worktree:` key, contested as before; an allocated writer's write target is the worktree admission allocates for it (C-6.6). Such a job waits through the retirement. A queued job whose recorded workdir matches the allocated worktree or a folder inside it keeps that tree at the commit (`worktree-in-use`, while the job has not ended), which rolls the retirement back; submission records a job's workdir in its one spelling (`folders.canonical`), so a workdir typed in another case or Unicode form than its volume stores, or through the Data volume's firmlink (`/System/Volumes/Data/…`), still names the tree it is in, as long as every name in it can be looked up then; a name that cannot (gone, or under a directory that cannot be searched at that moment) stays as typed. Of the leases on a folder above a turn's or a detached job's, only retention's fence holds it, never a detached writer's (C-6.5). Read-only detached jobs and allocated writers take no exclusive lease on their source folder and wait there only for retention; writable turns and in-place writers retain C-6.5's contention on their exact write target. Admission spells the folder a row will name again off the store lock before its reserving transaction; detached in-place writers take this answer before `_workspace` and reuse it at reservation. It reserves a row (a turn's, or an in-place writer's `worktree:`) only on a folder whose every name it looked up (`folders.present`), so a row names its folder as the volume stores it, whatever submit recorded: a native session's cwd typed `…/jOB/vendor/lib` for the tree `Job`, spelled at submit while the tree was in quarantine, kept the names that could not be looked up as typed, and a row on it matched none of the checks above once its job had ended (review of 8a112986, finding 1); and a folder submit could not spell in full (`unspelled` in `job.submitted`, judged on the spelling submit records) is found again at admission as submit finds it, from its checkout's top level, once per look and before the look for retention's fence, whose answer the reserving transaction uses (never for a push job, C-8.5); where git finds no checkout though a `.git` is at or above the folder (its tree away for git's look), submit and admission ask git once more, so a turn submitted from a subdirectory while its tree was away still holds its checkout, not the subdirectory (C-6.5; review of 3410b4f0, P1; review of fb674463, Q1, Q1b and Q1c). A folder with a name that is not there reserves nothing: it waits `lease-held` on a retention fence it is in, compared case- and normalization-insensitively (`folders.retiring(..., folded=True)`), and its queued job keeps the tree at the commit when its recorded workdir matches the allocated tree (`worktree-in-use`, with SQLite's `NOCASE` and `LIKE` comparisons); with no such fence it waits for its workspace (C-6.8). A `worktree:` lease held by anything but the job's own retirement (`retention:<job>`) keeps the job as `worktree-lease`: one on its recorded worktree keeps any job, and one on a folder inside it keeps a job with its own allocated worktree, as a turn row does (a detached in-place writer in a repository nested there holds `worktree:<that repository>`, and its quarantined attempt leaves its job `lost` with the lease kept); the retirement transaction reads those leases on the canonical worktree and every folder inside it (`folders.exclusive_inside`, one key range) before taking the fence, and the commit transaction reads them again on the journal's canonical spelling. Known limit (review of fb674463, P3-2; review of #141 at 7b9e7ca4, P3): `worktree-in-use` compares a job's workdir as submit recorded it (canonical for fresh submissions, resolved without spelling for legacy submissions) with SQLite's `NOCASE` and `LIKE`, which ignore the case of ASCII letters only and compare Unicode forms byte for byte. Legacy workdirs through the Data volume's firmlink (`/System/Volumes/Data/Users/…`), and names submission cannot look up (the tree already in quarantine, or a directory above it briefly unsearchable), can keep another case or Unicode form in any name of the path, so that pin can miss. Fresh fully spellable submissions record the volume's spelling, but can still miss the pin when allocation persisted an aliased state-root spelling: a descendant under STÄTE recorded as stäte misses ASCII-only `LIKE`; those queued cases are preserved today by an ownership-boundary error and rollback. Such a turn still waits on the fence: admission spells its folder again, every name that is there as the volume stores it, and compares the names it could not look up caseless and normalization-insensitively (`folders.retiring(..., folded=True)` folds the whole path). It takes no row, and fails after C-6.8's retries if the retirement commits. Folder strings are compared as recorded, with canonical spelling established outside transactions (`folders.canonical`), never filesystem inspection under the store lock. Pruning archives first (d635, `docs/desktop/retention-archive.md`): a job's files (worktree and job directory, byte for byte as clones or copies, except a tracked file whose raw bytes are a blob a network remote's refs reach in a repository that is not scratch, read back whole, and except regenerable output: inside the entries a tool writes at the top of a worktree directory whose structure says it is a virtualenv, `node_modules`, `__pycache__` (all of it) or a tool cache carrying `CACHEDIR.TAG`, which git tracks nothing in and ignores entirely (names compared NFC-normalized and casefolded) and no nested repository holds, none of them holding a `.git`, the files a rule proves regenerable (an installed distribution's file whose sha256 its RECORD names, from a distribution uv installed from an index or a network URL; bytecode with a magic number beside its source; a tool cache's own files; nothing of `node_modules`), which are deleted with the tree and not restored, while everything else in that directory is archived), its worktree's admin directory and every object that directory names, its salvage commits and baseline (parents of one anchor commit, the only head of a bundle of every commit no network remote holds; a salvage ref whose commit the verified anchor reaches is referenced elsewhere), and its rows (`rows.json`) are written to `$SUBFLEET_HOME/archive/<job>/` and read back before the rows are deleted; after that, an entry is deleted only if its signature (ctime included) is unchanged right before the unlink, or, for a file that had other links when archived and whose ctime alone moved, only if its bytes still hash to the archived sha256, and anything new or changed goes to `$SUBFLEET_HOME/retention-conflicts/<job>/`. A salvage snapshot's skipped paths are protected unless the archive itself holds their bytes and reads them back; a caller's `salvage_referenced_elsewhere` vouch releases only the ref pin, never the file-preservation check. A regular file with neither stored bytes nor a verified omission or regenerable proof keeps the job as `protected`, with `unarchived path` and its path in the result's `deferred` reasons, never `pruned`; this check also runs before committing a resumed archive, whose journal bypasses the builder. A job whose tree another tool holds aside, or whose tree left or came back while it was being retired, is kept. A pass retires the oldest eligible jobs in a bounded batch, without sizing every job first, parks a job whose archive needs more than its slice with its progress (a slice always makes new progress, and parked jobs take turns), defers a busy or failing one (keeping what it archived; a repeated error waits longer), counts a job it cannot measure as decided, and never runs a repository-wide `git worktree prune`; the daemon retains measured sizes and archive progress between passes. While work remains, a completed batch that advances (including a newly cached size, a parked archive slice, publication, partial verified deletion, or a prune) is followed 5 s later; an advancing batch that reaches its deadline warns and raises `TimeoutError`, on C-5.10’s worker clock. A deadline with neither pruning nor advancement marks `TimeoutError`, warns once with the store’s job count, rearms the hour and does not raise. A cancelled pass marks `CancelledError` and rearms the hour even after progress. A completed pass with nothing more, or a batch with no advancement, marks and rearms the hour from its end. In every rearming outcome `_last_maintenance` is set last, after status bookkeeping, so a bookkeeping failure leaves retention due. A deadline limits new starts, never commits of verified archives; even slow pre-selection work permits one job to start and receive its archive slice.
