A failed batch holder scan marks `ScanFailed` and rearms the hour, even after quarantine, rollback or archive work, to avoid cycling the store against an unavailable scanner. Delivered service notices older than 14 days are pruned first, once per retention call (one bounded batch); a failure there is logged and never stops job retention. Recovery finishes journaled retirements before new selection and prioritizes a job left with a journal-less `retention:<job>` lease. Its events, each pool in its result and the daemon's log lines report the bytes freed (tracked files a remote holds and regenerable output, deleted without a copy) apart from the bytes moved into the archive and the bytes the archive adds (its bundle, manifest, summary and rows, and byte copies), with the net. A job whose baseline no network remote holds, and whose bundle would carry more than `retention.remote_less_history_bytes` (default 64 MiB) of history, is kept; a job registered in a repository inside another job's tree pins that job while it has rows. Retention's children run under the `utility` QoS clamp (C-5.1). Archives are kept until a person removes them.

## 9. Evidence labels and classification

- **C-9.1** Reading labels: `provider` (a server reported it; source and age recorded), `stale-provider` (the same beyond `reading_ttl_s`), `admission-observed` (this model recently succeeded or was rejected on this lane; remaining quota unknown), `local-backoff` (a routing decision with an explicit expiry and reason), `unknown`. No other label exists. A percentage is rendered only from a `provider` or `stale-provider` reading, and a stale one is marked stale.
- **C-9.2** For a run without verified success, classification precedence is authentication, then admission, then quota, and the classifier records which evidence answered each. All text signatures (including CLI-version and transient failures) read only CLI/provider output: stderr and Claude `StreamSummary.cli_texts()`, or Codex failure events and stderr. Model-authored assistant/agent text and the text of a non-error result never supply provider failure signatures or closure clocks. Wording changes, including replacement by neutral text, preserve the adapter outcome class and closure when they preserve the selected deliverable's nonemptiness under C-12.6. Adding or erasing the deliverable can change acceptance and expose or supersede genuine CLI/provider diagnostics; the model's phrases never become those diagnostics. Verified Claude success requires rc 0, a non-error result from its authoritative stream (C-4.3) and a nonblank deliverable (C-12.6); verified Codex success requires rc 0, `turn.completed` in its own stream, a nonblank deliverable and no terminal failure or spawn error. Such success supersedes recovered text failures, while a terminal Codex `turn.failed` remains authoritative over earlier completion. An empty completion or agent frame alone is insufficient for `ok`. Success is never `limited` or `auth-dead` from text, even CLI text. Only a structured provider verdict can override it (Claude admission rejection, C-9.8; a structured refusal remains `content-filter`). The daemon validates captured deliverable content at every finalization, replay and restart (C-12.6), using ASCII whitespace for Codex bytes and Unicode whitespace for Claude text. A cached `ok` with a blank deliverable becomes `unknown`, detail `empty deliverable with rc 0`, under the existing empty-result rule; it is never accepted or exported, and its job fails unless another existing finalization override supplies a different failure class. Outcome classes: `ok`, `limited`, `auth-dead`, `cli-too-old`, `content-filter`, `transient`, `unknown`. The raw rc and signal are always kept beside the class. An attempt the daemon signalled (`killed_by` set: an operator's kill, the wall limit, recovery of a dead guardian's group) is never `ok`, whatever its rc, `turn.completed` and deliverable, unless its receipt proves the provider exited strictly before the first stop delivery of any kind: the kill protocol's group signals, the turn runner's provider interrupt, approval replies that cancel or abort the turn (Claude `interrupt:true`, Codex `decision:cancel` or legacy `decision:abort`), every relay `signal` frame (including late SIGINT and escalation), and relay `close`. Each conversation `Frame` explicitly declares `stops: bool` where its provider protocol is built; the runner audits this capability, independently of its tag or operation. Existing callers without the flag default conservatively to stop-bearing, and a source guard requires every production construction to choose explicitly. A tool-only denial (Claude `deny` without `interrupt`, Codex `decline`), an allow/answer reply, a permission subset, a queued-message cancellation or a cancelled MCP elicitation is not a turn stop. A cancel-turn approval may suppress the redundant queued interrupt, but does not reset or disable the wall's SIGINT, stdin-close or containment escalation if the turn remains live. The daemon commits an `attempt.kill_started` intent for the kill protocol or an `attempt.stop_delivery` intent with `via=relay` before transmitting each stop frame, then enriches it with `signal_sent` and the millisecond `signal_at` sampled immediately before transmission, after connection setup and frame encoding. Only an explicit `signal_sent=false` proves no delivery; a pending intent, uncertain acknowledgement or missing clock is conservative across restart. Millisecond `finished_at` must be strictly earlier than every delivery's `signal_at`; ties count as after, and second-precision receipts compare by the second (D-WALL1). Merely requesting or queuing the asynchronous interrupt supplies no delivery clock. A proven pre-signal exit retains its provider classification, including a failure from the provider's own signal; it is never blamed on the wall. Otherwise a wall-signalled attempt settles `interrupted`/`unknown` with `killed_by=max_wall_s`. A conversation driver's recorded `complete` remains the provider's message fact (C-24.4), but supplies no exception to this ordering rule for the attempt; replacing the wall with a client's request cannot restore `ok` after a signal. If the adapter classified a stopped attempt `ok`, finalization records it `unknown` with the detail `stopped by <killed_by>: exit <rc> after the daemon's signal is not a finished deliverable`. The daemon decides this, not the adapter, so it holds for every provider; like C-12.6's empty-deliverable rule it is recomputed from the attempt row on every finalization while `finalization.json` keeps the adapter's verdict, and whenever either rule overrides that verdict the attempt's evidence keeps it as `provider_verdict` (incident: 2026-09-24, three running Codex jobs were cancelled; Codex exited 0 after the SIGTERM and left its last interim progress message, 32 to 61 words such as "I am checking the newer validation code before finalizing", in `last.md`, which the adapter classified `ok`, "Codex completed with a deliverable"). A detached Codex attempt (fresh or `codex exec resume`) is `ok` only when its own JSON stream contains `turn.completed`, rc is 0, no `turn.failed` was read, no spawn error applies, and a nonblank deliverable exists (C-12.6); `last.md` alone never proves completion. Such an attempt is verified success, so recovered authentication, admission or quota text in its stream or stderr does not undo it (the rule above). An rc-0 attempt without `turn.completed` still passes through the existing authentication, admission and quota checks; absent those signals or a terminal `turn.failed`, it is `transient`, detail `Codex exited before its turn completed`, so C-4.5 retries it. Classification records `turn_completed` and `last_event_type` beside the existing evidence. Finalization independently verifies completion from this attempt's frozen stream (C-4.3: any existing frozen entry is authoritative; a non-regular entry supplies no events) before accepting any detached Codex `ok`, including cached verdicts, legacy receipts and restart recovery; that check reads only C-4.3's recorded copy, whose inode was freshly written from one candidate descriptor after comparison with the live output identities, so a later write through a provider's alias or an unlinked open writer cannot supply `turn.completed`; neither a name nor a link count proves freezing, and restart never combines a re-copy of a live path with cached verdicts or extraction; a cached `ok` without completion becomes `transient` with the same detail, and the original adapter verdict remains `provider_verdict` evidence. A verdict this daemon classified is written with `rule` in `finalization.json`, and every replay reaches its first pass's class while its recorded copy validates (C-4.3). An interrupted copy check preserves that verdict for a bounded retry; proven invalidity or exhaustion of the three checks in one daemon run replaces it with C-4.3's sticky capture failure (`copy cannot be checked` on exhaustion, with detail `provider stream copy could not be checked: retry checks exhausted` rather than a claim that the provider never completed). A cached non-`ok` verdict without `rule`, which an older daemon recorded, becomes `ok` only when the frozen stream's `turn.completed`, the captured nonblank deliverable and an rc-0 receipt prove verified success with no `turn.failed`, spawn error or `killed_by`; its verdict remains `provider_verdict` evidence. Incident: 2026-10-12, a host reboot cut off 34 of 40 running Codex attempts with rc-0 receipts and neither `turn.completed` nor `last.md`; the fallback accepted their last interim progress notes; the daemon sent no signal, so `killed_by` stayed null and the signalled-attempt rule above could not apply.
- **C-9.3** `auth-dead` requires a 401 from a usage endpoint, an explicit organisation-block message, a refresh-token-revoked event, or the provider's refusal of the lane's own key ("Incorrect API key provided", `invalid_api_key`; codex-1, -3 and -6 failed every job that way on 2026-09-25 while classed transient). For Codex any other 401 is `transient`. For Claude, a credential-failure signature (a 401, `authentication_error`, a revoked token) in a run that never reached `system/init` is `auth-dead`, because the credential never authenticated; after `system/init` it is not auth evidence. A 403 from the Claude usage endpoint on a setup token is expected scope and is not evidence. On a run without verified success, a CLI/provider limit phrase with a successful `system/init` is quota evidence, not auth evidence. A Codex conversation turn's `unauthorized` error is not evidence on its own: the turn is `unknown` and the lane's usage endpoint decides (design §7). Only what the CLI and the provider wrote is evidence of `auth-dead` (2026-10-03): stderr, the frames that are the CLI's own (`claude_stream.cli_placeholder`: Claude Code's placeholders, marked `is_api_error_message`, with a missing or `<synthetic>` model, or carrying a credential or organisation error kind), the result's `errors`, and its text when it is marked `is_error` (`StreamSummary.cli_texts`); Codex reads only its failure events and stderr, as before. A model's own words quoting these phrases (a review of this classifier) are not: an `auth-dead` attempt disables its lane (C-23.44) and its job may move on (C-4.5), so a job whose answer quoted them would disable each lane it reached. Every Claude and Codex classification records `model_answered` in its evidence, whatever its class: whether any event of the attempt's stream showed a model answering (C-6.14), or null when no event of it could be read. `system/init` is no such event: every attempt refused on 2026-09-30 had one, then the placeholder (`error` `oauth_org_not_allowed`, every usage counter zero), and a `success` result marked `is_error` with `api_error_status` 403 and `duration_api_ms` 0 (fixture `tests/fixtures/claude/org-block-synthetic`).
- **C-9.4** `limited` carries scope (`account` or a model id), the provider's reset clock when reported, else a guessed clock of now + 3600 s marked `clock_source: guessed`, and the evidence (event, stderr line, or rc). Text quotas and every prose or epoch reset clock read only stderr and Claude `cli_texts()` (CLI placeholders, result `errors`, result text only when `is_error`), or Codex failure events and stderr. A model report and a success result never supply quota or reset evidence. It writes a closure (C-9.6).
- **C-9.5** `transient` covers 5xx, stream disconnects, DNS and TLS failures, and "model at capacity" messages. It never writes a closure; it allows one same-lane retry after 60 s. A provider's refusal of the model itself for want of capacity (Codex: "Selected model is at capacity. Please try a different model."; `codex.MODEL_CAPACITY_MESSAGE_RE`, C-9.2) counts only when the provider message itself starts, after optional whitespace and `ERROR:`, with `model [is|was] [currently] at capacity` (bracketed words optional; spaces within this phrase may be underscores), optionally preceded by `Selected ` or `The `, or with `error code: model_at_capacity` (case-insensitive; trailing provider details allowed). The message is an `error.message` or `turn.failed.error.message` (also an error event's nested `error.message`), a direct stderr refusal, or the message of a non-tool `codex_core::...` tracing record. A phrase embedded after any other prefix, including `Tool execution failed ... Captured stderr:` or `Captured tool output:`, never counts. Tool diagnostics and their following quoted lines, even `ERROR:` payloads, stay within that record; a new tracing record (`[ISO timestamp ]TRACE/DEBUG/INFO/WARN/ERROR codex_core::<target>: <message>`) is classified independently, so a non-tool record ends the previous tool diagnostic's context. Model text and tool output never count (C-9.2). A qualifying refusal stays `transient` and records `model_capacity: true` in its classification, with that failure, not an earlier reconnect it recovered from, as `admission`; authentication, an old CLI, content and quota still answer first, and a verified success supersedes it. It is not an account's limit and not a lane's fault: the account, the lane and other jobs are untouched, with no closure, no lane disabled and no lane excluded (D-CAP1, 2026-10-13). On a job with no model pin and no lane pin that is no conversation turn, resume or revive (`scheduler.capacity_steerable`: a pin is the operator's choice and evaluates one model, C-11.2; a turn's failover is its conversation's, C-26.7; a resume or revive continues one native session, C-12.3) such an attempt steers the job. Its evidence records `capacity_steer`, the model id and `until`, `MODEL_CAPACITY_STEER_S` (900 s) after the provider exit recorded as the attempt's `finished_at`, retaining its fractional seconds (C-4.6). Classification, salvage, delayed finalization and replay never move that end; an already expired steer is not renewed. It sets no same-lane retry (`Daemon._retry_pin`), and the job is due at the next pass rather than 60 s on. Until `until`, each admission look reads the job's own live steers once (`Daemon._capacity_steer`, so the look's evaluation and its reservation's check consult the same recorded steers at their own clocks, C-6.3) and its route excludes those models while their steers are live (`scheduler.prepare`, C-11.2). The first admissible unsteered model in chain order takes the job. If none can, including a chain whose every model is steered, it waits as a capacity wait (C-6.10), with a recheck no later than the earliest live steer's end. When a steer ends its model is eligible again. The decision's reason names each such model (`<model>: at capacity for this job until <t>, skipped by capacity steer (C-9.5)`). The attempt is never success, counts toward `max_attempts`, and its partial output and salvage follow C-4.5 and C-13 as any `transient` attempt's; a second one on the same lane still excludes that lane (C-4.5). A job pinned to the model, or to a lane, keeps the same-lane retry after 60 s. Why the steer is the job's and not the fleet's, and why 15 minutes: the provider served most attempts of the model meanwhile (`docs/reports/2026-10-13-model-capacity-steer.md`). [owner unit, fake, e2e; module `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`, `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-9.6** A closure is (lane, scope, until, reason, clock source, source event, with an optional operator-hold note encoded in that source event). A new closure on the same lane and scope extends `until` but never shortens it. Holds and limits never share a row (2026-10-13, D-HOLD1): an `operator-hold` closure and an `auth-dead` closure each keep a row of their own, and a limit (`provider-limit`, `credits`, `cooldown`) never extends, replaces or releases a hold's row, nor a hold a limit's. So an operator hold is in force from `lanes hold` until its `until` or `lanes release`, whatever limit is reported on the lane meanwhile and whatever reset, reset credit (C-23.17) or `lanes release --limits` then releases that limit; and `lanes release` ends the hold alone, so a limit reported during it stays in force until it ends or is released. A lane is an admission candidate (C-11.2) only while no operator hold and no limit is in force on its account scope. Closures expire by clock; a later `provider` reading that shows the window reset can release a provider-limit closure. `subfleet lanes release <lane> --limits --note <text>` releases that lane's active, unreleased `provider-limit` and `credits` closures with `clock_source: guessed`, in one transaction with a `lane.limits_released` event recording the nonblank note, lane and affected closure ids. Provider-reported clocks are never released by hand; mixed clocks release only the guessed closures and report the ids of reported closures kept. When none are active, the command refuses as "no open provider-limit or credits closures"; reported clocks alone are refused as "reported clock; provider clocks cannot be released by hand". Expired/released closures, other lanes and other reasons are unchanged. Without `--limits`, release retains its operator-hold behavior. `subfleet lanes hold <lane> --until <iso> [--note <text>]` records the operator's reason (2026-10-24, D-STATUS-SRC1): with no note the hold's `source_event` is `operator`, as before; with one it is `operator: <note>`, and the `lane.held` event's data carries the applied `note` beside the applied `until` (no `note` key when the stored hold has none). The note is stripped and must then be one line of 1 to 160 printable characters (Python's `str.isprintable`: no newline, tab, control or separator character other than the space) that the C-23.14 scrubber leaves whole, read from its start or from just after any `-` or `_`, both as written and as JSON writes it (2026-10-25, review r1 of #263: a note that carries a credential is refused, not masked, as C-1.5 refuses such a request id and C-25.7 such a correlation id, since masking would change what the operator wrote; 2026-10-26, review r2: `status --json` and `status.json` write a letter outside ASCII as `\u00fc`, after which the scrubber's URL rule reads a password it does not read in `ühttps://u:<password>@host`); anything else is refused with exit 2 and reason `hold-note`, by the CLI before it asks the daemon and by the daemon before it writes, so no closure and no event is written. Each refusal names the rule the note broke, `--note must be one line of 1 to 160 printable characters` or `--note must not carry a credential (the C-23.14 scrubber would change it)`, and no refusal, log line, event or error detail contains the note. `lanes hold` is the only code that writes a hold (re-enrollment carries an open one to the new lane as stored). A hold's stored `source_event` is read strictly (2026-10-26, review r2 of #263): exactly `operator` carries no note, and exactly `operator: <note>` carries the note when this rule accepts it as it stands. Any other, which only a writer outside `lanes hold` can leave (`operator:<note>`, `Operator: <note>`, a bare token, a note the rule refuses, or one longer than 160 characters, which is withheld unread), reads as `[note withheld: it would carry a credential]` in status, `status --json` (whose `source_event` for that hold is `operator: ` and the same text), the fleet-empty body and the notice that body becomes, and in every other reader of closure rows: the `lanes` list, `readings`, `errors` and `lanes release --limits` answers, whose rows also gain `kind` and `note`, and the route decisions `why`, `run --dry-run` and `runs show` carry, where that hold's `source_event` is the only field changed. Historical pin evidence before D-RAMP-HOLD-LABEL omitted `source_event`: only in the pin adapter, an absent field means a hold without a note; a present invalid field still goes through the unchanged strict reader and is withheld. Holds on one lane and scope share one row, so a later hold that extends the row gives it its note, and one that ends no later leaves the row's end and note as they were. The `lane.held` event records that stored row's end and note inside the transaction, including on a non-extending refresh; it never records the requested values when they were not applied (2026-10-26, D-RAMP-HOLD-LABEL). The requested end is converted to UTC and truncated to whole seconds through `status_json.timestamp`, which the daemon uses for storage and the CLI uses before inferring `kept` from an older reply. The daemon refuses an `--until` at or before now with exit 2 and reason `hold-until-past`, before any closure or event write. Its answer carries the applied note and a `kept` flag when the call's end or note was not applied. The CLI prints the active stored closure the daemon returns, never one built from its arguments; on a kept refresh it says the stored until and note were kept and this call's were not applied. For an older #263 answer without `kept`, the CLI infers it from the returned row and uses that row's note instead of the old answer's requested-note echo, in text and JSON. If no active stored hold is returned it says the hold is not in force and exits non-zero. A CLI whose note an older daemon did not record (its answer has no `note` field) warns and exits 69, for text and JSON alike. Every text display of an operator hold uses `render._held_text` and reads `<home>: held by the operator[: <note>] (until <T> at the latest)`: status (its alerts and closures sections), `render.status` capacity, `lanes list`, the hold result, `brief`, delivered notices, a run's notice, pin notice and pin failure, `why`'s closure lines and rejected closure reasons (including the former raw `rejected codex-1: closed:account:<T>` form), the standing pin/retry hold lines in `why`, and both ordinary and pinned conversation waits. The home is the lane's home, else its credential reference, else its lane id when the recorded decision has no home. Conversation summaries inspect every applicable closure named by a lane's rejection labels, regardless of insertion order. They retain every hold's end and note separately from the earliest provider reset, and a lane with both kinds remains closed. The `held` wait kind is used when only operator holds block the lanes. Ordinary wait details and pinned-conversation refusals use canonical closure order; a pin refusal states every hold separately from provider closures with equal or different ends. Individual provider-limit, credits, cooldown and auth-dead descriptions retain their wording; aggregate closure ordering is canonical. The note never changes admission. The app label remains "Held" and the `status.json` verdict remains `held`, under D-STATUS-SRC1's precedence. No per-lane hold alert is raised. No reader compares a `source_event` with `operator`. The guard that checks this is syntactic only (`tests/unit/test_status_hold_source.py`, reviews r2 and r3 of #263). Over `subfleet/` and `tools/`, it fails on a comparison (`==`, `!=`, `in`, `not in`, `is`, `is not`) with the literal `operator` or a name or attribute `HOLD_SOURCE` on either side, or a set, tuple or list, or a one-argument `frozenset(…)`, `set(…)`, `tuple(…)` or `list(…)`, holding one, nesting included, whatever the other side; on a `match` case whose value pattern is one; and on SQL in a string or an f-string (read with each `operator` or `HOLD_SOURCE` it formats written in), in any letter case, where `source_event`, with an optional `)` after it (`lower(source_event)`), is followed by `=`, `==`, `!=`, `<>`, `IN`, `IS`, `IS NOT`, `LIKE` or `GLOB`, each optionally after `NOT`, then an optional `(`, optionally, any text without parentheses that ends in a comma (a list's earlier items), and the word in either quote or a `?` whose call has the word or `HOLD_SOURCE` among its other arguments; or where the word in either quote comes first, then `=`, `==`, `!=`, `<>` or `IS`, then `source_event` after nothing but letters, digits, `_` and `(` (`lower(source_event)`). Among what it does not see, each pinned by a test case: a comparison through `operator.eq`, `__eq__`, a dict lookup or a dict's keys (`in {"operator": 1}`); the word reached through `getattr`, a call (`hold_source(None)`, `"OPERATOR".lower()`), an f-string (`f"{HOLD_SOURCE}"`), a `+` concatenation (`"oper" + "ator"`) or a variable; a `?` in SQL first held in a variable; and in SQL, `COALESCE(source_event, …)` followed by the comparison, `BETWEEN`, `CASE source_event WHEN` and a named parameter (`:s`).
- **C-9.7** Codex windows are classified by duration (`window_minutes` 300 is `five_hour`, 10080 is `seven_day`, anything else keeps its minute count as the key), never by slot position.
- **C-9.8** Claude `rate_limit_event`: `unifiedWindows.<window>.utilization` is a fraction in [0, 1] and `resetsAt` is epoch seconds; `status: allowed` yields `provider` readings for the shared five-hour and weekly windows with scope `account`, and a scoped weekly Fable reading when `seven_day_overage_included` is present; over-cap utilization is stored as exhausted (1) with the original retained in evidence; `status: rejected` yields no utilization reading; it yields one `admission-observed` reading for the requested model whose `window` is the literal `admission` (the only value of `window` that is not a duration key), with `resetsAt` as the closure clock when present, and `errorCode` recorded. Named rejection types `seven_day_overage_included`, `seven_day_opus`, and `seven_day_sonnet` close their respective model scopes; shared and unknown limit types close the account. `overageStatus` is parsed independently and never treated as admission evidence. A structured `status: rejected` is the server's authoritative admission verdict and remains `limited` even beside rc 0 and a non-error success result; textual quotas and auth signatures cannot override verified success with a nonblank deliverable (C-9.2).

## 10. Lanes and credentials

- **C-9.9** The Claude usage sensor that costs no model turn is `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/usage`, read with the lane's own credential (source `oauth-usage`). It yields `provider` readings for the account windows `five_hour` and `seven_day` (scope `account`) and one `provider` reading per `limits[]` row of kind `weekly_scoped`, scope = the policy model id for the row's `scope.model.display_name`, window `seven_day`; utilization is normalised to a fraction. HTTP 429 yields no reading and the server's `Retry-After`, which the timer honours before asking that lane again; 401 is auth-dead; 403 is no-scope; anything else is unavailable and yields no reading. Reads are paced `reserve.usage_spacing_s` apart (3) because the endpoint penalises bursts (2026-09-06: fourteen back-to-back reads earned `Retry-After: 3035`). C-10.6 holds: the readings count only when the profile endpoint, asked with the same credential, binds the lane. The timer's periodic Claude probe is this read on idle and busy lanes, with pacing and Retry-After honoured in both cases; a model turn is spent only by the pre-launch probe (C-11.4) and keepalive. A Claude home lane's login lives in the config directory's `.credentials.json` or, on macOS, in the keychain item `Claude Code-credentials-<sha256(CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR)[:8]>` (observed 2026-09-06); its access token expires within hours and carries a refresh token the CLI renews when it runs. An access token past `expiresAt`, or a 401 on a home lane with a refresh token, is `expired-token`, never auth-dead: on an idle lane the timer spends one minimal turn under that home (C-23.47's heal, at most once per 20 minutes per credential epoch) and reads usage again. A busy lane runs no heal turn and leaves token renewal to its attempts (C-18.3).
- **C-9.10** Claude limit-reset cards and promotional credits are read, never spent. Every `claude_cards.interval_min` (360; the first read a minute after start) and on `subfleet cards --refresh`, the daemon reads every Claude Code login under `claude_cards.logins_dir` (`<state root>/logins`) on a worker of its own. The sensor's own requests are GETs: `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/profile`; `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/usage?cedar_ember=1&skip_spend=1`, paced as C-9.9 paces usage reads; and `https://api.anthropic.com/v1/code/promo/cloud_credit`. Each carries Claude Code's own User-Agent, `claude-cli/<installed version> (external, cli)`: asked with `claude-code/2.1.286` instead, the server answered the cards block `eligible: false, ineligible_reason: "surface"` with no grants (one comparison, 2026-10-05). A lane's setup token cannot read these (403, C-9.3), so the logins are read, not the lanes' credentials. A login is bound to every lane whose recorded identity its profile returned (C-10.6) and to every lane that recorded no identity whose label equals the login's folder name, shown as "by name"; before the login's own identity is known its folder name is matched against every lane. The name binding never records an identity; it decides whether a heal may be spent and whether a hold covers the login, which lanes are shown beside it, and which lane ids a declared plan end may be filed under. An expired login is healed by one minimal Claude Code turn under its folder (C-23.47: one Haiku turn, no MCP server, in a process group of its own, with no other credential in its environment; none starts once the daemon is stopping; the group is sent SIGTERM and two seconds later SIGKILL after 120 s or when the daemon stops, and is killed however the turn ends, after it exits or on an error while its output is read; its output is read as UTF-8, with any byte that is not UTF-8 replaced), at most once per `claude_cards.heal_interval_min` (60), and only when `claude_cards.heal` is true and the login backs at least one lane, enabled or not, none of them under an operator hold (read for every login, from the closures and from the `lane.held` and `lane.released` events): an account under an operator hold, or one that backs no lane, is never spent on. A stop also ends a read between its requests. A login the CLI says it cannot renew (its output names a refresh that could not be done) is `login-dead`, and no turn is spent on it until its stored login changes; a heal that could not run (timed out, no CLI, a stop, or a failure that names no login problem) is `unavailable` and is tried again. A `lapsed` or `login-dead` login is looked at again at most daily until its stored login changes. A 429 is waited out for the server's Retry-After or an hour, whichever is longer. The snapshot, `<state root>/claude-cards.json`, is published as C-8.1 publishes (mode 0600), after each login a heal was spent on as well as at the end of the pass, and keeps per login: a status (`ok`, `no-login`, `login-expired`, `login-dead`, `lapsed`, `no-scope`, `rate-limited`, `unavailable`, `held`); the plan (`organization_type`, `rate_limit_tier`, `subscription_status`, `billing_type`; `claude_free` or `canceled` is `lapsed`, either one on its own: per help article 17007452 a reset is no longer available once the account cancels, and the one scheduled cancellation observed read `active` in claude.ai's `subscription_details`); the `cedar_ember` cards, each with `resets_left`, `ends_at`, `usable_now` and `clears`; every dollar block with a positive `limit_dollars`, as a credit (the claude.ai frontend reads `iguana_necktie` as the claimed cloud-session credit, its expiry from the claim status's `expires_at` and failing that from the block's `resets_at`, and so does the sensor); the cloud credit's claim status, normalised as Claude Code normalises it, with when it was last read; and what was lost, one entry per card or credit, each recorded once with why: every card the last good read held unused and every credit with money left, `lapse` when a later profile read finds the plan lapsed (but `expired` for one whose end, as last seen, that read finds already past: past its end it was gone whatever the plan did), or `expired` when an `ok` read after its end, as that read gives the end, still shows it unused or unspent or no longer lists it (a read that is not `ok`, or whose cards block is missing or ineligible, records no card expiry). A login folder that now answers with another identity starts afresh: nothing of the previous account is shown for it, used for it or recorded lost, and that includes the previous claim status, which would otherwise date the new account's cloud credit. A read that fails keeps the last good cards, credits and claim status beside its status. An `ok` read whose cards block is missing or ineligible keeps the last block that listed cards and records itself beside it (`cards_unlisted`: when, the ineligible reason or a missing block, and when the cards were last listed); with no such block it keeps its own. Cards so kept are judged lost from the read that last listed them, as they are across failed reads, and their alerts say when they were last listed and which read listed none and why (a failed read since changes neither). `subfleet cards --help` says that a read, `--refresh`'s or the timer's, may spend a heal turn. `status`, `status.json` (`claude.cards`) and the alerts read the snapshot, and show nothing while `claude_cards.enabled` is false. `status` and `subfleet cards` say "no reset card" only for a login whose cards block lists none (a card that ended unused is listed), and their line for each warning names the card or credit it is about, a loss's included. An alert (`warn`) names an unused card ending within `claude_cards.warn_days` (5); an unused card, or a credit with money left, on a plan that is lapsing (a subscription status other than `active`, or an end declared in `<state root>/claude-plan-ends.json`, by login or lane, within `warn_days` and before the card or credit ends: none of the reads observed showed a cancellation scheduled for the end of a billing period, and claude.ai's `subscription_details`, which does, refuses OAuth tokens); money left on a credit that expires within `warn_days`; and an unexpired credit the account is eligible for and has not claimed, on a claim status read within two days. These are sent again daily and clear without a "recovered" notice. Each lost card or credit is alerted once, for `warn_days` after it was seen. Nothing in Subfleet redeems a card or claims a credit: no code calls `POST /api/organizations/{org}/reset_rate_limits` or a promotion's claim, and the heal's prompt is a fixed reply request. Spending a card is the operator's act: a card is worth more held for a model launch, and the Codex rule (one at a time, only for a waiting job) applies with more force.
- **C-10.1** A lane row is an immutable binding of provider, account key, credential reference, credential epoch, optional home, `owner` (`v1` or `v2`), and `desktop` flag. The credential reference is a keychain item name (`claude-quota-<email>`, as v1) or a home directory path; the store never holds a secret.
- **C-10.2** Enrolment probes before recording: Codex reads `auth.json`, refuses an API-key login and a free plan, and probes the usage endpoint; Claude runs one Haiku turn under the token with `--output-format stream-json --verbose` and reads the `rate_limit_event`. A re-enrollment runs only that turn through its process fence; the keychain reads that resolve a keychain-token credential run outside it.
- **C-10.3** A lane whose Claude identity (C-10.6) equals the identity of the desktop app's keychain credential is `desktop`. It is refused while Claude Code is using the desktop login, unless the job has `allow_desktop`, and is otherwise a candidate that sorts after every other lane (C-11.3) (2026-09-27, Max: excluding it "makes sense if we're using sf thru the cc app but not if thru the sf app"; until then it was never a candidate without `allow_desktop`). Claude Code uses the login while its own registry, `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`, has a row that is alive, is not a Subfleet run, and shows use. Alive: its pid is live and, where it records `procStart`, that process started then (`registry.validated`, against one process-table read; a stale row whose pid another process reuses is dead, and one without `procStart`, an older Claude Code, is alive on its pid). Not a Subfleet run: an `sdk-*` row is left out only when a live attempt owns its process (its process group is the attempt's `pgid`, or it is the attempt's child) or runs its session (`native_session_id`); the entrypoint alone proves nothing about the credential, so any other headless run counts like the Claude app's rows (`claude-desktop`) and a terminal's (`cli`). Use: a `busy` status, or a status time (`statusUpdatedAt`, else `updatedAt`) within `admission.desktop_recent_s` (1800) of now; a row with no status, or a status and no time to judge it by, is unknown and counts, whatever its start. A registry directory that cannot be listed is use, and so is each row file whose process is live but whose content cannot be read or whose `pid` is missing, not a number, or not the one the file is named for; an absent directory is none (`registry.listing`, `registry.desktop_login_in_use`). The daemon reads it at most every 2 s, never under the store lock, as part of the one registry read C-6.9's classes use (`Daemon._registry_read`: rows and answer are kept together, reused for 2 s after the read finished however long it took), keeps a read only if none that began later was kept meanwhile (two reads may overlap; the slower never replaces the newer, whatever either found), ages the answer from when that read began, and a reservation treats an answer older than `DESKTOP_IN_USE_MAX_AGE_S` (10 s) as use; and puts the answer on the desktop lane as `desktop_in_use`, a `scheduler.LANE_FACTS` field; a desktop lane without the field is in use. The reservation reads it again: the answer is refreshed off the lock before each reservation try, and `_route_rows` marks the desktop lane with it, so C-6.3's check judges that lane again when Claude Code became active (or idle) since the evaluation (review of the uncap plan: reusing the view's answer reserved the lane after Claude Code became active). The admission-probe reservation refuses the lane only while it is in use, reading the answer refreshed just before its transaction as the attempt reservation does. `status.json` judges the lane with the same signal (`Timers.desktop_in_use`). Each change, and the first answer after a start, is a `desktop.in_use` event (`in_use`, `was`, and the counts of `busy`, `recent`, `unknown` and `subfleet` rows that decided it), written by admission (at the start of the detached pass, and after each refresh a reservation or an admission probe places by, so a change a job was placed by is on record) and never by a read op (`status`, `why`, `lanes`), under a lock of its own: a read op never waits for that write (C-3.7). Timers (usage probes, keepalive, touch) still never use the desktop lane, and `status.json` marks it dispatchable only while it is not in use. The desktop identity comes from the profile endpoint using the keychain credential itself, re-read each probe cycle; `~/.claude.json` `oauthAccount` is a hint that `doctor` compares against the profile and reports when they disagree, never the authority. While the desktop identity cannot be verified, every lane whose label matches either the cached email or the last verified desktop identity is treated as `desktop`. For Codex, `~/.codex` is observed and never a lane, and a numbered lane that shares the app's account is `app_shadowed`: dispatchable, ordered like any other lane, and last for automatic reset credits (C-23.46).
- **C-10.4** A lane with `owner: v1` is never a candidate. Ownership changes only by `subfleet lanes transfer <lane> --to v2` (or `--to v1`), which records an event. For a Codex lane the transfer also relocates the home: `~/.codex-<n>` moves to `<state root>/lanes/<lane id>/` on `--to v2`, before ownership flips, and back on `--to v1`, after it flips, each in one rename. v1 discovers Codex homes by directory glob from every caller (CLI, gates, launch agents) and reads no roster, so the relocation is the fence and the roster entry is the record. The lane keeps its id: the credential is unchanged, only its path moved. A transfer to v2 is refused while v1's ledger shows a run live on that home; a transfer back is refused while a v2 attempt is live on the lane. A held lane (C-10.5) is refused either way, dry run or not, before anything is planned (`transfer-held`, exit 7, naming the lane and, unless it is a harness hook, its variable): it holds no credential either owner could use, and its row would write a refused variable into `lanes.json` (D-ENV2, review r5 of #222). A transfer of any other lane repairs each of that lane's `lanes.json` rows whose env-kind variable is refused, writing the store's own credential (its kind, variable or home, and epoch) in the row's own shape, dry run or not; it changes no other lane's row. So no transfer leaves one of its lane's rows that a store rebuilt from the file would refuse; another lane's refused row is left as it was, and startup and `--check` report it (review r1 of #248, P3-3; review r2, P3-1).
- **C-10.5** The credential value is resolved inside the adapter's launch builder and placed only in the child's environment. It is never logged, never written to `lane.log`, never in `manifest.json`. An env-kind credential names a daemon variable; it reaches only its own lane's launches, as that lane's token, never by its own name, and never one that some launch inherits without policy (the C-12.3 allowlist, a harness hook or a test session's name) or that selection reads to find a test session (`launch_env.SESSION_PROBE_NAMES`, `PYTEST_CURRENT_TEST` among them; review r5 of #222, P3-3). A `lanes.json` row naming such a variable for a lane the store does not hold is refused at `daemon start` and at daemon startup, exit 2, before anything is stored, naming the lane, `lanes.json` and the variable. A row naming one for a lane the store already holds never stops the daemon: seeding never stores it, so it is ignored, and the stored lane's own rule decides (review r5, P3-1). Its variable is no lane's credential: a harness hook it names is withheld from every launch, that lane's own included, exactly as a held lane's hook is (C-12.3; review r1 of #248, P3-1). A lane already in the store with such a variable (stored by an earlier release, or by a start before review r4 of #222 moved the check ahead of seeding) never stops the daemon: it is held, refused for every job as `credential-refused` (C-11.8). Both `daemon start` (on stderr) and the daemon (in `daemon.log`, at startup) give each held lane, and each lane with an ignored row, one warning naming the lane, where it came from, the variable and the fix, never a value. A fixed `lanes.json` row repairs a held lane at the next startup (C-1.3); with no row, or a refused one, it stays held. Doctor's lane-variable row names each held lane with the `lanes.json` edit that repairs it (C-17.8), and `lanes transfer` refuses one (C-10.4). `daemon start --check` lists each env-kind `lanes.json` row naming such a variable, by lane and variable, each printed as `daemon start` prints it, scrubbed (C-25.7), and exits 2 if it lists any or cannot read the file as a lane list, 0 if not; it reads `lanes.json` and nothing else, not the store, the policy, the daemon or any variable's value, so it cannot say which rows startup would refuse and which it would ignore (D-ENV2). Resolution refuses such a variable too (`credential-env-inherited`) before reading its value, the second line for every launch, probe, keepalive, heal and relaunch; enrolment takes no env-kind credential at all.
- **C-10.6** A Claude identity is the pair (`account_uuid`, `org_uuid`) returned by `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/profile` when requested with exactly the credential that produces the lane's usage reading. A usage reading is recorded only when the profile identity equals the lane's recorded identity. When the profile is unavailable, the reading is stored with evidence `identity-unverified` and no windows; when it differs, with evidence `identity-mismatch` and no windows, and the lane is not a candidate until an operator re-enrols it. A setup-token lane whose token lacks profile scope keeps the identity recorded at enrolment, and its stream readings carry evidence `identity-enrolled`. Incident: on 2026-09-05 v1 attributed the Axiom account's usage to RulesAtlas because it trusted `~/.claude.json`; the evidence is `~/axiom-eng-briefs/opus-expiry-20260905/identity-fix-live-result.json`.
- **C-10.7** A desktop credential and an enrolled inference-token lane are distinct lanes even when their labels match; identity, not email, joins two credentials to one account, and `doctor` reports two enabled lanes with one identity as a defect (C-23.45).
- **C-10.8** Codex provider launches (attempts, conversation turns, resumes, probes and reconstructed launch environments) set `SSL_CERT_FILE` to `providers.codex.ca_bundle`, default `/etc/ssl/cert.pem`, only when neither `SSL_CERT_FILE` nor `SSL_CERT_DIR` is present in the daemon's environment. Operator values, including empty values and both variables together, pass through unchanged. The policy accepts an absolute path or `null` (no default injection); an omitted setting uses the default. The bundle must be a readable regular file no larger than 4 MiB containing at least one parseable PEM certificate. Reads are capped and PEM blocks are extracted in linear time in the bytes read; an oversized or otherwise unusable bundle adds no variable, and the daemon logs the unusable path once. Each launch selects its CA additions once and reuses that selection for adapter inspection, guard preflight and the guardian; reconstruction after a daemon restart selects again. Claude launches receive no added CA variable. Tool commands started by Codex inherit the variable; the default system bundle supplies normal public trust roots and keeps certificate verification enabled, so this inheritance is harmless. A custom bundle is the operator's trust choice. Incident D-NF1, 2026-10-11: at the 19:00:31Z restart the daemon's environment gained `SSL_CERT_FILE=/etc/ssl/cert.pem`; by 19:40Z, 54 attempts started after the restart had 0 UnknownIssuer errors, while 4 of 36 older attempts still running logged 10 more. About 300 fresh probes had succeeded during the preceding lane failures. Intermittent native root loading is the inferred mechanism, not proven.

## 11. Routing evaluation

- **C-11.1** `policy.json` is the routing data. Required keys: `tiers`, `chains` (task to ordered tier entries, each a short model name or a non-empty preference list of distinct current short model names), `fallback: "upward-only"`, `permissions` (task to `read-only`, `workspace-write` or `worktree-write`; shipped `review: worktree-write`; a submit that names no sandbox, `sandbox: "policy"`, gets its task's entry, else `*`, else read-only, and an isolated review or gate round always reads only; `worktree-write` resolves to `workspace-write` only for a new committed git job worktree, and resolves from the start to `read-only` for in-place or non-git work and isolated reviews or gate rounds; this conditional authority is not a d261 downgrade. Explicit `-s workspace-write` outside a committed repository is still refused with the fix `-s read-only`, never downgraded; d261, 2026-09-24), `models` (short name to provider, id, optional effort and scope), `retired` (alias to short name), `desktop_login: "never"`, `caps` (C-6.4), `reset_credits` (`enabled`, shipped `false`, and `min_interval_min`; C-23.16; a `headroom_floor_pct` an older policy still names is checked and not read). Optional `network: {codex_workspace_write: bool}` (d260, default false in code, true in the shipped policy): a writable, non-isolated Codex job gets `-c sandbox_workspace_write.network_access=true`, and a Codex conversation turn under `bypass` gets `networkAccess: true` (under `ask` and `accept-edits` a network command still reaches the person as an approval); wherever the network is granted the launch also carries `-c features.unified_exec=false` (C-23.6), because unified exec's `write_stdin` feeds a shell text the never-rules guard never sees; read-only and isolated launches never get the network. The file's SHA-256 is the policy hash on every job. Optional `requires` maps known task names to lists of distinct requirements; only `"shell"` is accepted. Unknown tasks, unknown requirements, non-list values and duplicates are invalid policy data. Omission means no requirement; the shipped default sets no `requires`. A job resolves this requirement at submit, with explicit `needs_shell` true or false overriding its task, and stores the resolved boolean for its retries and recovery; a native resume inherits it unless explicitly overridden. Optional `env: {pass: [NAME, ...]}` deliberately permits exact parent variable names through daemon startup and lane/provider launch selection (C-12.3/4). The section must be an object and `pass`, when present, a list; each entry must match `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*` in ASCII. No globs, assignments or whitespace are accepted. Omission adds no names. Credential-shaped names are never implicitly permitted. Subfleet configuration uses C-12.3's exact names; mandatory launch removals take precedence over this policy. `env.pass` is for noncredential names: one that is an env-kind lane's credential reference is refused (exit 2, naming `env.pass[i]`, the variable, which is a name and not a secret, and each lane that names it, with `lanes.json` or `the store` for where that lane came from) wherever the lanes are known, by `daemon start` before it spawns and by the daemon at startup, before it seeds `lanes.json`. So is a `lanes.json` row of an env-kind lane the store does not hold whose variable some launch inherits without policy or the session probe reads (exit 2, naming `credential_ref`, the lane and the variable), before anything is stored; such a variable is no lane's credential reference, so `env.pass` may name it. A stored lane with such a variable is held, not refused, and such a row for a stored lane is ignored with a warning, not refused (C-10.5). The daemon reports either startup refusal on stderr, which is `daemon.log`, as its handler writes a record of it (C-25.7), and exits 2, with no traceback; `daemon start` prints its own refusal scrubbed the same way.
- **C-11.2** A chain entry is a short model name or a non-empty list of distinct known, non-retired short model names in preference order. Evaluation for a `--task`/`--tier` job flattens the entries from the tier's index upward, removes duplicates keeping the first occurrence, and walks that list in order. A model the job's own attempt found at capacity is excluded from that list while its steer lasts (C-9.5). The remaining models keep their order; an empty list waits for capacity, and expiry makes the model eligible again. Within a tier, earlier models are preferred: a later model is tried only when no lane of an earlier model admits the job. Order across tiers is unchanged, and `fallback: upward-only` never uses an entry below the job's tier. For each model, candidates are lanes of the model's provider that are enabled, `owner: v2`, not `desktop` while Claude Code uses the desktop login (unless allowed, C-10.3), not in the job's exclusions, not under an unexpired closure for scope `account` or the model id, and with a free slot (C-6.4; always, while no per-lane cap is set). The first model with a candidate wins. A `-m` pin evaluates one model and never falls back; `-a` or `-H` evaluates one lane. A pinned job evaluates one model, its `-m` model or else the first model of its task's chain from its tier, and so runs only on that model's provider. The pin is resolved to one lane id by `scheduler.resolve_lane` once, at submit, against the lanes admission evaluates named as the capacity view names them (each lane's row with its latest probe verdict merged in, as `Timers.enrich_view` merges it, so a Codex lane also answers to the email its usage probe reported, and keeps it through a probe that could not read the account; the same roster serves admission and recovery), and the job stores that lane id. The name as typed is kept in the `job.submitted` event (`pin.requested`, `pin.lane_id`) and, except for a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization, in the request digest; a retry of an accepted request whose name no longer resolves to one lane is answered from the lane it was accepted on, so a retry still finds its job (C-6.2). An authorized job's digest binds the lane id it was granted for (C-11.7a), so its retry after the name comes to name another lane is refused as a different payload. A lane id matches only itself, except that a disabled lane whose credential an enabled lane now holds (a re-enrolment, C-10.2: the same account on the same credential) resolves to that lane, so a job pinned before a re-enrolment runs on its successor, and attempts on a lane and on its successor are attempts on one lane when retries and exclusions are counted (C-4.5: one same-lane retry, then the next candidate), a resume included, whose launch accepts the successor of the lane it recorded because the native session lives under the credential's home, not under a lane id (C-12.3); a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization never follows, because the authorization names the lane id it was granted for (C-11.7). Any other name (an account key or its account part, a label, a Codex email, a home) may match several lanes, and narrowing drops only lanes that could never take the job: lanes of another provider than the job's, taken from the job's model or task, else from the flag that pinned it (`-a` a Claude account, `-H` a Codex home, sent as `pinned_provider`), a disabled lane when an enabled lane that also matches is bound to the same credential, and a lane whose credential proved to hold another account (C-10.6) when another lane of the same provider also matches (across providers the name stays ambiguous); such a lane keeps the email its probe read as `observed_email`, never as a name it answers to. A name given with `-a` or `-H` names a lane of that flag's provider only, so a job whose model runs on the other provider is refused at submit (exit 2), as v1 refused `-a` with a Codex model; a lane id says its own provider whatever flag carried it. A name that still matches several lanes is refused at submit with exit 2 naming the lane ids (`-a <lane id>` pins one). Resolving once means no later roster change (a probe reporting an email, a lane enrolled) can make an accepted pin ambiguous. A job accepted before this rule may still carry a name: each daemon start rewrites the pin of every unfinished job whose name resolves to one lane (`job.pin_canonicalized`, from and to; a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization is never rewritten) and logs one line for each it leaves. One that names several lanes is refused at admission (C-6.12). One that names no lane (`pinned lane ... is unknown`, C-17.3) is a pin no lane can admit: C-11.8 holds it as `unknown`, tells its caller, and fails it after the grace; `subfleet doctor` fails its `unfinished jobs pin lanes by id` row while any job carries such a pin; a repair that raises is logged and skipped, never holding up recovery. Exclusions (`-x`) are not narrowed: a name excludes every lane it matches, which errs toward not running. A route evaluation (`Daemon._pick`: admission and its check of a transient retry's pinned pair, `why`, `run --dry-run`) reads only the rows a route can depend on: the active attempts (`ROUTE_ATTEMPTS`, by the `attempts_live` index and without `evidence_json`) and the jobs that have a parent or an active attempt (`ROUTE_JOBS`), because only an active attempt occupies a slot or counts against a parent's cap, only a job with a parent extends an ancestry, and an active attempt's job says by its `kind` whether it counts as a turn (C-26.9); status and the operator's views still read every row. C-6.3's reservation recheck (`_route_rows`) takes its known jobs from these rows and reads any other job on an ancestry by id, a few primary-key lookups, and reaches the same answer as from every row (a differential property test). Over any store, `scheduler.evaluate` reaches the same decision over these rows as over every row (a differential property test over stores of detached and turn jobs with parent chains and cycles and attempts in every state, for detached and turn candidates; measured 2026-10-02 on `main` with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py --running 26 --history 2500 --waiting 12 --evidence-bytes 8192` on the live daemon's interpreter build, three alternating rounds: with every lane closed and 12 jobs rechecked, 8.9%, 8.3% and 8.8% of a core before, 1.5% in each round after; with 26 running, 24.0%, 19.0% and 21.5% before, 14.9%, 15.5% and 16.3% after). The opt-in shell requirement promises commands only, no writes (for tests use `-s workspace-write`). A job requiring a shell is a candidate only on a provider launch that offers command execution for its sandbox: Codex in either sandbox, Claude only in workspace-write (C-12.3–4). This guard is applied before ranking and in the reservation recheck. An unpinned job whose admissible shell-capable candidates are exhausted waits for capacity, never falls back to a launch without a shell; `why` names the requirement, for example `needs a shell: opus runs read-only jobs without one`. Later shell-capable entries remain eligible in chain order. A pin whose launch cannot meet the requirement is refused at submit with exit 7 and the fixes `-s workspace-write` or a Codex model.
- **C-11.3** Every comparator starts with the lane's `desktop` flag (false first, C-10.3), then for a turn `lane_id != affinity_lane` (false first, C-26.2), then load band `in_flight // admission.lane_spread` in the job's pool (default 2; null means band 0). The band spreads a burst before usage readings move (2026-09-27); it adds no cap or waiting. Claude next keeps its stranded term `not stranded_scopes` (false first, C-23.37); both providers then use `not ranking_measured` (false first), weekly reserve class, five-hour reserve class, binding weekly reset ascending, weekly headroom descending, in-flight ascending and lane id. A reserve class is true only when the corresponding known headroom is strictly below `admission.weekly_reserve` (default 0.02) or `admission.five_hour_reserve` (default 0.10); false sorts first. Both are finite fractions in [0, 1], preference only: every otherwise eligible lane stays eligible. Codex carries a five-hour window when its primary or secondary window has duration 300 minutes (C-9.7), so both providers receive the five-hour guard. Weekly headroom is `1 - utilization` of the least-headroom fresh applicable `seven_day` window (account or requested-model scope); its own reset binds too. Equal weekly headrooms bind the earliest known reset, then scope. A missing reset sorts after known resets within the same preceding keys and reserve class. If any latest applicable provider window observed no later than now and within `caps.reading_ttl_s` has a reset at or before now, the lane ranks as unmeasured until another reading arrives or that window ages past the TTL, with no invented utilization. Stopped windows outside the TTL cannot demote the lane indefinitely. This is ranking uncertainty only: admission freshness (`measured`), pre-launch probes and pick exclusions keep their existing predicates. Missing/stale readings remain eligible but unmeasured; weekly and five-hour headroom are unknown when unmeasured, reserves are false, reset unknown, then in-flight and lane id decide. Fresh means numeric provider evidence observed no later than now, at most `caps.reading_ttl_s` old (default 120 seconds), with any reset still future. Eligibility still uses the existing applicable-window utilization floor (`headroom_floor` default 0.15; the live policy remains 0.0), slots, closures and C-11.7 model reserve. C-11.7 slack no longer orders candidates. Defaults are justified by the seven-day replay in `docs/reports/2026-10-03-rank-earliest-reset.md`. A lane being proven (C-6.14) is no candidate for a detached job: the hold refuses it before this ranking, and the load band orders only what is left, so the band spreads work across the lanes that can take it but never stops the one lane above its floor taking every job, which the hold does; a proven lane rejoins at its band.
- **C-11.4** Before dispatching expensive work (writable, or tier `hard`) to an unmeasured lane, the daemon runs a probe with the model the job wants (C-10.2 style); a `limited` result closes the scope; `ok` records `admission-observed`. For other work the job's first attempt is the probe. A conversation turn never triggers a probe (C-26.9). A probe launch uses the fixed prompt `Reply with exactly OK. Do not use tools.` in a private read-only directory under the state root and carries `SUBFLEET_PROBE=1` in the child environment, so tooling and the fake providers can tell a probe from work. The probe's reservation re-checks, inside its transaction, that the lane is enabled, is not the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (C-10.3, the answer refreshed just before), and has no credential a timer found revoked or unusable since the evaluation (review of PR #72). A probe that ends `ok` proves its lane (C-6.14), as a keepalive or heal turn that ends `ok` does. A probe still running at its deadline (`admission.probe_deadline_s`, a whole number of seconds from 1 to 900, 180 s by default), counted from its reservation (the instant its lease is taken, not before the wait for the store's writer lock; review of #153), is stopped there and says so (`stopped: deadline` in its record and in the `probe.completed` evidence, however the probe completes: by its receipt, by recovery, or with an adapter that raised; a probe whose guardian was already gone at the deadline was stopped by nobody and says nothing; a look at the guardian that failed is no evidence that it is gone, C-5.3): what the adapter reads off a stream cut short is not the provider's own ending. On 2026-10-26, 86 of 174 gpt-6.1-sol probes and 28 of 134 claude-opus-5-5 probes ran to the 60 s deadline, and Sol's successes were clipped by it. On 2026-10-03, 23 of the 25 probes that read `unknown` or `transient` had run to the deadline; the `transient` ones read so only because Codex prints `failed to refresh available models: request timed out` at startup, as 60 of the 64 first Codex attempts of the jobs submitted in that window did, 53 of which succeeded (`docs/reports/2026-10-08-probe-vehicle-starvation.md`). After a probe of a model on a lane ends other than `ok` with a binding identity (C-10.6), `limited` or `auth-dead`, and was not quarantined, the job's next probe of that model goes to a lane of that model it has not had such an answer on this round: the first `evaluate` ranks once those lanes are left out (`Daemon._probe_choice`; in that decision they read `excluded`). A lane that needs no probe there is taken as it is; a model the chain promotes to is never taken this way, at the reservation either: if the check (C-6.3), with those lanes still out, walks on past the model rotation chose to a later one (`scheduler.promoted_past`; an earlier model of the chain is no promotion and is reserved), or chooses no lane where a lane left out was refused for that alone (`scheduler.left_out_only`), the job is not reserved: it is held `probe-pending` on C-6.10's clock and looks again when that is due (a verdict that holds whatever was left out, such as a full fleet, is recorded as any is; review of #154); when no other lane of the model would take the job, the round starts again; a lane pin has no other lane to go to. The same choice passes over a lane whose probe is an older job's turn (C-6.9), and a lane whose probe this look could not reserve (its `slot:0` held, the lane disabled, moved to v1 or the desktop's, or its credential latched since the evaluation), which the next look tries again (review of #153: a timer that took codex-1's slot between each evaluation and reservation kept a job from ever probing, while it held codex-2's turn). So a lane whose probes keep saying nothing costs a job one probe a round, not every probe, and every lane that would take a job is probed for it within one round, which bounds how long a job held behind it waits for a lane it could use. The round is held in memory per job, as C-6.10's records are, and a restart forgets it.
- **C-11.5** The decision record is JSON: chain walked, per-model candidate list with each rejected lane and its reason, readings and closures consulted, chosen lane and model, and the policy hash. It is stored per attempt and printed by `subfleet why <job>`; `subfleet run --why` and `--dry-run` print it without dispatching. Each candidate's explanation includes reserve class (clear, weekly, five-hour, weekly+five-hour, or unmeasured), binding weekly scope/reset/headroom, five-hour headroom and reading age in seconds. Judgement records a stable `reading_observed_at`; explanations derive age at the evaluation clock, rather than including a continuously changing age in the lane verdict. Age is the oldest fresh applicable provider window used for ranking; if none is fresh, the oldest latest applicable provider observation, or unknown with no reading. When the eventual model is unspecified, `pick` requires every configured model of the family to qualify and ranks the same aggregate weekly binding window and reserve flags it displays; the existing preceding keys use the first model's evaluation. `pick --all --json` retains each model's detail; text `pick` sends evidence to stderr, preserving its path/email stdout contract. The one-off `python -m subfleet.prune_decisions` pass may delete redundant attempt-less decisions only for terminal jobs. It preserves every attempt-linked decision, all unfinished-job decisions, the rows chosen by `why` and offline `runs show`, and the latest attempt-less rows under both reader orderings. It proves both readers' stored decision fields byte-identical for every job, including `why`'s evaluation timestamp. Full histories from `Store.list_decisions` and retention archives made after the pass intentionally contain fewer rows; every surviving row is unchanged. Existing archives remain readable and restorable. Before any DELETE the pass verifies that every table in the whole-store backup has the same row count and content digest as the planning snapshot, and verifies a backup of every deleted row. Row fingerprints retain column names and tag every cell with its SQLite storage class (NULL, INTEGER, REAL, TEXT, BLOB), preserving REAL bits and distinct TEXT/BLOB contents; planning, backup verification and the post-VACUUM check share this encoder. It holds the daemon's lock, refuses missing expected tables or a schema it would migrate or initialize, and applies no schema DDL before its baseline or backup. It proves that only decisions and append-only per-batch audit events changed; after VACUUM it also compares the complete post-delete event digest, including the new audit payload bytes (see `docs/migration.md`). The restore recipe the pass prints on failure, and `docs/migration.md` repeats, fences its shell block with a backtick run longer than any inside the block, and never shorter than three, so no backup path ends the block early (D-PRR1).
- **C-11.6** The observed 06:33 case (excluded desktop lane, Opus chain exhausted) must evaluate to Astra with the reason "opus: no candidate lanes after exclusions; promoted".

- **C-11.9** Human `subfleet status` and `status.json` show weekly quota projections per lane and scope: reported `used`, `projected_unused`, `rate_per_hour`, `resets_at`, and `basis` (`trend` or `rate unknown`). Use the nonnegative first-to-last slope over 24 hours of provider or stale-provider observations sharing lane, scope and reset; fewer than two observations or less than one hour of span means unknown rate and all remaining quota unused. For the forecast, advance usage along the trend from its observation to now, keeping a fixed trend stable as time passes; displayed `used` remains reported. Clamp unused to zero through reported remaining quota; omit expired windows. Human totals sum account windows once per lane, separately per provider, naming the last included reset and unknown rates. This is a projection from the provider's own readings, not a promise. History is bounded to 24 hours in the status snapshot and never changes admission. [owner unit; module `subfleet/quota_projection.py`, `subfleet/render.py`, `subfleet/status_json.py`]
- **C-11.10** (task holds, 2026-10-24, D-TASKHOLD1) An operator holds one task at admission and leaves every lane open. `subfleet tasks hold <task> --until <iso> --note <text>` holds task `<task>` until that instant; `subfleet tasks release <task>` ends the hold at once; `subfleet tasks` lists the holds in force (op `tasks`, `protocol.TasksArgs`, actions `list`, `hold`, `release`). `<task>` is a key of the policy's `chains`, the list submit and `scheduler.prepare` check a task against, else the request is refused `unknown-task` (exit 2); a release also takes a task the policy no longer routes while its hold's row is there. The note is required: no control character anywhere in it, its edges included (the C0 controls, newline and tab among them, DEL, the C1 controls, and U+2028 and U+2029, refused before anything is trimmed, so `"\nbooking\n"` is refused, not kept as `booking`), then one line of printable characters, at most 160 once surrounding ASCII spaces are dropped, and nothing C-23.14's scrubber would mask (`task-hold-note`); the CLI applies the same rule (`task_holds.note_text`) and refuses with exit 2 before it sends anything; `--until` is an ISO 8601 instant later than the clock read after the handler acquires the writer, kept to the whole second, one with no zone read as UTC, as `lanes hold` reads it (`task-hold-until`). A refused request writes nothing. A second hold on a task replaces the first, and the jobs the first kept waiting wait for the new end. Each hold and release samples its effective clock after acquiring the writer, then evaluates expiry and replacement in that transaction, with its append-only event (C-3.2): `task.held` with `task`, `until`, `note` and `replaced` (the end and note of the hold in force it replaced, else null), and `task.released` with `task`, the ended hold's `until` and `note`, and `woken`. A release with no row changes nothing and records nothing; a release of a hold already past its end still deletes its row and records `task.released`, gives each job no pass has woken since that end its clocks back from the end, and answers `was_held` false (`was_held` is a field of the release's response, not of the event). The hold's transaction also takes every job of the task that waits or is queued, but one waiting on a person (`approval`, `uncertain`) and one being cancelled, as a release gives them back in its own: each waits `task-held` from the commit, with its own `job.task_held` event, so its clocks stop at the commit whether or not a pass looks before the hold ends (review r2 of #260, F1). A job still `task-held` for an earlier hold that ended by its clock, with no pass since, is first given its clocks back from that end (a `job.task_hold_ended` event), then taken. While a hold on task T is in force, no attempt starts for a job whose task is T and whose kind is not `turn`: a first attempt, a retry, a resume (its source's task), a revive and a handoff alike (a gate round names no task, so no task hold holds one). Admission looks at the hold at the door, first in each job's look (before C-6.9's FIFO, the guards of C-6.13, C-6.17 and C-6.18, workspace preparation, routing and any probe), so a held job runs no git, carries no probe, registers as no waiter and queues for no lease: it holds no other job back and takes nothing another job could use. The door's transaction that takes a job or moves its hold clock re-reads the current task hold inside the writer (`Daemon._task_held_at_write`), including a release or replacement since its snapshot: it takes only the current hold, and writes no stale take if it has ended (review r4 of #260, R4-1). A job that comes to wait after the commit (one submitted since, one whose attempt has ended, one a person has answered) is taken there. It waits `task-held` with `next_check_at` the hold's end, and its hold (C-6.11) names the task, the end and the note; a job waiting on a person (`approval`, `uncertain`) keeps that wait, and is held once that wait is resolved. The reserving transaction (C-6.3) reads the hold again at its own clock, so a hold committed after a pass's door look still stops the reservation, and a pass in flight reads the holds again at its next job once a hold or release has committed. So does every other transaction of admission's that writes a job's wait, opens its C-11.8 episode or fails it on a clock (`Daemon._task_held_at_write`): the episode's opening, the failure when its grace has run out, its end when the lane can admit the job again, a retention fence's wait, a retry's pin let go, a probe's deferral or answer, a route or workspace deferral, and a door's look that finds an earlier hold ended, so a look already under way when a hold commits never writes over `task-held` (review r3 of #260, R3-1). If a hold covers the job, that transaction takes it for the hold instead, as the hold's commit would, and writes nothing of its own: an episode such a look opens opens all the same, at the look's clock, with its grace paused in the same transaction from the hold's `since` or the episode's start, whichever is later, and a job the commit took already whose episode then runs has that grace paused there, in a new `job.task_held` event that keeps what the first one kept. A probe the look ran that is quarantined still makes the job wait `uncertain`, held or not. That wait ends through a person cancelling the job or through automatic recovery when a verified-empty census completes the probe: recovery changes the job to `capacity`, and admission then takes the active task hold. `kill --confirm-dead` reads only quarantined attempts, so for a quarantined probe it answers `not quarantined` and leaves the wait `uncertain` (review r5 of #260, R5-4a). A hold is in force from its commit until its `until` or its release, by the clock: nothing reaps it, and a row past its end holds nothing. No clock of a held job runs while it is held (T6). The hold that takes a job keeps, in the job's `job.task_held` event, its C-4.5 retry deadline while that is still ahead (`kept`: while its latest attempt ended `transient` with no capacity steer, the deadline its finalizer wrote for the retry, `TRANSIENT_RETRY_DELAY_S` (60 s) after the finalizer ran, which may be long after the provider's exit, kept with that attempt as its evidence's `retry_due_at`, while that deadline is still to come; an attempt finalized before that field existed uses its own `attempt.accepted` event stamp plus `TRANSIENT_RETRY_DELAY_S`, not its provider exit or the job's rewritten `next_check_at` (review r4 of #260, R4-2). While the UTC wall clock runs forward, that fallback is never earlier than the deadline its finalizer wrote. It is later by the time that finalizer took from reading the deadline to committing, rounded up to the second, so a held legacy retry starts at most 60 s late. A clock stepped back between the two reads makes it earlier by the step (review r5 of #260, R5-4b); once the deadline has passed, the job's clock is a C-6.10 recheck, which is no deadline and is not kept, so the job is queued at the hold's end and capacity that frees up brings it forward as it would with no hold, review r2 of #260, F2) and, while a C-11.8 pin grace runs, the seconds of grace left at the hold's start (`pin_paused_s`: from the hold's `since`, or the episode's start if that is later); a replacement that still covers the task keeps its `since`, so the pause goes on (the replacement moves its jobs' `next_check_at` to its own end in its transaction; a pass that finds a held job's clock elsewhere while a hold still covers it moves it with a `job.task_hold_moved` event). Once a hold ends (by release; by its clock, at its `until` however late a pass, or a daemon started after it, notices; or by a replacement that ends sooner), each job it kept is given its clocks back in one transaction with a `job.task_hold_ended` event. An ending by the clock reads the job row's `next_check_at` inside that transaction and ends there, or at now if that clock is still ahead; given a pass's copied end (`ended_at`), it writes nothing unless the row still names that end. A release ends at its own instant, or at the row's clock if that passed unseen. Either way each hold ending is applied at most once, whatever a pass copied (review r5 of #260, R5-1): a paused grace runs again from the end with the seconds it had left (`pin_fail_at`), so C-11.8 fails the job only once that much more time has passed with its lane still unable to admit it; else a retry deadline still ahead is its next check, at `max(now, deadline)` (a hold that outlasts the backoff satisfies it and never extends it); else it is queued and looked at on the next pass. Time between two holds counts once against a grace, as time with no hold does, whether or not a pass saw the first one end, including when a hold commits between a pass's copy and its door writes (T6). A release does this in its own transaction and wakes the daemon's waiters. No job is cancelled, failed or lost because of a hold: its grace and its backoff are paused or kept, as above (a `kill` is the operator's own). Two things a hold does not stop, since it is no cancel: a workspace preparation already in flight when the hold lands runs to its end (C-4.1), and a held job keeps what any queued job holds (its row, and a workspace or lease already made or taken for it); a hold only keeps a new attempt from starting. Jobs of every other task, and jobs with no task, are placed exactly as with no hold; a conversation turn is never held (C-26.9); an attempt already reserved, starting, running or finalizing is never read, signalled or re-placed for a hold. Unlike a lane hold (C-9.6), a task hold closes no lane and is no closure.
