It raises no alert (C-18.4), counts toward no idle-admission record (C-6.11: it is not admission's to place), and is no demand for a reset credit (C-23.16, T7): the hold's commit takes the jobs that wait off their capacity waits, so for those neither `_reset_demand` nor the reset action's pending transaction, which then finds the job no longer waiting on capacity (`demand-gone`), counts one once the hold has committed, demand read before it included. A job of a held task still waits on capacity before a pass takes it when its attempt was live as the hold committed (a hold leaves a live attempt alone) and has since ended `transient`; for such a job `_reset_demand` reads the holds in force in the same snapshot as the capacity waits and leaves out every job a hold covers, and the pending transaction reads the job's hold again before it spends a credit (`demand-gone`, with `task_held`), each check the only one that stops it at some interleaving (review r3 of #260, R3-3: the timer's demand read between its copy of admission's waits and its snapshot, or between its last read and its pending transaction). Once the hold ends and a pass finds the job waiting on capacity again, it is demand again. A hold is one `leases` row, so it needs no schema change of its own (it was built at schema 7, and release/217's migrations 8 and 9 add only job and attempt columns; the retry deadline a take keeps is a key of an attempt's `evidence_json`, no column): key `task-hold:<task>`, holder `operator: <note>`, `acquired_at` when the task's hold was set (a replacement of a hold in force keeps it, so it is the hold's `since`), `expires_at` its end. No attempt, job or probe holds such a key; every lease reaper deletes by an attempt's, a job's, a probe's, retention's or a resume's holder, or by a key of its own, so none removes it; and admission's lease census (C-6.10) leaves these rows out, so a release frees no capacity wait early. Invariants T1 to T7, the admission audit and the mutant table: `docs/reports/2026-10-24-task-hold.md`. Tests: `tests/fake/test_task_hold_admission.py`, `tests/fake/test_task_hold_clocks.py`, `tests/fake/test_task_hold_reset_demand.py`, `tests/fake/test_task_hold_properties.py`, `tests/fake/test_task_hold_mutants.py`, `tests/unit/test_task_holds.py`.

## 12. Adapters

- **C-11.7** Reserve rule. A Claude Max account carries a shared weekly window (`seven_day`, all models) and a model-scoped weekly window for each reserved model (`reserve.models`, today `fable`). Use of the reserved model counts against both; use of any other model counts against the shared window only, so the shared window is what ends a week of the reserved model. For a job whose model is not reserved, a lane is a candidate only when fresh complete `oauth-usage` readings, or shared/scoped readings paired by event time and attempt from one `rate_limit_event`, give slack = (1 − shared utilization) − `reserve.cap_ratio` × (1 − reserved utilization) ≥ `reserve.min_slack`: a lane with no fresh paired measurement is rejected `reserve:<model>:unmeasured` unless an active provider-reported model-only exhaustion proves the reserved model cannot use capacity before its reset; that case requires a fresh supervised probe of the requested model and never overrides fresh measured reserve restrictions, a lane below the slack `reserve:<model>:reserved`, and a lane whose usage read shows no reserved window is free. Candidates that pass this model reserve follow C-11.3's weekly comparator; slack remains admission evidence and is not a ranking key. `cap_ratio` is the reserved bucket's size relative to the shared one; the payload does not state it, so it is set above any ratio the observed accounts allow (2.0 on 2026-09-06, from an account at 94 shared and 49 reserved, which bounds it below 1.92) and lowered only from measurement. Incident: 2026-09-05, Opus lanes on two accounts exhausted their shared window and ended their Fable week with half the Fable bucket unused.
- **C-11.7a** An operator may explicitly authorize only an unmeasured reserve verdict for one new dispatch using `--allow-unmeasured-reserve REASON` with both lane and model pinned. The daemon resolves the lane to its enrolled ID, persists the reason/evidence (nonblank, at most 2,000 characters), includes it in request idempotency and audit data, and requires a fresh same-model admission probe before reserving the work attempt, even for standard read-only jobs or previously observed admissions. The decision continues to call reserve capacity unmeasured. This exception never overrides measured reserve restrictions, account/model closures, identity or desktop protection, ownership, exclusions, or capacity caps; never rotates accounts or promotes models; and is not inherited by subsequent jobs or resumes. Dry-run remains side-effect free. An unsuccessful or identity-unbound probe cannot authorize work. Tests: `tests/unit/test_scheduler.py`, `tests/fake/test_reserve_authorization.py`.
- **C-11.8** A lane pin that can never admit its job holds nobody back, is said once to its caller, and ends after a grace. A lane refuses a job for a *standing* reason when no wait for capacity ends it: the job's own exclusions name it (`excluded`); it is the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (or that cannot be told) and the job lacks `--allow-desktop` (`desktop`, C-10.3); the job is a turn and the lane has its own config directory (`config-dir`, C-26.2); it is owned by v1 (`owner-v1`), disabled (`disabled`), or its credential proved to hold another account (`identity-mismatch`, C-10.6); it is an env-kind lane whose variable every launch inherits, which only a fixed `lanes.json` row and a restart end (`credential-refused`, C-10.5; the notice names the variable); its last probe found its credential revoked or missing, or, on a Codex lane, its token expired after the one heal its credential epoch allows had run (`credential-latched`, with `capacity.credential_gone`: a revoked epoch; `revoked`, `auth-revoked`, `no-auth`; or `expired-token` with `heal_spent`, which the timers' probe sets on a Codex lane when a `timer.heal` for its current epoch has run and the token is still expired, C-23.47), which only a new login or a re-enrolment ends; or it is closed for scope `account` or the job's model until more than `admission.pin_hold_far_s` from now (`closed:<scope>:<until>`; default seven days, the longest usage window Subfleet reads, `seven_day`: a closure that ends later is a hold, such as an operator's `lanes hold --until`, rather than a wait). Any other expired token latches the lane's slot too but may still come back with no one acting, so it is not standing: one no heal has been tried on yet, and one on a Claude home lane, whose heal the timers retry every 20 minutes (C-23.47). A job pinned to such a lane waits as for capacity and is neither told nor failed here; the timers' alerts already tell the operator of the expired token, with the login command (`alerts.py`). Nor are a slot, a reading, the floor, the reserve, or a nearer closure, which are capacity. A pin that names no lane in the roster is `unknown`. A decision carries no lane's credential, so a latch is judged on the lane's row (`scheduler.standing_refusals` takes it; `refused_for_good` is given the pass's lane rows), and `evaluate`'s decisions are exactly what they were. `scheduler.pin_unadmittable` resolves the pin and judges its lane exactly as `evaluate` does (`prepare`, `judge_lane`) and keeps only the standing reasons (`scheduler.standing_refusals`), so when it names one, `evaluate` over any view with the same lane rows, closures and latched credentials places the job nowhere, whatever the readings, attempts, probes and caps; and when it names none, the job is placed on its lane in the best capacity those facts allow (every count cap lifted, every lane measured idle, nothing in flight, every nearer closure ended, every expired token that may still heal healed). A pin whose route cannot be evaluated is C-6.12's, not this clause's. Admission asks it on every pass of every queued or waiting job with a lane pin, except one waiting on `approval` or `uncertain` (a person's to end), after a route wait's hold (C-6.12) and a turn's conversation hold (C-24.5), and before C-6.9's order, the machine guard (C-6.13), a workspace or a probe. It reads only the lane rows marked and merged as a view marks them (`capacity.mark_desktop`, `Timers.merge_lane`, as C-6.3's check does), the closures open now, and the latched credentials (`Daemon._pin_view`, read once per pass and only by a pass that needs it); whether Claude Code uses the desktop login is asked only when a lane is the desktop's, and the turn pass takes the last answer read (`_desktop_answer`, unknown reading as in use) rather than list the registry itself. A job so refused is held `pin-unadmittable` (C-6.11) and holds nobody back (C-6.9). The first pass that finds it begins an episode: one `job.pin_unadmittable` event (`lane_id`, `reasons`, `closures`, `since`, `fail_at`), the job `waiting` on `capacity` with `fail_at` as its `next_check_at` (30 s out when it never fails; admission looks at the job every pass whatever its clock), and one `daemon.log` warning. Once the episode has lasted `PIN_NOTICE_AFTER_S` (60 s), so that a state one pass saw (a re-enrolment between its two commits, a registry read that failed once) tells nobody anything false, the job gets its one notice, unless the store holds a `job.pin_noticed` event for it already that no withdrawal followed: a job that ran and came back, or outlived a restart, is never told twice. The notice is a `service_notices` row for its caller's session (else the policy's `alerts.operator_session`, as `ping` addresses one; with neither there is no one to tell, C-15.8, so no row is written, the `job.pin_noticed` event is recorded with no notice, the job is still told at most once, and `why` and `status.json` show its hold), recorded by a `job.pin_noticed` event naming it (id, session and creation time): `<job id>: waiting; its pinned lane <lane> can never admit it: <why>.`, then the fix (resubmit it unpinned, or pinned to another lane with `-a` or `-H`, then `subfleet kill <job id>`; or make the lane usable again: re-enable or re-enrol it, release its hold, or stop using the desktop login in Claude Code), when it fails, and that it holds no other job back. `notice.pending` names its job (`job_id`, from that event, matched by id, session and creation time together, because a service notice's id is reused once the newest row is deleted: it has no AUTOINCREMENT), so the session hooks surface it, also in a session Subfleet launched (C-15.2, C-26.13), as a message rather than a run's end (C-15.3); a ping, a nudge or an alert still names none, even one that got an old pin notice's id. A pin notice still `pending` (shown to nobody) is withdrawn, matched the same way, with a `job.pin_notice_withdrawn` event, in the same transaction as the job's terminal notice however the job ends (`Daemon._notice`), and when its episode ends because the lane can admit it again: it is no longer true, and one withdrawn unread does not count as the job's one notice. When a pass finds that the lane could admit it again (capacity allowing), the episode ends with a `job.pin_admittable` event and the job is due at once, not at the failure its episode had set. A job whose episode has lasted `admission.pin_grace_s` (default 1800 s; null never fails it and the notice says it waits until that changes; 0 fails it on the pass that finds it, and any grace under `PIN_NOTICE_AFTER_S` fails it before its service notice would go, since its terminal notice says it all; `fail_at` is computed from the one clock read that pass makes) fails with rc 3 (C-17.3): its terminal notice (C-15.1) reads `no lane: its pinned lane <lane> could never admit it from <since> on: <why>; fix: ...`, a `job.pin_refused` event carries the lane and the reasons, `why` prints that as `Refused at admission: ...`, and a worktree admission cut for the job and no attempt used is removed, as C-6.12's are. Never while an attempt of it is live or quarantined: it is held until that settles. A conversation turn is held and nothing more, with no notice and no failure: its message is the person's, and the conversation carries its end (C-26.12). The episode's clock survives a restart: the daemon reads each unfinished job's episode from those events when it opens the store. Why fail and not only notify: a notice that is not a job's end reaches a session only at its next prompt or start (C-15.2 layer 3), while the caller of a pinned job is usually an agent blocked in `subfleet wait` or armed by the PostToolUse hook (layers 1 and 2), which wake only on a terminal state (`waits.TERMINAL`); and a job that never started has no `max_wall_s` to end it, so it would otherwise wait for good. The grace lets a quick fix land first: the desktop login switched back or Claude Code closed, a lane re-enrolled (a pin follows a re-enrolment to its successor, C-11.2), a hold released. A job with no lane pin that every lane of every model of its chain refuses for a standing reason (a model whose provider has no lane enrolled is `no-lanes`) holds nobody back (C-6.9). If it has already made an attempt, every candidate permanently refusing it begins the same durable notice and `pin_grace_s` episode as a pinned job: after `PIN_NOTICE_AFTER_S` its caller receives one notice naming the refusal, and after the grace it fails with rc 3 and that reason. These retry episodes are checked on every pass before FIFO and routing, whether due or backed off, and are held `lanes-unadmittable` with `for_good` equal to their permanent refusal reasons. Their existing `job.pin_*` records additionally carry `all_lanes: true` and `lane_id: null`; their notice and terminal summary name every candidate instead of a pinned lane. A disabled lane, an auth-dead or unhealable credential, and a route with no supporting lane are permanent refusals. Operator holds and provider limits with a known reset never start this retry grace, however distant their reset; one candidate with only such a temporary refusal prevents an all-candidate permanent refusal. Once a candidate is usable again, including one waiting on a temporary hold or limit, the permanent-refusal episode ends and a later new refusal begins a new grace. A live capacity steer (C-9.5) removes models only from routing and current FIFO demand: both standing refusal and permanent retry grace judge the whole original chain, including steered models, with the attempt exclusions merged in. An empty steered chain alone starts no refusal episode. If every original candidate is permanently refused, the durable episode takes precedence over route evaluation and reservation checks, even when due or when such a check would move; the hold still carries `for_good` and keeps no slot. Its grace survives the steer ending and a daemon restart, and ends on candidate recovery. A candidate with only a timed hold or known provider reset prevents permanent retry grace, including when that candidate's model is steered away. Far timed holds may still be standing for FIFO under the earlier rule, so their due and route-moved empty decisions keep no slot without acquiring retry grace. A job with no attempt retains its existing admission hold and gets no new grace. For jobs that reach routing: `scheduler.refused_for_good` asks it of each decision with no lane, and `scheduler.unadmittable` of a job on its clock (once per model and job shape per pass), and its hold carries `for_good`. Incident: 2026-09-29 ~21:53Z, job 20260929-134236-salvage-r3-fix was submitted with `-a claude-9`; an account switch made claude-9 the desktop login; `subfleet why` said `no lane admits it (desktop)`, the job stayed queued with no word to its caller, and C-6.9's FIFO held 38 younger standard Opus jobs behind it while claude-7 was open. Review of 76c81f9c (2026-09-30): the one notice rested on memory a pass forgets while the job runs; an expired token was called "never"; the notice never reached a session Subfleet launched; grace 0 read the clock twice; a job no lane can admit kept a lease's place on its clock; and `notice.mark` never marked a service notice (C-15.3). Its re-review (fc6c146d): an expired token whose one heal was spent is standing (the fix for the first finding had exempted every expired token); a withdrawn notice's reused id labelled a later ping with the job; and an unread notice rode along with a job that ended some other way. Round 3 (b917c466): a Claude home lane's heal is retried every 20 minutes, so its expired token is not standing either, and only a Codex lane's spent heal is.
- **C-12.1** `subfleet/adapters/base.py` defines the interface; adapters return data and never touch the store. Methods: `enroll(credential) -> LaneInfo`, `probe(lane) -> list[Reading]`, `build_launch(job, attempt, lane, credential_env) -> Launch`, `classify(attempt_dir, exit_info) -> Outcome`, `attest(attempt_dir, launch) -> Attestation`, `deliverable(attempt_dir, launch) -> bytes | None`, `resume_launch(job, attempt, lane, prompt) -> Launch | None`.
- **C-12.2** A `Launch` is argv, env additions, cwd, and the paths for stdout, stderr, and any raw stream file, plus `native_session_id` when the adapter chose it up front (Claude `--session-id`).
- **C-12.3** Codex launch mirrors v1's `bin/subfleet-codex` argument construction: `codex exec --json -m <model id>`, effort via `-c model_reasoning_effort=<effort>` when the model has one, `--sandbox <read-only|workspace-write>`, `-c hooks=<guard override>` when the guard is armed, `--output-last-message <attempt dir>/last.md`, the prompt on stdin, `CODEX_HOME=<home>`, `CODEX_API_KEY` and `OPENAI_API_KEY` excluded by default from the environment. Every Codex launch that runs a model turn (`codex exec`, its resume, and the `app-server` a conversation turn runs) pins `-c features.shell_snapshot=false -c features.shell_snapshot_v2=false -c shell_environment_policy.experimental_use_profile=false` (`launch_env.CODEX_SHELL_CONFIG`, D-ENV3), so Codex replays no snapshot of the person's interactive shell startup (`~/.zshrc`) into a command. Codex still runs each command as a login shell, so `~/.zshenv`, `~/.zprofile` and `~/.zlogin` still run, and their exports still reach it. `--enable shell_snapshot` beats the pins wherever it stands (real codex 0.159.0), and so does a later `-c`/`--config` that sets one of these keys or a table holding it. No managed launch passes either. An isolated review's configuration carries the same keys. A read-only Codex launch has capability `commands`: commands run, but nothing can be written, temporary files included, so pytest cannot run. Workspace-write has capability `writable`. The review publish restriction in C-12.4 applies to Codex as well: a review's override carries C-14.2's publish hook, and a writable review launch whose override lacks it is refused (`codex-publish-hook-required`). The thread id is read from the `thread.started` event. `codex exec resume <thread id>` on the same home is the resume launch. Every launch selects the default names in `subfleet/launch_env.py` that are present in its parent, then adds exact policy `env.pass` names and its own explicit variables. Launch-owned values override parent values; mandatory launch removals are applied last and cannot be undone by `env.pass`. After them, no provider launch carries another vendor's credential (D-ENV3): a name holding a vendor's word (`OPENAI` or `CODEX` for Codex, `ANTHROPIC` or `CLAUDE` for Claude, in any case) and a credential word (below) is dropped from every launch of another provider, whatever `env.pass` or the launch's own additions say. A name holding both vendors' words is dropped from both. A provider's additions (`ProviderEnvironment.additions`) never hold another vendor's credential, the isolated review's metadata inspection selects as a Codex process, and a selection made without a provider keeps one only as the caller's own addition. Homes (`CODEX_HOME`, `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`) hold no credential word. The default starts with the twenty names observed in the working launchd daemon on 2026-10-14, plus individually named locale, certificate-directory, proxy and Subfleet configuration variables justified in the D-ENV1 report. Subfleet configuration is admitted by exact name only: `launch_env.CONFIG_NAMES` lists every `SUBFLEET_*` name the package reads, and a test fails when the package spells a `SUBFLEET_*` name that is neither listed, a launch/session marker (launch-owned) nor retired (read only to say it is ignored, never inherited). The test harness's hooks are admitted by their own prefixes, `SUBFLEET_FAKE_` and `SUBFLEET_E2E_`, and the exact name `SUBFLEET_LIVE`; no production code reads a name under them. A test session's own names (C-20.6: its three markers and each isolated store path in `home_paths.SAFE_HOME_PATHS` that is not configuration, `launch_env.TEST_SESSION_NAMES`) are admitted by exact name only while the parent carries the session marker and its session lives (a bare marker, with no session folder, counts), so every child a test launches stays isolated and expires with the session. Liveness is decided from the session probe's own names (`launch_env.SESSION_PROBE_NAMES`: the marker, the session folder and its identity, `PYTEST_CURRENT_TEST`), so no other parent value is read before its name is accepted; a `PYTEST_CURRENT_TEST` without the marker selects none of them, though `home_paths.under_pytest` still counts it as a test process wherever that only refuses more (review r4 of #222, P3-4). Otherwise none is selected, and `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` stays a lane's own addition. A name that leaves the parent between being listed and being read (another thread's `os.environ` pop) is skipped as absent. As a backstop, no name holding a credential word anywhere, in any case, is admitted, listed or not: `TOKEN`, `KEY`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD`, `PASSWD`, `PASSPHRASE`, `PASS`, `PWD`, `CREDENTIAL`, `CRED`, `AUTH`, `OAUTH`, `BEARER`, `COOKIE`, `JWT`, `PAT`, `PRIVKEY` and `WEBHOOK`, except the reviewed names a word matches by accident (`PATH`, `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, two harness paths and the test session's stand-in agent-secret path, `launch_env.CREDENTIAL_EXCEPTIONS`). Such a name passes only when `env.pass` names it exactly. Every variable an env-kind lane's credential names (C-10.5) is withheld from every launch, whether allowlisted, passed or present in the parent; a lane receives only its own, resolved into its credential. A held lane's variable, and that of a `lanes.json` row startup ignored (C-10.5), is no credential: it is withheld from every launch when it is a harness hook, which no production code reads, and otherwise left to the selection, since every launch needs HOME or PATH and a test's child its session's names. The daemon reads the ignored rows' hooks once, at startup, when it reads `lanes.json`, and adds them to the names its own launches and its timers' withhold (review r1 of #248, P3-1). A withheld name is rejected before anything reads its value. The probe's own names are read first, and none of them can be a lane's credential: each is a refused lane variable (C-10.5; review r5 of #222, P3-3). Other unknown names, including unknown `LC_*` names, and credentials are dropped unless named exactly in `env.pass` or supplied by the launch. This applies to detached jobs, resumes/revives, turns, probes, gate rounds, isolated metadata inspection, guardian exec, recovery, salvage/finalization helpers and the git calls of a review's uncommitted copy (C-6.6, whose scratch index is its one addition); local helpers receive the selected policy in a scoped operation/worker context and add only their own explicit variables. A local helper's scope carries the daemon's withheld names (the lanes' credential references and startup's ignored hooks, read at each selection) as well as its policy, so every helper subprocess the daemon starts withholds them, the isolated metadata inspection included, which the Codex adapter also selects with its own withheld names (review r2 of #248, P3-2). Git helper location, pathspec, and trace removals still take precedence. `daemon start` uses the same selection and reads the selected root's `env.pass`, and the daemon process alone also receives every env-kind lane's credential variable, named in `lanes.json` or the store (a held lane's is none); `daemon install` keeps its explicit plist environment. The guardian receives launch authorization by name only, filters again for each provider exec and keeps its package bootstrap separate from provider inheritance: a deliberately passed `PYTHONPATH` reaches the provider unchanged, and the guardian imports Subfleet from its package working directory without rewriting that value, and its settle setting never reaches the provider.
- **C-12.4** Claude launch: `claude -p --model <model id> --session-id <uuid4> --output-format stream-json --verbose`, permission flags per sandbox as v1 `bin/subfleet-claude` builds them, with the writable MCP restriction in C-12.9, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN=<token>` (or `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=<home>` for a lane home), `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` excluded by default from the environment, the prompt on stdin. `claude -p --resume <session id>` is the resume launch. The C-12.3 selection and explicit-authority rules also apply to every Claude launch: detached/resumed jobs, conversation turns, supervised and direct probes, enrollment, healing, metadata helpers and both guardian exec modes. Ambient credentials, session markers and unrecognized configuration names do not become launch additions. Claude Code itself adds its settings' `env` and a `CLAUDE_ENV_FILE`'s exports after the launch. A writable launch loads the user's settings, so names there reach its commands, and no launch selection removes them (D-ENV3). Every Claude launch that starts a model turn passes exactly one `--settings` whose `env` sets `CLAUDE_CODE_RETRY_WATCHDOG` to `0` (`LANE_SETTINGS_ENV`, d1031), and a writable review's publish hook shares that payload; the October 22 peer probe supports this pin against a user's settings file so C-4.5 can receive the lane's exit, while process-environment conflicts remain unverified. Existing managed-setting refusals for isolated reviews remain in force; those preflights inspect names only. A read-only Claude launch uses plan mode with `Read,Glob,Grep,WebSearch,WebFetch` (isolated reviews omit web tools), and offers no shell; a workspace-write launch offers a shell. Each attempt records `capability` (`none`, `commands`, `writable`) and its publish denial when its launch is built, never at reservation: an attempt that ends before launching (refused, cancelled, `reserved-no-launch`) records neither, its notice carries no capability line, and the last-24-hour status line does not count it (#221 review r1b, P3-2). That line counts the distinct jobs with a `none` or `commands` attempt reserved in the 24 hours up to now, read as a range on the indexed `reserved_at` in its stored spelling (P3-9). Claude read-only is `none`; either provider workspace-write is `writable`. Schema 9 migrates known historical `has_shell` values using the stored sandbox; unknown evidence stays unknown, and historical attempts never acquire an unproven publish denial. `runs show --json` carries the recorded capability of every attempt; `runs show`, completion notices and CLI wait summaries carry a sentence, with lane, sandbox and model, only for `none` and `commands` (design-r2 section 2): `none` says it could not run any command, and `commands` says nothing could be written, temp files included, so pytest could not run. A writable review says `publish: denied` alone, and a writable job that may publish (a build) carries no such line (#221 review r1b, P3-3). This evidence never changes the deliverable or its `-o` export. A job with task `review`, and every `gate-review` round, denies publishing regardless of sandbox, including resumes, in three layers. (1) A command rule (`subfleet/publish_rule.py`) runs in each provider's pre-execution hook on the command text before any shell starts, so zsh startup that reorders PATH (`~/.zshenv`, `~/.zprofile`, `/etc/zprofile`'s `path_helper`, Claude's shell snapshot; #221 review r1b P1) cannot route around it. A writable Claude review launch gets it through `--settings` as a PreToolUse hook with no matcher (`<attempt dir>/publish-bin/review-publish-hook`), with `--setting-sources user`, so the reviewed tree's `.claude/settings.json` and `settings.local.json` cannot add hooks or set `disableAllHooks` while the user's own settings (the never-rules guard's hook) still load; Claude Code enables the `--settings` source whatever `--setting-sources` names (2.1.284's own source selection adds it to every set, and `docs/reference/claude-help.txt` says "--settings still apply" under `--restricted`), which no Claude run here has exercised (#221 review r2 P3-6), and `subfleet hook PreToolUse` applies it wherever the launch marker `SUBFLEET_PUBLISH_DENIED=1` is set; a Codex review launch gets it as C-14.2's publish hook (`<state root>/publish-guard/review-publish-hook`). Before a publish-denied launch starts, the daemon feeds each publish hook that launch's argv installs a PreToolUse payload for `gh pr comment 1 --body x`, in the launch's environment with its C-5.1 markers, and refuses the launch (`publish-hook-self-test`) unless every one answers with the deny JSON, since a hook that exits without JSON lets the call through (`docs/reference/claude-hooks.md` section 3). A pass is cached for the daemon's run, keyed by the hook command and the SHA-256 of the script, its interpreter and the modules it imports; each file's digest is itself cached by the file's identity (real path, device, inode, size, mtime and ctime), so an in-place rewrite is hashed again even when its size and mtime are put back; a failure is tried again at the next launch. The verdicts ride in `launch.json` (`publish_hook_self_test`) (#221 review r2 P3-7). A read-only Claude launch has no shell and its `--safe-mode` turns hooks off, so it gets none. Where it can name the command, the rule judges `gh` fail closed: it refuses every call that is not a read (`pr view|diff|checks|list|ls|status`, `issue view|list|ls|status`, `run view|list|ls|watch`, `workflow view|list|ls`, `repo view`, `release view|list|ls`, `label list|ls`, `search`, `--version`, `version`, `auth status` with only `-a`/`--active` and `-h`/`--hostname`, and `api` that reads; `ls` is gh's own alias for `list`), and `git push` (`push`, `send-pack`, `http-push`, or a `-c` alias for one). A read may carry gh's `-R`/`--repo`/`--hostname` flags, each with its value, before its command and before its verb, since gh reads `gh pr -R o/r view 7` as `pr view` (#221 review r3c P3); any other flag there makes it no read. `gh api` is judged as gh's flag parser (pflag) reads it: a boolean shorthand may lead a cluster and a value shorthand takes the rest of its word or the next word, so `-HX-GitHub-Api-Version:…` and `-q.foo` are values while `-iXPOST` is `-i -X POST`, and the last method given wins. It reads only when that method is GET (in any case), given with `-X`/`--method`, or implied when no `-f`, `-F`, `--raw-field` or `--field` is given: with an explicit GET, gh sends the fields in the query string (real gh 2.100.0 against a closed localhost port, #221 review r2 P3-5). `--input`, `graphql`, `--` and a flag gh does not define are refused. It looks through wrappers (`env`, including `env -S` and `--split-string`, with env's options read as its getopt reads them, so `-S` counts inside a cluster (`-vS`, `-iS'…'`) and a value letter takes the rest of its word or the next word (`-uNAME`), and GNU env's long options by any unambiguous prefix; `command`, `exec`, `xargs`, `sudo`, `timeout`, `nice`, `time`, `nohup`, and zsh's `-`, `noglob` and `repeat N`), leading redirections, every shell it names (`sh`, `bash`, `zsh`, `dash`, `ksh`, `mksh`, `fish`, `csh`, `tcsh`) given `-c` anywhere in an option cluster (`-ec`, `-fc`, `-cf`; a cluster's `o` or `O` is read both as taking the next word, as bash and sh read it in `-euo pipefail -c`, and as taking none, as this host's ksh ran `-oc 'cmd'` and zsh reads `-O`; the C shells have no `o`; fish, not on this host, is read as carrying a script in any option word holding `c` or `C`, attached or in the next word, and in a long option's value) (#221 review r4 P2-2), `eval`, command and process substitution (quoted or not, in unquoted heredocs, and zsh's `=(…)`), `find -exec`, GNU `parallel` (its template and arguments, or its standard input), `watch`, shell input (a pipe, a heredoc, a here-string `<<<`, a process substitution given to a shell, `source` or `.`, and `xargs` into a shell), and command words known only at run time (a parameter, a substitution, a glob or a brace word), which it judges as gh when the words after them are a read or reach one of gh's commands, whatever flags come first (`$(command -v gh) -R o/r pr comment`, `{g,}h pr new`). Such a word is judged as gh whatever program it names, so `$HOME/.local/bin/uv run pytest` and `"$GIT" status` are refused as `gh run` and `gh status` calls; spelled out (`uv run`, `git status`) they pass. gh's command and subcommand must be spelled literally on every path (#221 review r4 P2-1): wherever the command word is gh, a word known only at run time (a parameter, a command or process substitution, a word-splitting expansion such as `${=a}` or `$=a`, a glob or brace word, or an arithmetic expansion) as gh's command, as its subcommand when the command has subcommands, or as a flag or a flag's value before either, makes the call no read, and so do arguments that come from `xargs` (`xargs`, `xargs -0`, `xargs -I{}`, with or without a wrapper or a runner the rule does not know, before or after `xargs`) or GNU `parallel` (its `:::` lists or its standard input), a shell script or `eval` text that `xargs` runs included; each is refused with the `publish` wording, or the `credential` wording when the literal words already print the token. So `gh $v comment`, `arch -arm64 gh "$v" comment`, `arch -arm64 gh ${=a}`, `… | xargs arch -arm64 gh` and `gh -R "$REPO" pr view 7` are refused, while `gh pr view "$n"`, `gh pr view 7 -R "$REPO"` and `gh api "repos/$r/pulls"` pass: after a command without subcommands, after the subcommand, or after a first word gh does not know as a command, the verb is settled. `xargs gh pr view` and `parallel gh pr view ::: 1` are refused too, since their words are not in the text. A run-time command word that may be gh (it names gh itself, as `"$GH"`, `${GH:-gh}` or `$(command -v gh)` do; it reads a variable the text sets to a value naming gh, with an assignment or a `for` loop; it is a glob or brace word whose last part matches gh and spells g or h, as `g?`, `{g,}h` and `g[h]` do; or, as the command word itself, it is only parameters while the text names gh) is judged the same way, so `{g,}h $v comment` is refused; one that cannot be gh is not (`"$PY" "$script"` passes). A program that is a text tool (`NOT_SHELL_VALUES`: `grep gh "$f"`, `xargs grep gh`) gets neither addition. An `env -S` value holding a backslash or `${` is refused: there env applies its own escapes (macOS env: `\_` separates words, `\c` ends the string, `\t` is a tab) and substitutes `${NAME}` from its own environment, which the rule does not model (#221 review r4 P2-2). A command word, or text given to `eval` or a shell, that is only parameters (`$c`, `${=c}`, `"$c"`, `"$@"`) is refused when the command text names gh anywhere, quotes and backslashes removed. Every command name the rule compares (gh, git, the shells, `eval`, the wrappers, `find`, the copiers) and every word it reads as naming gh is compared in any case: macOS's default volume is case-insensitive, so zsh and bash ran the recording gh for `GH pr comment 7` (D-SH1 round 9). Where it cannot name the command (a runner it does not know, such as `arch`, `lockf`, `taskpolicy` or `uv run`; a shell function; a value git runs), a fallback judges each word equal to `gh` or ending in `/gh` (after quote removal, without a leading `!` or `=`) as the start of a gh call, a run-time word followed by a gh command the same way, and a word naming git as the start of a git call; a word naming a shell, `env` or `xargs` there starts a command judged as the direct path judges it, its `-c` in any cluster and its standard input included (`arch -arm64 bash -ec '…'`, `… | arch -arm64 tcsh -f`, `arch -arm64 env -vS '…'`), except among gh's own arguments (`gh run watch`) and among the words of a program that only prints, finds or inspects them (`echo`, `printf`, `which`, `type`, `file`, `ls`, `stat`, `man`, `cat`, `head`, `tail`, `wc` and their kin), so `which bash` starts no shell. Assignment values (`GIT_EDITOR='gh …'`), the values of `-c`, `-e` and `--exec` (except for text searchers and other languages' interpreters, whose values are patterns or code), and git config values (`git -c name=value`, `git config <name> <value>`, a `!` alias) are read as shell text. Once a call the fallback finds, or the words after a run-time word, reach one of gh's commands as gh's own parser (cobra) finds them, the call gets the direct path's judgment: refused unless it is a read, so one predicate decides gh on every path (#221 review r3c P2). gh's parser takes the command, then the subcommand, as the first word that is not a flag or a flag's value, where a flag without `=` takes the next word as its value unless gh reads it as a switch there (`--help`, and `--version` before the command); `--` ends the search. So the calls judged include gh's built-in aliases (`pr new`, `issue new`, `release new`, `repo new`, `gist new`, `secret remove`, `variable remove`, `extension uninstall`, `skill add`, `skill show`, `pr co`, `ls`, and the command aliases `cs`, `ext`, `extensions`, `rs`, `at`, `agent`, `agents`, `agent-tasks` and `skills`, as each command's and subcommand's own `--help` lists them in gh 2.100.0), `co` (the alias gh writes into a new config), cobra's `help`, the hidden commands found by name (`credits`, `repo credits`, `repo garden`, `auth git-credential`), and any flag before the command or its verb (`gh pr -R o/r comment 7`, `gh --body x pr comment 7`, which real gh runs as `pr comment`). The fallback also refuses a push. A first word gh does not know as a command is not judged there, so `grep gh file`, `echo gh` and reads pass. Copying gh under another name is refused: `ln`, `cp`, `install`, `mv`, `rsync` or `ditto` given a word naming gh (or a run-time word when the text names gh), `dd if=…/gh`, `cat …/gh` into a file, and a git config value naming gh. A call that would print the GitHub credential (`gh auth token`; `gh auth git-credential`, git's credential helper, which printed `password=<the dummy token>` for real gh 2.100.0; `gh auth status` with `-t`, `--show-token` or any flag beyond its reads; `--show-token` on any call), whatever flags come before its subcommand (`gh auth -h h token`), is refused on every path, with its own reason (`credential`) and its own deny text (#221 review r2 P2 item 4, r3c P2). Text it cannot tokenize is refused when it names gh or git push. (2) The launch environment carries an empty `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL` allowlist and a push URL rewrite to a refused transport, given in `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS` so that no name matches Codex's default `*KEY*`, `*SECRET*` or `*TOKEN*` shell-environment excludes (#221 review r1b P3-1); both survive shell startup and also cover explicit remote push URLs and absolute git binaries. Every review launch pins both, with the launch marker: a Codex launch sets them again with `-c shell_environment_policy.set.<name>=…`, which Codex applies after the lane's `inherit` and excludes, so a lane whose `shell_environment_policy.inherit` is `"core"` keeps them (real `codex sandbox` 0.159.0 pushed to a local bare repo without the pins and refused with them, #221 review r2 P3-3); a Claude launch carries them in its environment. (3) Job-scoped git/gh wrappers on PATH apply the same rule wherever nothing reorders PATH. Unknown gh verbs, aliases and extensions fail closed. Local Git reads work; remote Git transports, fetch included, are disabled in these launches, which D-SH1 round 6 accepts knowingly: a review reads the checkout and history it was given. The same denial means a review cannot install a git-sourced dependency (`uv pip install "pkg @ git+https://…"` fails with `transport 'https' not allowed`, and a policy review's worktree has no caller `.venv`), which round 7 accepts knowingly too (#221 review r2 P3-4). Explicit host-push requests are refused for reviews. The recorded review notice says `publish: denied`. These are launch command restrictions, not an adversarial OS boundary. Exactly this remains outside the rule (#221 review r2 P2 item 5): code in another language (`python -c`, `perl -e`, `node -e`, or `sed`'s and `awk`'s own commands); a script on disk, however it is run (`sh p.sh`, `source p.sh`, `bash < p.sh`, `make`); git that discards its environment where the rule cannot see the push (from another language or a script on disk); a raw HTTPS call with a token found on disk; a gh binary given another name outside the command text, or by a tool the rule does not name; a gh alias or extension name, which gh keeps in its own config, reached through a runner the rule does not know or after a run-time word, since there the rule judges only calls that reach one of gh's own commands, or whose command is known only at run time, or that xargs feeds; a command assembled at run time from pieces that never spell gh and never put a gh verb after a run-time word (`a=$(printf 'g%s' h); b='pr comment 7'; eval "$a $b"`), or that name gh only through a variable set some other way (`read`, a file) or a glob naming neither letter of gh (`/opt/homebrew/bin/??`); `gh api` given its method or fields by a run-time word after `api`, where the verb is settled (`gh api ${=a}`); and a zsh alias, which zsh does not expand in these shells (`alias g=gh; g pr comment` ran no gh in any of the five command paths, #221 review r2). The byte-for-byte never-rules hook (C-14.1) is unchanged. The pinned `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL`, `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS` and `SUBFLEET_PUBLISH_DENIED` are the launch's own additions (C-12.3): they are added after the default selection, so neither the allowlist nor its credential-word backstop withholds them, and none holds a credential word or matches one of Codex's default excludes; the guardian receives them by name (`guardian_pass_args`) and passes them to the provider with the launch's values. A parent's own values of those names reach no launch, a build included, and `launch_env.LAUNCH_NAMES` lists the marker as launch-owned (D-SH1 round 8).
- **C-12.5** Attestation: Codex from the rollout's model field; Claude from the transcript located by session uuid under `~/.claude/projects/`, per-message `model` within the attempt's recorded byte range, exactly one transcript match required. Result is `attested`, `mismatch` (with served model), or `unattested`; never a false positive.
- **C-12.6** Deliverable: Codex from `last.md` when nonempty under its byte check, else the last `item.completed` agent message in the JSON stream. Claude from the transcript's final assistant text within the attempt's range (v1 `prefer_transcript_text` rule), else the stream's `result` text. Finalization first captures the stream on a fresh inode under C-4.3, so later writes through provider aliases or unlinked writer descriptors cannot change extracted text or acceptance. A missing or invalid frozen copy invalidates cached extraction together with classification; both run from scratch against a new capture. A missing, empty or whitespace-only deliverable with rc 0 is class `unknown`, not `ok`, unless CLI/provider failure evidence gives it another failure class (C-9.2) or a detached Codex turn lacks `turn.completed` (then C-9.2's incomplete-turn classification applies). Both adapters enforce this before returning `ok`; finalization independently rechecks captured content, including a cached `ok` on replay or restart, and retains the adapter verdict as `provider_verdict` if it overrides it. Nonblank means `bytes.strip()` is nonempty for Codex (ASCII whitespace), and `str.strip()` is nonempty for Claude (Unicode whitespace, decoding captured UTF-8 with replacement); these are the same boundaries the fresh adapters use. Accepted payload bytes are preserved. A cached blank `ok` follows the empty-result rule: class `unknown`, detail `empty deliverable with rc 0`, no acceptance or export; a one-attempt job becomes `failed`. Deliverable nonemptiness matters for acceptance; its wording never supplies provider failure evidence. Extracted text from a non-`ok` attempt is retained only as that attempt's artifact: it is never accepted as the job's deliverable or exported to `-o`; a retry reads its own attempt directory and only its accepted attempt may supply the result. `runs show` never prints unaccepted artifact text: the bare form keeps C-17.1's shape, with nothing after `--- out.md ---` when no result was accepted, names the unaccepted evidence paths on stderr, and returns 1 for a failed, cancelled or lost job without a result. `runs show --out` with no accepted attempt emits no stdout, reports `no deliverable recorded` on stderr and returns 1. A deliverable recorded only for another attempt is never the result, and then neither is an existing `-o` file; an accepted job with no deliverable recorded for any attempt (a v1 import that kept only its export) shows its `-o` export (C-8.3) unless that export failed. The 2026-10-12 reboot incident in C-9.2 confirms that an interim `item.completed` agent message is not proof that the turn completed.
- **C-12.7** Fixtures: `tests/fixtures/<provider>/<case>/` with `stdout`, `stderr`, `rc`, and `expected.json`; cases at minimum: success, hard limit with clock, hard limit without clock, model-scoped limit, credits rejection (the experiment-0 Fable payload), auth dead, refresh-token revoked, CLI too old, content filter, stream disconnect, model downgrade (Claude), spawn failure. Fixtures are redacted: no tokens, no cookies, no `Authorization` values.
- **C-12.8** Fake providers `tests/bin/codex` and `tests/bin/claude` are Python scripts that read `SUBFLEET_FAKE_SCENARIO` and replay the matching fixture, honour `SUBFLEET_FAKE_DELAY_S`, and for the `nested-setsid` scenario spawn a `setsid` grandchild that sleeps 30 s.
- **C-12.9** Writable detached Claude jobs start with `--dangerously-skip-permissions --strict-mcp-config --mcp-config <config>`; the config is `{"mcpServers":{}}` unless that job names servers with repeatable `subfleet run --mcp NAME`. The daemon resolves only those names from the sources Claude Code would read at the submitted workdir, using its scope precedence ([verified sources and launch audit](reference/claude-mcp.md)); an unknown name is refused at submit with exit 2 and the available names. The job records its opt-in names and a copy of exactly their entries; `runs show` displays the names, and every attempt and resume starts exactly that set from the job's copy. A resume inherits its source's set and cannot change it. A missing or damaged copy refuses launch rather than falling back to ambient configuration. No policy default opts a job into servers, and a job that names servers must route to Claude. Read-only launches, probes, and isolated reviews keep their existing empty MCP configuration and refuse an opt-in. Subfleet.app conversation turns are outside this ruling: their existing permission and MCP behavior is unchanged, including the read-only turn flags.
- **C-12.10** (D-TMP1) Before a provider starts, allocate its own mode-0700, user-owned directory under `confstr(CS_DARWIN_USER_TEMP_DIR)` on Darwin, never under the state root. Its name is `subfleet-attempt-` plus a path-safe attempt id and an exclusive random suffix. The name is written to the state root's journal (`tmpdir-intents/`, whose own entry in the state root is fsynced when it is first made) before the directory is made, and leaves it once the directory's marker is published. If anything fails once the directory exists, the directory, still empty, is removed and the allocation fails as before; one that cannot be removed is logged by name and keeps its journal entry, so the sweep removes it. Replace only `TMPDIR` in its environment, as the folder with one trailing slash as the platform spells its own, so `"${TMPDIR}name"` is inside it; the same value reaches guard preflight, the guardian, the provider and its children, for retries, resumes, conversation turns, capacity probes and enrollment. The store is the allocation's record: the attempt's evidence (a probe's or enrollment's probe record) holds the path, the holder, the daemon's creation time and the folder's device, inode and owner. An attempt has one allocation however many launch tries it takes; each try reuses the folder its evidence names, so the evidence names the folder the provider was given. An allocation that fails (`ENOSPC`, `EACCES`) ends the attempt as a launch failure, `attempt TMPDIR could not be allocated: <class>: [Errno N] <text>`, never an attempt left reserved. Codex workspace-write derives a writable root from its child environment's TMPDIR, so a Codex provider may write its attempt's TMPDIR, its working directory and `/private/tmp`, and no longer the rest of the user temp root; writable Claude launches include the TMPDIR with `--add-dir`, preserving their permission modes. On a Codex lane, a brief's scratch under either spelling below lasts one attempt. Inside Codex's sandbox, `getconf DARWIN_USER_TEMP_DIR` names the attempt's TMPDIR, so `$(getconf DARWIN_USER_TEMP_DIR)sf-<tag>` and `${TMPDIR%/}/sf-<tag>` are the same private folder (observed with Codex 0.159.0, review r2 of #246), and a retry starts with neither; a brief that resumes from a scratch journal keeps the journal in its worktree or another durable path. On a Claude lane the `getconf` path stays in the shared user temp root. That path is not private and the daemon never removes it, so the brief cleans it up, while `${TMPDIR%/}/sf-<tag>` on a Claude lane is private and lasts one attempt. A conversation turn is its own attempt: its scratch lives for that turn only, and a file a turn leaves in `$TMPDIR`, or a path under it shown to the person, is gone once the turn finalizes. A TMPDIR is held only while its holder is live or quarantined, or while its holder's census is not verified empty; salvage state never holds it, and nothing reads a keep flag. Here a census is verified empty when it could be verified and found none of the holder's own processes (C-5.5's attributed sources, `containment_empty`): a cwd observer in the job's worktree holds the worktree, never the TMPDIR, and costs it no census (review r4 of #240). Quarantine is resolved through `kill --confirm-dead`, after which the holder is terminal and the sweep removes its TMPDIR. A recorded allocation is released, with a durable receipt (`removed_at` and `outcome`), when its folder is absent (`absent`: neither its path nor its recorded tombstone holds it, whatever holds it: retention never pins a job for a folder that is not there), when it is removed (`removed`, with `bytes_at_cleanup` when it could be measured), or, for a terminal holder with a verified-empty census, when its path holds something else (`replaced`) or is not one of this temp root's allocations (`outside`); nothing is removed in those last two cases. A verified-empty census of a terminal holder also records `cleared_at`, which pins nothing even if removal fails; removal is then retried on each sweep with no new census. A sweep takes no census and measures nothing for a quarantined or live holder, nor for a terminal one whose census found members at its last look, until that holder's state changes (or the daemon restarts); a census that could not be verified is asked again on the next pass. A probe contained with a verified-empty census uses that census. A probe record is saved only when its TMPDIR is released or cleared. Removal renames the folder to its tombstone: a fresh `subfleet-attempt-removing-` name in the same parent that the store recorded before any rename, in the allocation's record (for an unknown folder, in its sighting; one recorded before D-TMP1-P3 has a name derived from that record). Before the rename it checks that the entry under the allocation's name is the recorded directory (device, inode, owner, not a symlink), and, when that directory lacks any owner permission, sets its mode to 0700: macOS 15 refuses to rename a directory without owner write, even within one parent, where macOS 26 allows it. Every mode change removal makes sets exactly 0700, never the directory's mode plus owner bits. It goes through the directory's descriptor, opened without following a symlink and checked again (against the record, or inside the tree against its lstat); only a directory whose open is refused with EACCES (no owner read: 0000, 0300) is changed by name, never through a symlink. So an entry swapped in between the check and the mode change can be set to 0700, only if it is this user's (a symlink's own mode, never its target's); it never gains group or other bits. After the rename it checks that the renamed entry is still the recorded directory, and only then deletes it through directory descriptors: symlinks inside are unlinked, never followed; another device is never entered; a tombstone a crash left, which no rename precedes, is set to 0700 the same way after its check and before it is emptied, and so is each directory inside the tree that lacks an owner permission, never one above it, so a read-only or owner-unreadable tree (Go's 0555 module cache, a TMPDIR set to 0000) is removed; nothing inside the tree is renamed; and a tree with a directory `retention_fs.MAX_DEPTH` (400) levels deep, which `size()` will not measure either, is refused, so removal holds at most that many descriptors and a few more. If either check or the deletion fails, the entry is renamed back if it was moved (never over a name that has reappeared; a put-back the system refuses leaves it at the tombstone, logged), and removal is refused and logged with its reason: the error's class, and for a refused tree a code of a closed set, never a name the provider chose. After a refused removal, the folder put back is at 0700 (one that already had owner read, write and search keeps its own mode, which removal never changed). It never fails finalization. Something else at the recorded tombstone's name refuses removal and is left alone. A crash during removal leaves the folder at its recorded tombstone, where it is not absent: the next release of its record, under the same holder gate, finishes it, and the sweep finishes an unknown folder's tombstone from its sighting. At startup, on a worker once recovery has finished and the daemon is serving, and on retention's cadence (even while archive retention is dormant), a sweep releases the recorded allocations of terminal and quarantined holders as above, then looks at the temp root's other `subfleet-attempt-` folders. A folder whose path any allocation record names, released or not, follows that record and is never aged, and so does the recorded tombstone of an allocation not yet released. Any other is unknown: only a real directory of this user carrying this store's marker, written for exactly that folder, or one the store's journal names (a crash between its mkdir and its marker), is considered; one without a marker, or with another store's, that the journal does not name is never recorded or removed. A journal entry goes once its folder is gone, carries this store's marker, or a record names it. The store records its first sighting of an unknown folder (`tmpdir.unknown_seen`: path, device, inode, owner, the daemon's time and the tombstone its removal will use), and removes it, by the same rename-and-check against that sighting, 24 hours after it, recording `tmpdir.unknown_removed`; a different folder at the path is a new sighting. Neither the marker's contents nor the folder's times ever make a folder known or old. Scratch bytes join the job's retention accounting while the folder is there (at its path or its tombstone), and retention pins a job for an allocation that is neither released nor cleared, reading only these receipts. Retiring a job takes its attempts' records with it, so retiring one whose allocation is cleared but not removed (a removal that keeps failing) logs one line naming the folder. Disk admission reserves `placement_reserve_gb` (1.5 GB by default) for each placed attempt, for `reserve_ttl_s` (600 s) or until its execution ends, from the free space it measures on the state root's volume (or the policy's disk path); that one provisional amount stands for the growth of the attempt's worktree and TMPDIR together while their bytes are not yet in the measured free space, and afterwards the bytes written are in it. It is an estimate, not a quota: measured scratch can exceed it (a test-heavy lane job's scratch reached 1.56 GB by block sum in the D-TMP1 build's measurement). Attended turns and probes keep their existing disk-admission exemption; their scratch is still evidenced and reclaimed.


## 13. Salvage and workspaces

- **C-13.1** Salvage runs only for a writable job, only at `finalizing`, `lost`, or kill, and only when the worktree's tree hash differs from the baseline recorded at `reserved`. It builds a temporary index from the working tree (tracked and untracked, honouring `.gitignore`), `commit-tree` with the baseline as parent, and writes `refs/subfleet-salvage/<branch>-<utc>-<attempt seq>`, the branch sanitized to `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` and cut to 200 characters, so the name is always one git can write. HEAD, the real index, and the working tree are untouched. Every ref write is create-only. A ref already holding the same tree on the same parent is a replay and is reused; otherwise the snapshot goes beside it under `<ref>-<first 12 of the tree>`, also create-only. If a concurrent writer creates either name after its absent-ref check, salvage re-reads that name once and applies the same rule: reuse the same tree and parent, or, at the base name, try the sibling. A different snapshot at the sibling, a collision whose ref is no longer present, and any other git failure still raise; no existing ref is overwritten and there is no unbounded retry. The ref actually used is recorded as an artifact with role `salvage`. A nested repository with no commit checked out, which git cannot index (a gitlink names a commit), is left out of the snapshot rather than failing it: when `add -A` fails, the untracked nested repositories that `ls-files -o` lists (one `path/` entry each, inside an untracked directory too, honouring `.gitignore`), and those where a tracked file was (`diff-files` names the path, now a directory holding `.git`; its removal is still recorded), and whose HEAD does not resolve (`rev-parse --verify HEAD` exits 1) are excluded by literal pathspecs, and `add -A` runs once more. Nothing git prints decides what is left out, and any other `git add` failure fails the snapshot as before, beside such a repository or not: an unreadable file, or an object git could not write (a read-only `.git/objects/xx`, a full disk). The same holds for the reservation's snapshot (C-6.8) and for a turn's two snapshots (C-26.14), so a turn's diff does not show such a repository; the turn's receipt (`trees.json`) and its evidence (`turn_trees`) list those its end snapshot left out (`skipped`). Salvage runs git under the C locale (`LC_ALL=C`, `LANGUAGE` unset), since it reads git's messages, and with no repository named by the environment (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY`, `GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_NAMESPACE`, `GIT_QUARANTINE_PATH`, and the daemon's own `GIT_INDEX_FILE`) and no way of reading pathspecs set by the environment (`GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_GLOB_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_NOGLOB_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_ICASE_PATHSPECS`: under the first the exclusion above was read as a file name, and the snapshot failed on the repository as before), which `subfleet daemon start` drops from the daemon's environment too, as a turn's diff (C-26.14) and submit's reading of where a job starts in its worktree do for their own git (review of the P3-3 fix: under the first a path's diff showed no changes, and under `GIT_GLOB_PATHSPECS` or `GIT_ICASE_PATHSPECS` `--literal-pathspecs` was a fatal error), and reads what git prints as the file system names things (`os.fsdecode`), never as strict UTF-8, so a branch or file name that is not UTF-8 is carried, not an error; every salvage error it records (receipt, evidence, notice, audit event) and every workspace error C-6.8 records is valid UTF-8, a byte that is not UTF-8 written `\xNN`, since an error quoting such a name would otherwise make the receipt unwritable and finalization raise on every try. The salvage receipt lists what was left out (`skipped`, each path one line of valid UTF-8, with bytes that are not UTF-8 and control characters escaped), whether or not a ref was written; the attempt's evidence gives `salvage_skipped` (the count and the first five); and the job's notice says `salvage left out <n> nested repositor(y|ies) with no commit, kept only in the worktree: <paths>; the worktree is kept: <path>`. At finalization a transient salvage failure (C-6.8's kinds, including git naming a lock file another git process holds or a full disk) is tried `SALVAGE_TRIES` (3) times in all within one daemon run (the count is in memory, so a restart starts it again); that failure, or any other, is recorded in the receipt and in the attempt's evidence (`salvage_error`), and finalization goes on without a salvage ref, so the attempt ends and frees its lane. The job's notice carries the failure when the job ends with that attempt; an attempt after which the job waits to try again (C-4.5) has it only in its evidence, since a notice is written only at the terminal state (C-15.1). When the job then ends without finalizing another attempt (cancelled while it waits to try again, failed or refused while its retry is prepared, or a retry that was cancelled before it launched or was quarantined), its notice says so (C-15.1) and names that last attempt, `attempt a<seq>: <class>, rc=<rc>: <detail>`, then what its salvage could not save as above (its own start snapshot, `baseline_ref`, holds the work of the attempt before it, so it is not what its salvage saved: read so, a retry whose own salvage failed lost `the worktree is kept`, review of the P2 fix) and the start snapshot held after it (below), `the worktree after attempt a<seq> is held under <ref>`; when that attempt never launched (its guardian could not be started, or its main/master re-check got no answer from git), it changed nothing, so the attempt before it is named the same way, back to one that ran. A retry that reached finalization with no provider started (a spawn error whose receipt names no child: refused on main at launch, a home or credential that could not be resolved, a binary that could not be spawned) changed nothing either, so its notice names the attempt before it the same way after its own summary. A `salvage.baseline_held` event that is not as admission writes it (a ref that is one line of valid UTF-8, a hex commit, a numeric seq, `skipped` as above) is left out, never a reason the cancel or failure cannot commit. It never says only `cancelled before launch` (review of ceacf18b: such a job's notice said that, and nothing of the failed salvage or of the held ref). That attempt's work is then only in the next attempt's start snapshot (C-6.8), a tree object no ref holds, which the next attempt goes on to edit: so when the job's last attempt carries `salvage_error`, admission holds that snapshot under `refs/subfleet-salvage/<job id>-a<seq>-baseline` (a commit on the first admission pass's HEAD; later admission passes reuse this first held snapshot even if the caller edits or commits while the retry waits), records it once in a `salvage.baseline_held` event before reservation, names it in the new attempt's evidence (`baseline_ref`) and records it as that attempt's artifact with role `salvage`; a job that ends before that attempt is reserved (cancelled while it waits for capacity) records the ref as its last attempt's artifact instead, when it ends, and names it in its notice, and a cancel that lands while admission writes the ref, before its event, is answered by admission recording the ref so and saying where it is in one more notice; the nested repositories with no commit that snapshot left out, which no ref can hold, are named in the event (`skipped`) and, for any writable attempt whose start snapshot left one out, in its evidence (`baseline_skipped`, the count and the first five), so retention must preserve those paths in its archive before removing the worktree; failing to hold it, including having no HEAD or baseline after a failed salvage, is a C-6.8 workspace failure, so the job waits or fails and nothing runs in the worktree meanwhile. A quarantine's release (`kill --confirm-dead`) salvages once and records a failure at once: `kill` has answered before the release runs, and the job's notice went out at the quarantine, so a failure or a path left out goes into the release's audit event, the attempt's evidence, and one more notice for the job (its C-15.1 first line, then `released from quarantine (attempt a<seq>)` and the lines above). A worktree that no longer exists has nothing to salvage, and that is no failure (D-SALV-MISSING1): when the file system reports no entry at its path (or a parent that is not a directory), before salvage or after a salvage that then failed, no git runs on it, the receipt records `gone: true` and no error, the attempt's evidence and a release's audit event carry `salvage_gone` (`worktree already gone: nothing to salvage`), the notice gives that line, and nothing says the worktree is kept; a path that exists but cannot be salvaged fails as above. The worktree is kept (C-13.4) unless retention itself archives and reads back the bytes the snapshot left out. A salvage ref, including one a caller says is referenced elsewhere, never proves preservation of those paths; archive retention copies nested repositories byte for byte instead of recomputing a tree with `add -A`.
- **C-13.2** A private salvage ref may be written from any branch. What is refused, at submit, at admission and again at launch, is a writable job that would write in a checkout on `main` or `master`: an in-place job whose workdir is on one, and any job whose allocated worktree has been switched to one between attempts. A job that is not in place never writes where its caller stands: the daemon cuts it a detached worktree from the caller's head (C-6.6), so it may be submitted from a checkout on `main`, and the caller's HEAD, index and files are untouched. Any push of a salvage ref to a branch named `main` or `master` is refused.
- **C-13.3** Before a new attempt on a writable job, the daemon reconciles: salvage if dirty, record the salvage ref and the current head as the checkpoint, and start the next attempt from the same worktree with a prompt suffix naming the checkpoint. A limit hit after an hour of work loses nothing committed or salvaged.
- **C-13.4** Lane worktrees the daemon allocates live under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/worktrees/`, never `/tmp`; a job's own is `jobs.worktree`, or, while that is NULL, the `worktrees/<job id>` admission allocated before recording it. A path left out of salvage keeps its worktree unless retention's archive itself holds and verifies that path's bytes, even when the salvage ref is referenced elsewhere. They are removed only after the job is terminal, no live conversation turn or reader holds their folder (I5: `folders.TURN` and `folders.READER`, C-8.4), retention has checked those rows and taken its canonical `worktree:<folder>` fence atomically against new turn reservations, no live process has its canonical cwd in the owned tree, and retention has archived and verified them (C-8.4); their registration is removed with them, after every object it names is in the archive's bundle. The cwd check is hold-only, uses the same in-process read as C-5.5 before moving the tree and at holder checks on the moved tree, including a retirement resumed from quarantined, and names pids with "cwd in worktree", never commands. It includes observers and excludes in-place workdirs. A worktree allocation that failed, and a conversation worktree rebuilt after an interrupted add, remove only their own unlocked registration, never others by a repository-wide prune. A cwd observer holds only the worktree (its lease until the job ends, then, however it ends, a reader row that excludes no writer, C-5.5, C-5.9) and its retention, never the attempt's TMPDIR (C-12.10) or its verdict (C-5.9), and never authorizes a signal.

## 14. Guard prerequisites (milestone 1)

- **C-14.1** The private never-rules hook and TRUST remain byte-for-byte copies of the reviewed v1-derived pair under `private/guard/`, excluded from the wheel and source distribution. The portable core resolves the installed operator overlay at `<state root>/guard/never-rules-hook.sh` and `<state root>/guard/TRUST`; doctor and launch preflight share file validation. Missing, malformed, or mismatched files refuse executable Codex work with code 7. Tests pin the private pair's original SHA-256 values and stage a separate synthetic fixture only inside isolated test state roots. See `docs/private-guard-overlay.md` for installation and old-launch preservation.
- **C-14.2** Before the first executable Codex job on each lane home, `doctor` and the daemon run the trust preflight: the installed `codex` version, the hooks trust hash the guard relies on, and the override string are checked against `TRUST`; a mismatch refuses Codex launches with exit 7 and names the fix. Only a `trust` verdict asks to verify hook SHA-256, TRUST and the pinned Codex version. `environment` and `config` verdicts establish no trust mismatch and instead ask to restore required directories and binaries or correct the preflight configuration; `version` asks for the supported Codex version. Each of these repairs names `subfleet doctor`; a `publish` verdict's (below) does not. The preflight's version and app-server subprocesses use C-10.8's same CA environment, including operator overrides and the configured policy, so it checks the trust path the lanes take. A verdict is keyed on the codex version, the home, the override string, the seeded-config fingerprint (C-23.5), and the selected CA variable names and values; a re-picked lane whose key differs is verified again before launch. A review launch (C-12.3, C-12.4) adds the publish hook as the override's second PreToolUse group (matcher `Bash`, status `review publish guard`, key `/<session-flags>/config.toml:pre_tool_use:1:0`) with its own trusted state, the identity hash taken as the guard's; hooks/list must then list it exactly once, enabled, trusted, with that hash, or the launch gets a `publish` refusal (`guard-preflight-publish`, exit 7), whatever the review's sandbox: the guard verified, so it asks neither to verify TRUST nor to rerun `subfleet doctor`, which never runs the preflight with the publish hook, and says "the review", not "the writable job"; its repair is the hooks/list answer in the attempt's `guard-preflight.json`, or a Claude model for the review (D-SH1 round 8). Codex 0.159.0 lists such a group that way (probe of 2026-10-18). The guard's group, key and hash, its bytes and TRUST are unchanged, and a launch without the publish hook uses v1's override string byte for byte.
- **C-14.3** A Codex launch with `workspace-write` always carries the guard override; a Claude launch relies on the global hook and `doctor` reports if it is missing from `~/.claude/settings.json`.
- **C-14.4** The isolation matrix test runs each fake provider under each sandbox and asserts the child could not write outside the workdir in `read-only`, could write inside it in `workspace-write`, and, without explicit C-11.1 policy pass-through, inherited no `CODEX_API_KEY`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`, or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`.

## 15. Notices and waiting

- **C-15.1** Every terminal job except a conversation turn writes a `notices` row for its caller session in the same transaction as the terminal state, after that transaction has written the state; a notice for a job whose row is not yet terminal is refused. A job has one such notice, and a quarantine's release whose salvage failed or left a path out writes one more, as does a retry's start snapshot that admission held while a cancel ended the job (C-13.1). The first line is `<job id>: <state>; rc=<rc>; deliverable=<path>; out=<-o path>`, read from the job row inside that transaction: the job's terminal state and rc, the pair `wait`, `runs` and `runs show` report, never the final attempt's outcome class and rc. The deliverable and `-o` paths name files only for an accepted job (`succeeded` with an `accepted_attempt_id`); for any other job both are `-`, whatever its attempt left on disk. The PostToolUse hook's line for a job with no notice row (C-15.2 layer 2) is rendered from the job row by the same function, so one terminal state has one rendering; likewise `wait`'s line names an `-o` file or deliverable only for a `succeeded` job, and `runs show` falls back to the `-o` path only for one. The second line is the summary: for a job whose last attempt reached finalization, `attempt a<seq>: <class>, rc=<rc>: <detail>`, and when the job did not accept that attempt's non-empty deliverable, `output kept, not accepted: <path>`, with `; -o <path> was not written` when the job has an `-o` path; for a job that ended without one, why (cancelled before launch, quarantined with its census, the refusal, the workspace error of C-6.8). A job that ends between two attempts says so first (`cancelled while waiting to retry`, or `<state> while preparing the retry: ` then the refusal or workspace error), and a retry that ends without reaching finalization names itself (`attempt a<seq>: ` or `attempt a<seq> quarantined: `); then comes the attempt before it (and, past one that never launched, the one before that), its salvage and any held start snapshot, as C-13.1 gives them; a retry that reached finalization with no provider started (a spawn error whose receipt names no child) gives its own summary and then the attempt before it the same way. Then a line for each lane the job moved on from (C-4.5, C-23.44), whatever ended the job. Then any uncertainty (`quarantined`, export failed, unattested) (incident: 2026-09-24, three running Codex jobs were cancelled and correctly became `cancelled` with rc 130, their attempts `interrupted`, nothing accepted and nothing exported, but each notice was written from the attempt, `ok; rc=0; deliverable=<path>; out=<-o path>` and "Codex completed with a deliverable", so the session was told the job had succeeded and where its output was; the hook fallback later announced the same jobs `cancelled; rc=130` from the job row). A conversation turn's outcome is its message's state (C-24.1), which the app shows. When recorded launch evidence exists, the final line carries it: for capability `none` or `commands` its C-12.3/4 sentence and, when enforced, `; publish: denied`; for a writable review `publish: denied` alone; a writable attempt that may publish (a build) has no evidence line; all preceding lines keep their established order, including later push or export results. A policy review's notice carries, just before that line, `the worktree included the caller's uncommitted changes as of submit: <n> files (<t> tracked, <u> untracked)`, or after a fallback `ran read-only in the caller's checkout: its uncommitted changes could not be copied into a worktree (<reason>)` (C-6.6); `runs show` prints the same, and its `--json` carries the record as `uncommitted`, from the daemon and offline alike. A job with no attempt, and an older attempt whose capability is unknown, carries no inferred capability.
- **C-15.2** Delivery layers, most to least reliable: `subfleet wait` in a background Bash call; a PostToolUse `asyncRewake` hook (milestone 4); SessionStart and UserPromptSubmit hooks surfacing rows with state `pending` or `offered` (inside a process Subfleet launched, only rows that name a job; C-26.13); a best-effort socket push through the v1 mechanism, kept until tickle, muster, and `ping` have tested replacements. A PostToolUse waiter examines a terminal result before any idle backoff. After candidates are read, it reserves the smaller of 2 s and half its remaining budget for `notice.pending` and `notice.mark`; polls, busy pauses and idle sleeps end before that reserve begins. A terminal result uses it immediately. `notice.pending` receives at most half the remaining delivery time, leaving the other half for `notice.mark`; both keep the hook's original absolute deadline. With `SUBFLEET_HOOK_TIMEOUT_S=6` and the existing 5 s harness margin, an immediate candidate read leaves approximately 1 s of work time. A terminal result at the end of the polling budget leaves about 0.5 s for delivery and gives `notice.pending` at most about 0.25 s. An immediate terminal reply leaves about 1 s for delivery and gives `notice.pending` at most about 0.5 s. `notice.mark` gets the remaining delivery time (about 0.25 s or 0.5 s respectively if lookup consumes its full share).
- **C-15.3** Notice states: `pending` (written), `offered` (a delivery attempt was made; transport recorded), `acknowledged` (`notice.ack` from the session, or the session ran `runs show <job>`), `surfaced` (printed by a hook). A notice may be offered more than once; it is acknowledged once. The states are a ladder, `pending` < `offered` < `surfaced` < `acknowledged`, and `notice.mark` never moves a notice down it: an offer whose `notice.pending` read came before a hook surfaced the notice leaves it `surfaced`, so no hook prints a notice a hook has already printed. `notice.mark` and `notice.ack` both take a service notice by its negated id (as `notice.pending` returns it) and set its state in `service_notices`; until 2026-09-30 `notice.mark` updated only `notices`, so a hook that printed a service notice left it `pending` and printed it again at every prompt. The session hooks print every notice that is not a run's end under their own header, `subfleet: <n> message(s) for this session:`, each with the time it was queued, after the runs' header and its footer: a service notice (a `ping` message: a restart nudge, `subfleet ping`, a timer alert, or a pin notice that names its job), told apart by its negated id, or a notice that names no job, such as a v1 outbox message the importer carried over (incident: 2026-09-29, 1,749 service notices were `pending`, and session 1cc3982f was shown service notice 1382, a restart nudge queued two days before, as "1 detached run dispatched by this session finished while it was not running"). `subfleet wait` and `run --wait` return terminal receipts without acknowledging notices, including when the waiter outlives its conversation turn.
- **C-15.4** `wait` is a server-side long poll with a per-call deadline of at most 60 s, counted from when the daemon read the request; the CLI loops until the job set is terminal or `--timeout` expires (exit 124), asking again after a busy answer (C-16.7). `wait` never wakes headless lanes and never targets a lane session.
