- **C-15.5** `wait` has one reader, the wait hub (`subfleet/waits.py`). A waiter registers the jobs it waits on before its first read, reads its answer when it starts, and reads again only when the hub wakes it and once more when its deadline passes, so that a job that ended then is answered, not timed out; it never re-reads on another waiter's wake-up, nor on a timer while the hub's thread runs. The hub looks at the store's generation (C-5.11) when poked by `Daemon._notify` and otherwise every `WAIT_POLL_S` (0.1 s). When the generation has moved, a waiter has registered, or `WAIT_RECHECK_S` (1 s) has passed, it reads the state of every watched job and which of them hold an `out:` lease, one statement each whatever the number of waiters, and wakes each waiter whose jobs may all be returned: ended, a succeeded job's export done, or unknown to the store (the waiter then reports the unknown job, as it always did). So a wait whose last job ends by a commit on the daemon's store returns within `WAIT_POLL_S` plus three reads, poked or not: the hub's read in progress when the commit landed, the hub's read that sees it, and the waiter's own; a commit the generation does not count (another process's, C-6.3) is seen within `WAIT_RECHECK_S` plus those reads. No read has a time bound of its own: each takes what SQLite takes, plus up to `READ_WAIT_S` for a read connection (C-3.7). Waiting costs one read per commit for all waiters together, and because registration comes before the first read, no wake-up is lost. A waiter is always woken or times out. A hub pass that raises, in its read or anywhere else, wakes every waiter to read for itself, and the hub goes on. If the hub's thread ends anyway, it wakes every waiter as it goes, and the next registration or the next wait starts another thread. It does not try to start one less than `WAIT_RECHECK_S` after its last try, and a start that fails (no thread to be had) is logged and tried again after that; until a thread runs, each wait is cut to `WAIT_RECHECK_S` and its waiter reads again, and a waiter's registration ends with its wait whatever happened to the hub. `daemon.status` reports the hub under `wait_hub`: its waiters, whether a thread runs, and its reads, deaths, restarts and failed starts. A stopping daemon wakes every waiter, which answers `{"timeout": true}`. Incident: on 2026-09-25 ~21 PostToolUse hooks each held a `wait`, and every worker pass woke every waiter to re-read its jobs behind the store lock.
- **C-15.6** A hook costs one connection per question and no inspection. Its client does not check `daemon.lock` before connecting (`Client(verify_lock=False)`): that check costs a `ps` and a `sysctl` per process, and the PostToolUse hook runs on every Bash call of every session, while a hook exits 0 silently whichever way the daemon is down, which a refused connection already says. Its reads (`list`, `notice.pending`) are C-16.5's lookups.
- **C-15.8** (a notice has a reader) A service notice is written only for a session named: the one a `ping` names, else the one `alerts.operator_session` names; in either, a blank or non-string value names none, and a name is used without the spaces around it. There is no default inbox. Text that is only whitespace is no text, and text that is not a string is refused with exit 2. A `ping` with text that names no session, when no operator session is set, is refused with exit 2 and writes nothing; a `ping` without text is a liveness question and writes nothing, whatever it names (`doctor --live` sends one with no text and no session, and `subfleet ping` with no text, or at a prompt with none piped in, prints `pong`); `subfleet ping` names the session given, else the Claude session it runs in, else none, and leaves the rest of this rule to the daemon; and a timer alert, when no operator session is set, is delivered by being shown (C-18.4) and writes no notice. `notices [--session ID] [--all] [--ack|--withdraw [--reason TEXT]] [--json]`, v1's spelling (C-17.1), lists every session's unresolved (`pending` or `offered`) notices, job and service, or one session's, a service notice under its negated id as `notice.pending` returns it; `--all` adds the `surfaced` and `acknowledged` rows retention still keeps. Listing (op `notice.list`) reads only and marks nothing, so a session's own hooks still surface what was listed, and with no daemon it reads the store (C-17.5). Every listed row carries its `fingerprint` (`store.notice_fingerprint`: its creation time and a digest of its text). An action names one session (`--session`; without it, exit 2) and acts on exactly the unresolved notices the listing returned for it, each only while it is still the row listed: a notice's id is reused once the newest row of its table is deleted (neither table has AUTOINCREMENT), so the action sends each row's fingerprint with its id and the daemon acts on a row only while both match, and a notice written after the listing is untouched; two rows alike in id, session, creation second and text are one notice to the session that reads it. `--ack` acknowledges them (`notice.ack` with fingerprints; without them, as `runs show` and the hooks send it, it acts on ids alone, as before): the operator's acknowledgement, for notices the operator has read. Its answer names the rows it acknowledged and the rows it kept (acknowledged already, or no longer the row listed), and the CLI reports those, not the ids it asked about. `--withdraw` deletes the service notices among them (`notice.withdraw`) and leaves the job notices, naming how many: a job's notice is its terminal record and is acknowledged, never withdrawn, and a `notice.withdraw` that names one is refused with exit 2 and changes nothing. A withdrawal deletes only the rows it names that are that session's, still `pending` or `offered`, and, when the call gives fingerprints (the CLI always does), still the row listed. A row a hook printed (`surfaced`, C-15.3), a session acknowledged or another took the id of since the listing is kept and reported as kept. Fingerprints, when given, are strings, one per id, or the call is refused with exit 2; an id named twice is acted on once, with its first fingerprint, by either action. The deletion happens in one transaction with one `notice.withdrawn` event naming the session, the reason (`--reason`, which needs `--withdraw`, even when empty), each id, the creation span, and how many of each subject went. It claims no delivery, and loses no record of what was withdrawn (incident: 2026-10-03, `alerts.operator_session` had never been set, so from 2026-09-19 every timer alert, about 120 a day, was parked for the literal session `operator`, which no Claude session holds, no hook surfaces, no verb listed and C-23.26 never prunes: 1,637 rows, none read, among them each `claude credentials: ... is auth-dead. Run: ...` line C-23.52 exists to put in front of the operator; every `doctor --live` parked a `doctor` row there too, seven in all).

## 16. Socket protocol

- **C-16.1** Unix domain socket at `daemon.sock`, mode 0600. Newline-delimited JSON, one request then one response per line. Request: `{"v": 1, "id": "<client string>", "op": "<op>", "args": {...}}`. Response: `{"v": 1, "id": "<same>", "ok": true, "result": {...}}` or `{"v": 1, "id": "<same>", "ok": false, "error": {"code": <exit code>, "message": "...", "fix": "..."}}`. The open-file limit, the connection cap and the busy answer are C-16.6 and C-16.7; which pool runs each op is C-16.5.
- **C-16.2** Ops: `submit`, `list`, `show`, `wait`, `kill`, `lanes`, `readings`, `why`, `notice.pending`, `notice.ack`, `notice.mark`, `notice.list`, `notice.withdraw` (C-15.8), `ping`, `daemon.status`, `gate.start`, `gate.poll`, `gate.continue`, `sessions`, `pick`, `operations`, `tasks` (C-11.10), and the section 25 ops. Argument and result shapes are the dataclasses in `subfleet/protocol.py`, except the section 25 ops', which are design §5's (C-25.2); unknown fields are ignored, missing required fields are exit 2.
  `daemon.status` is a current-capacity snapshot, not a full ledger (D-ST2). Its `attempts` contains only `reserved`, `starting`, `running`, `finalizing` and `quarantined` rows, ordered by `reserved_at,seq`, with fields `attempt_id`, `job_id`, `seq`, `lane_id`, `model_requested`, `state`, `reserved_at`; no `evidence_json` or other attempt columns. Neither renderer needs finished-attempt history, so there is no recent window. Its `jobs` contains every `queued`, `waiting` and `running` job plus the jobs of those attempts (even if terminal), with the existing job columns and `created_at,rowid` order. The daemon reads these through `attempts_live`, `attempts_quarantined`, `jobs_state` and the job primary key, so retained finished attempts and jobs do not grow this read or reply. All other status keys, capacity counts and rendering remain available. `route=True` reads keep their existing shapes and predicates. `status.json` uses the timer's separate snapshot and retains its recent-finished-job menu (C-18.2).
- **C-16.3** `submit` returns `{job_id, request_id, created: bool}` where `created: false` means an existing job matched the request id. `wait` returns the terminal states of the requested jobs or `{"timeout": true}` after the deadline. A client that connected and sent a request but read no complete, decodable response line (a timeout while sending or reading, a reset or broken pipe, a close with no line, a line past the size cap or one that does not decode) does not know the request's outcome and never reports it as not done: the daemon may have committed it and answered after the client stopped reading, and even a failed send may have delivered the whole line. A refused connection is the one failure that proves nothing was sent (exit 69, C-17.3). `submit` and `kill` are idempotent (C-6.2, C-7.1), so the CLI and the sessions kit send the identical request once more, the same arguments under the same request id, with a 60 s deadline, and its answer is the outcome: `created: false` there is the job the first, unanswered request created, which a request id the CLI minted makes this invocation's own (`created: true` in its output), and a kill answered "already finished" is followed by `show`, because the first kill may be what finished the job, and the CLI prints the job's state and rc. Only if the re-send is also unanswered is the outcome reported as unknown (exit 1, C-17.3), naming the request id and the command that settles it: the same command with `--request-id <id>`, which never creates a second job, or `subfleet kill <job>` again, which is safe; stdout then carries no job id, and a job whose kill is unknown is not waited on. A re-sent submit that is refused does not prove the first created nothing, because submit validates before it looks up the request id and a moved HEAD is a different payload (C-6.2), so the client looks the request id up: `list` accepts `request_id`, and the client filters the rows itself as well, for a daemon that ignores the field (C-16.2). A job found is the answer when the CLI minted the id and is named in the refusal otherwise; none found, the refusal stands; a lookup that fails leaves the outcome unknown. The different-payload refusal names the job that holds the request id. A daemon gone before the re-send leaves a submit's outcome unknown and sends a kill to offline mode (C-17.5). A re-send answered only `busy` (exit 69, C-16.7) was never read and settles nothing, since the first request may still be committing: a submit is still looked up by request id, and a job found is the answer when the CLI minted the id; otherwise, and for a kill, and when no job is found, the outcome is reported as unknown, never as busy (exit 69 would read as nothing sent) and never as refused (merge review 2026-09-25). The message also names `subfleet runs --request-id <id>`, which finds the job whoever its caller session is (a revive's job belongs to the session it continues, so `runs --mine` would miss it); every id in a printed command is shell-quoted, so pasting it cannot change the id. A quarantine resolution (`--confirm-dead`, `--force-release`) whose outcome is unknown is to be repeated with its own flag and note, not as a plain `kill`, which would answer "already finished" and resolve nothing. `revive` and `handoff` tell the settle whether their request id was minted for the call, as `run` does; the CLI mints it before calling them, because the staged prompt is named after it, so it says so explicitly, and only `handoff --request-id` is the operator's. A handoff's brief is rebuilt from a transcript that keeps growing, so for it the lookup is named first and a re-run is said to risk a refusal as a different payload, which names the job and creates none. The unknown entry of a batch names the lookup as well. A dry run creates nothing and is not re-sent (incident: 2026-09-24, a `subfleet run --batch` entry printed "no response from the daemon within 15s" and "NOT submitted" while the daemon had committed it at 11:47:04Z, about a second before the client's 15 s deadline (event 581051); a manual `subfleet run` retry minted a fresh request id and created a duplicate job at 11:50:38Z (event 581065); a kill reported failed three times, with "no response from the daemon within 15s" and then "daemon closed the connection without a response", had committed its cancel at 11:59:01Z (event 581283)).
- **C-16.4** The daemon serves requests on a thread pool; a request handler never blocks on a provider process, a probe, or `ps`. Long-running work is queued to workers with deadlines.
- **C-16.5** A liveness question and a hook's reads never queue behind slow requests. `ping` without `text` (none, or only whitespace, C-15.8) reads nothing and is answered on the connection's own thread. `list`, `show` and `notice.pending` (`LOOKUP_OPS`) run on their own 8-thread pool, `subfleet-read`; `wait` on the waiters' pool; `submit` and `gate.*` on the workers; conversation ops on the conversation pools (the long polls `conversation.events` and `conversation.watch` on their own, `subfleet-poll`, C-29.9; the file work on `subfleet-files`, C-25.3); every other op on the 16-thread request pool, where view builds and writes that wait for the store lock stay. With C-3.7 none of the lookups waits for a transaction either, nor longer than `READ_WAIT_S` for a read connection, and snapshots cannot take the connections kept for them. `wait` and the two conversation long polls, which hold a thread for up to 60 s and 50 s, have a thread for every connection the daemon may hold (C-16.7), so, when every held connection carries one request at a time, as the CLI's, the hooks' and the app's do, no long poll queues behind the others past its deadline. Pipelining breaks that for everyone: one client that sends more waits than the cap on its connections can hold every waiter's thread, and another client's wait, however well behaved, then queues behind them (reviews of 3c8fe55 and 02c6320). Incident: 2026-09-25, all 16 request threads were busy and a `ping` took up to 90 s.
- **C-16.6** The daemon does not exit because `accept` failed for want of a resource. `EMFILE`, `ENFILE`, `ENOBUFS`, `ENOMEM`, `ECONNABORTED`, `EINTR` and `EAGAIN` are waited out (50 ms, doubling to 2 s, reset by the next connection accepted; by sleeping, never by waiting on the stop event, whose lock the signal handler that sets it would meet held on the same thread) and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th consecutive failure with the count of open connections and descriptors; any other `accept` error still ends `serve_forever`. A stop is at most one pause late (review of the descriptor hotfix, F5). Failing without a break for 300 s (`ACCEPT_GIVE_UP_S`), the daemon ends `serve_forever` and exits, so launchd starts a fresh one: with client connections capped (C-16.7), a run that long is a leak outside them, and a daemon alive but deaf would keep the lock. On macOS the kernel drops the connection whose `accept` failed, so its client reads end of stream ("closed the connection without a response"): waiting out protects the process and the next client, and C-16.7 is what keeps a client from meeting `EMFILE` at all. At start, before anything else, the daemon raises its soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` to 65536 (`subfleet/descriptors.py`), never above the hard limit and never lowering it, trying 32768, 16384 ... 1024 in turn if `setrlimit` refuses, and logs the effective soft and hard limits and what the raise achieved. `daemon install` writes `SoftResourceLimits.NumberOfFiles` (65536, or `kern.maxfilesperproc` if lower) into the launchd plist, so even a daemon that cannot raise its own limit does not start at launchd's 256. `daemon.status` reports `descriptors`: the soft and hard limits (`null` is unlimited), the descriptors open now, and the C-16.7 connection counts. (Incident: `~/.subfleet/daemon.log` holds 14 tracebacks of `OSError: [Errno 24] Too many open files` from `self._socket.accept()` in `serve_forever`, 2 on 2026-09-24 and 12 on 2026-09-25, some a few log lines apart as launchd restarted a daemon that failed again at once. Each ended the process, and every client waiting on a reply lost it. launchd had started the daemon at a soft limit of 256, and every client connection held a descriptor.)
- **C-16.7** Client connections are bounded and never wait for a reader. The daemon holds at most `max(4, min(512, (soft limit - 64 - 2 × turns) // 2))` client connections at once, derived at each `accept`: 64 descriptors stay reserved for its own work, including the store, the log, the lock, the listener and its children's pipes; each conversation turn runner still going holds two more of its own, its relay connection to the guardian, open for the turn's whole life (C-26.4), and its stdout while it reads a chunk; and each connection is allowed two. A cap that falls as turns start closes no connection already held. Each connection it holds has a reader thread of its own, started at `accept`, so no connection waits for a reader; requests can still wait for a thread of the request pools, and when every held connection carries one request at a time those waits are bounded by the cap; one client that pipelines can make every other client's requests wait too. A request whose pool cannot start a thread is counted (`unscheduled`, as on main) and its reader goes on: the standard pool has queued the call before it tried, so it may still run and answer. `close()` waits at most 2 s in all for readers to return, then closes every connection it still holds. One accepted past the cap, or one for which no reader thread can start, gets `{"ok": false, "error": {"code": 69, "message": "the daemon is busy: ...", "fix": "try again shortly; ..."}}` at once, before its request is read, and is closed; nothing else the daemon sends over the socket carries 69. The client reads that answer even if its request met the closed socket, and because nothing was read, `Client.call` sends the same request again after 50 ms doubling to 1 s (less up to half at random), but only while less than half its deadline has passed, so a retry the daemon does read still has at least half the deadline to answer and a clean busy never becomes an unknown outcome (C-16.3); after that it reports busy. A retry whose connect is then refused (ECONNREFUSED: the busy daemon's full listen backlog) while `daemon.lock` names a living daemon reports busy as well, so no caller falls back to offline mode behind a busy one; a socket gone (a stopped daemon unlinks it), a lock holder that is dead, or any other failure is an absent daemon (`client.refused_while_busy`); a lock that cannot say whether its holder lives (no lock, no usable pid, `ps` blocked by a sandbox) counts as busy inside a call and in the hook, both bounded by their deadlines, and for at most 60 s (`REFUSED_UNVERIFIED_MAX_S`) in a CLI wait loop with no `--timeout`, which has none. A long poll that loops (`subfleet wait`, `kill --wait`, `run --wait`, the PostToolUse hook) does not retry inside the call: it takes busy as an empty poll and asks again, so each poll has its whole deadline, and it takes a connect refused after a busy answer as busy too, by the same rule, so a daemon that stops or dies after a busy answer ends the loop instead of holding it for ever. The first try has the whole deadline, and a lost answer's message names the caller's deadline. A caller with a faster answer than waiting takes busy at once (`Client(retry_busy=False)`): the prompt hooks read the store offline instead. So a re-sent `submit` or `kill` answered only busy settles as C-16.3 says; `subfleet gate`'s poll is one call with a 180 s deadline; and `subfleet wait`, `kill --wait` and `run --wait` go on asking a busy daemon, backing off, until `--timeout`. `subfleet daemon status` reports a daemon that answered, busy or not, as running (exit 0). Reads time out: a connection with no request outstanding that has sent nothing and received no reply for 60 s is closed at its next read timeout (so after 60 to 120 s of silence), and the same 60 s bounds sending one reply, so a client that stops reading frees its thread; after a reply that failed part way nothing more is sent on that connection: the daemon shuts it down while it still holds the connection's write lock, so a reply waiting for the lock meets the closed stream instead of joining the broken line (review of 3c8fe55). A request line (newline included) over 1 MiB is answered once with exit 2 and the rest of that line is discarded, and one that nests too deeply to parse or to describe, carries an integer past Python's digit limit, or is not UTF-8 is answered exit 2 too; framing does not depend on how the bytes arrive. A read-only request (`list`, `show`, `wait`, `readings`, `why`, `pick`, `daemon.status`, `notice.pending`, `ping` without text, and `lanes` listing, with no action or `list`; and the conversation ops that only read: `capabilities`, `models.list`, `conversation.list`, `conversation.open` naming a conversation (opened by native session it may create one), `conversation.history`, `conversation.events`, `conversation.watch`, `conversation.runs`, `message.status`, `approval.list`, `approval.get`, `turn.diff`, `conversation.diff` and `workspace.check` (create's folder checks as a preview, making no folder); they may record observations such as the desktop identity or a diff's tree objects, nothing a caller relies on) whose client has closed its whole socket is not run: when the reader sees the client leave, such requests not yet started are cancelled, so the connection and its place under the cap go at once, and one a thread reaches later is dropped. A stream the daemon ended itself after a reply that failed part way is treated the same way, without being counted as a client leaving. A long poll whose client leaves ends at its next look, its answer unsent and counted as dropped: a `wait` within 0.25 s, a `conversation.events` or `conversation.watch` within 0.5 s (their store's wait looks that often); the conversation polls also end when the daemon stops. The app sends one request per connection and a long poll waits at most 50 s, so its pending poll keeps the connection from the idle close, which the reply then ends. The conversation ops' replies are written as the others are: after one that failed part way nothing more is sent. On macOS `getpeername` tells a closed client apart from one that shut down only its write half, which still gets its reply. A request that writes still runs after its client leaves, because a client disconnect cannot cancel its durable job (C-5.2). The listen backlog is `socket.SOMAXCONN` (128, macOS's default `kern.ipc.somaxconn`), since a connect refused on a full backlog reads to the CLI as no daemon at all. `daemon.status` `descriptors` also reports `live_turns`, the `reserve` they make and `unscheduled`. Refusals, idle closes and dropped requests are counted in `daemon.status` and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th occurrence; accept failures are counted there too and logged per consecutive run as C-16.6 says. (Before this clause every connection held one of 32 reader threads for its whole life, so 40 clients in `wait` left no reader for anyone else; `tests/unit/test_daemon_connections.py` holds that load and requires a ping answered within 2 s.)

## 17. CLI

- **C-17.1** Verbs (v1 spellings permanent, plan amendment 1): `subfleet` and `subfleet status` (table), `run`, `runs [--mine] [--running] [--last N] [--kind K | --include-turns] [--request-id ID] [--json]` (turn jobs only when asked, C-26.12; `--request-id` names the job carrying that request id, whoever submitted it and whatever its kind, C-16.3), `runs show <id> [--out|--err|--json]`, `runs reap` (reconcile now), `wait <id>... | --mine | --last [--timeout S]`, `kill <id> [--wait] [--reason TEXT] [--confirm-dead|--force-release]`, `resume <id> [PROMPT]`, `lanes [list|probe|enroll <credential>|hold <lane> --until [--note]|release <lane>|transfer <lane> --to v1|v2]`, `tasks [list|hold <task> --until <iso> --note <text>|release <task>]` (C-11.10), `why <id> | --task T --tier X`, `daemon [start [--check] [--json]|stop|status|logs|install]` (`--check`: C-10.5's install check, which starts nothing), `doctor [--live]`, `ping [--session ID] TEXT`, `notices [--session ID] [--all] [--ack|--withdraw]` (C-15.8), `retention archives | restore <job> [--to DIR] [--repository R] [--check] | survey [--sample N]` (offline; C-8.4, d635). Aliases: `jobs` for `runs`, `show` for `runs show --json`, `capacity` for `status`, `notify` for `ping`, `resume-codex` for `resume`. Also first-class and permanent, from v1: `gate [pr|plan|continue]` with its 0 to 5 exit codes, `sessions [list|continue|revive|mirror]`, and `handoff <session> --to <model>`; they dispatch to the `subfleet-gate` and `subfleet-sessions` entry points. `doctor --guard-check <payload>` runs the never-rules check as a dry run that writes no denial log (C-23.24). The bare `runs show <id>` keeps v1's output shape, which agents parse today: the metadata object, then a `--- out.md ---` line and the deliverable; `--out`, `--err`, and `--json` are v2's single-artifact forms and print one thing each.
- **C-17.2** `run` flags: `--task`, `--tier`, `-m`, `-a EMAIL` (a Claude account), `-H CODEX_HOME` (a Codex home; either may name a lane id instead, and the flag's provider narrows a name, C-11.2), `-C DIR`, `-p PROMPTFILE`, `-o OUT`, `-n NAME`, `-s SANDBOX`, `--needs-shell` / `--no-needs-shell`, `-x EMAIL` (repeatable), `--mcp NAME` (repeatable; C-12.9), `--allow-desktop`, `--allow-tmp`, `--in-place`, `--independent`, `--parent JOB`, `--request-id ID`, `--wait`/`--attach`, `-d`/`--detach` (default inside a Claude session), `--batch FILE` (C-17.7), `--json`, `--dry-run`, `--why`, `--no-preamble`, `-I` (isolated review; requires `-s read-only` and `-D`; C-23.2), `-D REVIEW_ROOT`. Deprecated but accepted through milestone 8 with a stderr note: `-t CLASS`, `--overflow`, `-m sol` (remapped to `astra`). Model pins accept every short name the daemon's live `models` map names, including `sol61` and `luna`; unknown pins fail at submit with exit 7 listing those models. The CLI's known-model list is help text, not a second admission authority. Batch manifests accept boolean `needs_shell` with entry-over-default precedence. Explicit shell choices override policy requirements. `--needs-shell` help says "commands only, no writes; for tests use -s workspace-write". `runs show` and its JSON expose capability and publish denial per attempt. `status` (text and JSON) counts distinct run ids with any `none` or `commands` attempt reserved in the last 24 hours, includes those ids, and shows zero explicitly; retries count a run once, older attempts and unknown capabilities do not count.
- **C-17.3** Exit codes, one meaning each across every verb: 0 ok · 1 operational error · 2 invalid input · 3 no lane: no attempt could be admitted (message names the earliest reset; for a job whose pinned lane could never admit it, the lane and why, C-11.8) · 4 hard limit: the job's final attempt was `limited` and no candidate or attempt remained, whether or not a lane was pinned (message names the closure's clock) · 5 auth dead · 6 provider CLI too old · 7 refused (message names the rule and the fix) · 69 daemon unavailable · 75 queued (only with `--json` and `--no-wait-queue`) · 124 wait timeout · 125 job lost · 130 cancelled. `wait` and `--wait` return the job's rc when it is one of these; a provider rc outside the table maps to 1 with the raw rc in the message. Exit 1 also covers an outcome the CLI could not learn (C-16.3): the message says the outcome is unknown and names the request id, and such an outcome is never reported as refused, failed, or not submitted.
D-KILL1: each `job.cancel_requested` event records who requested the cancellation and why. A daemon request carries `{by: "daemon", reason, running_s}`; the wall's reason is `max_wall_s`. A client cancellation (`kill`, `message.cancel` or `turn.interrupt`) carries `{by: "client", session_id, pid, reason}`, with the session supplied by the client, the pid supplied by the socket's saved peer audit token (never a request field), and `client-request` as the reason when `--reason` is omitted. Conversation withdrawals use `message-cancelled`; a running turn interruption uses `stopped`, and its client request is persisted before containment can escalate or recover it. Internal cancellations such as `message-settled` and the wall keep the daemon shape. `runs show` (bare and `--json`, online and offline) adds `session_id_source: "client-stated"` and `pid_source: "socket-peer-audit-token"` for client records; these labels also apply when the identity value is null, and historical empty events remain readable. An unavailable session or peer pid is explicit as null. The first request wins, except that a client request during a pending wall stop is always recorded and becomes the effective requester. Repeated client kills preserve that client's event, timestamp and attribution. A receipt may supersede only an effective wall request, with `job.wall_cancel_superseded` retaining the original wall request in the audit trail; `runs show` then shows `cancel: null` and the provider outcome until a subsequent client request. Once a client has asked, the job ends `cancelled`, rc 130, with that client's attribution regardless of the provider receipt. The wall's dependent cascade waits until its parent attempt settles `interrupted` by `max_wall_s`; client and operator cascades remain immediate. Wall-stopped dependents record `killed_by=max_wall_s` and name their parent in details and notices (D-WALL1). A repeated family kill still traverses live dependent descendants even when the root was already cancelled by a single-job request: the root's first event and timestamp stay unchanged, and each newly cancelled descendant gets the new request's attribution. Family propagation retains requester and parent attribution on each cancelled job; independent children remain independent (C-7.3). If another session cancels a job, its caller receives a notice naming that session, the peer pid and the reason; an active job receives a service notice immediately, and a waiting job's terminal notice carries it.

- **C-17.4** Stdout carries the contract (job id on `run`, the table on `runs`, the deliverable on `runs show --out`); human progress goes to stderr; `--json` emits one JSON object per line and no prose. Online `status --json` passes through C-16.2's current-capacity snapshot: `attempts` and `jobs` have the bounded membership and attempt projection specified there, not retained history or attempt evidence. `runs --json` lists the ledger and `runs show <id> --json` supplies full attempt details and evidence, plus `cancel` with the effective request's timestamp, source, reason and requester fields (null when no cancel was recorded); the bare form includes the same metadata; offline status retains C-17.5's existing shape. `status --help` names this scope. Online and offline, `status --json` carries `task_holds` (C-11.10): each hold in force, by task, with `task`, `until`, `note`, `since` and `waiting`, the count of jobs it keeps waiting, counted over the whole store and not from the jobs the status lists (offline status lists 50), and an empty list when there are none; the `status` text shows a `task holds` section, each hold with its end, its note and that count, only while one is in force, and `readings`' capacity text a `Task holds` line. A job a hold keeps waiting shows `task-held` as its `wait_reason` wherever jobs are listed, and `why` names the task, the end, the note and the release command, `subfleet tasks release -- <task>`, the task shell-quoted as one argument after `--`.
- **C-17.5** Offline mode: when the daemon is unavailable, `runs`, `runs show`, `status`, and `kill` work by reading the store read-only and the receipts, and `kill` signals the recorded pgid only after C-5.3 identity checks; everything else exits 69 and prints `subfleet daemon start`.
- **C-17.6** `run` inside a Claude Code session (detected as v1 does, by the session registry and env) defaults to detached and prints the four-line hint v1 prints (out path, log path, wait command, status command).
- **C-17.7** `run --batch FILE` submits every job in a manifest in one call and labels them as one batch. The manifest is TOML when the file ends `.toml` and JSON otherwise (both in the standard library; the package takes no dependency): an optional `label` (default: the file's stem), an optional `defaults` table, and `jobs`, a non-empty list of at most 256 tables; a bare JSON list is `jobs`. A table says nothing `run` cannot. Its keys are `task`, `tier`, `model` (`-m`), `account` (`-a`), `codex_home` (`-H`), `workdir` (`-C`), `prompt` (`-p`), `prompt_text`, `out` (`-o`), `name`, `sandbox`, `exclude`, `allow_desktop`, `allow_tmp`, `in_place`, `independent`, `parent`, `no_preamble`, `mcp` (a list of server names; C-12.9), and `allow_unmeasured_reserve`; an unknown key is exit 2. Precedence is the entry, then `defaults`, then the other flags on the same command line. Relative `workdir`, `prompt`, `out`, and `codex_home` paths resolve against the manifest's directory, so a folder of briefs moves whole. A job with no `name` is named after its prompt file. Every entry passes `run`'s own client-side checks before the first submit; if one fails, nothing is submitted and the message names the entry (exit 2, or 7 for a client-side refusal). After that the entries are independent: a submission the daemon refuses is reported (`NOT submitted`, which names only an entry the daemon refused with an error response) and the rest still go, because a caller handing off K threads wants the K-1 that can run to run; the exit code is the first failure's, else 0. An entry whose answer is still lost after its re-send (C-16.3) is reported with its outcome unknown and its request id, and it ends the batch, because a daemon that answered neither is not answering: every later entry is reported as not sent and is not sent, the exit code is 1 unless an earlier failure's code stands, and stderr prints `subfleet run --batch <file> --request-id <batch id>`, which settles every entry and creates none twice. A daemon that refuses the connection ends the batch the same way, with exit 69, the count already submitted, and the same command. A batch that stopped waits for nothing. Stdout carries the job ids, one per line in manifest order, and with `--json` one object per entry (`batch`, `label`, `index`, `name`, `workdir`, `request_id`, `job_id`, `rc`, `outcome` (`created`, `existing`, `refused`, `unknown`, or `not-sent`), and `error` and `fix` for an entry that was not submitted, where the `fix` of an unknown or unsent entry is the command that settles it) (C-17.4); stderr carries the table and the `wait` command for the whole batch. `--wait` and a synchronous `run` wait for every submitted job. `--dry-run`, `--why`, `-p`, and a positional prompt describe one job and are exit 2 with `--batch`. The batch id is `ID` with `--request-id ID` and a fresh UUID4 without it, and entry n is always `<batch id>-n`, which must fit C-1.5's 128 characters (exit 2 before anything is sent), so submitting the same manifest again with `--request-id <batch id>` after a partial failure or a lost answer adds nothing twice (C-6.2) (incident: 2026-09-24, entries of a batch without `--request-id` each had an unrelated fresh request id, so no re-run could name them, C-16.3); an in-place entry whose checkout has since moved is reported as a different payload rather than resubmitted, because the digest covers the git head. The daemon records `{id, label, index, size}` in the `job.submitted` event and in `manifest.json`, returns it as `batch` on `show` and on each labelled row of `list`, and refuses a malformed one with exit 2. It is a label for people and the menu bar app: it is not a column, not part of the payload digest, and never an input to routing; a daemon that predates it ignores the field (C-16.2).

- **C-17.8** `doctor` reports the running daemon's environment names outside the default C-12.3/4 allowlist, including deliberately passed names and every unlisted `SUBFLEET_*` name, without reading or returning their values. `daemon.status.environment.extra_names` is a sorted list of names only, and the doctor row is `warn` for a nonempty list, `pass` for an empty list, and `unknown` when the daemon is unavailable or predates this diagnostic. The lanes' credential variables the daemon holds are listed apart, in `daemon.status.environment.lane_credentials`, on their own `pass` row ("daemon lane credential variables"), because they belong in the daemon. That row is `warn` when the daemon holds a lane as `credential-refused` (C-10.5), naming each such lane and its variable, with each lane's `lanes.json` edit (its lane id, provider and account, `credential_kind` `env` and a placeholder for a variable no launch inherits) as the fix (review r5 of #222, P3-2); each lane's words there are scrubbed as `daemon start` prints them (C-25.7; review r1 of #248); the scheduler's rule is applied to `daemon.status`'s lane rows. It is `unknown` when the daemon is unavailable or predates it. This check is included without `--live`; it requires no operating-system environment dump. Values never enter logs, notices, launch records or this diagnostic.

- **C-17.9** With no TEXT, `ping` reads a message from stdin without an unbounded wait. A terminal is never read; missing stdin or a terminal immediately sends an empty-text health check (C-15.8), as do EOF and blank input. Regular files and character devices such as `/dev/null` are read through EOF; pipes and sockets are read only while readable, up to one total `SUBFLEET_PING_STDIN_WAIT_S` deadline (default 2 s, finite values from 0 to 86400 s; malformed, negative, non-finite or larger values use the default). An open source with no data at its deadline is refused with exit 2 and usage/health-check advice; a pipe/socket that delivered data returns what arrived by the deadline. The stdin setting is exact-name launch configuration and reaches children through the C-12.3 allowlist, as `SUBFLEET_HOOK_TIMEOUT_S` does. Continuous delivery never extends the deadline. A closed or unreadable stream, including a failed `isatty()` call, is refused with exit 2. Files/devices still lacking EOF at the deadline, or input over 8 MiB, are refused after reading up to 8 MiB + 1 byte (also for regular files; the reader does not pre-check their size), which also bounds an endless character device such as `/dev/zero`. An unsupported kind or failed readiness check is refused unread; descriptorless in-memory streams are size-bounded, including text wrappers whose backing buffer is `BytesIO` (read as bytes under the same cap, then decoded with replacement). These are read/wait bounds, excluding OS scheduling, individual regular-file I/O and the separately bounded daemon request. `suggest --brief -` uses the same reader because its former `sys.stdin.read()` could hang a submission in the same way. Release's literal `ping -` and blank-text health check remain; there is no unbounded stdin escape. Message replies retain release's `parked for <recipient>` plus the reason, naming the daemon-selected session; an explicit transport delivery reply is retained when provided.

## 18. Timers (milestone 5, interface only here)

- **C-18.1** Probe cycles wait `probe_interval_s` after the preceding cycle completes (default 60 seconds). Every cycle, including an immediately requested repeat, attempts a usage read for every enabled, canonical v2, non-desktop Claude and Codex lane whether idle or busy. Existing operator-hold, auth-dead and revoked-credential latches, the server's `Retry-After`, Claude sensor failures still within the existing probe interval (durable across requested repeats and restarts), and a preceding read still behind its per-lane timeout fence remain exceptions (C-9.9, C-23.44, C-23.47). A fresh attempt reading never skips the usage read. Idle homes finish their read/heal phase, publish and release `slot:0` before busy reads start or consume Claude pacing, so busy reads never extend an idle lane's unavailability. An idle lane's probe holds `slot:0` from reservation through that idle publication; a busy lane uses the provider's no-turn usage sensor beside its attempts, with no slot lease or heal turn (C-18.3). A failed read preserves previous provider readings and their observation times; their age continues to increase. A cycle releases every timer hold it took, whatever raised (review of PR #72: a lane whose probe raised left every other lane's `slot:0` held until a restart). The default leaves refresh headroom within the 120-second reading TTL; configured intervals remain authoritative, and delayed lanes or failed reads still display stale readings honestly. Keepalive is `admission-observed` evidence every 5 h 05 m; reset-credit redemption is an action (C-19) under C-23.16 (its consume outcome table applies to the original response; redirects are never followed), evaluated after a probe cycle's usage-read phase completes, unless the cycle is cancelled or offline (a cycle with at least one Codex usage read, all of them network errors, evaluates none) and on an operator's `reset`; alerts fire on transition and at most every 6 h while persisting, as a notice for `alerts.operator_session` when one is set (C-15.8), and every alert in force is shown by `status`, `status.json` and the menu whether or not one is (C-18.4); retention runs every hour; `status.json` is published for the menu bar app every probe cycle. `status.json` judges a lane whose slot a probe holds (C-11.4's admission probe, a timer's turn, a re-enrolment's turn; quarantined or not, C-5.7a) as admission does: not dispatchable for as long as the lease is held, so it is not counted in `dispatchable_now` nor named `best_home`, and every probe lease counts toward `max_active_attempts`, when the policy sets one (C-6.4), as an active attempt does. The leases and their holders' records are read in the committed state the snapshot's rows come from (C-3.7), as admission's view reads them. Each lane row carries `probe_state`, the state of its holder's newest `probe.state` record (null when no probe holds the slot), and `probe_holder`. A lease no record names reads `uncertain`: a timer's usage read writes no record, and a timer's turn writes its first only after it took the lease. A turn's lane reads `completed` from its last record until the timer releases the lease. `codex.fleet` and `claude.lanes` carry `probe_held`, how many of their lanes a probe holds. These are additions: no field the menu bar app decodes changes name, type or meaning otherwise. The leases are laid over the cycle's snapshot after its reset-credit policy (C-19) and its alerts have judged it, so the slot an admission probe or a keepalive holds for its seconds neither triggers a credit nor raises an alert (2026-09-27: the timer's snapshot read no leases, so a lane held by a quarantined probe read as dispatchable, counted in `dispatchable_now` and could be `best_home` while admission refused it).
- **C-18.2** `status.json` also carries `jobs`, so the menu bar app shows work as well as quota: `live` (every `running`, then `waiting`, then `queued` job, oldest first within a state), `recent` (the 8 most recently finished jobs), and `counts` by live state, all three of detached work only: a turn job (kind `turn`, C-26.1) is its conversation's (C-26.12) and is shown under `conversations` (C-29.6). Each row has `job_id`, `kind`, `name`, `state`, `wait_reason` and `next_check_at` (only while `waiting`, so a finished job never shows a stale reason), `sandbox`, `workdir` (the allocated worktree once there is one), `model` and `lane_id` from the job's latest attempt (the pinned model before any attempt), `attempts`, the three timestamps, `rc`, and `batch` (C-17.7) or null. Batch labels are read from `job.submitted` events for the displayed jobs only. The section is always present, empty when nothing is running. The app keeps a batch's jobs together under one heading (`<label> · <size> jobs`) where the first of them appears, shows a waiting job's reason (`waiting: workspace`; any reason other than `capacity` is a warning), shows a failed job's exit code, and decodes a snapshot that has no `jobs` key, so an app newer than its daemon still works. The section is as fresh as the snapshot, one probe cycle (C-18.1); `subfleet runs` remains the live ledger.
- **C-18.3** A busy Claude or Codex lane is read beside its work with that provider's existing no-turn usage sensor (Claude `api/oauth/usage`, source `oauth-usage`; Codex's usage GET, source `wham`). Claude reads share the existing pacing with idle reads. A lane is busy when it has an attempt `reserved`, `starting`, `running` or `finalizing`, or any `lane:<lane id>:` lease; one transaction decides whether the lane is never read (disabled, not v2's, the desktop's, or under an open `operator-hold` or `auth-dead` closure, checked first), idle, or busy. A busy lane's read is the usage GET alone: it takes no lease and writes no reservation, so admission never sees it as a slot block or a reserved probe (C-6.10) and the next attempt on the lane takes the slot it would have taken without it; it runs no heal turn, because the lane's attempts renew its token (C-23.47); and it passes the per-lane read fence an idle read passes (one read per lane at a time, four at once, the 15 s deadline, a late answer dropped). Its result is judged after the busy read phase completes, with idle publication and lease release already finished, and it is judged and published in one store transaction: no slot keeps the lane's attempts away, so the store's write lock does, and a publication that raises rolls back whole, the settlement with it, leaving the store as it stood when the publication began, and the lane's previous verdict is put back under that lock; when it is the commit that fails, it is put back only if the publication's own verdict is still in place, checked and replaced as one step, so it never replaces one an attempt has recorded since. An `ok` or `limited` answer for the lane's own account (or a credential that names no account) publishes what an idle read of the same answer publishes: the readings, a `provider-limit` closure or its release, the reset-credit settlement that ends a confirmed override (C-23.17), and the verdict. A read whose credential names another account (the home's `auth.json`, whatever the request returned) publishes the identity mismatch as an idle read does (no provider readings, no settlement, no closure change, the lane disabled in the same transaction); no lease guards it, and a job admitted between the read and that commit runs as it would have had nothing been read; a half-written `auth.json` reads as no credential and is withheld. No other busy reading or admission verdict is published: not `auth-dead`, `revoked`, `expired-token` or `no-auth` (the lane's attempts are renewing its token as it is read, and a published `auth-dead` disables the lane until it is re-enrolled; an attempt that ends `auth-dead` disables the lane and records that verdict itself, C-23.44, and a credential failure the attempts do not classify as dead waits for the lane's next idle read, as before), and not a failed read. Failed Claude sensor reads record a separate `timer.usage-backoff` cooldown for at least the existing probe interval, extended by Retry-After, without refreshing provider timestamps, changing admission metadata, releasing closures or settling credits; requested cycles and restarts respect it. A Claude HTTP 429 carrying Retry-After is the sole exception: it publishes its cooldown verdict while preserving prior provider readings and observation times, releasing no closure and settling no reset credit. Whatever the read itself raises (its mark's query, its adapter, its request, an answer that is not a mapping) is a failed read, never a failed cycle. Nothing at all is published for a lane the timer would no longer read: one disabled, transferred, made the desktop's, held or closed `auth-dead` since its read. Every closure report leaves a `closure.recorded` event for its lane, including one that ends no later than the lane's open closure and so changes nothing in that row (`Store.put_closure` rewrites the row as it is); a busy read notes the store's newest event as it starts, and when such an event for the lane is newer than that mark at publication (a closure recorded, extended, or reported again, by an attempt, a conversation turn, an admission probe or an operator), the read publishes its readings and verdict but releases no closure and settles no reset credit, so an older answer never undoes a newer limit, whatever retention has pruned since; the next cycle reads again. Every read in a cycle, busy or idle, published or not, counts toward whether the cycle was offline, and the cycle's `timer.cycle` event names the busy lanes it read, the status of each busy read it did not publish, and, under `fenced`, each lane whose read was published while a limit was reported on it during the read. Both providers are read on every probe cycle while busy (C-18.1) (incident: 2026-09-30, codex-4 went from an operator hold straight to work with a fresh week from a reset credit; no cycle read it for 78 minutes, the credit's override stood, and the lane stayed unmeasured at one attempt while every other Codex lane was at its floor; `docs/decisions/2026-09-30-busy-lane-usage-read.md`, MaxGhenis/subfleet-v2#73).
- **C-18.4** (the alerts in force are shown) An alert is in force while its latch is active (C-23.52): from the cycle that sees its condition come back, which is the cycle it fires, except a daily alert seen again within a day of its last firing, which comes into force without firing (C-18.1), to the cycle that sees it gone. An offline cycle neither raises nor clears one (C-23.27). An alert whose notice could not be written does not latch, is not in force, and is tried again next cycle (C-18.1). With no operator session there is no recovery notice either: a recovery is the alert leaving the list. `status.json` always carries `alerts`: every alert in force, most severe first (`critical`, `warn`, `info`; an unknown severity sorts as `warn`), then the longest in force first, each with `key`, `severity`, `subject`, `body`, `since` (when it last came into force; null for one in force since before latches recorded it) and `last_sent`. The words are the latest cycle's when that cycle saw the condition, else the ones it last fired with, which its latch keeps; a latch that has none, from before alerts kept their words, shows its key. `subfleet status` prints the same list first, the body under each subject (a credential alert's body names the command to run, C-23.52; a limit's says when it resets), from `daemon.status` or, with no daemon, from the latches in the store. The menu shows them above the jobs, the body selectable so the command can be copied, and any alert above `info` marks the menu bar icon; a snapshot with no `alerts` key, from an older daemon, still decodes. Every publication of `status.json` carries the alerts in force, the probe cycle's (C-18.1) and any other, such as an operator's reset-credit pass, so an alert reaches the menu within one cycle and leaves it within one cycle of clearing, and no publication between cycles empties the list. What status and the alerts call a closed lane (2026-10-24, D-STATUS-SRC1): a lane's verdict, in `status.json` and wherever `status` and the alerts judge one, is a credential fault the cycle found (its verdict), else `auth-dead` (a closure) or `disabled`, each keeping the cycle's verdict when it has one, else `limited` (an open account-scope `provider-limit`, `credits` or `cooldown` closure), else `held` (an open `operator-hold` closure and no such limit), else the cycle's verdict, else the readings'. A cycle's `limited` is read from the closures and never kept: a held lane is not read (C-18.1), and a limit whose closure has ended is over. `dispatchable` is unchanged, so a held lane is never dispatchable. An operator hold raises no per-lane alert: it is the operator's own act and `status` shows it; a provider limit keeps its alert, key and words. The fleet-empty alert (`codex-fleet-empty`, `claude-lanes-empty`) keeps its key and subject, `<provider>: no dispatchable lanes`; its body names each cause with its count, in this order and only when it has a lane: held (each hold with its home, note and latest release bound, through C-9.6's shared wording), provider-limited (the earliest reset), credential-blocked, disabled, otherwise unavailable, and the counts sum to the provider's enrolled lanes. Only provider-limited lanes are called limited, and none is called exhausted. It is `warn` when every one of those lanes is held, else `critical` for Codex and `warn` for Claude as before. `subfleet status` lists each closure as what it is (`<home>: held by the operator[: <note>] (until <end> at the latest)`; `claude-10 provider limit on account until <end> (reported)`; credits limit, cooldown and auth-dead alike) and flags a lane row `held` or `limited` from the closures in force; `status --json` closure rows, the daemon's and the offline reader's, and those `lanes hold` answers with, the fleet's and each lane's alike (one conversion serves both daemon answers, review r1 of #263), gain `kind` (`hold`, `limit` or `auth`) and `note` (null when there is none), every other field unchanged but a withheld note's `source_event` (C-9.6). The menu shows `held` as "Held" in a neutral tone, distinct from "Limited", ahead of a held lane's stale usage. These stored closure projections retain strict source validation. The C-9.6 compatibility rule for historical pin evidence without a `source_event` field does not relax that validation: absent pin metadata renders no note, while a present invalid pin source is still withheld.

## 19. Actions

- **C-19.1** `actions` rows have kind (`merge`, `reset-credit`, later `send`), `op_key` (unique; for a merge the head sha plus PR, for a reset the account key plus the credit id), subject, state `pending` → `executing` → `confirmed` | `failed` | `unknown`, request and result JSON. The row is `pending` before the remote call; `unknown` after a timeout until a read of the remote state settles it. `status`, `why`, and `--dry-run` never create or advance an action.

## 20. Tests and release gates

- **C-20.1** Layout: `tests/unit/` (store, digest, state machine, policy evaluation, classifiers on fixtures, salvage on a temp repo), `tests/fake/` (daemon against fake providers in a temp `SUBFLEET_HOME`), `tests/process/` (guardian, containment, kill, `setsid` escape), `tests/live/` (opt-in with `SUBFLEET_LIVE=1`; one Haiku probe, one wham probe, one real launch per adapter). `tests/frontend/` compiles the Swift app's model and view layers with `xcrun swiftc` against fixture JSON the Python code produces, and is skipped off macOS.
- **C-20.2** Time budgets: unit under 30 s total, fake under 60 s, process and git under 20 s, all on this Mac. `uv run pytest -q` runs everything but live.
- **C-20.3** Crash matrix: for each attempt boundary in C-4.2 and for the notice, export, and salvage steps, a test SIGKILLs the daemon at that point with a fake job running and asserts the recovery column. Disk-full is simulated by a write hook that raises `ENOSPC` at each publication step.
- **C-20.4** Release gates before the `run` cutover are plan amendment 10, each as a named test or a recorded measurement in `docs/release-gates.md`.
- **C-20.5** Every test names the clause it proves in its docstring, for example `"""C-5.6 kill quarantines when a setsid grandchild survives."""`.

- **C-20.6** (D-ST1, test isolation) Guard production code, and fixtures that wrap or replace `subprocess`, `os` and file APIs, against accidental access to the person's live stores or keychain reader, including fixture undo. Before collection, `tests/conftest.py` gives every test an isolated HOME in a temporary session directory, sets safe store overrides and puts a recording reader first on PATH; those baselines survive fixture undo and are restored only at session cleanup. It also removes every variable whose name is credential-shaped (ending in `_TOKEN`, `_KEY`, `_SECRET`, `_PASSWORD` or `_COOKIE`, or holding `CREDENTIAL` or `_AUTH`, in any case), never the session's markers, so none is in the test process's environment, or in a child's that inherits it, while the session lives, and puts each back with its value at session cleanup (D-PRR1): pytest prints a failing traceback entry's arguments, and with `-l` its locals. The store that keeps the removed variables shows their names only, never a value, in its `repr` and `str`, and so in pytest's traceback arguments, `-l` locals and assertion operands and in Hypothesis's report; it is not a `Mapping`, which pytest diffs item by item. The baseline snapshot holds no credential-shaped value, and tests bind the removed names only. Pickling or copying the store raises `TypeError`, so neither carries a value. That is the whole guarantee: a test that reads a value out of the store (by name, `dict()`, `_values` or `__getstate__`) or copies one by hand can still print it, as can a test that prints a variable set after configure. The kernel also keeps the environment the process started with, which `KERN_PROCARGS2` returns, so after the removal `ps -E`, and `peers.process_chain` on the test process itself, still read every inherited value. A test that needs such a variable sets a dummy itself. A test needing its own HOME selects a temporary directory with `monkeypatch.setenv("HOME", str(tmp_path / "home"))`, never the account home. The audit guard folds bare reader names, searches PATH only for a bare name, as Popen does, and refuses a launch of sh, bash, zsh, dash, ksh, csh, tcsh, env, xargs, nice, nohup, taskpolicy, time, script, caffeinate, lockf, arch or stdbuf whose child can search a system binary directory (`/usr/bin`, `/bin`, `/usr/sbin`, `/sbin`, `/usr/local/bin`, `/usr/gnu/bin`) before a live session recorder. It judges every PATH key the child receives (a text and a bytes key both; a missing PATH is the wrapper default); env's options as macOS env's getopt (`-0C:iP:S:u:v`) reads them, grouped or not, with `-P`'s path, the child's PATH and `-C`'s directory; and arch's `-c`, `-d` and `-e`. It compares spellings without probing a directory: leading slashes collapsed, dot segments resolved, case and Unicode folded. A listed wrapper other than a shell is also judged by the command it runs: the first operand after its options, read as its macOS binary reads them (env's and arch's as above, each other wrapper's with the getopt string its binary holds), after env's assignments and after script's and lockf's file. nice's obsolete `-<number>` is read as `-n`, and script given no command runs `$SHELL -i` by path, or `/bin/sh -i` when SHELL is unset. That command, given by path, is refused if it is the reader or a registered stand-in; a bare command is found through the child's PATH, which the rule above judges. A command that is itself a listed wrapper is judged again with the PATH, SHELL and directory the outer one leaves, up to 8 listed wrappers deep. A `~` entry, an `env -S` string using `$`, `\` or `#`, a longer chain, and any other launch whose check cannot finish (a relative entry under a deleted working directory, an option a wrapper does not have, a bare name searched with a text and a bytes PATH key or with a PATH value that is not text or bytes) are refused. A legitimate wrapper test keeps the recorder first on its child PATH. Out of scope, requiring isolated inputs: other PATH-searching programs (`find -exec`, which nothing in this repository launches; `sudo`, `sandbox-exec`, `xcrun`, interpreters); the arguments xargs reads from its input; a shell's script operand; a wrapper run under another name; path aliases (a symlink to `/usr/bin`, or a symlink to the reader that a wrapper's bare command finds through PATH); PATH changes made inside the shell (a shell string, a login shell's profile, `BASH_ENV`, `ENV` or `ZDOTDIR`); deliberate shell strings naming the reader's absolute path; `DirEntry.stat`; and native file access through `sqlite3`. The filesystem test trap checks Python path APIs, including firmlink spellings, `io.FileIO` and `os.chdir`; it is not a native I/O fence. Tests may read non-store account-home paths (v1 source, the home directory's spelling) using `pwd`, and the original `UV_CACHE_DIR` is carried before HOME changes. The session's markers and isolated paths reach every child a test launches while the session lives, through C-12.3's selection. Cleanup deletes the sentinel, so a child environment captured during the session is then an ordinary process's: copied markers revive no isolation, even beside a copied `PYTEST_CURRENT_TEST`. A copied `PYTEST_CURRENT_TEST` without the marker selects none of the session's names either (C-12.3), whether or not a session folder was copied with it; it only makes `home_paths.under_pytest` count the child a test process, so the child refuses personal fallbacks and keychain reads, which only refuses more. A pytest process refuses whatever its os.environ holds (D-ST3). For this process `home_paths.under_pytest()` is the environment rule above or the process rule, `home_paths.pytest_loaded()`: pytest's own modules (`_pytest` or `pytest`) in `sys.modules`, which pytest's entry point imports before any conftest, plugin or test module and which no module under `subfleet/` imports. A child given an environment (`under_pytest(environ)`) keeps the environment rule alone, since it has no pytest loaded; a child that imports pytest is a test process by the process rule. Every refusal above takes either rule, each before any access. They are a missing override; an empty one, except for the callers that pass `accept_empty` (`home_path(..., accept_empty=True)`, `guard/preflight._state_root` and `daemon.main`'s default state root), where `""` names the working directory: a test process judges it as `.`, so a working directory inside a live root is refused, and otherwise returns it as `""`, as release does; a HOME that is the account's home (`is_account_home`: also the lexically normalized spelling, the one `Path.resolve` builds on, such as `home/missing/..`, and a HOME spelled `~`, `~/...` or `~<name>`, read through pwd and, literally, against the working directory, which `Path(HOME) / ...` builds on; one naming no user has only the literal reading; with no readable working directory its literal reading cannot be judged, so it is refused too, and `protected_provider_homes` names that cause) or lies in a live root or under `/.vol/` (`home_paths.personal_home`; `production_state_root` and `protected_provider_homes` refuse it); an override beginning `~` or `~<name>` that expands to such a home, whatever follows it; and an override that names the account's home, one of its live roots (`~/.subfleet`, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, `~/.claude.json`) or a path inside one, by any spelling of its name: after `~` expansion, lexically normalized against the consumer's base (the state root for a relative `SUBFLEET_CODEX_GUARD_CACHE`, otherwise the working directory; an empty override is judged as `.`), and followed as `Path.resolve` follows its links (`home_paths.names_live_root`); and any override under `/.vol/`, macOS's volfs, which names a file by its device and inode rather than by a name, so no spelling there says whether it is a live root. That one is refused by any spelling (case folded, slashes and dot segments collapsed, through the `/System/Volumes/Data` firmlink, relative to the consumer's base, or reached through a link). The judgment makes no call on a live root or anything beneath one by its name, nor on `/.vol`: a spelling is judged first, a live root by its parent, and the link walk stops before its first call on or beneath one, or on `/.vol`. A path inside this process's own code (the tree `subfleet` runs from, its interpreter and environment, where none of them holds a live root) is the process's own, since a job's checkout can sit under `~/.subfleet/worktrees`. Each raw read of a path-valued variable, an empty value included, takes the same override refusals through `home_paths.checked_override`: `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` in `adapters/claude_mcp.global_config_path`, `SUBFLEET_GUARD_TRUST`, `SUBFLEET_CODEX_GUARD_CACHE` and `CODEX_HOME` in `guard/preflight`, `CLAUDE_LANE_AGENT_SECRET` in `credentials.keychain_command`, `SUBFLEET_DAEMON_BIN` in `cli._daemond_argv`, `SUBFLEET_DEV_APP_EXECUTABLE` in `conversations/service` and `TMPDIR` in `retention_git.temp_roots`, and each searched `PYTHONPATH` entry in `doctor.check_pythonpath`. `compat.v1_binary`'s default v1 install (`SUBFLEET_V1_BIN`) and `python -m subfleet.prune_decisions`'s default state root go through `home_path` as well, and in a test process `v1_binary` refuses rather than fall back to PATH's `subfleet`; a test names its own v1 binary. A refusal is `home_paths.PersonalPathRefused`, a RuntimeError. `python -m subfleet.daemon` and the `subfleetd` console script both run `daemon.run_program`, which reports one in a single line, written as C-25.7 writes the daemon's own stderr (`daemon_log.write_scrubbed`), and exits 7; `daemon.main` raises it for a caller in the test process. A test of production behaviour (`under_pytest()` returning False, a `test_only` default) waives the process rule only through the `production_process` fixture (`tests.conftest.waive_process_rule`); the waiver holds only while HOME is neither the account's home nor inside a live root, by any spelling, read again at every call, so such a HOME, or none, keeps the rule. A test's own selection stands: an override naming any other path, under the account's home or not; a hard link to a live file, out of scope because the check judges names, not inodes, so a test that makes one names the live file itself; an explicit argument such as `--state-root`; and a PATH search (`cli._daemond_argv`'s `shutil.which("subfleetd")`, a bare-name launch), which the audit guard above judges only for the keychain reader. [owner unit; modules `tests/conftest.py`, `tests/live_store_trap.py`, `subfleet/credentials.py`, `subfleet/home_paths.py`, `subfleet/compat.py`, `subfleet/prune_decisions.py`, `subfleet/adapters/claude_mcp.py`, `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/retention_git.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/doctor.py`, `pyproject.toml`]

## 21. Milestone acceptance

| Milestone | Accepted when these tests pass |
|---|---|
| 1 | `fake`: a Codex job survives the submitting process exiting; `kill` finalizes with a salvage ref after verified containment; daemon SIGKILL during `running` re-adopts; SIGKILL during `starting` with no receipt recovers per C-4.2; `nested-setsid` scenario quarantines; guard trust preflight blocks a mismatched hash; isolation matrix; `runs`, `runs show`, `wait`, `kill` offline and online; exit codes table |
| 2 | Claude adapter parses all experiment-0 payloads to `provider` readings; a rejected event closes the model scope with the reported clock; attestation `mismatch` on the model-downgrade fixture; no percentage rendered without a `provider` reading; a hard limit closes the lane and the next attempt carries exclusions |
| 3 | C-11.6 routes to Astra with the recorded reason; `why` prints the decision; pins never fall back; both comparators apply C-11.3's weekly reserves and binding reset; unmeasured lanes rank after measured within preceding keys and use configured slots; golden decision cases from `docs/reports/D-surface.md` and the 06:31 to 06:33 log |
| 9 | `fake`: a Claude and a Codex conversation through fake providers speaking the recorded protocols: submit, acknowledgement, events, approval allow and deny, interrupt, follow-up; a lost acknowledgement retried by UUID creates one message; daemon SIGKILL mid-turn replays with no duplicate event or frame; an exit without a terminal event reconciles and never resends. `process`: guardian relay across a daemon SIGKILL. `frontend`: the app decodes every result shape in design §5 from Python-produced fixtures. `live` (recorded in the release record): one real Claude and one real Codex conversation with a follow-up, an approval, and attested served models |

## 22. Open experiments

Numbered as in plan B rev 4: 1 (optional; Claude lane homes), 2 (usage-endpoint rate limits under 14-lane probing), 3 (long-poll `wait` under the harness), 4 (`asyncRewake` in desktop and terminal), 5 (rewritten by amendment 2: `setsid` escape quarantines), 6 (process-group semantics under launchd), 7 (Codex hooks trust hash drift). Each has a written result before the milestone that depends on it.

## 23. Carried-forward invariants

Version 2, 2026-09-05. These clauses close the 92 rows of the v1 invariant ledger that version 1 left uncovered (`docs/invariants.md`, `docs/invariant-gaps.md`, where each clause's ledger rows, rationale, and incident are recorded). A clause here binds like any other; the milestone names when it is first tested. Proposal numbers `P-23.n` in the gaps document are these clauses' `C-23.n`.

- **C-23.1** (a caller's files are read-only to subfleet) No component of subfleet deletes, truncates, or rewrites a file the caller supplied. A job runs from the daemon's own copy of the prompt at `jobs/<job id>/prompt.md` (C-2.3), and the caller's `-p` file, the contents of `-C` that the provider does not itself change, and any file named in the prompt are read only to the daemon, the guardian, and every adapter. [rows 8 (`replace`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.2** (an isolated review inherits no context and holds no hosted capability) `run -I` (isolated review) requires `-s read-only` and a review root `-D`; the review root and any further sources are exposed only through the provider's read-only source flag (`--add-dir` for Claude), and the Claude launch's tool allowlist is `Read`, `Glob`, and `Grep`. A `read-only` Claude launch removes `CLAUDE_CODE_ADDITIONAL_DIRECTORIES_CLAUDE_MD`, `CLAUDE_MEMORY_STORES`, `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE_MEMORY_DIR`, and the `CLAUDE_COWORK_MEMORY_*` family from the child environment before the provider initialises. An isolated Codex review runs `--ephemeral --ignore-user-config --ignore-rules` and disables every hosted capability v1's `prepare_isolated_review` disables — apps, plugins and remote plugins, hooks, multi-agent, tool suggestion, computer and browser use, image generation, goals, memories, shell snapshots and profile sourcing, project docs, web search, and each MCP server in the home's inventory by name — with `history.persistence` set to `none`. [rows 29 (`keep`, safety-guard), 31 (`keep`, safety-guard), 45 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1 for the CLI and Codex halves, 2 for the Claude environment; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.3** (an isolated review is refused when operator policy could override it) An isolated review is refused with exit 7, naming the variable or layer, when the submission inherits `CLAUDE_CODE_MANAGED_SETTINGS_PATH`, `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE_SETTINGS_PATH`, or `CLAUDE_CODE_MOCK_REMOTE_SETTINGS`, or when the Codex home reports managed configuration requirements or a nonempty `system` or `project` configuration layer. Operator policy is never silently discarded to make an isolated review possible. [rows 30 (`keep`, safety-guard), 46 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.4** (isolation is prepared per attempt) An adapter prepares isolation from the attempt's own lane on every launch: the configuration layers, the managed-requirements check, and the MCP inventory are re-read for that lane, and no isolation state computed for a previous lane or attempt is reused. [rows 47 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.5** (how long a guard preflight verdict is good for) A guard preflight verdict is reused only while the installed Codex version, the lane home, the override string, the fingerprint of the seeded configuration, and C-10.8's CA variable names and values are all unchanged; any difference re-verifies, and a lane home the daemon has not verified under that key is verified before its first launch on that lane. A cached verdict older than 30 days is discarded. A re-picked lane whose preflight fails refuses that launch rather than launching unguarded. [rows 41 (`keep`, safety-guard), 52 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.6** (the `write_stdin` hole is closable) A Codex launch reads `SUBFLEET_CODEX_UNIFIED_EXEC`; when it is `off` the launch carries `-c features.unified_exec=false`, leaving `shell_command` as the only shell tool. `doctor` reports which setting is in force. [rows 65 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.7** (reset credits are gifts, not purchases) The reset-credit client lists and consumes only gifted entitlements. Its request URLs are an allowlist that contains no purchase, checkout, or add-credit endpoint, and no verb, action kind, or alert offers one. [rows 170 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.8** (a gate approves an exact revision) An approval binds to a fingerprint the caller attests — `--expect-sha256` for a plan, `--expect-head` with `--expect-base` for a pull request — and the gate never infers approval from a fresh read of the artifact. The fingerprint is re-captured after each peer round returns, and a round whose artifact changed while the peer was reviewing blocks instead of counting. [rows 193 (`keep`, safety-guard), 196 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.9** (what counts as a peer verdict) A peer verdict is exactly one sentinel-delimited JSON object naming the revision that was reviewed, with no text outside the sentinels; anything else is not a verdict and the round does not count. An `approve` verdict carries no findings and no notes, and a `changes-requested` verdict carries at least one finding. [rows 194 (`keep`, safety-guard), 195 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.10** (where a peer round runs and what reserves it) Each peer round is reserved under a lease held for the round's lifetime; output from a round whose lease is no longer held is discarded and never counted as an approval. The peer runs `read-only` from a neutral directory the gate allocates under `$SUBFLEET_HOME`, never inside the repository under review. [rows 198 (`keep`, safety-guard), 199 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner fake; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.11** (what a merge requires before it is attempted) A merge action runs its preflight immediately before the remote call and proceeds only when the pull request is open and not a draft, its head and base are the approved ones, its mergeability is clean, and its checks have reached a terminal green state. The merge command pins `--match-head-commit` to the approved head. [rows 201 (`keep`, safety-guard), 202 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.12** (how a landing is verified) The gate verifies a landing against the merge commit's own parents — the approved base, and for method `merge` also the approved head — never against the base branch's current tip. Merge methods are `merge` and `squash` only; a rebase landing is refused. A landing whose parents do not match is recorded as a mismatch on the action, and the gate neither retries the merge nor reverts it. [rows 203 (`keep`, safety-guard), 204 (`keep`, safety-guard), 207 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.13** (only the holder publishes an action's result) An action moves to `executing` by recording the identity of the worker that holds it, and only that holder may write its result; a result offered by any other worker is discarded and recorded as an event. A `confirmed`, `failed`, or `unknown` action is never overwritten by a later result for the same `op_key`. [rows 206 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 5 for `reset-credit`, 7 for `merge`; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.14** (what a handoff may carry) A handoff excerpt is scrubbed before it is written: private keys, JWTs, prefixed API tokens, and `Bearer` values are replaced, encoded binary is omitted, and the result of any tool call whose input matches a credential-reading pattern (`agent-secret get`, a keychain read, `env` or `printenv`, `auth.json`, `.env`, a credentials file) is omitted by pattern rather than redacted. Ordinary code, commands, and tool output are retained verbatim. Each mode (the excerpt and whole-text scrubs, `scrub_bounded` and `clean`, daemon.log's folded line and block form, `detail_line`, a suggestion's line and brief) runs release/217's pipeline for that mode, frozen in `subfleet/sessions/scrub_release.py` (release/217 at `2c981cba5`, its fold and second scrub included), and the head's pipeline for it, both over the same text, and writes release's output but for each whitespace-free run of it that the head's output does not hold, which becomes `[REDACTED]` (`handoff.shown_by_both`); a longer text is excerpted where both scrubbers allow a cut (the head's, which refuses every break release refuses), each excerpt kept for the runs the head's scrub of the same excerpt holds. A bounded mode writes its text so before it cuts it: within the budget `scrub_bounded` and `clean` write the whole-text scrub so written, cut by `truncate` (C-23.36; #239 round 9); past it, each excerpt so written, cut as release cuts its own (a cut that, like release's own, can fall inside a placeholder, release's or one the head's scrub added, and leave its start or end, `[REDA` or `ACTED]`; such a fragment shows nothing either scrub hides), with release's marker between them, the count of what release's own cut of the same excerpts leaves out; and a suggestion's line is release's line so written, then cut (#239 round 10, review r9b P2-1 and P2-2).
