So no mode shows a character that release/217's scrub in that mode takes out, by construction, nor a value that the head's hides; the head's holds per value, not per place: a value the head hides at one place may show there where the same value shows elsewhere in every output (above `echo SECRET7co1`, `token:--x_token SECRET7co1` is `token:--x_token [REDACTED]` in the head's output and `[REDACTED] SECRET7co1` in the mode's, which shows which value is the token's; #239 review r9b P3-1). A bound cuts the text so written, so it can keep a character that release's own cut of its output leaves out, but never one either scrub takes out. Approvals mask what release's masking leaves (`mask_approval`). The rules below are the head's pipeline, and each example gives that pipeline's output, which a mode writes only where release's output holds the same runs (#239 round 8). A secret assignment's key may be bare or quoted, with its closing quote escaped by a run of backslashes (a repr's `\'password\'`, or JSON encoded more than once: `\"password\"`, `\\\"password\\\"`), before a `:` or `=` with any whitespace around it. A value whose opening quote such a run escapes closes at the next quote of its kind escaped by a run of the same length (on its line, except in a whole-text scrub), so the text after it on its line is kept; a value with no closing quote is redacted through the end of its line or record, so a missing closing quote never leaves the value visible. Overlapping assignments are masked as one union in whole-text and excerpt scrubs alike, so a first value that pairs with a later assignment's opening quote (`PASSWORD='a TOKEN='b`) hides both; in an excerpt a union spans a line break only between a key and its value, where no excerpt is cut. A header's name is an identifier ending in `authorization` or `cookie` (`Authorization`, `Proxy-Authorization`, `X-Authorization`, `Cookie`, `Set-Cookie`), and it takes a header's value, never a plain one. The name keeps its header word, but the part before that word goes when it holds a run that reads as a secret (six or more word characters or `-`, with a letter and a digit, as for a flag below), but for the name's leading `-`, which stays (`-SECRET7…Cookie:` becomes `-[REDACTED]Cookie:`, so a fold refuses the run after redaction as it does before it), whatever becomes of the value, wherever a header rule reads the name: an inline one before spaces or tabs and `:`, and one that starts its line before any whitespace and `:` with a value after it, a line break, `\x0b`, `\x0c`, `\xa0` or `\r` included (`x`, `SECRET7…Cookie` and `: v` on three lines becomes `x`, `[REDACTED]Cookie` and `: [REDACTED]`): `secret:"x"SECRET7…Authorization: B` becomes `secret:"[REDACTED]"[REDACTED]Authorization: [REDACTED]` in every mode. A name the rule leaves starting with `[REDACTED]` at its line's start, after its leading `-` if it has one (`[REDACTED]Cookie:`, `-[REDACTED]Cookie:`), is still read as a name that starts its line, so its value goes whole, already redacted or not, and daemon.log's folded line keeps nothing of that header's line (`SECRET7…Cookie: x` and `SECRET7…` on the next line becomes `[REDACTED]Cookie: [REDACTED]` there). One that starts its line takes the rest of that line, as before (and more only where stated below). A header, inline or at a line start, whose value would start on a later line than its colon leaves that line's own header to it, and takes nothing: a header the inline rule reads there, or one the line-start rule reads there, a name or value that a line break parts from its colon included (`curl -H "Cookie:` with `Cookie: sid=…"` on the next line; `x Cookie:`, `Set-Cookie` and `:v SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `x Cookie:`, `[REDACTED]` and `:[REDACTED]`, as in both references; `Set-Cookie:`, `Cookie: --api-key` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Set-Cookie:`, `[REDACTED] [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`, the named-value rule taking `Cookie:` as `Set-Cookie`'s plain value). One anywhere else on a line, unquoted and followed by spaces or tabs and `:` (not `=`), is an inline header (`curl -H 'Authorization: Basic …'`, `wget --header='Cookie: …'`, a header in log prose, a header a refusal's daemon.log prefix puts mid-line): its value starts at the first visible character after the colon, on the next line if none follows on its own, and ends at the closing quote matching one that opens right before the name (the same kind of quote, escaped by a run of backslashes of the same length: `-H '…'`, `--header="…"`, JSON's `\"…\"`) on the value's line, or else at that line's end. So an unquoted inline header loses the rest of its line, as a line-start header does. A header's value is redacted whole and counted once per secret-looking run; an inline value that already starts with `[REDACTED]` is left alone (but for a name the rule above leaves starting with `[REDACTED]` at its line's start), which keeps the words daemon.log's folded line puts after a redacted header, so a text that already holds that marker before a secret keeps the secret. A second scrub of a scrubbed text never shows anything the first hid, but it may hide more (`cookie:AUTHORIZATION :` with a value on the next line loses the rest of the `:` line), so scrubbing is monotone, not idempotent. The token and header rules run before the named-value rules and never take what one of those rules owns. A `Bearer` run is not a token when it ends in a credential word and an assignment's separator (`token_type: Bearer` and `access_token: …` on the next line, `scheme=bearer oauth.client_secret=…`) or in a credential flag that may own a value (`--api-key …`); the run glued before such a flag is redacted, token or not (`Bearer SECRET7--secret x` becomes `Bearer [REDACTED]--secret [REDACTED]`), and the run before a key's credential word is still redacted when it reads as a token itself, eight or more characters with a letter and a digit (`invalid Bearer SECRET…_token: expired` becomes `invalid Bearer [REDACTED]_token: [REDACTED]`), and approvals mask the same token. Conversely, in that key form only, a run before the word that does not read as a token is shown with the key: `invalid Bearer SECRET7_token: expired` keeps the seven-character `SECRET7`, which 473fe2941 and release/217 at c2e080e44 show too and the scrubber before #231 (3fbc4b7c5) hid by taking the key; 3fbc4b7c5 is no reference, and release/217's own pipeline, which every mode runs (above), shows it too. That is decided once per match, from the run's end, so the head's rule costs what release/217's pattern did before #231; every mode also runs release/217's own rule, #231's lookahead, so a scrub costs release's work and the head's. A credential key, credential flag or `Bearer` that ends a header's value, a `Bearer` run, a plain value, a credential flag's value or a quoted value with no closing quote, and owns a value past it, is left to its own rule with what follows it, and its value is redacted exactly as it would be without the enclosing rule: a key whose value is past the line (`Authorization: client_secret=` and the value on the next line), of which a header keeps only the credential word, so a secret glued to the word stays in the redaction and a compound key's own name before its word goes with it (`Cookie: a=…; session_token=` keeps `token=`) (`Cookie: sid=SECRET…token` and `====` on the next line); a flag that whitespace or the text's end follows (`Authorization: x --password`, `api_key=x--password …`, `--password x--token …`, `api_key='x --password` and the value on the next line), and one that whitespace parts from the closing quote that ends a quoted inline header's value, which owns the quoted value after that quote as with no header around it (`curl -H 'Cookie: a=1 --password 'SECRET7…'` becomes `curl -H 'Cookie: [REDACTED] --password [REDACTED]`, as `--password 'SECRET7…'` becomes `--password [REDACTED]`); and a `Bearer` that ends an inline or unclosed value, or a key's plain value within a quoted match (`api_key='x Bearer` and the token on the next line; `api_key='x token:Bearer` and the token on the next line, as without the quote; `secret:'Cookie:` and `token:Bearer` on the next line). But a credential flag that ends a quoted value with no closing quote, and whose name is itself a key whose separator is on the next line, ends nothing: the quoted value takes the flag with that key's value, as it takes any other such key, and as both references do (`Authorization:"--password` or `Authorization:"--1_token`, and `=r SECRET7…` on the next line, becomes `Authorization:[REDACTED]` in the excerpt, whole and `clean` modes, and, after an empty line, in daemon.log's two lines too). So does such a flag that ends the value of a header's name that a quoted match swallows, read to its line's end, or a key's plain value within a quoted match (`Authorization:"Authorization:`, `--password` and `:r SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Authorization:[REDACTED]`, and `Set-Cookie:"Authorization`, `:--1_token` and `=r SECRET7…` becomes `Set-Cookie:[REDACTED]`, in every mode but `detail_line`, which shows `SECRET7…` there as both references do). Three cases fail closed instead. A credential flag whose name reads as a secret (a run of six or more word characters or `-` with a letter and a digit: `--SECRET7…_token`) owns nothing and is itself redacted: the enclosing rule takes it, or with none around it the flag rule does, and either also takes the value daemon.log's folded line would give it: the next run past any whitespace and blank lines unless it starts with `-`, or, where that run starts at a header's name (`Cookie:x …` on the next line), that header's whole line to its last visible character, since the run would take the name and leave the rest of the line to no rule; but where the header's value starts on a later line (below), or a key, flag or `Bearer` ends that line and owns a value past it, the fold takes nothing, and the line is its own header's, whose rules take that item and its value as they would with no fold before it (`Cookie: --password`, `Cookie: --api-key` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]`, `Cookie: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`). That value ends before a key, flag or `Bearer` that ends it and owns a value past it, and before any credential flag that ends it and may own a value past it, a secret-named one included, which the flag rule then takes with its own value as it would with no span around it (`sent Cookie: x --SECRET7…_token` and `SECRET7…` on the next line becomes `sent Cookie: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]` in every mode; `deploy --SECRET7…_token value` becomes `deploy [REDACTED] [REDACTED]`; `--SECRET7…_token` and `KEY=--db3-password SECRET7…` on the next line becomes `[REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED][REDACTED] [REDACTED]`; `Cookie: --password` and `Cookie:x SECRET7…` on the next line becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`). Failing closed over-redacts, and that is its stated cost, of these kinds. An ordinary flag name that holds a digit among six or more word characters or `-` reads as secret-named, so `deploy --k8s-token` and `echo done` on the next line becomes `deploy [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED] done`, and `kubectl --oauth2-token abc --verbose` becomes `kubectl [REDACTED] [REDACTED] --verbose`; such a flag's fold chains too, past the flag a fold ends before (`--SECRET7…_token`, `KEY=--db3-password` and `echo done` on three lines becomes `[REDACTED]`, `[REDACTED][REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED] done`). A fold that starts at a header's name takes the name with its line (`Cookie: --password` and `Cookie:x ok` on the next line becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`), and at a quoted name it takes the line on past the name's closing quote (`curl --oauth2-token "Authorization: Bearer abc" -X POST https://api.example.com` becomes `curl [REDACTED] [REDACTED]`; `deploy --SECRET7…_token "Cookie:x" --password SECRET7…` becomes `deploy [REDACTED] [REDACTED]`, the `--password` flag's name with it). And the part of a header's name before its header word reads as a secret whenever it holds a digit among six or more word characters or `-`, secret or not (`X-Auth0-Authorization: Basic x` becomes `[REDACTED]Authorization: [REDACTED]`, `curl -H 'X-Amz1-Cookie: a=b' …` becomes `curl -H '[REDACTED]Cookie: [REDACTED]' …`, `session_v2_cookie: abc123` becomes `[REDACTED]cookie: [REDACTED]`). A line-start header reaches past its line as an inline one does. A header whose whole value is a credential flag takes the flag with that value the same way, whatever took its line from the line before: a fold, a refused `Bearer`'s line, or a header whose value would start on that line (`Cookie: --password` and `SECRET7…` on the next line becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`; `Authorization: Bearer`, `Set-Cookie: --api-key` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Authorization: [REDACTED]`, `[REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`): kept, daemon.log's folded line would read the header's value to the record's end, past any cut (C-25.7). It does not reach two such headers, whose value shows in the excerpt, whole and `clean` modes, as in both references: a quoted header on that line (`Authorization:`, `'Cookie: --api-key` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Authorization:`, `[REDACTED] --api-key` and `SECRET7…`), and a header on that line that isn't at the value's start (`x Cookie:`, `x Cookie: --api-key` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `x Cookie:`, `[REDACTED] --api-key` and `SECRET7…`). And a header that a `Bearer` ends, whose run on the next line `Bearer` refuses as a key, takes that line to its end, ended as a folded value is (before a key, flag or `Bearer` that owns a value past it, and before any credential flag that ends it, whose rule takes it with its value), as release/217 did by joining the two lines (`Authorization: Bearer` and `client_secret=x SECRET7… tail` becomes `Authorization: [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`; `x cookie: Bearer`, `client_secret=x --SECRET7…_token` and `SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `x cookie: [REDACTED]`, `[REDACTED] [REDACTED]` and `[REDACTED]`); but where that run is a header's name and the line would end so, the header takes nothing there, and the line is that header's, as a fold's is (above). No fold, no line a refused `Bearer` takes, and no run the `Bearer` rule redacts before a key's credential word (above) reaches into a header the line-start rule reads on a later line: where one would end before the colon of a header that starts its line, its name parted from the colon by a line break or, past a fold's run, by other whitespace than spaces and tabs (`Set-Cookie` and `: v SECRET7…` on the next line), or before that header's value, which a line break after the colon puts on a later line (`Set-Cookie :` and `;v SECRET7…` on the next line), it takes nothing there, and that rule takes the header's value whole, its name's secret part too (`Cookie: --password`, `Set-Cookie` and `: v SECRET7…` on three lines becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]`, `Set-Cookie` and `: [REDACTED]`, as in both references; `Cookie: --password`, `Set-Cookie :` and `;v SECRET7…` becomes `Cookie: [REDACTED]`, `Set-Cookie :` and `[REDACTED]`, as in both; `Authorization: Bearer`, `SECRET7…Set-Cookie` and `: v SECRET7…` becomes `Authorization: [REDACTED]`, `[REDACTED]Cookie` and `: [REDACTED]`, where both references show both secrets). A flag or `Bearer` that ends a value running to its line's end is read at the line's last visible character, so whitespace after it does not hide it. A header's name is such a key, and the inline header rule is its rule: a name that a header's value would take, with the name's own value past the line, takes a header's value of its own (`X-Authorization:` with `Authorization:` on the next line and its value after that; `Cookie:` ending another header's line), because the inline rule reads the text a second time after the line-start rule, names at a line start included. That second reading always runs, so it also reads a header that a token rule's replacement makes (`Bearer` joining a value's line to its token's line, or redacting a token before `Authorization:`), whatever else the text holds. A flag's value is otherwise read on the flag's own line only, so a value on a later line, past any number of blank lines, is redacted only where daemon.log's folded line joins it to the flag's line, with or without an enclosing rule; a shell continuation (`--password \` and the value on the next line) gives the flag the backslash as its value, so the next line's word shows in every mode, the folded line included; `curl -u user:…` has no rule. The JWT, prefixed-token and long base64 rules read only their own token shapes, so a key of such a shape (`sk-proj-…_token=`) is read as a token. Secret assignments and flags are scanned by identifier in linear time, with quoted spans and removed values scanned without repeated suffix searches: the unclosed values on a line, and the header names a quoted match swallows there, read the line's end once, however many share it (a test counts it for the unclosed values; the swallowed names read through the same memo, which no measured input needs), and so do the folds at header names on one line that a key, flag or `Bearer` ending it leaves taking nothing (each thread keeps that answer for its own scrub, and drops it, with the text, when the scrub ends). No rule but those for blocks that span lines runs over more than 262,144 characters at once. In a longer block, reminders, private keys and data URIs are first resolved over all of it, exactly as in a scrub of the whole block, by scans whose work is linear in its length; if it is still longer, every other rule runs only on a head excerpt (its whole lines within the first 131,072 characters) and a tail excerpt (its whole lines within the last 131,072), joined by a marker giving the characters left out, and the result is bounded like any other text. Those rules match within a line, except across a line break after a `:` or `=` that follows a key (an identifier with a sensitive ending, headers included), after a key or its closing quote when a `:` or `=` is the next visible character, after `Bearer` when a token character is, or after a credential flag when anything but `-` is (the rules that take a flag with a value on a later line, above; the `Bearer` and header rules look past a break to decide about a key only after that key, and an inline header's value crosses a break only between its colon and its first visible character, where no excerpt is cut); an excerpt is cut only at a line break where none of these applies, so no match spans a cut and each excerpt scrubs exactly as these rules scrub the whole block there: the excerpts never show a value that the same rules over the whole block remove. Release's own rule for a cut allows every break these refuse but the credential flag's, so the excerpts are cut where both allow; a mode writes release's scrub of each excerpt kept for the runs the head's scrub of it holds, and a bound then cuts it, so an excerpt may hide a run the whole block keeps (a run kept only for its other excerpt), and never shows a value the whole block's hides. (A whole-text scrub also masks a quoted value across line breaks, so it can remove more than its excerpts would.) An excerpt with no such line break within reach (a first or last line longer than an excerpt, say) keeps nothing. The assembled handoff brief is scrubbed whole, once more; C-23.36 keeps its sections within the budget. Credential-read suppression matches a tool input of any size in linear time (a wrapper's flags, as in `sudo -E env`, are walked once), so a large input is classified by what it holds, not by its size. - **C-23.15** (transcript usage is summed once per message) Token usage read from a Claude transcript is summed once per `message.id`: a repeated assistant message replaces the record already held for that id and never adds to it. The count is a `readings` observation, not a job column. [rows 128 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`]
- **C-23.16** (when a reset credit may be spent) A gifted reset credit is spent only for a job waiting on Codex capacity, on a lane of that job's own route, one credit at a time, and never while a lane that job could use has room (incidents: 2026-09-22, all six banked credits spent in about four hours, the first with nothing waiting and each later one because the lane just reset was busy at its slot cap and so read as exhausted; 2026-09-30, a credit spent on a lane under an operator hold). `ResetCredits.evaluate` spends at most one credit per evaluation, and only when (a) to (f) all hold, for the timer after a probe cycle's usage-read phase completes, unless the cycle is cancelled or offline (C-18.1) and for an operator's `reset codex <lane>` alike. (a) Demand: a job is `waiting` with `wait_reason` `capacity` and is not being cancelled, its latest admission look evaluated its route and no lane would take it (`lane_wait` in C-6.10's record), and admission's latest hold of it is for that same reason: the hold the pass now running gave it, once that pass has reached the job, else the last whole pass's. The daemon supplies these jobs in admission order (`Daemon._reset_demand`), each judged on a fresh evaluation of its route as admission makes it: the lanes its earlier attempts exclude stay excluded, a transient retry waiting for a slot on its own lane is waiting, and a reset lane's readings are held out as C-23.17 holds them. A job no look has held since the daemon started, and a job held at the door (`disk`, `machine-busy`), behind an older job, for a full fleet, a lease, a probe or a person, is never the reason a credit is spent, from the moment that hold is made, not only once the pass that made it ends. Nor is a job whose route evaluation finds the fleet or its parent at its cap (the decision's `capacity_blocks`): it is `capped` when no route lane has room, else `lane-has-capacity`; neither verdict is demand, because a reset would buy it no slot. With no such job, or no admission view at all, nothing is spent, and the queue is read only once the cheaper gates have passed: the marker, the switch, one unsettled action, the interval, (d), and at least one lane a credit could go on. (b) No room: the job's route leaves every lane limited, closed or out of its reach, and at least one is a limited Codex lane. Limited means out of quota in a way a confirmed reset clears (C-23.17): an account-wide `provider-limit` or `credits` closure, a `cooldown`, or `below-floor` on a fresh measured reading. Closed means a `provider-limit` or `credits` closure scoped to the job's model, which a reset leaves standing: no room, and never a candidate. Out of reach means no quota would let the lane take the job: excluded, the desktop login, a config directory a turn cannot use, another owner, disabled, a mismatched or latched credential, the reserve (C-11.7) holding it for another model, an `operator-hold` or `auth-dead` closure. Any other lane is room, and the job waits for it: a busy lane (no free slot, a probe or a pilot holding it, the fleet or a parent at its cap), a lane not yet measured, and a lane freshly reset. A lane is judged by its best state across the route's models of either provider. (c) One credit, on the job's own lane: on a limited Codex lane of that job's route whose usage endpoint reports the account `limit_reached` in a fresh read of that same account, with no C-23.17 override standing and, for the timer, no fresh weekly reading with headroom above the floor (then only a shorter window is full, and it reopens within hours); and whose entitlements list holds a concrete `available` credit of type `codex_rate_limits` never attempted before (C-19.1 `op_key`). Neither the limit nor the credit alone admits it. C-23.38 orders the job's candidate lanes and C-23.46 excludes; jobs are taken in admission order, a job whose lanes hold no gift yields to the next, and no lane's entitlements are listed twice in one evaluation. Listing takes seconds, so the chosen job is judged again just before the spend (`demand-changed` if a lane of its route has room by then, or the chosen lane is no longer limited for it), and the chosen lane's eligibility, its holds and (d) are judged again on the lanes as the store then has them, at the time the daemon's clock then reads, just before the spend and once more in the transaction that writes the action (`lane-changed`, `reset-lane-open`); the job must still be waiting on capacity when the action is written (`demand-gone`). The action's request records the job and the demand it was spent for. A confirmed consume makes that job due at once if it still waits on capacity, and so does an `unknown` one when a usage read later reconciles it open (C-19.1) and the job has had no attempt since (event `reset-credit.job-due`). Admission keeps no lane for the job: with no slot caps (C-6.4) a reset lane takes every waiting job it can. (d) One at a time: no credit is spent within `min_interval_min` of the latest `updated_at` among `confirmed` or reconciled actions, measured from the evaluation's start. Normal publication stamps that row with the daemon's clock, clamped to the evaluation's start, once the bounded consume call returns, to the store's whole second; listing time before a completed consume therefore cannot shorten its interval. Recovery of an executing orphan changes only its state to `unknown`, retaining its claim timestamp, and reconciliation appends an event without changing that timestamp. The interval uses this durable timestamp; it does not reconstruct the actual time of an ambiguous request. No credit is spent while any lane reset in the last seven days (a `confirmed` consume, or an `unknown` one a usage read reconciled open) still has room (`reset-lane-open`): a busy, unmeasured or freshly reset lane has room; a lane under an account-wide limit closure, with a fresh reading below the floor outside the override, held, or disabled has none. One unsettled action (`pending`, `executing`, or an `unknown` not yet reconciled) blocks every other. The C-23.16 (d) outcome table below covers every HTTP status and submission outcome. A consume is `failed` only when an explicit refusal proves this request spent no credit (`refused`), or nothing was sent (`no-auth`, the marker's `inhibited`, or `aborted-before-submission`: the deadline or a cancel came before the call started, or a request still running on the lane held its slot). Every other answer that is not a confirmed reset is `unknown`: the credit may be spent, so it blocks every spend until a fresh successful account usage read reconciles it open, and none of its lane's closures is released until then. Only a 2xx with a recognized reset body directly confirms a reset; a non-2xx with that body remains unknown. All of this is read from the `actions` table and its reconciliation events at every evaluation, never from memory, so a restart grants no redemption. (e) Never on a held lane, and the operator's lane only: a lane that is disabled, owned by another, under an `operator-hold` or `auth-dead` hold, latched, or holding another account's credential takes no credit, whatever demand says, and its entitlements are never listed, not even to apply C-23.46; holds are read again before each listing. A hold is read from the lane's open closures of any scope and from an operator hold's own `lane.held` and `lane.released` events, because `Store.put_closure` keeps one open closure per lane and scope and a hold that ends before an open account limit leaves no `operator-hold` row. An operator's `reset codex <lane>` obeys (a) to (f): it spends only on the lane it names, only for a job whose limited lanes include it, and never on another lane in its place; a held lane is refused as `lane-held`. Only the timer applies (c)'s weekly-headroom test. `reset codex --dry-run` judges the same demand and lists and writes nothing; it answers `would-evaluate` with the candidate lanes when a spend could follow. (f) Switches: `reset_credits.enabled` false refuses every spend, the timer's and an operator's (`disabled`), as before this rule, and `default_policy.json` ships it false. A `no-reset` file in the state root (a dangling link included) refuses every evaluation before anything is listed, again before each listing and before the action is written, and at the consume itself, whatever the policy says (`inhibited`). The daemon logs at startup whether automatic redemption is on and whether the marker is present. The importer carries v1's `auto_reset.enabled: false` into `reset_credits.enabled: false` and v1's hold file into the marker, never the other way (`docs/migration.md`, row `reset-settings`). Each consume carries a fresh UUID4 `redeem_request_id` in the action's request JSON. Only a response with code `reset` and a positive integer `windows_reset` (not a boolean) moves the action to `confirmed`. [rows 161 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 164 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 165 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`; tests `tests/unit/test_reset_credit_demand.py`, `tests/unit/test_reset_credit_properties.py`, `tests/unit/test_reset_credit_review_r1.py`, `tests/unit/test_reset_credit_review_r2.py`, `tests/fake/test_reset_credit_incident.py`]

  C-23.16 (d) consume outcome table (every row is covered by round-2 and transport regressions):

  | Answer | Action state | Submission evidence |
  |---|---|---|
  | 2xx with `code=reset` and positive integer `windows_reset`, excluding booleans | `confirmed` | Recognized reset confirmation |
  | Any other 2xx, including malformed/non-object JSON or unfamiliar body | `unknown` | May have spent |
  | Any 3xx, including a reset-shaped body; redirects are never followed | `unknown` | The original consume response does not prove refusal |
  | 409 with `error.code=already_redeemed` or `already_consumed`, or 429 with `error.code=rate_limited`; body contains only `error`, whose only fields are `code` and optionally a string `message` | `failed` (`refused`) | Explicit rejection of this consume |
  | Every other 4xx, including empty/unknown bodies or fields, 408, reset fields, or a refusal code on the wrong status | `unknown` | No proof nothing was spent |
  | Any 5xx, regardless of body | `unknown` | May have spent before answering |
  | Any other HTTP status outside 2xx–5xx | `unknown` | Unrecognized response |
  | No credential, inhibit marker, or deadline/cancel/occupied HTTP slot before the call starts | `failed` (`no-auth`, `inhibited`, `aborted-before-submission`) | Nothing submitted |
  | Timeout, connection failure, or exception after the call starts | `unknown` | May have submitted |

- **C-23.17** (a confirmed consume is the authority) A `confirmed` consume is the later `provider` reading C-9.6 requires: it releases the lane's weekly closure, sets the weekly reset clock to now plus seven days with `clock_source: guessed`, and leaves the lane dispatchable even while the usage endpoint still reports the old window. It also clears the lane's `local-backoff` dispatch closure. [rows 167 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 169 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.18** (an unreadable count is not a small count) A fleet-wide credit total is rendered only when every lane's count was readable; if any lane's count is unknown the total is `null` and is displayed as unknown, never as a sum over the readable lanes. [rows 168 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.19** (when a five-hour window is open) A keepalive reading's observed-at instant is the moment the provider request is sent, not when the worker was queued or the credential was read. A lane whose window was opened by any request inside the last five hours — a keepalive, a completed attempt, or an attempt still running — is skipped and recorded `skipped-open`. An attempt that ended rc 5 without a recorded provider session did not open the window. [rows 172 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 173 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 174 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/keepalive.py`]
- **C-23.20** (revive measures the lane it is about to use) Revive admits a lane only on a `provider` reading taken in the same pass; a stored reading, a `stale-provider` reading, or the absence of a closure never qualifies a lane on its own. [rows 189 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 6; owner fake; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.21** (how a provider binary is found) A provider binary is resolved to an absolute path before launch, never left to `PATH` alone. Claude resolves `SUBFLEET_CLAUDE_BIN`, then `~/.local/bin/claude`, then `PATH`; Codex resolves `SUBFLEET_CODEX_BIN`, then `PATH`, then its known install locations. The resolved path goes into the launch argv, is recorded on the attempt, and is printed by `doctor`. [rows 26 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 158 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.22** (accounting never changes an outcome) Usage accounting runs synchronously inside finalization, before the notice is written, and never changes a job's class, rc, deliverable, or state. A hook failure or a parse failure records an `events` row and finalization continues, exactly as a failed export does under C-8.3. [rows 35 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 129 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 1 for the finalization ordering, 2 for the parser; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.23** (how the guard preflight probes) The guard preflight never writes a lane's provider home: it seeds a scratch home under `$SUBFLEET_HOME` with copies of the lane home's `config.toml` and `hooks.json`, copies no credential or session store, and probes there. The probe carries a deadline: it fails at once if the app-server exits without answering, and at the deadline it SIGTERMs the probe's process group, waits, then SIGKILLs it. [rows 50 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 53 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 1; owner process; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.24** (a guard check writes nothing) The guard check verb evaluates a payload against the copied hook and prints the verdict without writing the hook's denial log, the preflight's cache, or any other guard telemetry. [rows 56 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 8 (guard parity); owner unit; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.25** (installing a hook into a shared settings file) Before changing `~/.claude/settings.json` the installer writes a timestamped backup beside it, and the change preserves every key the installer does not own. A second install of the same version makes no further change and writes no further backup. [rows 70 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/hooks.py`]
- **C-23.26** (secondary records are pruned by age, never by a caller's window) The maintenance pass deletes notices in state `surfaced` or `acknowledged` whose row is older than 14 days, and never prunes a `pending` or `offered` notice by age. A `pending` or `offered` service notice leaves the store only by an operator's withdrawal (C-15.8), which records what it removed, or by C-11.8's withdrawal of a pin notice no one has been shown. A provider scan-cache entry is dropped only when its file no longer exists or its record is more than seven days old; a narrower scan window never evicts an entry. [rows 80 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 141 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2 for the scan cache, 4 for notices; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.27** (one monitoring cycle, one verdict) A cycle heals before it persists: `status.json`, the readings the cycle writes, its alert conditions, and its history all see post-heal verdicts. At most one refresh probe runs per provider home per cycle, and two probes of the same home are at least twenty minutes apart. A cycle in which every provider probe failed with a network error is recorded `offline` and emits no alert of any kind, including scoped-limit conditions. [rows 152 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 153 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 154 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 5; owner fake; module `subfleet/timers.py`, `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.28** (mirror health is a state file, not a quiet log; health is not reach) Mirror health is judged from the mirror's per-pass state sidecar, never from log recency: a pass the sidecar records as in flight is healthy until thirty minutes after its recorded start, and only then is the mirror `stalled`. Health is not reach: the desktop app lists a session folder into its sidebar only when it loads that folder (at launch, on an account or org switch, at the first login after a logout, and when a new app window builds its session API), so a record the mirror copies into the loaded folder afterwards is missing from the running app's sidebar until its next load, and a flag the mirror writes there is overwritten by the app's next save of that record. The mirror therefore puts each new session whose transcript exists into every folder within about `mirror_hot_interval_s` (2 s, a `policy.json` cap under C-6.4) of the write while no full pass holds its worker. Both passes sync changed sessions' archive, star, title and setting fields. A full pass keeps `mirror.lock` (flock `LOCK_EX|LOCK_NB`) and, once this process has completed its first inventory, synchronously services flag-only hot passes while it inventories, copies, restores or prunes, using a separate inventory snapshot: service is due after `mirror_hot_interval_s` from full-pass start or the previous service's finish, and begins at the next checkpoint (between entry reads, every 64 directory entries while listing, and between other work units). Zero disables this service as it disables the hot timer. A warm full inventory therefore cannot hold flag sync until its completion; cold startup completes one inventory before any hot snapshot is forked. This is a scheduling bound, not a fixed wall-clock completion promise: the current indivisible operation or flag transaction finishes first, the hot inventory and publication take their own time, and a cold process must discover all copies before it can decide flags. A stalled filesystem or unscheduled thread has no wall-clock bound. Embedded service never spreads, repairs, revives or prunes, so it cannot change the identities or liveness on which the full pass is acting; its projected records are immutable and its cache containers and payload reference counts are separate, and each known copy, flag write or prune invalidates both inventories even if the directory timestamp does not move. Only the flock holder writes, and a flag transaction (decision through matching base publication) never nests another one. After embedded service, the full pass refreshes its flag snapshot while continuing to service hot checkpoints; if a service leaves a copy write standing (including a failed put-back), cannot observe the outcome of a successful copy write, or publishes a merge base whose `(isArchived, isStarred, title)` differs for any session (including a new row), it invalidates that refresh. Merely considering or holding candidates, or changing transcript metadata alone, does not invalidate it. The full pass repeats an invalidated refresh, at most twice, then holds its decision for the next full pass if changes continue. It never commits a stale snapshot over a newer merge base. Hot progress records its own start, finish, error and held causes under `hot` in the sidecar and in `sessions mirror --status`; idle starts and finishes are sampled every 60 seconds, every recorded start is finished, and completed changes, errors and hold transitions are recorded immediately; it never changes the full pass's `pass`, `updated_at` or `last_ok_at`. The mirror decides each session's flags from every copy or not at all (a folder that fails to list is read by name from its last listing only while its directory is unchanged since, and otherwise holds every session; an account that fails to list keeps the org folders its last listing named only if each was listed itself, and otherwise holds every session, in the hot pass as in the full one; a copy that exists and cannot be read holds the session its folder's last listing saw in it, including when the hot pass rereads that copy; only sessions individually held by flag sync enter the hot retry set, blind holds wait for the 60-second full pass, and an identity with no copies left is dropped from retry; what the app cannot read either (permission denied, a directory, FIFO or device, or a symlink loop, where a record should be) is in no sidebar and holds nothing; a store that fails to list fails the pass; bases of sessions a pass did not see survive it; held sessions and their causes are recorded in `flags_held` and `held_by` and shown by `sessions mirror --status`), skips publication of an unchanged merge base, writes each session's flag changes whole or not at all, only over copies still as it read them (a write that finds its copy rewritten since the check puts back the copies the batch already wrote, and a pass cancelled before it publishes writes nothing; an app rename in the instant between the mirror's last signature check and its own rename is the one save the mirror can overwrite), and reports, from a journal of its own writes and the app's log, how many of its copies into the loaded folder postdate that load and still wait for a relaunch (`sessions mirror --status`, `sessions list`, `doctor`). The daemon runs the mirror on the timers' own worker, which `Daemon.close()` waits for (`Timers.stop()`), so the mirror opens what it reads outside its state root (the store's records, transcripts, archived transcripts, the app's log, `cc-mirror.json`), and its own lock, only as regular files and never blocks in `open()`: a FIFO, device or directory there reads as unreadable at once, and one where a transcript belongs is no transcript, which revival leaves in place; a transcript that is a symlink is checked again on every pass, since its target can be replaced without its project directory changing. What it writes there (a copy into the store, a flag write, a revived transcript) it makes as a new file under a name of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW), writes, stamps and fsyncs through that one descriptor, and puts in place only while the name still holds that file; it never opens a name that already stands. Revival puts its copy in place create-only (a hard link; on a filesystem without hard links, a rename after a fresh look, which leaves the one race `os.replace` had), so a transcript another writer made after the check is never replaced; and a sweep removes the mirror's temporaries that a killed pass left, beside the store's records, in the projects directory and among its own state files, once they are an hour old. Inventory reads use the opened regular file's descriptor metadata instead of a preliminary path stat, read in 64 KiB chunks, and still validate the path after reading before caching; warm sweeps reuse the listing's `DirEntry.stat`. The ten-minute stat sweep still detects in-place writes, and only a completed full sweep advances its clock. The flag protocol is specified in `docs/formal/MirrorFlags.tla`; its invariants (convergence, change wins both ways, idempotence, cancellation safety, no lost update, all or nothing, base agreement, intent wins) are checked over every reachable state of its executable twin `tests/mirror_flags_model.py` (TLC has not been run on the spec), and the mirror is held to that model on random interleavings, including writes between two of a publish's writes. They hold while the app saves only what it has seen, and while no copy the app could not read either becomes readable again with an old value; an app save from memory that predates the mirror's write, or such a copy, reads as the user's change and wins (`docs/reports/2026-09-25-mirror-consistency.md`). After the 2026-10-12 monkeypatch.undo() incident, the C-20.6 (D-ST1) harness guards accidental access to the person's store or log even after a test undoes its own patches: session-level safe overrides survive undo, and path resolvers refuse personal fallbacks under pytest. C-20.6 defines the guarded APIs, exclusions and isolated HOME. [rows 159 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/mirror.py`, `subfleet/sessions/desktop.py`]
- **C-23.29** (a keepalive pass is bounded) A keepalive pass runs at most `keepalive_workers` (4) lanes concurrently with a per-lane deadline of `keepalive_timeout_s` (60), both `policy.json` caps under C-6.4. A lane that exceeds its deadline is recorded as timed out and is not retried inside the same pass. [rows 177 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 5; owner fake; module `subfleet/keepalive.py`]
- **C-23.30** (which registry row speaks for a session) When the session registry holds more than one row for a session, the row used is the one whose recorded pid is live, then the one whose socket is present, then the newest by start time. [rows 72 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.31** (a headless lane run is not a session) A headless lane run is never a `ping` or notice target, never appears in a session listing unless lanes are explicitly included, and is never revived or continued. A request naming one is refused with the reason. A session is a headless lane run when the daemon recorded it as one it launched (`lane_sessions`), or when its transcript says so. Claude Code stamps its entries with the `entrypoint` of the process that wrote them: a transcript is a lane run when an entry names `sdk-cli`, `sdk-ts` or `sdk-py` and no entry names another, so one entry the desktop app (`claude-desktop`), a terminal (`cli`) or any other process wrote makes it a session, whatever wrote after it. Only the transcript's first 5,000 lines and 64 MB are read, as before this change: an entry past them is not seen. Prompts that name no entrypoint (older Claude Code) are read as v1 read them: a typed prompt, one with no `promptSource`, or a third `sdk` prompt makes a session, and a transcript that names no entrypoint is a lane run only when it holds one or two text prompts, all `promptSource: sdk`. The desktop app sends its prompts as `sdk` too, so a prompt that names an entrypoint is never read that way. [rows 76 (`keep`, session-continuity), 187 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 4 for notices, 6 for the listing and revive; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`, `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.32** (the importer never rewrites what it imported) An imported v1 record is never rewritten. A legacy Codex run whose thread id was not recorded resolves its resume identity in memory, from the saved `err.log` and the rollout name, at the moment a resume needs it. [rows 95 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 8 (cutover); owner unit; module `subfleet/importer.py`]
- **C-23.33** (when a session may be nudged) A nudge is sent only for a `SessionStart` whose source is `startup` or `resume`, never `compact` or `clear`, and only when the interruption is younger than `nudge_max_age_s` (8 h). A session is nudged at most once per interruption point and no more often than `nudge_cooldown_s` allows; both are `policy.json` caps under C-6.4. [rows 179 (`keep`, session-continuity), 180 (`keep`, session-continuity), 181 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.34** (the worker decides against the transcript it can see) The hook records the wake and decides nothing: dedupe, cooldown, and eligibility are re-decided by the worker against the transcript as it reads after the nudge delay. A session whose last real turn changed during the delay is skipped, and a sweep started by hand requires a longer quiet window than a `SessionStart` wake. The transcript reader recognises the app's synthetic resume stub and judges the turn beneath it. Inside a process Subfleet launched the hook records nothing and spawns no worker (C-26.13). [rows 182 (`keep`, session-continuity), 183 (`keep`, session-continuity), 184 (`keep`, session-continuity), 185 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6, with the hook half in milestone 4's `subfleet/hooks.py`; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.35** (which sessions revive admits) Revive admits only a session whose recorded permission mode is `bypassPermissions` and whose retirement flag is unset. Retirement is a durable session flag set by the operator; a retired session is absent from every session listing and is never a revive candidate until the operator clears it. Desktop metadata inspection, for revival and cold inspection alike, skips unreadable records, symlinks, non-regular files and records over 1 MiB through a bounded no-follow, nonblocking descriptor read, including records for unrelated sessions. A skipped record may be the session's own, and so may one in a directory of the store that could not be listed: when any was skipped and no readable record names the session, its ownership is unknown (`desktop_owned` null, `unreadable_records` naming what was skipped) and revive refuses automatic revival as for a desktop-owned session, naming the first of them (`--revive` or `sessions.auto_revive_desktop_owned` still admits it). A record naming the session without a cwd still makes it desktop-owned. [rows 188 (`keep`, session-continuity), 191 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.36** (a handoff is bounded and points at its source) Every section of a handoff is bounded by an explicit per-section character cap. The caps of the sections a brief carries (`original_task`, `recent`, `progress`, `repository`) may total at most 245,760 characters, the scrubber's 262,144-character budget less 16,384 for the brief's header, because the assembled brief is scrubbed whole (C-23.14); policy refuses caps that total more. The handoff records the absolute path of the source transcript, which stays the durable record. [rows 210 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`, `subfleet/policy.py`]
- **C-23.37** (stranded capacity is spent first) A lane under an unexpired closure scoped to a model above the one the job needs, with no `account` closure and a free slot, is model-stranded. The Claude comparator orders model-stranded lanes ahead of unstranded ones and then applies C-11.3's order within each group. [rows 108 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 3; owner unit; module `subfleet/policy.py`]
- **C-23.38** (which lane a reset credit is spent on) Reset-credit redemption orders its candidate lanes (the limited lanes of the waiting job it is for, C-23.16 (c); for an operator's `reset codex <lane>`, that lane alone) by weekly reset furthest out first, then fewest in-flight attempts, then lowest lane number; a shadowed lane comes after every unshadowed one (C-23.46). This ordering governs redemption only; C-11.3 continues to order routing candidates. [rows 162 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.39** (revive keeps the session's own tier) Revive launches on the tier recorded in the session's registry row. A different tier is used only when the operator passes `--model`, and the substitution is recorded. [rows 186 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.40** (finding the transcript attestation reads) The transcript for a session uuid is looked for at `<projects>/<session id>.jsonl` and `<projects>/*/<session id>.jsonl` and nowhere deeper; no search walks a workdir or a worktree. Attestation waits for a transcript that has not landed yet at most `transcript_wait_tries` (4) times with a one-second backoff, and then the verdict is `unattested`. [rows 19 (`keep`, provenance/attestation), 218 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`]
- **C-23.41** (a nested submission mints its own identity) A submission made from inside a running attempt takes no identity from its environment: the daemon mints a fresh job id and, absent `--request-id`, a fresh request id. An inherited `SUBFLEET_JOB` is read only as the default `--parent`, and an inherited `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT` — which C-5.1 and C-5.5 require the child to keep — is never an identity claim. [rows 32 (`replace`, provenance/attestation); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.42** (a notice says what the recipient may do) Every notice envelope declares the recipient session's own recorded permission class. `SUBFLEET_NOTIFY_MODE` in the recipient's environment overrides the recorded value, and the override is recorded on the notice. [rows 74 (`keep`, provenance/attestation); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.43** (an unattested round is not a verdict) A Claude peer round counts only when its attestation is `attested` for the model the gate asked for, because the provider can silently serve another model. A Codex peer pins its model at launch and its CLI has no such fallback, so a Codex round counts when its attestation is `attested` or `unattested`, and the gate's round record notes which (the certificate keeps v1's exact content); installed Codex 0.153.3 persists no rollout for an isolated ephemeral run, so `unattested` is the common case there. A round whose attestation is `mismatch`, or which carries a downgrade record, is discarded and re-run on either provider; it is never counted as agreement or as changes requested. (Amended 2026-09-05 16:45 from the gates lane's finding.) [rows 197 (`keep`, provenance/attestation); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.44** (what an auth-dead lane costs) An `auth-dead` result disables the lane at once. A disabled lane is not a routing candidate, not a keepalive target, and not probed — no request is sent to it — until `subfleet lanes enroll` rebinds its credential, which records a new lane id under C-1.3; the CLI exits 5 and names that command. The lane's auth-dead detail is logged at most once a day. One exception (2026-10-03): the `auth-dead` of a job that already moved on from one lane fault (C-4.5) does not disable its lane. That job ends `failed` with rc 5, the attempt records `auth_dead_again` (`lane_id`, `lane_left_enabled`), `daemon.log` and the notice say the lane was left enabled, and the next attempt there, another job's, decides. With the pilot hold on (C-6.14) that lane is one a model answered on within `admission.prove_idle_s`, or one Subfleet's probe just answered on, since a job that moved on reaches no other lane unprobed (C-4.5); with the hold off, a job that moved on can meet a second dead lane as its first attempt there, and then that lane stays enabled until another job's first `auth-dead` or a keepalive or heal turn finds it. The job whose attempt found a dead lane moves on to the next candidate when that attempt is a lane fault (C-4.5: no lane pin, no turn, its workspace as it found it, its first), without the attempt counting, and `daemon.log` says so; a pinned job, or one whose workspace changed, ends `failed` with rc 5 as before. The job's notice (C-15.1) names each lane it moved on from: ``attempt a<seq>: lane <lane id> went auth-dead (<detail>); it is disabled until `subfleet lanes enroll` rebinds its credential, and the job moved on to the next lane without counting the attempt``, and a second lane as ``attempt a<seq>: lane <lane id> answered auth-dead too; two lanes refusing one job points at the job, so <lane id> was left enabled``. Since C-6.14 a lane that has not answered for `admission.prove_idle_s` takes one detached attempt before any other, so a dead lane disables after one attempt, with the rest of a burst still queued. [rows 116 (`replace`, identity), 175 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for the disable, 5 for the keepalive and log cadence; owner fake; module `subfleet/credentials.py`]
- **C-23.45** (one account, one enabled lane) At most one lane is canonical for an account key. Enrolment and each probe cycle detect a second lane bound to an account key another lane already holds, mark the later binding non-canonical, and raise a critical alert naming both homes. [rows 136 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for detection, 5 for the alert; owner unit; module `subfleet/credentials.py`]
- **C-23.46** (what shadowing changes and what it does not) A lane whose account is a provider desktop app's current login is recorded as shadowed; that fact alone never changes its dispatch order. Reset-credit redemption excludes a shadowed lane while any unshadowed lane a credit may go on (not held, C-23.16 (e)) holds a concrete `available` credit, and redeems on a shadowed lane only when none does; a held lane is not listed to find out. [rows 138 (`keep`, identity), 163 (`keep`, identity); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.47** (the auth store belongs to the provider CLI) subfleet never writes a provider auth store and never refreshes a token in process. A lane whose Codex token looks merely expired gets exactly one automatic heal — a minimal `codex exec` turn under that lane's home, so the CLI refreshes and persists its own `auth.json` — followed by a re-probe. A `refresh token was revoked` result latches the lane: it is probed no further until the credential epoch of C-10.1, the home's `auth.json` `last_refresh`, changes. [rows 139 (`keep`, identity), 150 (`keep`, identity), 151 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for the prohibition, 5 for the heal and the latch; owner fake; module `subfleet/credentials.py`, `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.48** (what the session hook counts as a launch) The session hook blocks a provider CLI or a v1 runner only in command position; a mention inside a quoted string, a heredoc, or an inspection command such as `bash -n` is not a launch. A runner's own detach flag passes, and `SUBFLEET_ATTACHED_OK=1` in the tool call's environment is the explicit one-off override. [rows 68 (`keep`, UX-contract), 69 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/hooks.py`]
- **C-23.49** (one envelope per notice) A notice is delivered as exactly one envelope. Any sequence in the body that would close the envelope early is neutralised before the envelope is written. [rows 75 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.50** (the push waits behind a live waiter) The best-effort socket push is skipped while the job has a `wait` or `--attach` waiter whose recorded identity is still live, and is attempted once that waiter is no longer live. The push is never the reason a caller learns about a job twice. [rows 79 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner fake; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.51** (a running job never falls off the list) `runs --last N` bounds terminal jobs only. Every job in a live state is listed whatever N is and however old it is, and `--json` output carries the same set. [rows 89 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`]
- **C-23.52** (what an alert says and when a recovery is one) subfleet never performs a provider login; every alert about a credential names the exact command the operator must run. A recovery notice is emitted only when a condition has cleared and no other condition on the same home is active; a move from one condition to another is reported as the new condition, not as a recovery. [rows 149 (`keep`, UX-contract), 156 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.53** (a gate stops) A gate stops after `gate_max_rounds` (4) peer rounds without agreement and reports the blocker, naming the last verdict. The cap is a `policy.json` cap under C-6.4. [rows 200 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.54** (one dispatch path) Every provider launch is a `subfleet run` submission, including subfleet's own — a handoff is dispatched detached through the ordinary submit path so it inherits routing, the guard, salvage, the ledger, and notices. The session hook blocks a provider CLI or a v1 runner invoked directly from a session's Bash tool and names `subfleet run` as the replacement. [rows 67 (`keep`, process-survival), 211 (`keep`, process-survival); milestone 4 for the hook, 6 for handoff; owner fake; module `subfleet/hooks.py`, `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`]
- **C-23.55** (one live revive per session) A session has at most one live revive: the revive lease `session:<session id>:revive` is taken in the transaction that admits the attempt, and a session that already holds it is skipped rather than launched again. The census the sweep skips on is the lease rows, read inside the admitting transaction, not a snapshot taken at the start of the pass. [rows 192 (`keep`, process-survival); milestone 6; owner fake; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]

## 24. Conversations and messages

Milestone 9, the desktop workspace. Specification: `docs/desktop/design.md` revision 3 (cited as design §n or D-n). Every clause in sections 24 to 30 names its owner layer and module in the trailer, as section 23 does.

- **C-24.1** (a conversation is a daemon row) A conversation binds one provider, at most one native session id (unique per provider; recorded only once the provider holds that session: a Codex thread id its server returned, or a Claude session with a terminal event, an acknowledgement or a transcript on disk, so a session id Subfleet minted is never resumed before the provider created it), one workspace directory for its life, settings `{model, effort, fast, permission, auto_continue}`, and an origin (`new`, `native`, `handoff`, `legacy`); a Codex conversation also names the lane whose home holds its thread. Conversation state lives in `conversations.sqlite3`, written only by the daemon; `state.sqlite3` keeps schema 5. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/store.py`; design D-4]
- **C-24.2** (a message is accepted once, in order) A message carries a client UUID, its client predecessor, and a digest over its conversation, text, attachment SHA-256s and settings. A repeated UUID with the same digest returns the stored receipt with `created: false`; a different digest is exit 2 `message-id-conflict`; a predecessor the daemon has not committed is exit 2 `out-of-order`. Neither refusal changes anything. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/store.py`; design D-22]
- **C-24.3** (intent before dispatch) A message's text is published under `conversations/<id>/messages/` (C-8.1) and its row committed before `message.submit` returns, and before any job exists for it. A receipt is local acknowledgement only. The conversation store publishes a text, like an approval's request (C-27.1), only while it is open, and makes the directories below the state root (each 0700) but never the root itself: once closed it refuses (`store-closed`, exit 1), and with the root gone it fails `state-root-gone` (exit 1). A text published just before the store closed stays with no row, as after a crash between the two. Reading accepted text uses a bounded, nonblocking, no-follow descriptor for a private regular file owned by the daemon user with no second hard link, verifies the message digest over its original text, attachments and settings, and only then normalizes newlines; changed text is refused. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`]
- **C-24.4** (states are the provider's facts) Message states are those of design D-12. `running` requires the provider's acknowledgement of that message (Claude `command_lifecycle started` or the replayed user message carrying its UUID; Codex a `turn/start` response carrying a turn id). A provider-reported success is `complete` even when a stop was requested. A message in `waiting` always has a non-empty `state_reason` (2026-09-29, I3; the store refuses `waiting` without one), `<kind>: <detail>` or one of the reasons the dispatcher writes before its turn job exists: `dispatching` (its claim, C-24.7), `deferred: <why>` (C-26.1), `external-writer: pid <n>` (C-26.3) and `readmit:<why>` (C-24.6). Once the job exists it is `admission: sent to the daemon, which has not placed it yet` until admission's turn pass looks at the job, and after every turn pass it says why that pass left the job unplaced (`subfleet/conversations/waits.py`, from C-6.11's hold; written only when it changes, and looked at again at least every 30 s while one hold stands, since a lease's holder or a title may change under it; two workers run turn passes, and the notes of a pass older than the last one noted are dropped, never written over a newer pass's; a note that found its message not yet bound to the job is tried again a second later): `lease: <who>` naming the job that holds what it needs and, for a turn, that conversation's title; `capacity: <detail>` only when some lane would take it once room frees (its slots busy, its usage at the floor or kept for the reserved model) or a turn cap holds it; `closed: <detail>` when every lane that could take it is closed, naming the first reset; `usage-unknown: <detail>` when no lane has a fresh usage reading to take it on; `no-lane: <detail>` for refusals no wait ends (disabled, signed in to another account, a probe a turn never waits for), the lanes' reasons counted in every lane case, and for the lane a Codex conversation keeps (C-26.2) when it refuses the turn so (C-11.8's `pin-unadmittable`), that lane and its refusals named; `blocked`, `workspace`, `route` and `admission` for the other holds; and `placed: starting the provider` once placed, until its runner starts. Incident: on 2026-09-28 four messages waited 29 minutes to 13 hours for another conversation's turn in their folder with no `state_reason`, and the app, which fell back to "Waiting for capacity", said capacity while lanes were free. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/waits.py`]
- **C-24.5** (one turn at a time per conversation) A conversation's next message gets a turn job only when the previous turn job is terminal and has released its `conversation:` and `native:` leases, and the conversation is not blocked. A conversation is blocked while its `blocked_by` (the service's own: `unfinished-turn`, `delivery-unknown`, `quarantined-turn`) or its `legacy_hold` (the legacy import's, C-30.4) is set. While it is, no message is dispatched or re-admitted, and a turn job created before the block places nothing at admission (hold `conversation-blocked`, C-6.11). Different conversations run concurrently under admission, also when they share a folder (one write target, C-6.5): each writable turn holds a row of its own on the folder, `worktree-turn:<target>:<job id>`, and none holds `worktree:<target>`, so no conversation waits for another's turn there (the owner's ruling of 2026-09-28, "nothing should be queued"; the Claude app lets several sessions share a folder). A read-only turn holds `worktree-read:<folder>:<job id>` (its checkout's top, or the directory outside git), which excludes no writer; of the leases on folders, only retention's fence on that folder or on one above it holds it (C-8.4). Two turns of one conversation, or on one native session, still never run at once (`conversation:` and `native:`, C-26.3). A turn and a detached writer in one folder still exclude each other: a writable turn waits `lease-held` on `worktree:<target>` while a detached writer (or retention's fence, C-8.4) holds it, a detached writer waits at admission while any `worktree-turn:` row is on its folder, and one submitted then is refused (C-6.5). A turn, writable or read-only, also waits `lease-held` on `worktree:<folder>` of a folder above its own while retention's fence holds it (a repository nested in a job's tree being retired, C-8.4), never for a detached writer's lease there: a repository nested in a checkout is a hold of its own (C-6.5). Why they still exclude each other: C-6.5 promises a detached in-place job that it writes its checkout alone, so the salvage ref taken at its end (C-13.1) is its own work, and salvage never snapshots a folder while a conversation writes in it (I5); detached writers normally run in worktrees of their own (C-6.6), so this meets a conversation only for an explicit `--in-place` job or one whose worktree a conversation continues, and the wait names the job that holds the folder (C-24.4). What another conversation changed in the folder during a turn is labelled on that turn's changes (C-26.14). A detached writer's edits are not: none is made from the turn's reservation to its end, but admission takes the turn's start snapshot before its reserving transaction, so a detached writer that held the folder after that snapshot began and released it before the reservation can leave edits in the turn's diff that nothing names (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-1; a known limit). `steering` is live and holds the queue until its host settles; `steered` is terminal and never gets its own job. A missed steer keeps its original sequence and runs next: the dispatcher takes it ahead of every queued message that was not steered, behind only a repair message, and in sequence among missed steers (C-24.9); its reason keeps the `steer-missed:` mark the queue orders by when a submit of its turn is deferred, and when a handoff that cancelled its turn job is rolled back (C-30.3). Handoff refuses a live steer. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`; design §4]
- **C-24.6** (no re-send after ambiguity) A message whose delivery is uncertain is never sent to a provider again automatically. Every turn that ends without the provider's terminal event (Claude `result`; Codex `turn/completed` or its answer to `turn/start`), whether stdout ended or the driver's own check ended it, is reconciled as design D-14: `delivered` when the attempt's stdout acknowledged the message (C-24.4) or the native record holds it (Claude: a `user` record with the message id as `uuid` in the session transcript after the attempt's `transcript_offset`; Codex: a rollout record whose `client_id` is the message id); `not-delivered` only with the process verified gone (the attempt's exit receipt), the native record read without it (or no native session exists for the attempt), and a relay log that was read whole and consistent and holds no record of the message frame. Otherwise the message is `delivery-unknown` and blocks its conversation until a person's `message.resolve` with the JSON literal `confirm: true`, which records who and when and never resends. A person's Stop does not count as delivery evidence: it preserves a genuinely unknown delivery until resolved; once resolved, a recorded personal stop settles as `interrupted` with reason `stopped`, without an unfinished-turn block. A driver's refusal before sending that another admission may pass (`external-writer`, `fast-unavailable`, `provider-init-failed`, `guard-refused`; review IR-23) re-admits the same message as a new turn job only when reconciled `not-delivered` and the person has not stopped it, at most 3 times (counted by the message's other turn jobs a provider reached, so a handoff's rolled-back cancels count for nothing, less those whose `turn.json` shows a wait for the session's other writer; one whose outcome cannot be read counts), and then fails it `not-delivered`. A wait for the session's other writer (`external-writer`, and the legacy hold's `legacy-owner`, C-30.4) is re-admitted however often it happens and uses up none of those 3, then or later. Each steer settles before its host: consumed/delivered becomes `steered`, an echoed Codex steer with no following agent output becomes `steered-unanswered:<host>`, proven unsent/refused/cancelled returns to `queued`, and a written or ambiguous relay intent with no provider fate becomes `delivery-unknown`. Positive consumption evidence takes precedence over a subsequent cancellation. Resolving one unknown does not unblock other unknown messages. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/reconcile.py`]
- **C-24.7** (stop is scoped and ends the turn) `message.cancel` withdraws a message only before its job has an attempt, checked in the transaction that records the cancel, except a message with no job bound, which it withdraws even if a job the dispatcher made for it meanwhile has been given an attempt: the stop recorded with that withdrawal keeps the attempt from ever writing the message (below). Withdrawing an id the daemon never received (naming its conversation) records a tombstone that is cancelled in the transaction that creates it; a later `message.submit` of that id returns the tombstone and nothing is dispatched (review IR-7). The dispatcher claims a message (`waiting`, reason `dispatching`) in the conversation store before it creates the message's job, so a handoff that sees the claim refuses rather than racing the submit (IR-28); `message.cancel` still withdraws a message with no job bound, claimed or not, and the job the dispatcher then creates finds it withdrawn and is cancelled before any attempt (IR-2); `turn.interrupt` of a waiting message with no job bound withdraws it too. A cancel records the person's stop in the transaction that withdraws the message, so a runner admission started for that job meanwhile never writes it: a turn whose message has a person's stop recorded before the relay log shows the message handed over is stopped before it is ever written (a message frame whose send was already under way when the stop was recorded, its answer lost, counts as handed over when the relay's log, read after a fresh `status` answer, holds it). A person's stop is recorded, and a runner hands the message frame to the relay, under one lock per message, and the runner reads the stored stop again under it just before the handover, so a stop recorded after the runner started (while the provider answers `initialize`, say) still keeps the message unwritten, and one made during the handover is recorded after it and stops the turn as below; a stop the runner was asked for before the handover (the daemon's wall limit or kill) keeps it unwritten too. A relay slow to take one message's frame holds up the stops and cancels of that message alone. The withdrawal also cancels such a job while it has no attempt, and admission cancels any turn job whose message has settled (hold `message-settled`, C-6.11). A cancel refused (too late, or the message being handed to its job) records no stop, so nothing it did stops the turn it lost to, and clears none; a person's cancels and stops of one message are taken one at a time. A claim is released (a message that was queued, or whose claim a crash interrupted, goes back to `queued`) when its submit is deferred. `turn.interrupt` targets only that message's attempt and escalates as design D-13: the provider's interrupt, SIGINT to the provider child through its guardian's relay, closing stdin, and only then C-5.6 containment. Each later step is taken only while the turn has no terminal event, at policy `conversations.stop_sigint_after_s`, `stop_close_after_s` and `stop_contain_after_s` seconds after the stop was requested (defaults 10, 20, 30); the policy loader refuses a value that is not a positive finite number and an order other than SIGINT before closing before containment. No other conversation, job, or process is signalled. A steer cancellation succeeds only before its relay handover; afterwards it refuses `too-late`. Steer frames take both host and message handover locks, so neither a recorded host stop nor its own cancellation permits a late write. Stop applies to the entire host: `turn.interrupt` of a `steering` message stops its host (a steer has no attempt of its own), and the steers the provider shows it had not read (never handed over, or shown not taken: Claude's `cancel_queued` receipt or a cancelled or refused lifecycle; Codex's refusal, or an accepted steer never echoed before a proven interrupted end) return to the queue and run next (C-24.5); a written steer the provider reports nothing about settles `delivery-unknown` (C-24.6, C-26.5). Stop never pulls them back into the composer. Claude requests `cancel_queued: true` when supported and the turn has steers (a turn with none sends the interrupt it always sent), and uses the receipt to settle pending steers. A recorded personal stop settles a turn without a terminal event as `interrupted` with reason `stopped` once delivery is known, whether delivered or not delivered, and never sets `unfinished-turn` or re-admits that message. Settlement and the person's stop are serialized per message. Waiting messages remain queued and run next after the stopped attempt releases its leases; a provider success still wins (C-24.4), and genuinely unknown delivery still blocks (C-24.6). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-24.8** (an unfinished Claude turn blocks) A Claude attempt that ends without `result` after its message was delivered (C-24.6: acknowledged, or in the session transcript; review IR-5), including a system stop that got no `result` and a driver's own stop (a model mismatch) that no `result` followed, sets the conversation `blocked_by: "unfinished-turn"`; a written message frame whose delivery is unknown blocks it as `delivery-unknown` (C-24.6), and a person's `message.resolve` of a Claude message as `delivered` turns that block into `unfinished-turn`. A recorded personal stop is the exception: it settles without `unfinished-turn`, including after resolving an unknown delivery (C-24.7). For an unfinished turn, the next message waits for a person's `conversation.unblock` (`continue` or `leave`), recorded. `leave` sends a one-line note ahead of any queued message, so the next turn does not resume the stopped work; `continue` sends nothing. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/reconcile.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-24.9** (steer) A person may steer any queued person message into the conversation’s running or approval-needed host with a steerable runner (the messages queued for later keep their order), except while a repair message (an unblock note or a failover continuation) is queued, which refuses `not-next`. It runs under the host’s settings, and a message whose permission is narrower than the host’s refuses (`settings-narrower`). A `/` command or `!` shell input is never steered: the app queues it (C-29.7) and the daemon refuses it (`not-steerable`); leading Unicode White_Space is skipped, as Swift’s `Character.isWhitespace` does. A request that names the host it was meant for (`into`) is refused `no-live-turn` once another turn is the live one, so a late or retried steer never joins a different turn; a live turn whose runner the daemon has not taken back yet after a restart (or that is settling) refuses `not-steerable`, which the app asks again about (C-29.7). The service atomically claims the message as `steering`, reason `steer:<host>`, before enqueueing the runner command. Delivery becomes `steered`, reason `steered:<host>` or `steered-unanswered:<host>`; a proven miss returns to `queued`, reason `steer-missed: <why>`, keeping its sequence, and runs next (C-24.5).
