A steer whose input cannot be built (an image gone or changed, a driver failure of any kind) returns to the queue the same way and never ends its host’s runner; its images resolve by digest (C-28.1), not through the host’s. No steer is both delivered and requeued, lost, written after close/stop/cancel, or sent twice on replay. A turn with no steers retains its existing delivery/outcome rules: for every recorded history with no steer (`tests/unit/no_steer_histories.py`, 172: both providers, acknowledged or not, allowing, denying or stopping the turn from an approval, stopped or not, a model mismatch, an initialize or `turn/start` refused, ending with success, an error or stdout's end), every frame, event, approval and outcome its driver produces are those of the pre-steer code (commit 52ffde17), checked against a recording of it (`tests/fixtures/steer/no_steer_presteer.json`); for the 31 of them a provider writes alone (no person's answer or stop mid-turn), so is what the runner loop writes and the service settles. Outcomes and `turn.json` carry an empty `steers`, and anything in it fails the check. The one deliberate difference: once a Codex turn's id is known, a notification naming another turn is ignored (C-26.6); a recorded history checks that it differs there alone (the pre-steer code showed another turn's text as this turn's). Invariants 1 (exactly once), 4 (restart) and 5 have generated or differential coverage through the runner's own loop, the real drivers, store and settlement; invariant 2 (queue order) through the real store and settlement; invariant 3 (no write after close, stop or cancel) through the real drivers and the runner's handover checks against a relay journal. Driver, runner, service and fake-provider end-to-end tests exercise the boundary races. [milestone 9; owner unit/fake; module `subfleet/conversations/turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`, `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/codex_turn.py`]

- **C-24.10** (durable unattended wakes) The daemon batches terminal outcomes of runs dispatched by a conversation turn into one message with origin `wake`, displayed as Subfleet and beginning `[Subfleet]`; each run says `<id> finished: <state>; deliverable <path>`. The turn job is the run's parent; older jobs are associated by the caller session used by `runs --mine` and notices, within a recorded turn's lifetime. Automatic completion discovery starts at the persisted activation timestamp, so upgrade does not announce historical runs. A one-time snapshot excludes jobs already completed at activation; the time floor is rounded to the stored second precision and comparisons normalize ISO timestamps. A run started earlier can still wake when it finishes after activation, including that same second; legacy completions without finished_at use created_at. Explicit requests may still name older runs. Completions already surfaced by a hook or acknowledged are not offered again automatically. Runs covered by a pending all-of request are gathered into its single wake; an already-delivered runs kind is satisfied without sending a message or charging the throttle, while that request's timer and PR alternatives stay pending. A request is never satisfied silently as a whole until every kind is resolved; a fired request names its accepted wake message. A registered run whose row was pruned is finished with state `pruned`. A live turn finishes before a wake is submitted: C-24.9's steer admits person messages only. Accepting the message, consuming requests and run ids, and incrementing the unattended counter share one durable transaction. Completion notices become surfaced with transport `conversation` afterwards, repaired from a durable queue in batches of at most 500 after a crash. Historical receipts are backfilled once on upgrade; completed repairs do no further writes. Notice lookup by job is indexed. Automatic discovery and request evaluation run at most once per second on the control loop, eligibility uses a read, and target reads are batched. An idle evaluation opens no write transaction and notifies no readers. `subfleet wake --runs ID... --pr OWNER/REPO#N... --at ISO --note TEXT [--request-id UUID]` records against the calling turn or its bound session (`conversation.wake`, capability `wake.v1`). A run trigger waits for all named runs; a PR trigger waits for any watched PR to merge, close, finish checks, or gain a submitted review; a time trigger waits until its instant. Kinds within a request are alternatives: the first ready kind consumes the request once. At most one pending request per kind (`runs`, `pr`, `time`) exists per conversation; a new request supersedes the old request of that kind. Each kind has at most 16 targets, each at most 200 characters, and notes at most 4,000 characters. `at` requires a timezone and must be at least five minutes away when first accepted; an identical request-id retry remains accepted afterwards. PR polling uses one batched `gh api graphql` query across conversations per cycle, a persisted timestamp at least 60 seconds apart, a 20-second timeout, and durable baselines and ready events. A transport failure consumes nothing. Alias-specific errors retain valid data for other PRs and produce one explicit refusal wake for the affected watch. Refused targets persist per conversation; re-arming one is refused visibly without another wake. Re-arms carry the last fired request's observed snapshot forward; an observation never delivered cannot become a new baseline. A first observation without a previous snapshot fires only for events timestamped strictly after registration, or after turn-job creation for a final-text request (message creation if no job exists), so results arriving during that turn remain visible. A partial check set cannot prove completion. The bounded PR worker does not block control-loop dispatch and is joined before store shutdown. A wake waits while blocked (including legacy holds), archived, while any person message waits, while a turn runs or is queued, or while its conversation lease is held. At eight wakes without a person message it is throttled to one every 30 minutes: eight permits several build/review rounds, and the cooldown bounds check-back loops while permitting unattended progress. Person acceptance resets the counter. A wake waiting at capacity yields its unstarted job and retains its message behind personal input; wakes never change the personal predecessor chain or reorder personal messages. The final text may end with consecutive `WAKE-ME:` lines: `WAKE-ME: runs=ID[,ID...] prs=OWNER/REPO#N[,OWNER/REPO#N...] at=ISO note="TEXT"`. Fields are optional, unique, whitespace-separated with shell quoting; at least one trigger is required. Only top-level trailing lines are parsed, allowing bullets, bold markup and one standard DONE, WAITING ON MAX or HANDED TO close-out. Empty target fields are ignored when another trigger exists. Fences, quoted lines and indented code cannot register requests. Each line is independently accepted or refused, with stable replay ids. Final-text timers validate the five-minute floor against both now and turn-job creation (message creation if there is no job); past or nearer timers are refused. Identical request-id replays retain their original acceptance. Invalid requests are logged and displayed as a refusal notice without failing a successful turn. [owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/wakes.py`, `store.py`, `service.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`; design continuation amendment]
- **C-24.11** (a superseded block clears) A service block records `blocked_at` independently of subsequent queued messages. At most once per five seconds per unchanged block, a native Claude transcript's catalog mtime strictly later than that timestamp makes `unfinished-turn`, `delivery-unknown`, or `quarantined-turn` moot once admission's fresh C-26.3 `external_writers` check finds no outside writer, no Subfleet turn remains live or queued, and no conversation/native lease is held. Clear only the observed block and timestamp, preserving legacy holds and handoff fences, and record `conversation.unblocked` with reason `continued-elsewhere`. An unknown message superseded this way settles `failed: continued-elsewhere` without claiming delivery or resending it (an exception to C-24.6's personal resolution requirement). An older store recovers the block timestamp from its last failed/unknown/interrupted message, before later queued input. [owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `store.py`, `catalog.py`; design continuation amendment]
- **C-24.12** (one real native history) `conversation.open` includes a bounded native-history page; it and `conversation.history` run on a separate bounded history read pool and use the catalog's resolved transcript paths, including bound sessions excluded from the sidebar. Before a catalog record exists, a recorded attempt path or exact Claude workspace/session path is used. Neither scans native trees. Claude prompt UUIDs and Codex turn-context ids classify native turns as Subfleet or `other-app`; interrupts, compaction summaries, tool results and task notifications within a Claude turn do not start new prompt groups; partial groups whose ownership cannot be read within the cap retain the conservative timestamp filter. The frontend removes Subfleet duplicates, labels other-app turns "Made in another app", and merges them chronologically with Subfleet events, including after binding and after event replay. Opening or switching conversations scrolls to the newest row at the bottom. [owner frontend; module `subfleet/conversations/history.py`, `catalog.py`, `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `ConversationStore.swift`, `UIWindow.swift`; design continuation amendment]

## 25. Protocol additions

- **C-25.1** (discovery before mutation) `capabilities` returns protocol version 1, the daemon version, `conversation_schema` 1 (the version of the conversation ops' argument and result shapes, C-25.2, which is not the conversation store's schema, C-26.14), capability names and limits. A client sends no conversation op to a daemon that answers "unknown op", and sends a field whose silent omission would change an outcome only after the daemon advertised it. `conversation.status.v1` advertises the read-only focused state and message-identity refresh (C-25.2). The app calls that operation only after discovering this capability; a daemon with `conversations.v1` alone remains usable and focused refresh falls back to the full `conversation.open` re-read, so its watch cursor can advance. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 1. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/protocol.py`]
- **C-25.2** (the ops) The conversation ops are `capabilities`, `conversation.list`, `conversation.status`, `conversation.open`, `conversation.create`, `conversation.settings`, `conversation.rename`, `conversation.unblock`, `conversation.history`, `conversation.events`, `conversation.watch`, `message.submit`, `message.status`, `message.cancel`, `message.steer`, `turn.interrupt`, `message.resolve`, `approval.list`, `approval.get`, `approval.respond`, `attachment.add`, `catalog.refresh`, `models.list`, `conversation.runs`, `turn.diff`, `conversation.diff`, `workspace.check`, `conversation.handoff`, `conversation.wake`, and the suggestion ops `suggestion.create`, `suggestion.list`, `suggestion.start`, `suggestion.dismiss` and `suggestion.withdraw` (C-25.7), listed in `subfleet/protocol.py` (`CONVERSATION_OPS`) and answered by `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, with the arguments and results of design §5. `conversation.status {conversation_id}` is read-only and returns `{conversation, message_ids}`: the full conversation view and all message identities in sequence order, including queued and settled messages, with no transcript or message-body reads. It is advertised as `conversation.status.v1`; clients hydrate unknown or changed identities with `message.status`. Receipts from `conversation.open` and `message.status` carry the message's `text` (at most 20,000 characters, `text_truncated` when cut), so a client that did not send a message can show it. Errors are `ok: false` with an `Exit` code. An op added to this list is advertised by a `capabilities` name (`diff.v1` for the two diff ops, `runs.v1` for `conversation.runs`, `suggestions.v1` for the suggestion ops), not by a new `conversation_schema`. `steer.v1` advertises `message.steer {message_id, into?}` and `steer_providers: ["claude", "codex"]`; `into`, when sent, is the host the steer was meant for. Its person-only result is a Receipt, idempotent for `steering`/`steered`; refusal codes are `not-queued`, `not-next` (a repair message is queued), `no-live-turn` (no live host, or not the one `into` names), `settings-narrower`, `not-steerable` (the host's runner cannot take it now: not taken back yet or still catching up after a restart, the provider not ready, the turn stopping or ended; or it is a `/` command or `!` shell input). Receipts, message status and change rows include nullable `steered_into`, derived from `state_reason`, with store schema still 2. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/protocol.py`]
- **C-25.3** (handlers never wait) A conversation op handler validates, writes at most one transaction per store (a worktree conversation's create writes its row, then its worktree), wakes the control loop, and returns. It never waits on a provider, a probe, `ps`, git, or a catalog scan (C-16.4), with two exceptions: `turn.diff` and `conversation.diff` run git (C-26.14), and `conversation.create` of a worktree conversation runs git to cut its worktree (C-26.10, design D-16). Those three ops (`conversation.create` whatever its workspace kind), `workspace.check` (the same bounded folder and git admission checks), `suggestion.create` and `suggestion.start` (the same checks, and a start's worktree, C-25.7), file copies and transcript reads run on a dedicated bounded pool (the file pool), never on the pool the other ops and the session hooks use. The conversation service's `close()` lets the file ops already running finish before it returns, while the state root and the conversation store are still there, drops the ones not yet started, and the pool accepts none after. A file op writes into the state root as it stands and never creates it: with the root gone it fails `state-root-gone` (exit 1). The conversation service (its ops, its control-loop steps and its turn runners) opens every file it reads only as a regular file, and never blocks in `open()`: what it reads of a native session (a Claude transcript, a Codex rollout, Codex's `session_index.jsonl`, a Claude session-registry file), a workspace's `PROGRESS.md`, the real `.git/index` a diff copies, and in the state root an admission job's `manifest.json`, a message's text, an attachment's stored copy, an approval's request, `catalog.json`, `catalog.lock`, `conversations/models.json` and an attempt's files (`stdout`, `stdin.jsonl`, `turn.json`, the JSON records). Admission reads a manifest through `O_NONBLOCK|O_NOFOLLOW`, checks its descriptor with `fstat`, and reads at most 16 MiB; an unreadable manifest holds that job as `conversation-blocked` with its manifest error, without delaying later admission. A FIFO, device or directory there reads as unreadable at once; a stored attachment or a message's text is read only up to its limit (20 MiB, 1 MiB). Contents of the listed service-owned state files are read through a separate no-follow regular-file reader, including attempt stdout and relay logs; `catalog.lock` is opened only for locking, with its existing nonblocking regular-file check. Native transcript readers retain their existing symlink support. A file it writes it makes as a new file of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW) and puts in place by rename, never opening one that stands there, and an attachment's stored copy that does not hash to its name (a FIFO, a partial copy) is replaced by the new copy. Some of what they read of a native session is capped too: a scan for a first task or a session's prompts reads at most the first 64 MiB; a reader that reads lines forward skips a line longer than 64 MiB rather than hold it, so a page of history reads its budget (C-29.8) and, for results past its cursor, the rows that start within its window, each at most 64 MiB, and looks no further back than its budget for a row's start; a registry file is read up to 1 MiB and Codex's index up to 64 MiB, of which a lookup of one thread parses and scrubs only the rows that name it. Settling a turn scans its session's file from the turn's start to its end, and a diff copies the whole of `.git/index`: those are bounded by the file's size alone. So `close()`'s wait for the file ops already running is bounded by those caps and sizes, git's time limits, and the walks of a provider's session trees that find a rollout, which have no budget of their own; and only on a filesystem that answers: `O_NONBLOCK` bounds a FIFO's open, not a read from a stalled mount. A FIFO where a rollout belonged had held a file op, and with it `close()`, until a writer came. The catalog run (C-30.1), a process of its own, opens those files plainly: a FIFO holds that run until its service stops it, never the daemon. `close()` also stops every turn runner and waits, at most 5 s in all, for each to finish the iteration it is in, so what a runner was writing (its events, an approval, `turn.json`, the model catalog in `conversations/models.json`) lands before `close()` returns; it logs each runner still going when the wait ends. The control loop adopts no runner once `close()` has begun, and keeps none whose thread could not start (a thread limit): its next pass adopts that turn again, and a step `close()` overtook is logged as stopped, not failed. After `close()` the conversation store refuses every read and write (`store-closed`, exit 1), and an op that meets the refusal fails with it (`message.status` answers `unknown` only for an id the store holds no message by, and a cancel makes a tombstone only for such an id); a runner's containment write to the main store (D-13) is refused the same way; and a runner's files, the model catalog and message texts (C-24.3) are written only while the store is open, which `close()` waits for; none of them creates the state root. A runner whose last writes are refused still ends, closes its relay and is marked finished, and `close()` logs one it stopped with its outcome unreported; a later daemon replays the attempt from its stdout (C-26.6), and settles a message whose attempt has ended with no runner of its own by replaying that attempt once, so a turn whose report was cut short is settled whichever of finalization and adoption comes first. A settlement writes the conversation's block before the message's state, so one cut short leaves the message live for that replay. `Daemon.close()` waits for its request pool after the service has closed, so an op still running then finishes before the daemon's `close()` returns, its store work refused. The daemon's other work that `Daemon.close()` waits for (the timers' workers through `Timers.stop()`, and its requests and workers pools: `submit`, `gate.*`, `lanes`, an attempt's finalization) reads a file only as a regular file too, never waiting in `open()`: a lane's or the desktop's login (`auth.json`, `.credentials.json`, `.claude.json`), the guard's TRUST, hook, seed files and cached verdicts, `conversations.sqlite3` for `status.json` (checked by `stat` before SQLite opens it by name: a swap between the two is not covered), an attempt's records and artifacts, a Codex rollout a turn attests, a job's prompt, a gate's brief, plan and state, and v1's rosters, plists and run records (a roster that is not a regular file is refused, never read as an empty one an edit would replace); and a file it writes beside one it does not own (`lanes transfer`'s roster and its backup, a raw stream copied from stdout) is a new file of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/sessions/transcripts.py`]
- **C-25.4** (a bounded cursor, batched writes) `conversation.events` returns events after a sequence number, long-polls at most 50 s, cuts a page at 256 KiB, and says `reset: true` exactly when the cursor is below the conversation's compaction floor (a cursor of 0 included), the highest sequence number compaction removed; each page carries the floor. Compaction removes an attempt's `text.delta` and `thinking.delta` rows, only once the job store has ended the attempt (not `quarantined`), no runner reads it, and its message has been terminal for `conversations.compact_after_s` (default 300 s), at most `conversations.compact_per_tick` (20) attempts per control-loop pass; a replay never stores a removed delta again (review IR-6). Driver output is written at most once per attempt per 250 ms or 64 KiB. It is polling with a wake-up, and no document calls it streaming. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/events.py`]
- **C-25.5** (events and results carry only what a person may see) Events and op results hold user-visible text, provider-displayed reasoning, tool names, scrubbed input summaries of at most 500 characters and scrubbed result previews of at most 2 KiB; a credential-reading call is shown as hidden. Codex raw reasoning, Claude redacted thinking and signatures, and credentials never appear in them. Unscrubbed provider content at rest (attempt stdout, `stdin.jsonl`, prompts, message text) is 0600 in 0700 directories, retained under C-28.2, and never exported or put in a notice. Text scrubbing follows C-23.14's linear scans and character budget before the display cap is applied: a text or thinking block, a result preview or an input summary over the budget keeps its scrubbed head and tail excerpts within its cap (60,000 characters for a text or thinking block), with the marker between them. Approvals are shown in full with token-shaped values masked in place (C-27.1). [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/redact.py`]
- **C-25.6** (person-only operations) `approval.respond`, `approval.get`, `message.resolve`, `message.steer`, `conversation.unblock`, `suggestion.start`, `suggestion.dismiss` (C-25.7), any settings change that widens the permission policy or sets `allow_main`, and `conversation.create` or `conversation.handoff` of a conversation above Ask or on main (IR-21) are refused (exit 7) when the caller (whose pid is word 5 of the `LOCAL_PEERTOKEN` audit token saved immediately after accept) or an ancestor is a live guardian's descendant or carries the C-5.1 markers, and accepted only from the installed app's executable (the kernel's canonical path for the process/image instance described by the saved token, read with `proc_pidpath_audittoken`, never a split command line or a later bare-pid lookup); a daemon on any state root other than `~/.subfleet` also accepts the development build that `SUBFLEET_DEV_APP_EXECUTABLE` named when it started (design D-21). The daemon saves the 32-byte token as its first work after accept, before logging, connection admission or starting the reader, including its pid version (word 7). After the full request line is read and before acting on a person-only operation, it re-reads the token and requires the same pid and pid version; a difference is refused with the changed process/image named. A missing token, a failed re-read, an unavailable or failed token lookup, an exec after the token was saved, an exited process or pid reuse is a refusal. The allowed path set is computed once at daemon startup from the configured executables and their real paths; the kernel's caller path is compared verbatim, without resolving it through the current filesystem. A genuine symlink launch reports the canonical app path and is accepted. An app update that moves the running bundle aside requires the app to be restarted; the refusal names this fix, because the token identifies a process/image instance without proving that a moved pathname was an allowed executable. A controlling terminal admits no one (D-PB1): the vetoes read only the caller's ancestry as it is now, and a process an agent started can drop its markers, hold a pseudo-terminal and outlive its parent, or fork a child with a terminal of its own once launchd has adopted it; an SSH login has a terminal and no markers too. A caller whose image is outside the allowed path set, a person's Terminal included, is refused with the app named as the fix. A same-user process that replaces its image with the app's executable before the daemon reads its token can still be taken for the app, even for request bytes written before exec: the kernel reports the process as it is now. The check therefore separates the app from Subfleet's own launched processes only as far as those processes do not do that. The follow-up, an app-held credential that proves each person-only request, is what closes this remaining case. Approval nonces appear only in person-only results. [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/conversations/peers.py`; design D-8]
- **C-25.7** (suggested conversations) An agent proposes a separate conversation, an out-of-scope follow-up worth a session of its own, with `subfleet suggest --title T --tldr S (--brief-file F | --brief -) [--workspace DIR | --no-workspace] [--worktree] [--provider claude|codex] [--model M] [--request-id UUID]` (op `suggestion.create`, capability `suggestions.v1`, its limits in `capabilities`). A person starts it (`suggestion.start`) or dismisses it (`suggestion.dismiss`), both person-only (C-25.6); a refused start or dismiss, including a reparented caller with its own terminal, says `person-only: starting or dismissing a suggestion is a person's decision` and leaves an open suggestion open; the conversation or session that made it may withdraw it while it is open (`suggestion.withdraw`; anyone else is refused, exit 7 `not-creator`); and `suggestion.list` returns the open ones newest first, then the 50 most recently closed, each brief cut to at most its first 2,000 characters, never inside what scrubbing put in, unless the request names the suggestion (`suggestion_ids`, at most 50), with `watch_seq`, the `conversation.watch` sequence the rows were read at (every feed row up to it is in them). Only a caller C-25.6 accepts starts a suggestion's conversation; `suggestion.create`, `suggestion.withdraw` and every other op open to agents never start one. A suggestion is a row of `conversations.sqlite3`, in an additive table as the wake tables are (the store stays schema 2, C-26.14), holding its id; its request id and the digest of what it stands for (a retry with the same id and words answers `created: false`; other words are exit 2 `suggestion-id-conflict`); its origin conversation and message (inside a turn, from the calling turn job the CLI sends from `SUBFLEET_TURN_JOB`, C-26.13; else the conversation a calling session is bound to, C-26.3; else none) and the caller's session and job; the title (one line, 1 to 60 characters), the summary `tldr` (one line, 1 to 300) and the brief (1 byte to 32 KiB of UTF-8, its newlines normalized as a message's text is read back, C-24.3), each scrubbed when it is made (C-25.5, C-23.14), with complete raw text scrubbed before whitespace folding or newline normalization, and folded title/summary text and newline-normalized brief text scrubbed again before any cut so what a person is shown is what Start sends, with the count of values scrubbed returned; the folder, checked then as `conversation.create` checks one (C-26.10) for the start it would make (Ask, or read-only for Codex while Codex conversations are, C-26.11), none being a new private scratch folder made at the start and named apart from those `conversation.create` makes; `worktree`, what the agent asked for; where it starts, decided then and kept (`workspace_kind`: `worktree`, with the checkout's top level as `repository`, when `worktree` is set and the folder is a git checkout, otherwise `in-place`); the provider (the origin's by default, else Claude) and a model hint, one this fleet routes for that provider and has not retired (C-26.1, C-26.8; a policy short name becomes the value `models.list` names; none is the provider's default model at the start); and its state, `open`, `starting`, `started`, `dismissed` or `withdrawn`, which never moves back. At most 20 suggestions are open (`open` or `starting`) per origin (per creating session when there is no origin, and one bound for callers with neither) and 200 in all, counted in the transaction that would add one; past either a create is refused, exit 7 `too-many-suggestions`, and closing one frees its place. `starting` is a person's claim: the transaction that takes it from `open` also writes a new conversation's row and chooses its first message's id, a random UUID no view carries until that message is accepted. The conversation has no request id: the suggestion's `started_conversation_id`, written in that transaction, is its only link, and Start never adopts a conversation found by any key; request ids beginning `suggestion:` are refused to `conversation.create` and `conversation.handoff` (exit 2 `reserved-request-id`). It has the suggestion's title and its hinted or default model at Ask unless the person names a permission, one above Ask only with `confirm_widen: true`; it is a worktree conversation when `workspace_kind` is `worktree`, otherwise in place, and a worktree start whose folder is no longer a git checkout is refused (exit 7 `not-a-repository`, the suggestion staying open), never run in place. So a suggestion that has a conversation can no longer be dismissed or withdrawn (exit 7 `too-late`), and one dismissed or withdrawn never gets a conversation (`suggestion.start` refuses it, exit 7 `not-open`). Until its brief is accepted the conversation takes no other message: every write of a message (`message.submit`, a wake's, a continuation's, an unblock note, a tombstone, legacy history) checks in its own transaction that no `starting` suggestion names the conversation, and is otherwise refused (exit 7 `conversation-starting`), whatever its id, text or predecessor. The start then cuts the worktree (C-26.10) and submits the brief as the conversation's first message with `message.submit`'s rules (C-24.2, C-24.3: its text published before its row, its digest, and no job until the dispatcher makes one) under the id the claim chose, into the empty conversation, in the transaction that records `started`, so `started` holds exactly when that message exists, and it is the conversation's first. Every step is idempotent: a retry, a second click or a restart finishes the same start with the same conversation and the same message (`created: false` once started). A start that fails after its claim keeps the claim and records why (`start_error`: a fixed diagnostic). Every client-visible and persisted suggestion error (wire message and repair field, stored suggestion row, view, list, feed, event, card, app notice, CLI output and retry log) is built only from our own message templates, fixed exception class names, validated errno numbers and their `os.strerror` text, and values we validated ourselves (ids, enum members and counts). Caller text, exception text, exception arguments and filenames never reach those error channels: no `str(exc)` or `repr(exc)` is used to construct them, including for structured arguments, byte filenames and Unicode failures. Model hints must be known aliases or model ids this fleet routes, with invalid and retired values refused without echoing them. The CLI accepts daemon error reasons only by exact matching against this finite template vocabulary and renders its own templates; optional exception repair fields are never echoed. Its argument parser, brief-read and workspace-resolution errors follow the same rule. The generic protocol layer applies the same structural rule to framing, envelope and argument refusals for every operation: a fixed template names the field and reason, without quoting its rejected value, parsing exception or repair text. A handler's refusal (a `ProtocolError` or `AdapterError` an operation raises, a `lanes transfer` refusal included) is not an argument refusal: it reads its own fixed template, chosen by a closed reason (`subfleet/refusals.py`), which names only values checked against their form or a closed set (a job id or lane id of its form holding no recognized credential, read as C-1.5 reads a request id, so `20261013-000000-sk-…` is left out, the job's task, a known isolation variable or Codex configuration layer, a branch, a provider, a count, a checkout refusal and its fixed repair, a preflight verdict kind, a resume/revive verb, or an errno name). Suggestion refusal templates separately check protected-folder labels, folder and checkout statuses (`gone`, `no-longer-repository`, `secret-shaped`), and the `unreadable-spelling` protected-path status. An existing folder refused for its secret-shaped path says the path cannot be used because it looks like it holds a secret, without echoing the path, and asks for another folder or a new scratch folder. An unreadable protected-path spelling asks to make every directory above it searchable, or choose Ask or read-only; `workspace.check` and `conversation.create` give the same repair. The guard template names SHA-256, TRUST and the pinned Codex version only for `trust`; `environment` asks to restore the required directories and binaries, `config` asks to correct the preflight configuration, and `version` asks for the supported Codex version; each keeps the doctor repair. So the different-payload refusal names its job (C-16.3), a job that writes by policy outside git keeps the fix `-s read-only` (C-11.1), an isolation refusal names its variable or layer (C-23.3), and an unknown job is named. A value that fails its check is left out, never quoted, by a less specific form of the same refusal, which keeps its repair; a writer's refusal of a job that writes by policy keeps its own reason and adds `(<task> jobs write by policy)` and `pass -s read-only`; and a refusal with no known reason reads `operation: request was refused`, without its exception's text or repair. Conversation refusals outside suggestions keep their `reason: text` form through the C-25.5 scrubber: the text is our own and may name the ids, states and paths the request or the store holds, and errno text, but no exception's text, and neither an approval's decision nor a handoff's native session id is quoted. A message's `state_reason` after a turn submit the daemon refused (`not-delivered: …`) or put off (`deferred: …`), or a steer it sent back to the queue because its input or driver failed (`steer-missed: input-unavailable: …`, also its `steer.missed` events), which `message.status`, `conversation.open` and `conversation.watch` show, is a conversation refusal's reason, a request or handler refusal's template message (an argument check's exception keeps its own words for its direct callers only), or an exception's class with, for an OS error, its errno and `os.strerror` text; a refused wake request's `wake-refused` event (C-24.10) shows the wake module's own fixed words for a `bad-wake` or `wake-id-conflict` refusal, which name a refused run target only by its position among the request's targets (`run target 3 of 16`), never its id, else the exception's class. Correlation ids are admitted before routing as ASCII letter/digit/`-_` tokens of at most 128 characters (nonnegative decimal integers are accepted for older clients; empty or missing means no correlation), and an id holding a recognizable credential, read as C-1.5 reads a request id, is refused even within that alphabet; an invalid id receives an exit-2 refusal with the id omitted. Retained suggestion events are normalized on every read, preserving their recorded state and admitted content while allowing only current public fields and exact diagnostic templates; legacy free-form `start_error` becomes a fixed fallback. This is a read boundary, with no stored-data migration. Existing scrubbing, whitespace folding and marker-safe 500-character bounds remain defence in depth; the guarantee comes from excluding caller and exception text, rather than recognizing every possible secret format. If details are needed for diagnosis, they go only to the daemon's local `<state root>/daemon.log`, never to a client-visible or suggestion-persisted channel. The details of the request responders', a job's and a turn submit's refusals reach it through `refusals.detail_line`, scrubbed whole (C-23.14), including credential assignments whose key or value has escaped or repr-escaped quotes or whose value lacks a closing quote. Every line and traceback the daemon logs is scrubbed again at the daemon's handler (`subfleet/daemon_log.py`), whoever logged it: a one-line message in C-25.5's line form (`refusals.scrubbed_line`), and a message of several lines, a traceback or a stack in the block form, which keeps its lines and is scrubbed whole; folded, a block is exactly the uncut line form (when folding lets the scrubber find more, the folded text is written), so neither form shows a value the other hides; an exception group's traceback, whose members' lines Python writes behind a `    | ` margin, keeps line by line only what both it and the same text without the margins show, so its block may hide more than the line form, never less; a part of a record is read as a group's when, before it is cut, it holds a group separator's mark (`+` and sixteen `-`), and any text with a line that starts with a group's margin is read as one. Before it is scrubbed, a record's text (its line, traceback and stack together) is cut to 16,384 characters (`daemon_log.RECORD_SCRUB_MAX`), each part to its share, keeping whole lines of each part's head and tail with `… [N characters omitted] …` between them, at line breaks no match of the whole-text scrub crosses, folded to one line or not (`handoff.cut_whole`: `scrub_bounded`'s neutral breaks; no break between a credential flag and the value folding joins to it, as the flags rule reads them; outside a quoted value, a private key, its first and last lines read as folding lets the scrubber read them, and a data URI's body; an exception group's margins read as spaces); a part with no such break in reach, as one long line, keeps only that marker, and so does a side of a part whose window holds more than eight distinct escaped quote kinds (a quote and the length of the run of backslashes before it); a text holding a reminder tag is not cut. That rule stays for `cut_whole` itself, which cuts each record the handler writes, whose words may reach a model (an agent that reads `daemon logs`), for two reasons: which tags a quoted value hides, and so which reminders the whole-text scrub strips, is known only after a pass of the quoted-value rule over the whole text, which the cut's work bound rules out (its regexes read only its windows); and a record is cut once, when it is written, so keeping only the marker loses that record's words alone. What a person reads of daemon.log rereads the whole file every time, so it resolves the file's reminders before it cuts it (below). Every regex the cut runs reads within its two windows, at most 16 times the bound in all however long the text is, counted by what each call reads, not its span: each character test of a backtracking match, at each position a scan tries (`tests/regex_reads.py`, whose matches are checked against `re`'s). The quote regex starts a match only where a run of backslashes starts (`(?<!\\)(\\*)(["'])`), so a run that no quote follows is read three times, not again from each of its backslashes (review r5 of #233: round 6's `(\\*)(["'])` read a run of 8,000 about 32 million times, 0.7 s a cut and 0.3 s a `--follow` feed, while its span, all round 6's meter counted, was within the bound). Past them it reads only in C scans of `str` and `bytes`: a fixed number of scans of the text, plus at most 20 needle looks. The fixed scans include, where a look past a window meets a group's margins, a few scans of each run of lines holding only whitespace and margins, each run read once. A needle look is one for each quote kind open at a candidate cut (`_event_from`, `_event_before`), two plain kinds and at most eight escaped ones a side, and is at most 64 finds of its quote, which together read the text at most once. Both are counted (`test_daemon_log_cut_windows`): with nine kinds open in the head and nine in the tail, review r4's shape reads about 21 passes over the text, about 18 of them its 18 looks, and review r3's and the round-2 shapes read 2 to 6 passes in all, their few looks (at most 10, on the round-2 shape with eight escaped kinds) each a small part of a pass. A group's margins are blanked by the regex only within the windows and recognized past them where a look reaches one, a reminder tag is found by C scans and only confirmed by its regex, and a leading header (C-23.14's header name, an identifier ending in `authorization` or `cookie`) is matched at the text's first visible character, its name by a C scan of the characters a name may hold and its word within the name's last 13 characters. So a cut's work is linear in the text: past a fixed amount for its windows, a fixed number of C scans and at most 20 looks, each at most a pass. That is the bound the code gives, and it gives no ratio to plain text, whose own cut makes some of the same scans. Ratios are measurements, not acceptance bounds. Measured on this tree (GIL Python 3.14.7, best of five CPU times under the hub's suite lock; the report's round 7 and its follow-ups, `tools/measure_cut_cost.py` and `tools/measure_cut_scaling.py`): margin-only lines in four-byte text cost 3.4, 8.1 and 15.7 times plain text at 1, 4 and 16 MiB in round 7's run, and 5.3, 4.2, 6.6, 7.6 and 8.0 times at 1, 4, 8, 16 and 32 MiB in the follow-ups' (13.4, 46.0, 89.1, 178.8 and 345.0 ms: 10.8 to 13.4 ms a MiB at every size); the ratio moves with plain text's own few milliseconds. That shape's cost is linear, as the count test holds it: its regexes read the same at 1 and 16 MiB, and its C scans come to four passes of the text, where the test allows eight. Margin-only lines among plain lines cost 0.62, 0.84 and 1.22 times plain text, and the 8,000-backslash shape 2.1, 12.3 and 33.4 ms, at 1, 4 and 16 MiB. Python's own traceback formatting, outside the lock, cost 3.7 and 3.5 times the cut on the run of `<` at 4 and 16 MiB, 3.2 times on the data-URI body and 1.1 times on the quote kinds at 16 MiB. A one-line message whose cut keeps only the marker is written as the marker with the record's level and logger name, never its words. The handler formats a record, scrub included, before it takes its lock, which it holds only to write the record's lines, so no line waits for another thread's record to be formatted. A record that cannot be scrubbed, or whose message cannot be rendered, is written as one fixed `[scrub failed]` line naming its level, its logger and the class of any exception it carries, and nothing raised there reaches its caller; a write that fails is reported by a fixed line, never logging's report with the record's arguments. The daemon process's stdout and stderr are daemon.log too, under `daemon start` and launchd alike, so `daemon.main` installs, for the life of the process, `sys.excepthook`, `threading.excepthook`, `sys.unraisablehook`, `warnings.showwarning` and `logging.lastResort` writing through the same formatter: an uncaught exception, a thread's, an unraisable one, a warning and a record no handler takes are scrubbed the same way. The only other writers there use our own words or names the policy holds: faulthandler's stack dumps (C-3.6, C-5.8a) give each frame's file, line and function, never an exception's message, a local or a source line, and on Python 3.14 an all-threads dump also names each thread Python started; C-5.8a's managed stop snapshot (`daemon._dump_stop_stacks`), a raw `os.write` to daemon.log, names every thread on every Python version and gives each frame's file, line and function, never a local, a source line or an exception's message; every daemon thread's name is fixed words or a turn runner's `turn:<attempt id>`, which a lint of every thread `subfleet/` starts holds; the C-5.8a stopping line names classes; and the argument parser answers only the arguments `daemon start` or the plist passed. What the daemon says there itself outside a logger goes through the same formatter (`daemon_log.write_scrubbed`), a line as the handler writes a record of it: a start refused for a held lock, a start refused for its policy or lane roster (C-11.1), whose message keeps its words with any value the scrubber recognizes hidden (`subfleet daemon: <root>/policy.json: admission.token=[REDACTED] is not an admission setting (…)`; the scrubber reads a colon right after a value as the value's), and a retired model alias's note (C-11.1), which names the alias and its target as the policy's `retired` table holds them. No daemon module writes its stdout or stderr but through `daemon_log`, whatever it writes, and none passes a caught exception's words to any write past the handler, to a standard stream or by `os.write`; a lint of every module of `subfleet/` but the CLIs', the v1 shim's and the hook entry point's, each its own process whose streams are a person's terminal or a hook's caller and none of which a daemon loads at start, holds both. Within the modules it reads, the lint exempts from its first rule (any stream write past `daemon_log`) only two entry points that run in a process of their own: `publish_rule.hook_main`, which the PreToolUse hook script `review_publish` generates calls, and `review_publish.wrapper_main`, which the review PATH wrappers it generates call; their stdout is a hook's reply and their stderr the review's shell, never daemon.log. A daemon loads both modules (through the adapters) but calls neither function, and a guard test holds that: each function exists and writes its streams, no module under `subfleet/` calls or imports it, and the generated script names it. Inside those two, as everywhere, a write that passes a caught exception's words is still a finding, and so is any stream write elsewhere in those modules. Every child a daemon module starts has its stdout and stderr redirected or captured, never left the daemon's own (a lint of every `subprocess` start; the guardian's `posix_spawn` opens /dev/null on all three). Each daemon module also logs a caught exception only through `refusals.scrubbed_words`, its class, or its errno, whether it passes it to a log call or builds a message another function of the module logs from it, so the rule does not rest on the handler alone. What `daemon start` prints is in scope too, though it is a person's terminal, because it is the daemon's text: a refusal of its own check before it spawns (C-11.1), a held lane's note, and each row `daemon start --check` lists, its lane id and variable in text and in `--json` (review r1 of #248, P3-2), are printed as the handler writes a record of them (`daemon_log.scrubbed`), and the tail of daemon.log it prints when the daemon does not come up (as `daemon install` does) is the file's text scrubbed as one record, cut to its bound first, whoever wrote it, then its last 20 lines, each kept: where folding lets the scrubber find more, each whitespace-free run of the block is written only where the folded rescrub shows it too, else `[REDACTED]` (`daemon_log.scrubbed_tail`). No reminder tag reaches what a person reads of daemon.log. The handler writes `[reminder-tag]` (`daemon_log.REMINDER_MARK`) for each tag its scrub leaves (an orphan, in any case, folded or not: `İ`, `ı`, `ſ`), so daemon.log this daemon writes holds none. A file an older daemon wrote may hold tags; one long enough to be cut is read with its reminders resolved first (`daemon_log._reminders_resolved`). Where no opening tag comes before a closing one, no reading of the text strips anything, and each tag is replaced by the marker, which every scrub rule reads as it reads a tag. Otherwise the reminders the whole-text scrub strips, found as it finds them, after its first pass has masked quoted values across lines, are taken out and every tag left is replaced by the marker, but only where the first pass over what is left replaces exactly what the first pass over the file replaces outside those reminders, each with the same words (`handoff.quoted_value_spans`). That scrub strips reminders only after its first pass, while the cut and the tail's scrub run every pass on what is left, so a reminder between a key's separator and a quote (`password=<system-reminder>…</system-reminder>"…`) could open a value there that ran past a quote the first pass had masked, or took a private key's or a data URI's first line to its line's end (review r5 of #233). Where the check holds, the whole-text scrub of what is left is, pass for pass, the whole-text scrub of the file. A file holding a group's margin is checked the same way read without its margins, as the block form also reads it: there its reminders must be the same ones, and taking them out must leave the margin-free reading of what is left, but for the line breaks that reminders ending the file leave before them, which the block form does not read (review r6 of #233: a margin file ending in a reminder had kept only the marker for that alone), unless what is left, read without its margins, ends in a data URI body (`;base64,` and body characters) that those breaks bring to the 32 characters its rule needs and its own trailing breaks leave short: the whole-text scrub would hide that body and the tail show it. That costs two passes of the quoted-value rule over the file, four where it holds a group's margins, plus tag scans, span lookups and copies. On round 6's realistic daemon.log (admission lines, tracebacks, a group's margins, quoted words) with one whole reminder on its first line, `daemon logs -n 40` took 405, 1,900 and 6,360 ms of CPU at 1, 4 and 16 MiB in round 7's run and 465, 1,685 and 6,626 ms in the follow-ups': about 400 to 475 ms a MiB, linear in the file, and about twice round 6's single check. A file that must be cut and holds a reminder tag keeps only the cut's marker, as at round 5, exactly when one of these holds: taking its reminders out changes what the first pass replaces, as above; it holds a group's margin and, read without its margins, the whole-text scrub would strip other reminders, or taking them out there changes what that reading's first pass replaces, or does not leave the margin-free reading of what is left but for such trailing line breaks, or leaves it with them after such a data URI body; or taking them out makes a tag where none was (`<sys`, a reminder, `tem-reminder>`). These are conservative fallback conditions; their frequency depends on the input. The round-7 report identifies each census's provenance and completion status. A file short enough not to be cut is scrubbed whole, as before, its tags left marked; and on a file with no tag the tail is exactly what it was. `daemon logs` prints daemon.log the same way, since the file is never rewritten and may hold what a daemon before D-LOG1 wrote raw: its last N lines through `scrubbed_tail`, cut to twice what they hold and the bound more. Scrubbing a range costs what the whole-text scrub of that much text costs. On that realistic daemon.log this tree took 1.9 to 2.3 s of CPU a MiB of range, and 62 to 144 ms for the default 40 lines on files of 1, 4 and 16 MiB, the cut's C scans of the whole file included (round 7's run and the follow-ups'). No flag prints it raw. With `--follow`, it first prints what `daemon logs -n N` prints of the file as it is then, through the same resolution (review r5 of #233: it had printed only the lines its follower had settled, which stop before the first reminder tag in the file's last 24 KiB); the file's end that is not settled yet (at most 16,384 characters of whole lines) is printed then and read again with what is written after it, by the break rule and by the scrub, until a break after it settles. Each new whole line is held until no match of the whole-text scrub could cross the break after it, whatever is written next (`handoff.settled_end`: not after a line that ends in a credential flag, a key, its separator or `Bearer` with nothing visible after it yet, nor inside a value a quote may have opened, a private key or a data URI's body, nor in a private key's first line that more text could still complete, `-----BEGIN` and uppercase words to the text's end, nor after a reminder tag), then written in the block form with its lines kept, each run only where those lines scrubbed after up to 8,192 characters of what was already written show it too, with no reminder tag; ^C writes what is held. The holds apply only to what is written after following starts: past a reminder tag, in the file's unsettled end or in what arrives, new lines are held up to 16,384 characters of new text. Its residual limits: held text past 16,384 characters is written as it is, so the part of a quoted value, a private key (its first line split across more than that included) or a data URI longer than that, or of text after a reminder tag, written before its end may show what its end would hide; a value still open when following starts (a quote, a private key, a data URI) shows in the first lines as `daemon logs -n N` shows it then, and the rest of it, written later, is hidden beside it; a last line still being written then is printed as it stood, and again whole once it ends; a match that starts more than 8,192 characters before the lines being written, and before the file's unsettled end, is not seen; and a held line is written only when more is written or following stops. A job's records follow the same rule: its notice and failure event, a `workspace` hold and the error of a `conversation-blocked` one (`why`, `status`, a turn's `state_reason`), C-6.8's workspace record (`job.workspace_deferred`, `job.workspace_failed`, `show`), an attempt's detail and launch receipt (`spawn_error`), a salvage receipt and the evidence that carries it (C-13.1), and a turn's end-snapshot record, which `turn.diff` shows (C-26.14). A handler refusal there (at admission, a push workspace's build or launch) reads its template, as on the wire; a guard preflight that timed out or could not probe says trust is unverified, not mismatched (C-14.2). A git failure there names git's verb and its cap, exit status or signal; an OS error, its class, errno and `os.strerror` text; any other exception, its class; with the ids and paths the store holds. What git printed (a repository's own hooks and filters print there: a `post-checkout` hook on `git worktree add`, a clean filter on `git add`), an exception's message, args and filename, and a handler refusal's own words and repair go to daemon.log only, scrubbed whole; git's words still decide whether a failure waits (C-6.8). Outside this rule, as before: C-6.12's route records (a route hold, a route refusal's notice and event, `why`), which keep the scheduler's message or the evaluation error's class and text; host-push results (the job's `push_error` and the notice's push line), which keep `host_push`'s own messages and may name the pushed branch and paths in the pushed tree; an attempt's outcome as its adapter reads the provider's output; C-6.17's `disk` hold, which keeps its reading's OS error for the policy's path; a quarantine's containment record; and `gate.*` results. Responder, retry and control-loop errors otherwise use fixed diagnostics; Start again finishes it, and so does the daemon, on the file pool and never on the control loop, once the claim is 30 s old and then after 2, 4, ... s, at most every 5 minutes. Every change of state writes, in its transaction, one `conversation.watch` row (C-29.9) naming the suggestion (`suggestion_id`) and carrying its view as it is now (`suggestion`, its brief cut as a listing's), under its origin, or under conversation id "" when it has none, which a client older than this clause reads as a list to refresh; and, for one with an origin, one `suggestion` event in that conversation on the turn's message (C-25.4), carrying the view and the whole brief where the 64 KiB row allows it. A view holds only what C-25.5 lets a person see: the agent's scrubbed words, where and how it would start, who decided and when, and what it started; never its request id, digest, start settings, calling job or, before it is accepted, its first message's id. A folder path, caller session or calling job the C-25.5 scrubber would change is refused when the suggestion is made (exit 2 `bad-workspace`, `bad-session-id`, `bad-calling-job`), and a character cut that would split any redaction marker (a title's, a summary's, an error's, a Start's recorded verdict's or a listed brief's) retreats to the marker's start; if the remaining text is a recognizable sensitive assignment fragment, it retreats by words while preserving whole markers at each cut, so no string in a suggestion's row, view, feed row or event is one the scrubber recognizes. An agent inside a conversation turn learns of the verb from the `SessionStart` hook (C-15.2), which adds one stable line naming `subfleet suggest` and when to use it (`hooks.SUGGEST_HINT`) to the session's context when the session is new, cleared or compacted, and only inside a turn (`SUBFLEET_TURN_JOB`): a turn's command line, environment and frames are unchanged (C-24.9's recording, d714's argv). Codex turns have no such channel that leaves their verified frames and hooks override unchanged; their agents find the verb in `subfleet --help`. The caller's turn job and session are taken as the caller states them, as `conversation.wake` takes them: a process that names another conversation's turn can withdraw that conversation's open suggestions, never start one. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/suggestions.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/hooks.py`, `subfleet/protocol.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/daemon_log.py`, `subfleet/refusals.py`, `subfleet/ids.py`, `subfleet/salvage.py`]

## 26. Turns under admission

- **C-26.1** (a turn is a submission the daemon makes) Each turn is a job of kind `turn` with request id `turn:<message id>:<n>`, created only by the daemon's dispatcher through the ordinary submit validation, with `max_attempts` 1, no parent, `in_place` in the conversation's workspace, and the message digest as its payload digest. It is admitted by `evaluate` inside the reserving transaction like any job (C-6.3; C-23.54's one dispatch path). The socket `submit` op refuses `kind: "turn"` and request ids beginning `turn:`. A turn submit the daemon refuses before the job exists sent nothing: a refusal of the message as it is (exit 2 or 7, such as a checkout on `main` without `allow_main`, a missing attachment, or a model the fleet does not route) fails it `not-delivered: <reason>`; any other refusal (exit 1, such as git timing out while inspecting the workspace, or a store or file error) leaves it in its state with `state_reason` `deferred: <why>`, submitted again after 2 s, then 4 s, doubling to at most 300 s, never on every pass. A message withdrawn while its job is being submitted takes that job with it (C-24.7). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.2** (routing by provider) A Claude turn routes by model across Claude lanes whose credential resolves to the default config directory (`keychain-token`, `env`); a `home`-kind Claude lane is not a candidate (`config-dir`). A Claude turn prefers the lane that served the conversation's previous turn while that lane stays eligible, and each move is recorded with its reason. A Codex turn is pinned to the lane whose home holds its thread; a new Codex conversation's first turn routes by model and pins the conversation to the lane it ran on. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/scheduler.py`]
- **C-26.3** (one writer per native session) A turn holds `conversation:<id>` and `native:<provider>:<native session id>` from reservation through finalization or quarantine. Resume, revive and handoff reservations wait while either is held, and `resume` refuses a `turn` source. A live process outside Subfleet holding the session (Claude: a pid in `~/.claude/sessions/*.json` naming it, in whichever case either spells its UUID, without Subfleet markers and not an owned identity; Codex: the thread reports an active turn) makes the turn wait `external-writer`, shown to the person, not a refusal: a Claude message is not dispatched while the process lives (a dispatch wait, with no job made), and an attempt that finds either writer when it starts (the Claude app may take the session while a job waits for admission) ends before its message is written and re-admits it, however often (C-24.6). Admission holds nothing for a writer. Conversation-bound sessions are never listed, nudged, revived, cold-swept or handed off by the sessions kit (C-26.13), whichever case a UUID is spelled in, and the daemon refuses to record a nudge of one. Native UUIDs compare in canonical lowercase form in conflict checks and all native-session lease keys, including old raw keys; opaque native ids and display spellings remain as supplied. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/sessions/`]
- **C-26.4** (the guardian relays, it does not interpret) A turn's guardian owns the provider's stdin as a pipe and accepts connections on `run/<16 hex>.sock` (0600 in a 0700 directory) only from the process `daemon.lock` names (pid, boot id, start time). Frames carry a number, a tag and the SHA-256 of their line; each is logged as an intent with fsync before the pipe write and then as `written` or `failed`. A resent number is acknowledged as a duplicate only when its hash matches a written frame; other content is a conflict; a failed or unfinished write is never reported as applied and ends relaying. It never parses provider messages. The relay advertises its version and frame cap in `start.json` and answers `status`, which is not a frame (no number, never logged, answered only after any frame being applied is logged), with the frames applied, whether stdin is closed, whether the child runs, and the cap. The runner accesses its relay only by assigning it or calling `status`, `send` and `close`. `send` accepts an optional per-call `before_send` callback: after frame encoding, cap validation and connection setup, the client invokes it immediately before writing the frame to the socket. The runner records the durable stop intent before calling `send` (C-9.2). The callback is never stored on the client, used by `status` or later sends, or invoked when local cap validation or connection setup prevents transmission. A runner asks for it before it sends or replays anything and then sends nothing on a guess: a relay whose count differs from its log, or whose log shows a frame not written, is not written to; a relay that answers `status` as a malformed frame (version 1) is read from its log as before. `message.submit` refuses a Claude message whose text and images cannot fit one frame (`capabilities` limit `relay_frame_bytes`); a frame over the cap is never sent, stdin is closed instead and the turn is reconciled (C-24.6). An unanswered frame or `status` is retried at most 5 times after doubling pauses; then, as after `conflict` or `failed`, nothing more is written. Every relay loss permanently ends the runner's relay I/O, separately from whether the child lives: no later frame is transmitted, including one generated by replaying a buffered initialize response, a steer, an approval response, a title request, a signal or stdin close. A loss after the child has verifiably exited is the provider's end. The runner keeps that exit proof even if `exit.json` never arrives, drains every stdout chunk to an empty read (stdout is a file), within the bound below, and lets the provider's result or EOF decide the turn (`ended-without-result` when no result arrived), without setting `relay_failed` or a `relay-failed` stop. The relay's reply (its optional `child_exited: true` refusal field), a fresh `status` naming the child `exited`, or the attempt's exit receipt proves exit; a pipe error alone proves none. The relay reads its child's exit from Popen's own record (`returncode`, then `poll()`, which reaps only while Popen's lock is free and stores what it reaps), or else from the kernel by `waitid` with `WEXITED | WNOHANG | WNOWAIT`, which never reaps, since the guardian's blocking wait holds Popen's lock and `poll()` then reports no exit even for a child that has exited. It peeks through `os.waitid`, or through libc's `waitid` by ctypes where `os` has none (CPython 3.12 on macOS); `relay.EXIT_PEEK` names the binding, chosen at import. ECHILD from the peek means the guardian's wait has reaped the child, so it has exited; the relay logs any other failure once and takes it as no proof. No peek reaps: the guardian's wait reaps the child, or Popen's `poll()` does if the relay reads the child before that wait takes Popen's lock, and Popen stores the real status either way, which the guardian's wait returns. Signal delivery reads the same evidence: the relay answers a signal frame for a child that has exited `no-child`. A `status` naming the child `exited` is that proof whenever a runner reads one, whatever the relay log shows: a runner rebuilt after a restart, whose predecessor's proof was never followed by a receipt, proves the exit again from its own status, even after a `closed` refusal, which logs nothing. A relay loss while replaying an owner-ended attempt follows the same path using that recorded execution boundary, which may be forced and does not by itself prove every writer exited. Continuous stdout, for example from a surviving descendant, cannot hold that drain forever. Whichever way the runner enters the drain (a proved exit, with or without a relay loss, the exit receipt or an owner-ended attempt), it records the size stdout has on disk then and reads all of it, however long that takes. A failed read of that size, other than the file's absence (size 0), is tried again up to three times, 50 ms apart; a size still unknown is never taken as zero, and the reader has then caught up at its first read that reaches the end of the file (one short of a whole chunk, or empty), since stdout is append-only. Only output written after that entry is bounded: `EXIT_DRAIN_S` (30 seconds) starts when the reader has read up to that size, and the runner checks it after each chunk. A runner rebuilt after a restart records its own entry size and starts its own clock, so each rebuild can add up to one bound. At that deadline it requests containment without relay I/O, records `stdout-drain-timeout` unless a stop reason already exists, and settles from the output already read, retaining a provider result or applying EOF if there was none. Without exit evidence or an owner-ended replay boundary, the child is treated as live for this decision, and a turn without a terminal event is stopped as C-24.7 escalates, skipping the steps that need the relay; a refusal while the child lives remains `relay-failed`. Exit proof counts as the runner classifies a loss: a receipt or `status` that arrives after a loss was classified `relay-failed` lets the drain read stdout but does not undo that classification. A runner that ends on an unexpected exception settles its turn as such a loss: no frame is written after it, unwritten steers are missed, and its stop reason is `relay-failed` unless the turn already had an outcome or a stop reason; it requests containment at once unless the attempt's exit receipt exists or the job store ended the attempt, then settles from the stdout already read. A runner its service's close() stopped, or whose store refused it as closed, is left to a later daemon (C-26.6). Replay applies the same exit distinction and permanent I/O boundary to an unwritten frame in the relay log. When the child exits, relay shutdown wakes idle reads by shutting down only the connection's read half, and waits for the exchange spanning a read, its application and its reply before returning to publish `exit.json`, for at most one second (`REPLY_TIMEOUT_S`, the bound a reply write has on its own). Frames already read that finish within it, including a `close` that caused the child to exit, retain their normal acknowledgements. An exchange still going at the bound is cut. Its connection is shut down both ways, each half on its own (macOS refuses `SHUT_RDWR` once the read half is shut), so its client reads end of stream and no acknowledgement, and the frame's fate is read from the log as for any unanswered frame. A stdin write still waiting for the pipe to drain gives up and is logged `failed` with errno `ECANCELED`; the provider may have read part of it. A write that finds the pipe already closed by shutdown's cleanup is logged the same way. The pipe's write end is non-blocking for this, and a waiting write looks for the cut every 50 ms. The relay writes one fixed line, which names no frame, tag or client, to its standard error by a raw write of descriptor 2, not through `logging` or `sys.stderr`, so a write held there (a pipe nobody reads) holds no lock that interpreter shutdown takes (`logging.shutdown` takes every handler's lock, and the exit's flush of the standard streams their buffers' locks) and cannot hold the guardian's exit. The guardian's standard streams are `/dev/null` under the daemon (C-5.1), so the durable record of a cut write is its `failed` entry. Shutdown then waits at most `JOIN_TIMEOUT_S` (one second) for the relay thread and at most `CLEANUP_TIMEOUT_S` (one second) for its cleanup, which runs on a thread of its own: it closes the listening socket, closes the pipe, writes the cut line and unlinks the socket file, each step taken whether or not the one before failed, and it takes no lock but the pipe lock, which nothing at interpreter shutdown takes. If that thread cannot start, shutdown runs on its own thread only the steps measured not to wait: it closes the listening socket, and closes the pipe if no write holds the pipe lock at that moment, and returns. It does not write the cut line. It leaves the socket file, on which nothing listens (a connect is refused, as for a guardian gone), for the daemon's finalization of the attempt, which unlinks a turn's relay socket as it does for a guardian stopped by containment; a later bind at that path replaces it. A pipe that a write still holds is closed by the guardian's exit. So relay shutdown returns within `REPLY_TIMEOUT_S` + `JOIN_TIMEOUT_S` + `CLEANUP_TIMEOUT_S`, three seconds, whatever the child, a stdin reader, a client, the disk or the host's thread limit does, and an idle client, a client that stops reading, a process that keeps the child's stdin open without reading after the child exits, and a stalled journal write or fsync cannot hold the receipt; nor can a stalled write of the cut line hold the guardian's exit after it. A frame's `stdin.jsonl` records are written and fsynced under a journal lock held for the whole frame (its intent, its pipe write, its outcome), and never under the pipe lock, which covers only the pipe's writes and its close. So the records keep their order (each frame's intent, then its outcome, frame by frame) and their fsyncs, `status` still waits for a frame being applied, and only a pipe write still under way can delay the pipe's close. A cleanup still running at its bound finishes on its own: the pipe closes as soon as such a write lets go, and what is still open when the guardian exits, the exit closes. Past the bound, `exit.json` can come before the cut frame's outcome record is written or durable. A reader then finds the frame `pending` (or `failed`, not yet fsynced), as for a guardian that died mid-write, and its delivery is unknown either way (C-24.6). A connection accepted after the shutdown wakeup checks the stop flag before its first read. stdout and stderr remain files; `exit.json` still ends the attempt (C-5.1). [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/relay.py`, `subfleet/guardian.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`]
- **C-26.5** (a turn's end) The provider's terminal event (Claude `result`; Codex `turn/completed`) ends the turn, after which the daemon closes stdin. Output after it is attached to the same message and never changes the outcome. A Claude turn launches with `CLAUDE_CODE_PRINT_BG_WAIT_CEILING_MS=120000` and without `Monitor`, `CronCreate`, `ScheduleWakeup` and `RemoteTrigger`; a process alive policy `conversations.after_result_s` seconds after its terminal event (default 135: the ceiling plus 15 s) is stopped as C-24.7 escalates. Claude steer sends a UUID-bound stream-json user row with `priority: "next"` only with `msg_lifecycle_v1`. It handles both folding at a tool boundary and a separate queued turn, tracking lifecycle, replay rows and result UUID lists. Pending steers keep stdin open across intermediate results. While the host’s result is held for steers the CLI has not shown taking, and no steer runs as its own turn, a 15 s watchdog asks the CLI to cancel those unseen steers (`cancel_async_message`); 15 s later, with no receipt or a `cancelled: false` one, it gives up those steers alone as unknown (delivery-unknown rather than risking duplication) and the turn ends with the held result. A held result whose `queued_turn_count` is positive with no steer pending is bounded the same way, with nothing to cancel. The watchdog never runs during a steer’s own turn, never cancels or ends a delivered steer, and refuses new steers only while its round is open. A cancel receipt refreshes it. A steer the CLI starts after the held result runs as its own turn until the next result, even one a stop’s receipt or lifecycle then calls cancelled; a result ends every turn a started steer is part of, whether or not its bounded `user_message_uuids` lists it. A result held only for a steer that is then not written (its cancel or a stop won the handover, its frame over the relay cap, its input unreadable) ends the turn at once. stdout ending while the host’s result is held ends the turn with that result; only a steer’s own turn cut short leaves the outcome to reconciliation. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`]
- **C-26.6** (drivers replay) Turn drivers are pure: the daemon rebuilds one after a restart by replaying the attempt's stdout; a runner that finds the provider gone reads all of stdout before it ends the turn, and ends a Codex turn whose thread went idle as complete (C-26.5); a later runner restores a recorded no-send execution boundary before starting or replaying its driver, keeps the outcome an earlier runner for the attempt recorded in `turn.json` (it knew what a replay cannot: a legacy withhold, a stop's reason, an idle settle), and writes no new provider work for a recorded terminal turn (neither the user frame nor a person's approval answer), adding only what stdout said after it; an interrupt is not new provider work, so the rebuilt driver's interrupt (a model mismatch's, C-26.8), the close and the stop's signals are still sent; a frame whose tag the relay log shows as written is not sent again; replay applies each recorded approval answer to the driver as soon as its request becomes pending, before feeding the next stdout line; a written answer restores driver state without rebuilding or resending its reply, while an unwritten answer keeps the relay and terminal-outcome guards; live answers still go through the command queue; replay reconstructs the state and events of a handed-over user frame without rebuilding its image payload, even if attachments have disappeared or the state root moved, and an interrupt it shows written is one the rebuilt driver asked for; a person's or legacy hold's stop recorded after the message was handed over is the rebuilt turn's stop too, its interrupt sent (unless written) once the replay has read the stdout there is, so the turn settles as stopped, as it would have without the restart; events are unique by `(attempt, source, position, ordinal)` with no NULL part and are not stored at or below the attempt's watermark. Codex steer sends `turn/steer` with threadId, expectedTurnId, input and clientUserMessageId, waiting for the turn id when necessary. A matching userMessage.clientId establishes delivery, later agent output establishes an answer, and late responses/items remain readable after turn completion. Once the turn's id is known, a notification whose `turnId` names another turn is ignored; before it is known, none is dropped (a steer is sent only once it is known). Both drivers expose steer(message_id, text, images) and Outcome.steers. The runner tags frames `steer:<mid>`, records written/unsent/refused and fate in turn.json, replays written intents without resending, and reissues unwritten claims only after stdout replay catches up while still steerable. Oversized steer frames are refused without closing host stdin. A steer’s images resolve by digest as the host’s do (C-28.1): the Claude driver reads verified bytes and the Codex driver publishes a sha-keyed copy into the attempt directory; any failure building or taking a steer requeues it (`steer-missed: input-unavailable: <its class>`, for an OS error with its errno, C-25.7) and the runner goes on. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `runner.py`]
- **C-26.7** (limits end the turn; continuation is new) A limit or credit refusal after a message reached the provider ends the turn `failed` (`limited`); its closure is recorded at finalization (C-9.6) before anything else is submitted. With the conversation's `auto_continue` on, a Claude conversation gets a continuation message (new id, `origin: failover`, fixed text, excluding the limited lane), one per limited message and at most two per original message, written before the limited message shows `failed` (it cannot run while that message is live), ahead of any queued person message; the original message is never sent again. A Codex conversation waits for its lane. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-26.8** (served facts, not requested ones) Each turn records the lane, account label, served model, effort, and Fast state the provider reported. Settings are validated against the provider's own catalog for that account before the message is sent (design D-19): the lane's claimed account is its email label (C-1.4), compared with the email Claude's `initialize` reports; a Claude model is a catalog value (`opus[1m]`) or a model id, resolved through the entry with that value or else the entry that resolves to the model admission routed to; a Codex turn always asks for the routed model id; a served model that differs from the request (after removing `[1m]`, ignoring `<synthetic>`) ends the turn `failed` (`model-mismatch`). A turn attempt is classified from the driver's recorded outcome in `turn.json` and structured provider evidence only (design §7), never from prose.
