Turns are exempt from C-4.3's provider-stream freeze, store no frozen stdout copy, and a capture-path failure cannot override the driver's `complete` outcome. `models.list` reports what each provider's catalog offers; a value not yet observed is checked by the driver before the message is sent. Claude Fast is shown as billing usage credits and needs a per-conversation confirmation; a served Fast state that differs from the request is a visible warning. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `classify.py`] Claude's ultracode (xhigh effort plus standing dynamic-workflow orchestration) is an effort a conversation may name wherever the model's catalog offers xhigh, and `models.list` offers it there: the turn runs with `--effort xhigh` and `{"ultracode": true}` in its command-line settings (the settings key 2.1.280 documents; its undocumented `--effort ultracode` alias is not used), or with the xhigh effort alone when read-only, whose tools include no Workflow tool. The effort a Claude turn records as served is the provider's own report: right after the message the driver asks `get_settings`, and its `applied` answer gives `ultracode` when that flag is on, else the effort applied (`none` when the provider applied none); what the command line asked for is kept as `effort_requested`. A CLI without `get_settings` leaves the served effort unrecorded, and a runner that finds the message already sent by an earlier runner (a replay, including an attempt from before this rule) does not ask. A message that names no effort runs at the policy's `conversations.default_effort` for its provider (ultracode for Claude, the provider's own for Codex), resolved per turn and only where the catalog a turn last reported for the model offers it; `default_effort: null` turns it off for every provider. A default is never refused before sending: where the account's own catalog lacks it the turn still goes ahead and the provider applies what the model allows (2.1.280 drops xhigh and ultracode for Haiku), which the served effort then shows; an effort the person named is refused as before. The stored settings keep `effort: null`, the turn records `effort_default`, and `models.list` names the default (`conversation_default_effort`); the composer shows the efforts and default of the model it has picked, saved or not.
- **C-26.9** (attended turns: first, not reserved) Turn jobs have an admission pass of their own. `_admit` runs it before the pass over detached jobs (unless the turn worker is in one), and the control loop also runs it alone, beside the detached pass, on its own worker (`admission:turns`), so a turn submitted while a detached pass works through a backlog, prepares a workspace or waits on a probe is placed by the next turn pass, not after the detached pass; a turn half that raises does not stop the detached half, and its error is raised after it (C-6.12) (incident: 2026-09-26, at load 110 to 170 with 48 jobs queued, a turn waited about 725 s to be placed and ran in about 20 s; `why` said no pass had reached it 12 minutes in). The two passes never look at the same job, each keeps its own holds and freed-lease record, placements are noted for C-6.11 as each is made, and C-6.3's reservation finds its job no attempt in flight. With no turn queued and none held by the last turn pass, the turn pass is one indexed statement, so offering it every tick costs a starved daemon nothing it needs. Within a pass, turn jobs sort ahead of detached jobs of the same tier; a turn that is not placed never enters the detached jobs' waiters, holds none of them `behind-older-job`, and causes no `slot-kept`. Among turns, C-6.9's hold applies only while the policy sets a turn cap: with neither cap set (the default), an unplaced turn holds no later turn `behind-older-job` or `slot-kept`, since no turn slot exists for a later turn to take (review of 5549d87d: an older turn waiting on its closed pinned lane, or on a checkout another turn held, kept a third conversation's turn waiting while other lanes were free). With a cap set, a turn waiting for a lease still holds none back (C-6.9: it waits for no slot); under `max_active_turns` the last turn slot is kept for it. What an older turn waits for is kept for it lease by lease instead: a later turn that needs a lease an older turn of the pass is queued for is held `lease-held`, the lease under `queued` and the older turn under `queued_behind`, capped or not, and one that needs none of them is not held. A key a turn needs free but never takes (`worktree:<target>`, held by a detached writer or retention, C-24.5) is not queued for: every turn waiting on it may start when it is released. A turn skipped on its clock keeps its place for the keys its last hold named under `leases` or `queued`. Turns trigger no admission probe; a lane whose verdict would require one is not a candidate for a turn (`probe-required`). Turns have their own capacity, counted apart from detached jobs: at most `conversations.max_active_turns` turns at once across the fleet and `conversations.turn_slots_per_lane` on one lane, each null by default, which is no cap (2026-09-27; until then 3 and 1), and a positive whole number when the policy sets one; detached jobs count only detached attempts against `max_active_attempts` and `max_in_flight_per_lane`, and a full pool holds back only its own kind, so an attended turn never waits for a background job's slot (the installed daemon's first turn, 2026-09-24, waited on `no-slot` on every open Claude lane while detached jobs held them). The slot leases are numbered apart too: a turn takes the lowest free `lane:<lane id>:slot:turn-<n>`, a detached attempt `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` from n = 1 (C-6.9), so no attempt holds `lane:<lane id>:slot:0`, the lease a detached job's admission probe takes (C-11.4). With one numbering, the turn pass, which runs first, gave each new turn `slot:0`, and an older writable job waiting on a probe of that lane stayed `probe-pending` through ten turns in a row with no probe (review of d04b8b3; e053b2c had done the same to a job of the turn's own tier). A probe may therefore run beside a live turn on its lane, as it already could whenever the turn held another slot; while it runs it keeps every job off the lane, a turn too. A turn held off a lane by a detached job's admission probe waits for exactly that lease, whenever the probe began: the turn pass counts admission probes' leases (never timer probes', which come and go every cycle) among those whose release is freed capacity (C-6.10), adding the probes a held turn's decision names, so the next turn pass looks at the turn as soon as the probe ends, not on its backed-off clock (review of 5d14f98). The single pass of e053b2c ended every probe before a turn behind it was looked at. Turns are the `attended` class (C-6.9), the first placed, and the machine guard never holds one (C-6.13). Tool approvals wait without a time limit by default: policy `conversations.approval_wait_s` defaults to null (no limit), including when omitted. A positive finite number of seconds remains supported; a turn waiting longer on a tool approval is then stopped with reason `approval-timeout`, never answered automatically. The former 3600-second default protected turn capacity that became uncapped by default on 2026-09-27. A question (approval kind `question`, Claude's AskUserQuestion) always waits for its answer with no limit, even when a tool-approval limit is set (2026-09-28: an agent's question left overnight stopped its turn at the hour and blocked the conversation behind an unfinished-turn banner, with the person's later messages queued behind it). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.10** (attended workspaces) A conversation's workspace may be outside git. A checkout on `main` or `master` requires the conversation's `allow_main`, set by a person (C-25.6). A writable turn (Claude `accept-edits` or `bypass`, a writable Codex turn) is refused (exit 7) when its workspace equals or contains the state root, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, an enrolled lane home, or the guard hook's directory, each side compared in its one spelling (`folders.canonical`, C-6.5), so a workspace or a `HOME` typed in another case than the volume stores is still seen to contain it (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-4). `workspace.check` and `conversation.create` (as does any conversation op that refuses it on the wire) both refuse an unreadable protected-path spelling with the same checked template, `protected-workspace: the folder cannot be checked because a protected path's spelling cannot be read`, and the repair "make every directory above it searchable, or choose Ask or read-only", selected by checked status without exposing the unreadable path or error; a refusing op's own words, which name both, go to daemon.log only, scrubbed. Turn jobs write no salvage ref, at finalization or when a quarantined turn is released (C-5.7); the receipt records HEAD before and after, and a writable turn's working tree is written as tree objects at its start and end, with no ref (C-26.14). C-13.1 and C-13.2 keep binding every job that is not a turn. A worktree conversation (design D-16) is cut by `conversation.create` on branch `subfleet/<conversation id>` at `<state root>/worktrees/conversation-<conversation id>`; the store's update records the worktree on its row (path, branch, source directory, repository, base commit), every conversation view returns it, and the conversation's workspace is the worktree (the same subdirectory when the person picked one). Repeating the create with the same request id finishes a cut that failed or was interrupted, adopting a worktree already on that branch; until then the conversation refuses `message.submit` (`worktree-missing`) and never dispatches in the checkout it was to be cut from. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-26.11** (the never-rules hook applies to turns) Every Claude turn outside `read-only` passes `--settings` with `"disableAllHooks": false`. Every Claude turn, `read-only` included, passes exactly one `--settings` whose `env` sets `CLAUDE_CODE_RETRY_WATCHDOG` to `0` (d1031), intended to end a turn at its plan's ceiling rather than keep retrying; real-Claude user-file precedence is supported by the October 22 peer probe, and this change did not verify process-environment conflicts or safe-mode settings application. Every Codex turn runs only after the C-14.2 preflight and the same-server `hooks/list` check of design D-10, and carries every guard flag an exec launch carries (C-23.6). Codex turns are `read-only` until a live test shows the hook denying a forbidden command and an `apply_patch` in an app-server turn. The Codex thread is opened read-only and a turn leaves no project-trust entry in a lane's `config.toml`. [milestone 9; owner live; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-26.12** (turn jobs are the conversation's) A turn job creates no caller notice, exports no deliverable, and is retained under its own budget (policy `retention.turn_jobs`, default 2,000 jobs, and `retention.turn_bytes`, 4 GiB), separate from C-8.4's, so neither kind of job is pruned to make room for the other: pinned while its message is non-terminal, its conversation blocked, or a live runner reads its attempt (the conversation service answers, and retention asks again inside each delete transaction), and for `retention.turn_keep_days` (14) after it ends (review IR-17). It is not detached work: `list` leaves turn jobs out unless the request names a `kind` (`turn` lists only turns) or sets `include_turns`, and refuses a malformed filter (exit 2), so `runs`, `wait --mine` and `wait --last` never resolve to one; an explicit job id still reaches it, and `runs reap` still reconciles it. `status` shows live turns under their own line and heading, never as running or waiting jobs, and `why` names a turn as its conversation's. The daemon advertises the filter as the capability `jobs.kind.v1` (C-25.1), and `runs` sends `kind` and `include_turns` only after `capabilities` listed it. Against a daemon that does not (one older than `capabilities` answers "unknown op"), `runs --kind` exits 69 naming the restart without asking for the list, `runs --include-turns` sends neither field because such a daemon lists every kind, and `runs` drops the turn rows it returns (every row carries `kind`) and says on stderr that the list may be short when `--last` was applied first. `runs` also drops any row whose `kind` contradicts the request, whatever the daemon advertised. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/offline.py`, `subfleet/render.py`]
- **C-26.13** (a conversation's session is not the sessions kit's) Inside a process Subfleet launched, one whose environment carries a C-5.1 marker (`SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT` or `SUBFLEET_JOB`), `subfleet hook SessionStart` hands no wake to the sessions kit, and `SessionStart` and `UserPromptSubmit` surface and mark `surfaced` (C-15.3) only the notices that name a job (C-15.1); a notice that names no job (a `ping`, a sessions-kit nudge, a timer alert, an imported v1 continuation) is neither printed nor marked there and stays pending. Every launch path sets `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT`; the guardian passes the markers to the provider, and Claude Code passes them to its command hooks (observed with 2.1.280, new and resumed sessions, `-p` and stream-json input). A job that `subfleet run` submits from a turn's Bash tool names the session in `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` as its caller (`cli.session_id`), the conversation's, so when it ends after the turn its notice reaches the conversation's next turn by this layer: `PostToolUse` (C-15.2 layer 2), which runs there unchanged, as does `PreToolUse`'s attached-runner guard (C-23.54), waits only on jobs still running, and a turn's process does not outlive its result by more than C-26.5 allows. The `sessions` op's `state` reports `conversation_sessions`: every native session id a conversation binds, archived or not, and every one a turn attempt ran; `lane_sessions` (C-23.31) still leaves turns out, because a turn may resume a session Subfleet did not create and is no lane run. A `state` reply without `conversation_sessions` comes from a daemon older than this clause, which may already run conversations (`PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 1, C-25.1): every sessions-kit verb that reads it exits 69 with the fix `subfleet daemon stop && subfleet daemon start`, as for an op the daemon does not know, and never reads the silence as an empty list. The sessions kit never lists, nudges, revives, cold-sweeps or hands off one of these sessions, whatever `--all`, `--revive`, `--force` or `--dry-run` say. A request that names only such sessions, or only such sessions and headless lane runs (C-23.31), is refused (exit 7, under `--json` too) by `sessions continue` in every scope (v1's `tickle`, `muster` and `revive` spellings included), by `sessions revive` and by `handoff`, with the reason, checked before C-23.31's lane check, and the fix to continue it in the Subfleet app; a request that also names other sessions acts on those and reports each refused one with its reason. A cold handoff checks each canonical session id against the daemon's lists again at handoff, and a refusal there holds only that session. The daemon refuses the same continuations whatever client asks: a `resume` whose source job is a turn or whose native session is one of these, and a `revive` of one, are refused at submit (exit 7) with that fix and nothing stored, and one accepted before its session became a conversation's is failed with exit 7 by the admission pass that would have launched it, before any lease or attempt; nor does it record a nudge of one of these sessions. Every one of these checks, the kit's and the daemon's, compares a UUID whatever its case on either side: the daemon's list, a registry row, a transcript's name, a conversation's binding, a turn attempt's record and the request may each spell it in upper or lower case. A retry of a request the daemon already accepted is answered as C-6.2 answers any retry (its job id, exit 0; exit 2 for another payload), not refused: the refusal at submit applies only to a request id no job holds. Session ownership uses that same canonical native identity, including brace, urn:uuid: and 32-hex UUID spellings, and is re-read inside the reservation transaction after preparation, refusing a newly bound resume or revive before leases or attempts. A public native open that creates a binding refuses a session whose native: lease is held by a live job (including a quarantined attempt after its job becomes lost); its SQL check and binding serialize with main-store reservation, closing the reservation-to-launch window. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/hooks.py`, `subfleet/sessions/`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.14** (a turn's changes) A writable turn (any permission but `read-only`) whose workspace is a git checkout with a commit has two snapshots of its working tree, each a tree object written through a temporary index by the C-6.8 snapshot, with no ref and with HEAD, the real index and the files untouched: the start is the one admission takes (the attempt's `baseline_tree`); the end is taken at finalization before the turn's leases are released, or when a quarantined turn is confirmed dead, and a forced release with writers still live records that it has none. Both are kept per attempt in the conversation store (schema 2; an older store is migrated in place, a newer one refused) and in the attempt's receipt, which also lists the nested repositories with no commit that the end snapshot left out (C-13.1); recording the end writes one `conversation.watch` row for the message with `state` null, which, as for an approval asked or answered, tells a client to fetch the message again. While git fails transiently the end is tried at most three times in one daemon run (a restart counts again), and once when a quarantined turn is released (an operator's request is not offered again); then the failure is recorded and finalization goes on without an end snapshot. `turn.diff` compares a message's latest turn attempt's start and end snapshots (the attempt recorded last: C-24.5 runs one turn of a conversation at a time) or, before the end exists, its start with the working tree now, saying so (`to.live`); `conversation.diff` compares the start of the conversation's first writable turn (the first recorded) with the working tree now. A `to` that is the working tree now also lists the nested repositories with no commit that its snapshot left out (`to.skipped`, C-13.1), which the diff therefore does not show; a stored end's list is in the turn's receipt and evidence. Both run on the file pool (C-25.3), use git's plumbing with no external diff driver or textconv filter, list at most 1,000 changed files with status and line counts (the stats count every changed file, not only those listed; if git's listing passes 4 MiB they count the files it listed, say `complete: false`, and `files_truncated` is true) and at most 512 KiB of unified diff (the UTF-8 text returned, measured after decoding and scrubbing) cut at a line with `truncated: true`, scrub the diff text (C-25.5) one line at a time, a hunk's line without its `+`, `-` or space prefix, including private-key lines that a hunk's edge, the cut, or a hunk header's function context separates from the armour line at either end, each replaced in its line so every hunk keeps its line counts, and take `path` only relative to the checkout's top level, naming a file or a directory (then every changed file under it) (exit 2 otherwise). With nothing to compare they answer `available: false` in the same shape with a reason (`no-turn`, `read-only-turn`, `no-snapshot`, `snapshot-failed`, `snapshot-pruned`, or `workspace-gone` when git can no longer open the workspace as a checkout, which is never reported as a pruned snapshot). Neither commits, stages, checks out, or writes a ref. Neither claims that one conversation made what it shows (2026-09-29; conversations share folders, C-24.5): a turn's window runs from just before admission began its start snapshot (the attempt's `baseline_at`) to its recorded end (open until then; an attempt that ended with no end recorded, such as one never launched after its runner recorded the start, or a row an earlier build left open, is closed by the service's next tick at the attempt's `finished_at`, a whole-second stamp taken to that second's last millisecond, and the marks its closed window no longer meets are dropped on both sides, each message whose marks changed getting a `conversation.watch` row with `state` null; a quarantined attempt has not ended, since its writers may still be live and it still holds its folder, so its window stays open until its release records the end; a row whose job retention has removed is closed when found, keeping its marks; review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-5), in its folder (C-6.5's write target, the attempt's `folder`), both kept per attempt (`turn_trees` columns `window_start` and `target`, additive, with no schema step; a row written before them keeps its workspace as its folder). Whenever a writable turn's snapshots are recorded, in the same transaction, it and every writable turn of another conversation in that folder whose window meets its own (closed windows; an open end reaches on) are marked as sharing it, each in the other's `shared_json`, and a turn marked later (one whose window began before an ended turn's end but was recorded after) gets a `conversation.watch` row with `state` null. So a turn is marked exactly when another conversation's writable turn's window met its own, whatever order the starts and ends are recorded in (I4, tested as a property over generated schedules in `tests/unit/test_turn_overlap.py`); two turns of one conversation never overlap (I2) and never mark each other. `turn.diff` carries `shared`: one entry per such conversation, `{conversation_id, title, message_ids, from, to}` (its title now; `to` null while one of its turns still runs); `conversation.diff` carries the other conversations whose writable turns in the folder reached into the time since its first writable turn's window began. The app says so above that diff (C-29.10). `shared` names conversations only: no detached writer holds the folder while a turn does, but one that released it between the turn's start snapshot and its reservation can leave edits in the diff that it does not name (C-24.5). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/diff.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`; design D-25]

## 27. Approvals

- **C-27.1** (real requests, bound replies) A provider permission or question request becomes one approval bound to its attempt and the provider's request id, with the exact request stored (0600) and its SHA-256, a random nonce, and options from design §8; the message becomes `approval-needed`. The approval, its `approval.requested` event and that move commit in one transaction, so no reader sees the event while `approval.list` lacks the approval. The display includes every field that changes what is granted. Approval views expose `provider_request_id`, and the same id as `request_id` (C-27.5), so event cards join by message and exact provider request, never by kind or display alone. A view from a daemon older than both fields gets an independent actionable card keyed by immutable approval id; unmatched event cards have no answer controls, and the daemon's whole pending list withdraws them rather than transferring their drafts (C-27.5; `docs/desktop/app-needs.md` 5). `approval.respond` requires the approval id, the nonce, the request SHA-256 the person saw, a live attempt, and a decision among the options; a reply to an answered, withdrawn, or mismatched approval is exit 2, and a duplicate of the recorded reply returns it unchanged. Before `approval.get` returns a request, it reads a private regular file through a no-follow descriptor, bounds it by the relay frame cap, and verifies the returned bytes against the recorded request SHA-256; changed bytes or an unsafe file are refused. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/approvals.py`]
- **C-27.2** (never granted by Subfleet) No approval is answered without a person's `approval.respond` (C-25.6). No answer grants more than the provider asked: no permission updates, execpolicy or network amendments, and no one-tap allow where the provider requires interaction. The only change Subfleet makes to a request's input is adding `answers` to a question. Tool approvals offer Allow and Deny on the inline conversation card, with details and an optional note available on request; masked values retain the explicit review guard. AskUserQuestion stays inline, with option buttons and number keys 1–9 while choices are focused, multiple selections when `multiSelect` is true, Other free text, and Skip. Multiple questions step forward and back through preserved drafts, then send one combined `answers` map keyed by question text; multiple choices are joined with comma and space, skipped keys are omitted, and skipping every question denies the request without stopping the turn. Sending a composer message leaves a pending question and its draft answers intact. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-27.3** (approvals outlive the daemon) After a daemon restart pending approvals are re-derived by replay and remain answerable; a request the provider re-announces is matched by its id. An answered request stays answered during replay: a later provider cancellation writes no withdrawn resolution, and exactly one `approval.resolved` records the person's decision. A stopped, ended, or timed-out turn withdraws its pending approvals, including a turn whose provider exited without a result. Every resolution of an approval says so in the log and the change feed in the commit that makes it: a withdrawal writes `approval.resolved` with decision `withdrawn` (unless the provider's own withdrawal is in the same batch) and a change-feed row; a person's answer writes `approval.resolved` with the decision, a change-feed row and, once nothing of the message is pending, the move back to `running`. So no reader meets a pending card for an approval the daemon has resolved, or a resolved card for one still pending. [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/conversations/approvals.py`]
- **C-27.4** (unsupported requests are refused, visibly) Provider requests Subfleet does not support get the refusal of design §8 or JSON-RPC -32601 and an `error` event; credential requests are never answered with a credential. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-27.5** (a waiting approval is within reach) While a card in the focused conversation is pending, the app shows the strip pinned above the composer, and the strip carries a Review button that opens the oldest pending card (by when it was asked), labelled with the count when several wait; with none pending there is no Review. A question is answered on its inline card (C-27.2), so for a question Review brings that card into view rather than opening the request sheet, which has no form for its answers. Answering the card it opens moves Review to the next, so every pending card is reachable from the strip without scrolling. A card is joined to its approval by the provider's request id, which `approval.list` and `conversation.open` carry as `request_id`, so Review opens the approval of the card it shows or says it is no longer pending. A card is not pending once its message has ended or its delivery is unknown (C-27.3), or once the daemon's whole pending set no longer lists its approval or cannot identify its event card. Legacy views remain reachable on independent approval-id cards (C-27.1). Once the conversation has read its event log to the end after opening, it scrolls each new pending card into view once, when it appears, wherever the person was reading; it brings the oldest into view when the conversation is opened, when the person asks (the strip's Review, or the sidebar's hand badge, which opens its conversation), and once more when the first page of older history that opening loads adds rows above it. The timeline shows turns in the order they began, using each turn's first event rather than its message sequence. Messages waiting behind something sit in the tray above the composer (C-29.7), ordered as the daemon dispatches them: repair origins first, then missed steers, then ordinary messages, with wake messages last, and sequence order within each group (C-24.8, C-24.9, C-26.7). A queued failover continuation remains a timeline notice and counts as ahead of a person message. The view follows the newest row of the turn that began last if and only if the end marker is on screen (apart from the person’s own send and following it until the person scrolls, a new timeline reveal starts or the view is removed, and the initial opening pin). The end marker’s appearance and disappearance, and the measured settlement of an own send at the end, set that visibility; a row-scroll notice never sets it. A reveal that scrolls only the tray leaves the timeline’s follow, opening pin and catch-up unchanged. Each conversation opens with fresh view identity; on macOS 15 and later its initial offset is bottom-anchored. Reading the log after opening moves to the end without animation unless an explicit approval/search reveal that scrolls the timeline has suspended catch-up; later history or event pages then preserve the revealed position while suspension is active, even before the end marker publishes its disappearance. A reveal is outstanding until its own row is presented in the clip, until the person scrolls or sends, or until it is abandoned; while it is outstanding an end that comes into view, as when the document shrinks under the clip, releases nothing. A reveal is abandoned at once when its target row leaves the model before it is presented (a reset, a history change, a rebuild) or is not in the model when it is requested; with the end then on screen, suspension is released as the marker would release it, and otherwise the marker's next appearance releases it. The row's presentation with the end visible beside it releases suspension without scrolling, and once no reveal is outstanding the marker appearing again, or the end in view after the person's own scroll, releases it; catch-up can jump on the next changed key. The person's own send ends any reveal and releases suspension, catch-up included, and goes to the end: it glides there, or during catch-up the settling move jumps there. After the own send, following continues until the person scrolls, a new timeline reveal starts or the view is removed: throughout it, the view follows as if the end were on screen and keeps the clip at the end as the document grows. Separately, the landing check waits for 300 ms without a document or clip change, re-pins a clip left short, and ends once measured end visibility confirms a quiet landing, firing the landing callback once. The marker’s first appearance alone does not end that check, and a quiet landing does not end following. Otherwise changes follow only while the end is on screen. Opening at a pending approval also positions its card without animation. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `app/Sources/UIWindow.swift`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]

## 28. Attachments and drafts

- **C-28.1** (attachments by content) `attachment.add` copies a regular file owned by the daemon's user, opened without following symlinks, at most 20 MiB, with PNG, JPEG, GIF, or WebP magic bytes, into `attachments/<sha256>.<ext>` (0600, fsync) through a temporary file of its own (a random name created exclusively, drawn again if taken), which it renames into place or, when the copy fails, removes if the directory still lets it, and verifies the copy's hash. It copies whenever that name does not hold the daemon's own copy, a regular file of that size with those bytes, owned by the daemon's user, with no second link and no group or other mode bits (so none, other bytes, one it cannot read, a symlink, which it does not follow, a FIFO, a hard link another name writes through, or a copy others can read), so adding an image again repairs a changed stored copy, and the row names the copy where it now is; a copy gone when it is verified is `copy-mismatch`, to try again. It fsyncs `attachments/` after the rename and before the row, as C-8.1 publishes, also when it finds the copy already in place. Adds of the same bytes at once each return the same receipt and leave one copy. A message names at most 8 attachments by hash; when a turn is submitted, the driver re-checks each stored copy the same way, reading at most one byte past its size, never following a symlink and never blocking on a FIFO. At final frame construction, an undelivered image is resolved by digest in the current state root, and its expected size, SHA-256, owner, private mode and link count are verified on the same descriptor whose bytes are used. Claude receives those verified bytes; Codex receives a separate 0400 attempt-local copy published from those bytes. A missing or invalid image ends the driver explicitly as `attachment-missing` and is reconciled, never left running by a payload-read exception. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/attachments.py`]
- **C-28.2** (retention) `message.submit` updates each attachment's last use. An attachment is deleted only in a transaction that re-checks that no non-terminal message references it and it is 30 days unused, then unlinked. Copies inside attempt directories follow turn-job retention (C-26.12). [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`]
- **C-28.3** (drafts and sends survive the app) The app journals drafts and every mutating op with its idempotency key under its Application Support directory (0600) before acting on it, keeps at most one `message.submit` outstanding per conversation in journal order, and resends unacknowledged ops with their original keys after a restart. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/ConversationStore.swift`]

## 29. The desktop client

- **C-29.1** (an explicit endpoint) The app connects only to `<SUBFLEET_HOME or ~/.subfleet>/daemon.sock` and reads only `status.json` and `catalog.json` there. It never runs a v1 executable, never reads the v1 state directory, never starts a broker, and never falls back to either. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/DaemonClient.swift`]
- **C-29.2** (unavailable is a state) A daemon that is down, lacks `capabilities`, or reports another conversation schema shows an actionable status; drafts stay editable and journaled ops stay queued. A daemon that answers busy (exit 69, C-16.7) is shown as busy, never as incompatible, and a send it answered busy stays queued and goes again under its key. The first `conversation.watch` answered after one failed checks `capabilities` again, so a check answered busy never leaves the app waiting. Reloading `status.json` is labelled as reloading a snapshot, never as a new observation (C-18.1). [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.3** (a normal app) The app has a main window with standard window controls, a Dock icon, the standard menus, reopens its window from the Dock, and keeps the menu bar quota panel with an always-present "Open Subfleet" action. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/SubfleetApp.swift`]
- **C-29.4** (a separate development identity) A development build uses bundle id `org.maxghenis.subfleet.dev` and refuses to run against `~/.subfleet`; `app/build.sh` never writes into `/Applications`. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/build.sh`]
- **C-29.5** (`sessions continue` keeps its meaning) C-17.1's `sessions continue` remains interruption recovery; sending a message is `message.submit`. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/cli.py`]
- **C-29.6** (per-account windows, conversations apart) `status.json` publishes, per Claude account, the five-hour, weekly and every model-scoped weekly window (including Fable) with percent, reset time and evidence label, from `provider` and `stale-provider` readings only, and the app shows them. The windows are one list keyed by (`scope`, `window`), the key readings are stored under (C-9.8, C-9.9), so a model-scoped window never replaces the account window of the same name; the older per-account `probe` and `live` fields read scope `account` only. `claude.earliest_reset` is the soonest future reset of an account window on a lane admission could use (enabled, `owner: v2`, not the desktop login, its identity not mismatched: C-10.6). Every job row carries `kind`; turn jobs are left out of `jobs` (C-18.2) and shown in a `conversations` section: counts of active conversations (a message queued or live), conversations needing approval and blocked conversations; live turn jobs by state; and at most 20 conversations that are active, need approval or are blocked, each with id, provider, title, state, pending approvals and its live turn job. The section is read from `conversations.sqlite3` on the timer thread that publishes the file, through a read-only connection of its own and never the store's lock, a request thread or the control loop; a store that could not be read (a SQLite or an OS error, a newer schema) or a reader that failed is `available: false` with the error's name and no counts, never zeros, and never stops the file being written (C-18.1). The menu groups turn work by conversation. Shape: design §12. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `subfleet/status_json.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/timers.py`, `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.7** (the composer) Return sends; Shift-Return and Option-Return insert a newline; IME composition is respected; pasted and dropped images become attachments; the composer stays editable while a turn runs. While a live turn takes steers (`steer.v1` for the provider; the turn running or asking, not stopping, its outcome not yet known; the conversation’s permission not narrower than the turn’s), Return steers the message into that turn and ⌘Return queues it for later, as Claude Code does; the send button is then a split button, primary “Steer” with “Queue for later” and “Stop” in its menu, and when the composer’s picks differ from the turn’s a one-line hint says a steer runs under the turn’s settings. `/` commands and `!` shell input always queue. There is no Tab action. Until the person first scrolls a conversation or the app explicitly scrolls to an approval or search row (C-27.5, C-29.12), every growth in content height keeps the view pinned to the end without animation, including late row measurement and a first events page arriving before the view’s first update. After the first scroll, the person’s own send glides and other changes follow only while the end is on screen, under the existing follow rule. A queued person message, including a send awaiting its receipt, initially belongs in the compact queue tray pinned above the composer when it was not sent as a steer and a live turn, a conversation block (including a turn open elsewhere), an earlier dispatchable message, or a deferral is ahead. Its placement then only moves forward: a tray row stays through an unbound dispatch claim, requeue, deferral and cleared block until a job binds the message (or it is withdrawn or steered into a bound host); a message shown in the timeline stays there through later deferrals, requeues and holds, and its status names the wait and offers Withdraw. message.submit answers queued even for an idle send, which stays in the timeline across its receipt. Before publishing a focused watch or events page, the app fetches message.status for unknown ids; after an unblock or daemon continuation it learns new ids too. A watch re-read uses conversation.status for conversation state and message ids without reading the transcript when conversation.status.v1 is advertised, and otherwise uses the full conversation.open re-read (C-25.1); turn.completed sets the message state. Opening with status support refreshes the complete message-identity set and hydrates only receipts absent from both the app’s known receipt identities and the opening snapshot, in message.status batches of at most 200 before publishing a tray count, even when the queue exceeds the latest 50 receipts returned by conversation.open. Reopening fetches no receipt the app already holds; receipt batches fold with at most one sort, and unchanged sequences do not sort. Opening’s main-thread work stays bounded as known settled history grows: open and reopen each apply in under 50 ms with three queued and 5,000 settled messages. Tray rows show one or two lines of the message, ordered as next_dispatchable (C-27.5), with Withdraw on each person row. A queued unblock note may show there but offers no Withdraw: canceling it would silently change Leave it to Continue it. A queued failover continuation counts as ahead even while it remains a timeline notice. The head person row offers Send now while the live turn takes steers and that message's input and permission permit it; later rows and rows being withdrawn offer no Send now. Send now names the turn the tray showed when it offered the action (`into`), captured with the action, never the turn live when a click lands: one that lands after that turn ended is refused, `no-live-turn` while the message still waits (C-24.9), so it stays queued and never joins the next turn, and its status line says why. These actions belong to tray rows. Withdraw uses message.cancel for a received message and the existing outbox withdrawal for a send with no receipt. A dispatching refusal is retried after 0.1, 0.25, 0.5 and 1 seconds; on too-late a status read identifies only its own live turn, never a steer's host. If that turn ends before interrupting, not-running triggers another status read. Repeating Withdraw on a withdrawn or ended message is not an error. A withdrawal before the turn began leaves one compact line, including an outbox tombstone. Up to four queued rows show in full; longer queues show three and Show N more. An expanded queue uses at most 200 points for its rows, scrolling within the tray; short queues shrink to their content. QueueTray.notice is an optional seam for future per-message notices; the conversation view does not currently supply it. A message sent as a steer is a sent bubble in the timeline from the moment it is sent, through sending, its queued receipt, steering, steered, steer-missed and delivery-unknown. It never enters the tray, is never counted in its heading and never receives the tray’s Send now action, including after a miss. If the daemon dispatches a missed steer as its own turn, it remains in the timeline. Its read state comes from the turn it joins: “Unread until Claude’s next step.” while the model works, “Unread until the current step finishes.” while a tool runs, “Unread. Claude needs your approval first.”, and “Unread until the current turn ends.” once it missed a turn that is still live; after that turn ends, the status names its queue, deferral or hold (it runs next); at its host’s `steer.delivered` it is drawn there, once, and reads “Read”; a Codex steer recorded after the model’s last step reads “Read after the turn’s last step; ask again for a reply” A refused steer stays queued in the timeline and its status line says why; one the daemon answers `not-steerable` (the turn's runner not taken back yet or still catching up after a restart, the provider not ready) is asked again briefly first when it names its turn (`into`), at 0.5 s doubling within the same five tries a lost answer gets, holding that conversation's later sends meanwhile (at most 7.5 s), since `into` has the daemon refuse any try that comes after that turn ended. Esc while a turn runs takes back the latest unread steer, withdrawn from the outbox while the daemon may not have it, else with `message.cancel` (never `turn.interrupt`): its words and images return to the composer and to the conversation’s draft, so they survive leaving the conversation or quitting. A steer the daemon answers `too-late` for is passed over only while the app still sees it where it was then (steering in that turn, or shown just as it was); the words say whether the next Esc takes back an older or a newer steer or stops the turn. With no steer to take back, Esc stops the turn. Stop keeps unread steers and queued messages: they run next. A Withdraw of a queued message whose withdrawal the daemon answers `too-late` (it left the queue meanwhile) reads its status before sending `turn.interrupt`, and interrupts only a message it reads as its own turn’s (waiting, starting, running or asking): a steering or steered message is not interrupted (an interrupt of a steering message stops its host, C-24.7), one queued again or settled is left alone, and a status it cannot read is reported, with nothing interrupted. A journaled steer names the turn it was sent to (`into`): for Return, the turn the composer steers into when Return is pressed (with no such turn, the message queues); for Send now, the turn its row was offered for. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/UIComposer.swift`, `app/Sources/QueueTray.swift`, `app/Sources/UIQueueTray.swift`, `app/Sources/ConversationStore.swift`, `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `app/Sources/UIModel.swift`]
- **C-29.8** (bounded rendering) Conversation Markdown renders with bounded cost: long messages and code blocks are truncated with a control to expand, and history loads in pages. A page ends after a whole transcript row, so a row's blocks are never split; its cursor is that row's byte offset from the start of the file, which later turns never move, and each page's cursor is below the last. A page reads at most 4 MiB of rows below its cursor (or the one row there, up to 68 MiB, the cap plus 64 MiB, when that row is larger) and up to 8 MiB past the cursor for its calls' results; a page that reaches that cap, or a row too large to read, hands on a cursor; only the file's start ends the history, and the page that reaches the file's first row (after any blank lines) ends it. A page that adds nothing to the timeline but hands on a cursor is followed at once, up to 16 pages per "Load earlier"; opening a conversation reads one page and follows none. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/Markdown.swift`, `app/Sources/UIModel.swift`, `subfleet/conversations/history.py`]
- **C-29.9** (one feed and notifications) The app long-polls one `conversation.watch` and `conversation.events` only for the focused conversation; both run on a dedicated daemon pool with at most one of each per client, never on the pool `message.submit` uses. A turn that completes, fails, needs approval, or becomes `delivery-unknown` while unfocused posts a notification; the Dock badge counts pending approvals. An Allow once notification action is offered only for one identified, pending, unmasked tool request with an `allow` option; responding rechecks its message, conversation, approval id, request hash, state and masking. A stale or masked request opens the conversation for review. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/events.py`, `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.10** (changes and detached work) The app shows each turn's and each conversation's changes (`turn.diff`, `conversation.diff`); above a diff whose `shared` names other conversations it says that the folder was also changed by them during the turn (or since the conversation's first turn began), each by its title (one line, at most 60 characters, cut with an ellipsis; untitled when blank) and whether it still runs, and that the diff may include their edits (C-26.14; `diffSharedWords`). It never commits, pushes or merges on its own, and keeps a Compose view for detached jobs that previews with `submit {dry_run: true}` and dispatches with `submit`. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.11** (launch at login and honest states) "Start at login" uses `SMAppService`. Capacity waiting, fleet-full, external writer, authentication failure, stale usage, an unsupported setting, and a model mismatch each have their own visible state, distinct from completion; a waiting message's status is its `state_reason` (C-24.4) in words (`TurnTimeline.waitingWords`), its kind read from the reason's start (an external writer is `external-writer: …` or `readmit:external-writer`), never from text inside it, which may quote a conversation's title (review of 5e9f2fbd, P2-1), only a `capacity:` reason reads "Waiting for capacity", and a waiting message whose daemon gave no reason reads as such, never as capacity (2026-09-29); a capability the daemon does not offer is disabled with its reason. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.12** (search from the keyboard) ⌘K, and File > Search…, open a palette over the main window (⌘K again closes it) that finds conversations and native sessions whatever the sidebar's search text and provider filter. A candidate matches when every word of the query appears, ignoring case, diacritics and width, in its title, its workspace as the sidebar shows it, its provider, or its messages' text: the person's messages, the answers and the transcript rows of every conversation whose timeline the app holds (those opened since it started; not thoughts, tool calls, errors or notices), and each session's first prompt. After a pause in typing, `conversation.list` with `query` (the query's longest word) adds sessions from the whole catalog, past the page the sidebar loaded, which the daemon matches by title, folder and first prompt; sessions a conversation already continues are left out. Results rank the exact title first (white space aside), then titles holding every word, then titles, workspaces and providers holding them, then message matches, then fuzzy titles: a word found nowhere, of two or more letters, has them in the title in order within three times its length ("sbflt" in "Subfleet"), as Claude Code's palette matches titles, and the query's other words are found as above; within each, the most recent activity first, undated last, then title and id, so the order never depends on the order candidates arrive in. Matching folds text (case folding, canonical decomposition with the marks removed, and width folding) once, keeping unchanged messages' folds between openings, so a word matches part of a character folding makes several of ("s" in "ß"). Former titles are not kept by the daemon or the app, so a renamed conversation is found by its current title only. Matched words (a fuzzy title's letters) are highlighted, by the same folding, and a message match shows who wrote the message holding the most of its message-only words (the newest such), on one line, cut around the first match with "…" where it was cut; opening it scrolls the conversation to that message. Whenever a search reveal targets a row in the queue, the tray’s state projection includes every queued row, independently of background tasks. The tray stays expanded after that reveal is consumed. Once layout reports the matched row, search reveals it within the tray’s own scroll view, leaving the timeline’s follow and opening pin unchanged. A search request scrolls at most twice, after 250 ms and then another 550 ms from when its target is present in the chosen container; other row arrivals neither restart those delays nor repeat the reveal. An empty query lists everything by recent activity. After the results the palette offers to start a new conversation (the sheet File > New conversation opens), selected when nothing matches. Up and Down (Control-P and Control-N, Tab and Shift-Tab) move the selection, Page Up and Page Down by eight, Return (or ⌘↩) opens it, and the composer takes the keys once the conversation shows (a locked session has none); Escape or a click outside closes the palette and gives the keys back to where they were. While it is open, the window behind it takes no clicks or shortcuts; the menus still work, and File > New conversation closes it to open its sheet. At most 50 results are listed and the rest counted; words past twelve are counted and not searched. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/SearchPalette.swift`, `app/Sources/UISearchPalette.swift`]
- **C-29.13** (readable text) Conversation text (the timeline, its Markdown and code blocks, the composer, the sidebar's rows and provider badges, the conversation header, status lines and served chips, tool rows and approval cards and sheets, the Changes pane, and the search palette) is set in reading sizes larger than the system's, except three small controls other changes were rewriting (the Stop links beside a turn's status and on the live strip, and the sidebar's approval count), which stay at the system's caption size until those land: at actual size body 16 pt (Claude Code's Large; the system's body is 13), headings 24, 20 and 17, secondary prose 15, code 14.5, metadata 13 and 12. macOS's text styles do not follow Dynamic Type, so the sizes are points times one scale. View > Bigger (⌘+, and ⌘= in the window), Smaller (⌘−) and Actual size (⌘0) step it as Claude Code's zoom does, half a zoom level (×1.2^½) at a time, from 1.2^−1 (83 %) to 1.2^5 (249 %); it persists in UserDefaults under `conversationTextScale`, where a value outside that range reads as its nearest bound and one that is not a number as actual size. The conversation column's widest (56 times the body size, 896 pt at actual size) and a message bubble's (40 times) grow with it. At every scale no size is below 10 pt, metadata stays smaller than body, and code lies between them. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/TextScale.swift`, `app/Sources/UIReading.swift`]
- **C-29.14** (suggested conversations in the app) Against a daemon that advertises `suggestions.v1` (C-25.1), the app shows suggested conversations (C-25.7) in two places: a card in the timeline of the conversation that suggested one, on the turn that suggested it, one card per suggestion folded from its `suggestion` events and updated in place; and a Suggested section at the top of the sidebar listing every open or starting suggestion, with or without an origin, newest first, under the sidebar's provider filter and search, with a count of all of them. A card shows the title, the summary, where Start will run it as the daemon decided (`workspace_kind`): a new worktree of its folder's checkout on its own branch, its folder in place (saying so, and that there is no worktree, when the agent asked for one in a folder that is no checkout), or a new scratch folder; then the provider and model; the brief behind Show the brief (its whole text, which the card fetches by id when a listing cut it), and, while it is open, Dismiss and Start. Start starts it at Ask, or read-only for a Codex suggestion while Codex conversations are; its menu offers the wider permissions, each behind a confirmation that the agent will act without asking from its first message. Start opens the new conversation. A sidebar row opens the card in the detail column, naming the conversation it came from. A person-only refusal of Start or Dismiss keeps the suggestion open and displays C-25.7's fixed reason; a refused secret-shaped folder is never echoed. A card says Starting… while a start is under way, why a start cut short has not finished, with Start again, Started with a link to the conversation, Dismissed, or Withdrawn by the agent that suggested it. Cards and the section follow the watch feed's suggestion rows without another read and never move a suggestion back to an earlier state, and a listing's preview never replaces a whole brief already read; an answer read at a feed sequence (`watch_seq`) older than a suggestion's latest feed row neither removes that suggestion nor replaces it, and a whole listing as new as that row removes one that closed and aged out of it; `suggestion.list` is read on connecting and with the conversation list. A row with no origin changes no conversation. Their text is set as C-29.13 sets conversation text. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/Suggestions.swift`, `app/Sources/UISuggestions.swift`, `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `app/Sources/ConversationStore.swift`, `app/Sources/UIModel.swift`, `app/Sources/UIWindow.swift`]

## 30. Catalog and legacy continuity

- **C-30.1** (catalog out of process) Discovery of existing native sessions runs in a separate process with a 20 s wall-clock cap per run, writes `catalog.json` atomically with titles and prompts scrubbed, re-reads only files whose size or mtime changed, indexes every session (exclusions of lane runs and Codex exec or subagent rollouts come before any limit), and never writes a native store. The daemon never scans the Claude projects tree or Codex session trees on a request or control thread. The conversation service starts a run every `conversations.catalog_interval_s` (default 60 s; 0 means only on `catalog.refresh`) and on request, never waits for one, and stops a run still alive after 60 s by its process group. Before each file it publishes, a run checks that the service that started it is still open and that the state root it locked is still there: it inherits the read end of a pipe (the fence, above descriptor 2) whose only write end the service holds, and reads end-of-file there once the service closes or dies; and the lock it holds must still be `<root>/catalog.lock`. It publishes by path and never creates a root, so a root removed or replaced between a check and the write after it can fail that write (the run then exits 1) or receive it. A run that finds the service gone or the root removed or replaced at a check publishes nothing more and exits 3; one whose fence descriptor is not a pipe writes nothing and exits 4; one that finds another run holding the lock exits 0. A probe of the lock (`conversation.list`'s `refreshing`, the check before a run starts) takes it shared, and a run tries for it for up to a second before it leaves the catalog to the run holding it, so a probe is never taken for a run. The service logs a nonzero exit of a run it still tracks and did not stop itself, naming the reason for 3 and 4. `close()` stops tracking the service's run and closes the fence, sends SIGTERM to the run's process group and, if a bounded wait does not see it end, SIGKILL and a second bounded wait, all before it returns, and starts no run after; a run that declines after `close()` is therefore logged by no one. `conversation.list` answers whether `catalog.json` is absent, damaged or old, and reports its `state` (`absent`, `unreadable`, `stale` when older than three intervals, `fresh`), `age_s`, `stale_after_s` and `refreshing` (design D-23). Each conversation carries `last_activity`: the later of its row’s `updated_at` and its bound native transcript mtime from the last catalog run, even when that catalog is stale. The list sorts by that activity before applying the limit, reading only cached state and never scanning transcripts or the projects tree. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/catalog.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-30.2** (what continues in place) A Claude session in the catalog is continuable when it is not a lane run; a live process outside Subfleet makes it wait (C-26.3). Its workspace is the cwd of the transcript copy it continues (the newest, when a moved session left one under each project directory): the latest of that copy's cwds that its project directory names (a session that moved to a new project and then within it keeps the new project), else its first. The catalog shows a Claude session once, by the copy it continues, with that workspace, and judges its continuability by it, as `conversation.open` does; it reads again a transcript whose cached record predates the workspace. A Codex thread is continuable only in an enrolled lane's home; a thread in `~/.codex` continues only by labelled handoff (C-30.3), because no lane owns that home and C-10.3 keeps the desktop login out of admission. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/catalog.py`]
- **C-30.3** (handoffs are labelled) A cross-provider or out-of-home continuation is a new conversation whose first message is a scrubbed continuity brief (C-23.14; Claude transcripts and Codex rollouts both have brief readers, bounded by C-23.36's caps) and which records `handoff_from` (provider, native id, transcript path, brief SHA-256). It is never presented as the same native session. It is made by `conversation.handoff` (design D-18, §5), idempotent by request id, on the bounded file pool and without running git (C-25.3). The source's pending person messages (queued or waiting with no job and not claimed by the dispatcher, or waiting on a job with no attempt; a claimed one refuses the handoff until its job exists) move behind the brief in their order, each withdrawn from the source under the guard `message.cancel` uses (C-24.7); a source with a live turn is refused and nothing changes; the source keeps its history. Texts are published and each moved attachment's stored file checked (present, hashing right) before any job is cancelled. A source with jobs to cancel is first fenced with `blocked_by='handoff:<request id>'`, which prevents dispatch; each cancellation is guarded by no attempt and audited with that request id (IR-2). The new conversation, moved messages, source withdrawals and fence lift commit in one conversation-store transaction. On failure, prepared files are discarded and messages whose jobs this handoff cancelled return to `queued` in their original positions, unbound from the cancelled jobs under a new turn sequence, in the transaction that lifts the fence. The control loop retries a failed lift and, on its first pass after restart, recovers stale fences before dispatch, leaving active handoffs alone. A retry also recovers messages cancelled by the same request before planning, including cancellations from before fences were introduced. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/conversations/codex_brief.py`, `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`]
- **C-30.4** (legacy cockpit import) The v1 `outbox.sqlite3` is the legacy cockpit's message outbox, not a notice outbox, and `cockpit-client/pending-messages.json` its client's journal of unacknowledged sends (manifest rows `outbox` and `cockpit`, milestone 9). Each outbox message is classified individually by its row status, the cockpit's own: `finished`, `error` and `cancelled` are terminal, every other status is not. A session is named in one spelling: a UUID in lower case, and a Codex id (`codex:<home>:<id>`) by its thread id. A terminal message of a Claude session whose transcript is found becomes a read-only history row of that session's one conversation (C-24.1): the one it already has, whatever its origin, while that has no message of its own; otherwise one the import creates with origin `legacy`, bound to the native id and created from the transcript as `conversation.open` creates a native conversation (workspace as C-30.2, design D-9's settings); the row keeps the legacy message id, has origin `legacy`, state `complete` (finished), `failed` (error, or a cancelled unknown delivery a person marked handled) or `cancelled` (withdrawn before dispatch), no job and no predecessor, and settings holding only what the outbox recorded. A history row is never queued or dispatched, and is written only ahead of every other message of its conversation. A non-terminal message, and every journal entry whatever the outbox says of its id (an entry is a send the cockpit app has not seen acknowledged), keeps its legacy owner and is reported, and so does every message of its session. So does a session a live cockpit worker holds now (a pid in `native-workers.json` that is alive), whatever its outbox rows say. A session a live Claude process outside Subfleet is registered for (no `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT` in its environment) gets no history this pass, but its conversation is not held for it: dispatch and launch already make a turn there wait while the process lives (C-26.3), and a hold would outlast it. A journal that cannot be read (an OS error, not JSON, not an object keyed by provider-qualified session ids with requests naming such ids, or an entry with no request or whose request's message id is not a string or image paths not a list, or any text UTF-8 cannot hold, a lone surrogate a JSON escape made, each checked before anything is counted) is never read as empty: it holds every session, while a missing journal holds none. The same holds for an outbox that exists and cannot be read (`unreadable-database`: not a database, or not the cockpit's columns, a `sequence` that is no integer included; `no-messages-table`), for a workers file that cannot be read (a lone surrogate included), and while the cockpit broker holds `broker.lock` (probed with a shared, non-blocking lock on a read-only handle, released at once; the file is never created), since a running broker may dispatch into any session. A missing outbox holds nothing, and the journal, the cockpit's activity and the fence are read and run whether or not the outbox can be. A `--v1-state` holding none of the manifest's entries (missing, empty, another directory), or holding a v2 state root's files (`state.sqlite3`, `conversations.sqlite3`, `daemon.lock`), is not read as a cockpit that holds nothing: `--legacy-cockpit` refuses it (exit 7) before it takes `daemon.lock` or opens a store (unless a store exists, which may record a retirement), and a milestone pass reads nothing from it and changes no hold. `--legacy-cockpit` and a milestone pass also refuse a `--state-root` that looks like a v1 state directory, so no `daemon.lock` lands in one. `--legacy-cockpit --cockpit-retired` reads no v1 state and, in one transaction, lifts every legacy hold, forgets every held session and records the retirement (creating the conversation store if there is none), for a cockpit that will never run again; every later pass then reads nothing from the cockpit, neither its outbox nor its journal, holds nothing, and is not refused for a v1 state that is gone. The import never moves, queues or sends a legacy message. Every conversation bound to a native session a pass finds held, whatever its origin or provider, gets the import's hold: its `legacy_hold` column, set to the reason, beside and never in `blocked_by`. The hold keeps every turn off the conversation (C-24.5). A turn that kept running across the daemon restart is stopped when the daemon adopts it: one whose message the relay log does not show handed over is stopped before any of its output is replayed, so its message is never written, and is re-admitted once the hold lifts unless a person had asked to stop it; any other is stopped through D-13 only after replay restores its delivered state. A durable no-send outcome still fences replay after a crash even when the legacy hold has since lifted. The pass records the sessions it holds (and whether it holds every one), creating the conversation store to do so if there is none yet, and a conversation bound to one of them afterwards (`conversation.open`) is bound held. The pass reports each held conversation `bound-session-held` with its `blocked_by` and every message of it that is not settled, which runs once the hold lifts unless it is cancelled first (with the daemon running, as `message.cancel` is a daemon op). The first pass that finds its session settled lifts the hold and reports `bound-session-released`. Only the import sets or lifts the hold: no turn outcome replaces it, `conversation.unblock` refuses a conversation only the import holds, and `conversation.unblock` and `message.resolve` clear `blocked_by` alone. The fence runs before any history is placed. A terminal message that a pass cannot place is reported with its disposition: its session held, its transcript not found, a session that cannot continue here, or a transcript that raised while it was read (`transcript-unreadable`), each imported by a later pass once that changes; and its conversation already has messages of its own, its id is already a message of the store's own, its row cannot be read (a message id, a payload, the payload's image paths or service tier, text in the payload or receipt that UTF-8 cannot hold, or a timestamp, each checked before anything is written for the row), or a session that is not Claude's, none of which a later pass changes. One session or row never ends a pass. Every message and journal entry appears in the report with its disposition, by id and never by content. Repeated imports create nothing new: history is idempotent by legacy message id. Notices an earlier import made from outbox rows stay as they are. `python -m subfleet.importer --legacy-cockpit [--dry-run]` runs these two rows alone and opens no `state.sqlite3`; a real run holds `daemon.lock` for its whole length and is refused while a daemon holds it; a dry run imports into a scratch copy of `conversations.sqlite3`; neither writes under the v1 state or a native transcript. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/importer.py`, `subfleet/conversations/legacy.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`]
